Noncommutative Geometry, Spectral Asymptotics, and Semiclassical Analysis
Raphaël Ponge

Abstract
. Semiclassical analysis and noncommutative geometry are two pillars of quantum theory. It’s only recently that bridges between them have been emerging. In this monograph, we combine various techniques from functional analysis and spectral theory to obtain semiclassical Weyl laws and extensions of Connes’ integration formula for a large class of noncommutative manifolds (i.e., spectral triples). These results generalize and simplify recent results of McDonald-Sukochev-Zanin. In particular, all the regularity assumptions and restrictions on dimension there are removed in our approach. Moreover, the Tauberian condition used by McDonald-Sukochev-Zanin is replaced by a weaker spectral theoretic condition, called Condition (W). That condition holds in fairly greater generality and significantly open the scope of applicability of the main results. We also give Tauberian conditions that imply Condition (W). These Tauberian conditions are easier to check in practice than the Tauberian condition of McDonald-Sukochev-Zanin and are satisfied in numerous examples. The need for these conditions was highlighted by Alain Connes in an online seminar. The main results of this memoire are illustrated by semiclassical Weyl’s laws and integration formulas in the following settings: (i) Dirichlet and Neumann problems on Euclidean domains with smooth boundaries; (ii) closed Riemannian manifolds; (iii) open manifolds with conformally cusp metrics of finite volume; (iv) quantum tori; and (v) sub-Riemannian manifolds. In the settings (i) and (ii), we recover well known semiclassical Weyl laws, as well as Weyl laws for Steklov eigenvalues. The only novelty is obtaining them from old results of Carleman, Minakshisundaram, and Minakshisundaram-Pleijel on heat kernel asymptotics. Moreover, we get a new proof of the Weyl laws of Birman-Solomyak for negative order pseudodifferential operators. We actually prove the result for suitable closures of the spaces of pseudodifferential operators. This then leads to a far-reaching refinement of Connes’ trace theorem for (classical) pseudodifferential operators on closed manifolds. This subsumes its recent extension by Kordyukov-Sukochev-Zanin. In the setting (iii), the results are established for fractional powers of the Dirac operator and magnetic Laplacians, since those operators have discrete spectra. The results in this geometric setting are new. Note that the metrics at stake need not be complete. Therefore, our semiclassical Weyl laws complement recent semiclassical Weyl laws of Braverman and Dai-Yan for complete Riemannian manifolds. In the setting (iv), i.e., quantum tori, the semiclassical Weyl laws provide a positive answer to a conjecture of Edward McDonald and the author. The integration formulas are refinements of several previous analogues of Connes’ integration formula for quantum tori. In the setting (v), i.e., sub-Riemmannian geometry, the semiclassical Weyl’s laws and integration formulas are new results. The former connect with a recent string of results on spectral asymptotics and quantum limits in sub-Riemannian geometry. In the case contact manifolds, the integration formulas are refinements of integration formulas of Kordyukov-Sukochev-Zanin and the author. The main focus is on equiregular sub-Riemannin structures, but we also discuss the extension of the results to the singular setting in the special case of the Baouendi-Grushin example.



Chapter 1
Introduction

1.1.
Main Results

Noncommutative geometry and semiclassical analysis are two important fields within the broader area of quantum theory. Recently, bridges between them have been emerging (see, e.g.,
[
137
,
156
,
157
,
165
,
192
]
). This has its roots, on the one hand, in the functional analytic approach to semiclassical analysis of Schrödinger operators pioneered by Birman-Solomyak
[
21
,
30
]
and Simon
[
180
]
, and, on the other hand, in the paramount role of operator ideals in noncommutative geometry program of Connes
[
53
]
.

In the framework of noncommutative geometry, the role of manifolds is played by spectral triples
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D)
, where
\mathscr{A}
is a (unital)
*
-algebra represented by bounded operators on the Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}
and
D
is an unbounded selfadjoint operator on
\mathscr{H}
with compact resolvent which commutes with elements of
\mathscr{A}
up to bounded operators. The notion of dimension is replaced by
p
-summability, in the sense that
D^{-1}
lies in the weak Schatten class
\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
.
The main prototype of a spectral triple is the Dirac spectral triple
(C^{\infty}(M),L^{2}(M,\not{S}),\not{D})
associated with any closed Riemannian spin manifold. A closely related example is the square-root Laplacian spectral triple
(C^{\infty}(M),L^{2}(M),\sqrt{\Delta_{g}})
.

In a recent article
[
137
]
McDonald-Sukochev-Zanin established a semiclassical Weyl law for spectral triples
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D)
, i.e., for the counting functions of Schrödinger operators
h^{2}D^{2}+V
under the semiclassical limit
h\rightarrow 0^{+}
. This semiclassical Weyl law is established as a consequence of a general Tauberian theorem for non-commuting pairs of operators on Hilbert space (see
[
137
, Theorem 1.2]
). In order to apply this Tauberian theorem three conditions for the spectral triple are required:

(a)

p
-summability with
p>2
.

(b)

Lipschitz regularity, i.e.,
[|D|,a]\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
for all
a\in\mathscr{A}
.

(c)

A Tauberian condition for the zeta functions
\operatorname{Tr}[a^{z}|D|^{-z}]
,
a\geq 0
,
\Re z>p
(see Condition
4.2
for the precise statement).

It would be desirable to remove the technical conditions (a) and (b), especially the former, since it prevents us from dealing with 1-dimensional and 2-dimensional examples. It is actually conjectured in
[
137
]
that the main results there should hold for
p\leq 2
.

In addition, it would be good to relate the Tauberian condition (c) to more standard Tauberian conditions in terms of zeta functions
\operatorname{Tr}[a|D|^{-z}]
or heat traces
\operatorname{Tr}[ae^{-tD^{2}}]
,
t>0
, which do not involve powers of
a
. The need for this was pointed out by Alain Connes during an online seminar in May 2021. He also pointed that closedness under holomorphic functional calculus should play a role.

The main aim of this monograph is to show that, in the setup of spectral triples, results such as semiclassical Weyl laws and Connes’ integration formulas, as well as Weyl laws for Steklov eigenvalues are mere consequences of
classical
Weyl laws for some Laplace-type operators, namely, operators of the form
aD^{2}a
, where
a
ranges over positive invertible elements of
\mathscr{A}
. This simplifies the approach of
[
137
]
and leads to stronger results. In particular, we remove the conditions (a)–(b) in the approach in the approach of
[
137
]
and replace the Tauberian condition of
[
137
]
by a more general spectral theoretic condition (see Condition (W) below). That condition is weaker and easier to check in practice, which allows us to widen significantly the scope of applications of the results and cover various new examples. We also exhibit Tauberian conditions (Condition (Z) and Condition (H); see below) which imply Condition (W) and answer the above-mentioned comments of Alain Connes.

1.1.1.
Spectral Condition (W)

Let
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D)
be a
p
-summable (unital) spectral triple, where
p
may be
any
positive number. We denote by
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
the closure of
\mathscr{A}
in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
. We also let
\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
be the cone of invertible positive elements of
\mathscr{A}
(i.e., selfadjoint elements with positive spectrum), and set
\mathscr{A}_{++}=\mathscr{A}\cap\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
. Moreover, given any
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
, we let

\lambda_{0}(aD^{2}a)\leq\lambda_{1}(aD^{2}a)\leq\cdots

be the
positive
eigenvalue of
aD^{2}a
(counted with multiplicity).

The approach of this monograph relies on trading the Tauberian condition of
[
137
]
for some spectral condition (Condition (W) below) which does not involve any additional regularity conditions or restriction on the degree of summability for spectral triples. As we shall see this condition is actually weaker than the Tauberian condition of
[
137
]
and will allow us to cover a wealth of new examples. This condition is formulated as follows.

Condition (W)
.

For every
a\in{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
, we have

(1.1)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{-\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(aD^{2}a\big)=\tau\big[a^{-p}\big]^{-\frac{2}{p}},

where
\tau:\overline{\mathscr{A}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a given positive linear map.

We can see Condition (W) as requiring to have Weyl laws for conformal deformations of the square
D^{2}
, where conformal deformation is meant in the sense of Connes-Moscovici
[
57
]
.

1.1.2.
Spectral asymptotics for Birman-Schwinger operators

As we shall see, Condition (W) allows us to get spectral asymptotics for Birman-Schwinger type operators

|D|^{-q/2}a|D|^{-q/2}
,
q>0
, as
a
ranges over the
C^{*}
-algebra
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
. Once we have these spectral asymptotics, routine arguments enable us to get semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas (see below).

In what follows, given any
q>0
, we denote by
\mu_{j}(|D|^{-q/2}a|D|^{-q/2})
,
j\geq 0
, the singular values of

|D|^{-q/2}a|D|^{-q/2}
(i.e., the eigenvalues of the absolute value
||D|^{-q/2}a|D|^{-q/2}|
). If
a^{*}=a
, then
|D|^{-q/2}a|D|^{-q/2}
is selfadjoint, and so we further denote by
\lambda_{j}^{\pm}(|D|^{-q/2}a|D|^{-q/2})
,
j\geq 0
, its positive/negative eigenvalues (counted with multiplicity).

More precisely, we establish the following spectral asymptotics,

Proposition 1.1

(Spectral Asymptotics)
.

Assume Condition
(W)
holds. Given any
q>0
, for every
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}
, we have

(1.2)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\mu_{j}\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\tau\left[|a|^{\frac{p}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}},

(1.3)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\lambda_{j}^{\pm}\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\tau\left[\big(a_{\pm}\big)^{\frac{p}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}}\quad(\text{if}\ a^{*}=a).

Here
a_{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}(|a|\pm a)
are the positive/negative parts of
a
. In particular, by Theorem
1.7
the above spectral asymptotics hold under any of the Tauberian conditions
(H)
and
(Z)
.

In the special case
q=p/2
, a version of (
1.2
) for
a\geq 0
is provided by
[
137
, Theorem 1.4]
, and a version of (
1.3
) is provided by
[
137
, Theorem 1.5]
. Those results in
[
137
]
further requires Lipschitz regularity and
p
-summability with
p>2
. These two extra conditions are removed in Proposition
1.1
. The spectral asymptotics (
1.3
)–(
1.2
) for
q\neq p/2
are new.

1.1.3.
Semiclassical Weyl laws

Combining the spectral asymptotics (
1.3
) with the Birman-Schwinger principle enables us to get semiclassical Weyl laws for (fractional) Schrödinger operators,

H_{V}^{(q)}(h):=h^{2q}\left(D^{2}\right)^{q}+V,\qquad h>0.

More precisely, in our setup the spectrum of
H_{V}^{(q)}(h)
is discrete and bounded from below. Given any energy level
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote
by
N(H_{V}^{(q)}(h);\lambda)
the number of eigenvalues of
H_{V}^{(q)}(h)
that are
<\lambda
(see Eq. (
3.27
) for the precise definition).
We then obtain the following semiclassical Weyl law.

Theorem 1.2

(Semiclassical Weyl law)
.

Assume Condition
(W)
holds. Given any
q>0
and
V^{*}=V\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}
, for any energy level
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

(1.4)

\displaystyle\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{p}N\big(H_{V}^{(q)}(h);\lambda\big)=\tau\Big[\left(V-\lambda\right)_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}\Big].

In particular, this semiclassical Weyl law holds under the Tauberian conditions
(H)
and
(Z)
.

For
q=1
we recover from (
1.4
) the semiclassical Weyl law of
[
137
]
without any extra regularity or requiring
p
to be
>2
. The semiclassical Weyl law (
1.4
) for
q\neq 1
are new even for
p>2
.

It is known that the semiclassical Weyl law for
q=2
implies a classical Weyl law for the eigenvalue distribution of
D^{2}
. Therefore, we may interpret Theorem
1.2
as some kind of converse of this statement.

1.1.4.
Integration formulas

The Weyl laws (
1.2
)–(
1.3
) have further implications in noncommutative geometry. In the framework of noncommutative geometry the role of the integral is played by positive traces in
\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}
(see
[
53
]
). An important class of such traces is provided by Dixmier traces
[
69
]
. Following Connes
[
53
]
an operator
A\in\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}
is called
measurable
if it takes the same value on all Dixmier traces. Equivalently,
A
is measurable if and only if

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int A:=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{j<N}\lambda_{j}(A)\ \text{exists},

where
\{\lambda_{j}(A)\}
is an eigenvalue sequence for
A
(see
[
129
,
157
]
). The limit
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int A
is precisely the NC integral of
A
. A stronger notion of measurability (called
strong measurability
or
positive measurability
) requires
A
to take the same value on
all normalized positive traces
.

Given a closed Riemannian manifold
(M^{n},g)
, Connes’ integration formula asserts that, if
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, then the operator
f\Delta_{g}^{-n/2}
is strongly measurable, and we have

(1.5)

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int f\Delta_{g}^{-\frac{n}{2}}=c(n)\int_{M}f(x)d\nu_{g}(x),\qquad c(n):=(2\pi)^{-n}|\mathbb{B}^{n}|,

where
\nu_{g}(x)
is the Riemannian measure (see
[
52
,
108
]
). This shows that the NC integral recaptures the Riemannnian measure.

Spectral asymptotics of form (
1.3
) with
q/p=1
implies an even stronger form of measurability. If
A^{*}=A
it can be shown that if
\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\lambda_{j}^{\pm}(A)
exists, then
A
is strongly measurable, and
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int A
agrees with the difference of these limits (see, e.g.,
[
157
]
). This is extended to non-selfadjoint operators by looking at real and imaginary parts. If these conditions are satisfied we shall say that
A
is
spectrally measurable
. Thus, spectral measurability is an even stronger form of measurability than strong measurability.

It actually follows from results of Birman-Solomyak
[
26
,
27
,
28
]
that, on any closed manifold
M
, the
\Psi
DOs of order
-\dim M
are spectrally measurable. Birman-Solomyak’s result actually predates Connes’ integration formula (
1.5
) and implies a stronger form of this result.

In the light of this, the spectral asymptotics (
1.2
)–(
1.3
) imply far-reaching extensions of Connes’ integration formula to our class of spectral triples.

Theorem 1.3

(Integration Formulas)
.

Assume Condition
(W)
holds. For any
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}
, the operators

a|D|^{-p}
,
|D|^{-p/2}a|D|^{-p/2}
, and
||D|^{-p/2}a|D|^{-p/2}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

(1.6)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int a|D|^{-p}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}=\tau\big[a\big],

(1.7)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\big||D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}\big|^{\frac{p}{q}}=\tau\left[|a|\right].

In particular, the above formulas hold under any of the Tauberian conditions
(H)
or
(Z)
.

The integration formula for
a|D|^{-p}
is alluded to in
[
137
, p. 77]
. The integration formula for
||D|^{-p/2}a|D|^{-p/2}|
seems to be new.

1.1.5.
Extensions to unbounded potentials

It is important to understand to which extent the spectral asymptotics (
1.2
)–(
1.3
), semiclassical Weyl law (
1.4
) and integration formulas (
1.6
)–(
1.7
) above continue to hold whenever the corresponding r.h.s. make sense. Note that, from a spectral theoretic perspective, this involves considering
unbounded
potentials. On
\mathbb{R}^{n}
or bounded domains in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the extension of the semiclassical Weyl laws to optimal
L^{r}
-spaces of potentials was made possible by the CLR inequality of Cwikel
[
64
]
, Lieb
[
127
,
128
]
, and Rozenblum
[
163
,
164
]
.

The approach of Cwikel
[
64
]
relied on proving some weak-Schatten quasi-norm estimates for Birman-Schwinger operators that were conjectured by Simon
[
180
]
. Combining these estimates with the perturbation theory of Birman-Solomyak further enables us to extends the spectral asymptotics for eigenvalues of Birman-Schwinger operators and the integration formulas to the relevant
L^{r}
-classes. We refer to
[
124
,
165
,
168
,
186
,
191
]
, and the references therein, for various extensions of the Cwikel estimates.

In this monograph, we lay down a general process for extending to “
L^{r}
-potentials” the spectral asymptotics (
1.2
)–(
1.3
). This will then allow us to extend to this class of potential the semiclassical Weyl law (
1.4
) and integration formulas (
1.6
)–(
1.7
). Conceptually, this is only a mere elaboration of the approaches of Birman-Solomyak (see, e.g.,
[
24
]
) and Simon
[
180
]
to semiclassical Weyl laws for
L^{r}
-potentials. The only technical difference here is that, as we are working in a noncommutative setting, we need to work with
noncommutative

L^{r}
-spaces.

We proceed as follows. Suppose that Condition (W) holds and
\tau
is the restriction of a positive faithful trace
\tau:\mathscr{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, where
\mathscr{M}\subseteq\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
is the von Neumann algebra generated by
\mathscr{A}
(i.e., its weak closure in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
). As
\tau
is finite, for any
r\in[1,\infty)
we may define the noncommutative
L^{r}
-space
L_{r}(\mathscr{M})
1
1

1

Throughout this monograph we shall use subscripts for the exponents of NC
L^{p}
-spaces.
as the closure of
\mathscr{M}
with respect to the Banach norm
x\rightarrow\|x\|_{r}:=\left(\tau\big[|x|^{r}\big]\right)^{\frac{1}{r}}
.

Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
)
.

Let
r>0
, and set
\hat{r}=\max(r,1)
. There is a continuous
*
-invariant norm
\|\cdot\|_{(r)}
on
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
such that

(i)

The inclusion of
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
into
L_{\hat{r}}(\mathscr{M})
is continuous with respect to the
\|\cdot\|_{(r)}
-topology.

(ii)

There is a constant
C_{r}>0
such that

(1.8)

\big\|(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{p}{4r}}a(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{p}{4r}}\big\|_{r,\infty}\leq C_{r}\|a\|_{(r)}\qquad\forall a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}.

If Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
) holds, then we denote by
\mathscr{V}_{r}
the Banach space completion of
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
with respect to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{(r)}
. For instance, for bounded domains
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
we have
\mathscr{M}=L^{\infty}(\Omega)
and we can take

\mathscr{V}_{r}=L^{r}(\Omega)\quad(r>1),\qquad\mathscr{V}_{1}=L^{s}(\Omega),\ s>1,\qquad\mathscr{V}_{r}=L^{1}(\Omega)\quad(r<1).

In fact, in the critical case
r=1
we may even take
\mathscr{V}_{1}
be the Orlicz space
L\!\log\!L(\Omega)
if
n
is even (see
[
168
,
185
,
186
]
).

If
r=pq^{-1}
and
x\in\mathscr{V}_{r}
, then the estimate (
1.8
) ensures that
|D|^{-q/2}x|D|^{-q/2}
is in the weak Schatten class
\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty}
. Birman-Solomyak’s perturbation theory
[
21
,
29
]
then enables to spectral asymptotics (
1.2
)–(
1.3
) continue to elements in the spaces
x\in\mathscr{V}_{r}
. Namely, we obtain the following result.

Proposition 1.4
.

Suppose that Condition
(W)
is satisfied. Let
q>0
, and assume further that Condition
(
\textup{C}_{r}
)
holds with
r:=pq^{-1}
.
Given any
x\in\mathscr{V}_{r}
, we have

(1.9)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\mu_{j}\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}x|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\tau\left[|x|^{\frac{p}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}},

(1.10)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\lambda_{j}^{\pm}\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}x|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\tau\left[\big(x_{\pm}\big)^{\frac{p}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}}\quad(\text{if}\ x^{*}=x).

Moreover, if
V=V^{*}\in\mathscr{V}_{r}
the compactness of
|D|^{-q/2}V|D|^{-q/2}
ensures that
V
is
(D^{2})^{q}
-form compact, and so the fractional Scrödinger operator

H_{V}^{(q)}(h)=h^{2q}(D^{2})^{q}+V
,
h>0
, make sense as form sums and are bounded from below operators with pure district spectrum. We then obtain the following extension of the semiclassical Weyl law (
1.4
).

Theorem 1.5

(Semiclassical Weyl law;
\mathscr{V}_{r}
-version)
.

Suppose that Condition
(W)
is satisfied. Let
q>0
, and assume that Condition
(
\textup{C}_{r}
)
holds with
r=p(2q)^{-1}
. Then,
the semiclassical Weyl law (
1.4
) holds for all
V=V^{*}\in\mathscr{V}_{r}
, i.e.,
for any energy level
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

(1.11)

\displaystyle\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{p}N\big(H_{V}^{(q)}(h);\lambda\big)=\tau\Big[\left(V-\lambda\right)_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}\Big].

We further have the following extension of the integration formulas (
1.6
)–(
1.7
).

Theorem 1.6

(Integration Formulas;
\mathscr{V}_{1}
-version)
.

Assume that Condition
(W)
and Condition
(
\textup{C}_{1}
)
are satisfied. For every
x\in\mathscr{V}_{1}
, the operators
|D|^{-p/2}x|D|^{-p/2}
and
||D|^{-p/2}x|D|^{-p/2}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

(1.12)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}x|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}=\tau\big[x\big],

(1.13)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\big||D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}x|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}\big|=\tau\left[|x|\right].

1.1.6.
Tauberian conditions

There are various ways to establish Weyl laws. They are often deduced from Tauberian theorems. The approach of
[
137
]
relies on some new Tauberian condition. As mentioned above, the need for a more standard Tauberian condition was pointed out by Alain Connes. From this perspective, we establish the following Tauberian criteria for Condition (W).

Theorem 1.7

(Tauberian Theorem)
.

Assume there exists a Fréchet subalgebra
\mathscr{B}\subseteq\overline{\mathscr{A}}
which contains
\mathscr{A}
and is closed under holomorphic functional calculus such that one of the following conditions is satisfied:

•

Condition (Z)
. For every
a\in\mathscr{B}
, the function

\operatorname{Tr}\big[a|D|^{-s}\big]-p\tau(a)(s-p)^{-1},\qquad\Re s>p,

has a unique continuous extension to the halfplane
\Re s\geq p
.

•

Condition (H)
.
There is
\delta>0
such that, for all
a\in\mathscr{B}
, as
t\rightarrow 0^{+}
we have

\operatorname{Tr}\big[ae^{-tD^{2}}\big]=\Gamma\left(1+\frac{p}{2}\right)t^{-\frac{p}{2}}\left[\tau(a)+\operatorname{O}\big(t^{\delta}\big)\right].

Then the Weyl law (
1.1
) is satisfied for all
a\in\mathscr{A}_{++}
, i.e., Condition
(W)
holds.

Theorem
1.7
is a refinement of the Tauberian theorem of
[
137
]
. First, all the extra summability and summability assumptions in
[
137
]
are removed from the picture. Second, the proof of Theorem
1.7
involves showing that Condition (Z) and Condition (H) implies a weaker version of the Tauberian condition of
[
137
]
and showing that this weaker version implies Condition (W) (see Chapter
3
). Therefore, we see that we get the Weyl law (
1.1
) under a weaker form of the Tauberian condition of
[
137
]
.

Condition (Z) and Condition (H) are the types of Tauberian conditions sought for by Alain Connes in his comments mentioned above.

In practice, they are easier to check than the Tauberian condition of
[
137
]
. As we shall see in the second half of this monograph, they allow us to deal with a wealth of new examples.

Corollary 1.8
.

The main results of this monograph Proposition
1.1
through Theorem
1.6
hold true if we replace Condition
(W)
by Condition
(H)
or Condition
(Z)
.

1.1.7.
Functional analysis – main technical lemmas

The main results mentioned above follow are consequences of Proposition
1.1
and Theorem
1.7
. Therefore, the bulk of our approach is proving these two results. This involves a significant amount of functional analysis and spectral theory. More specifically, the proofs of both results rely on three main technical lemmas on weak Schatten properties of commutators and differences of powers of operators naturally associated with spectral triples.

In what follows, given any
r>0
, we denote by
\|\cdot\|_{r,\infty}
the quasi-norm of the weak Schatten class
\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty}
.

The first new technical result is about the weak Schatten properties of fractional commutators
[|D|^{-q},a]
,
a\in\mathscr{A}
.

Lemma 1.9

(Commutator Lemma)
.

Given any
q>0
, for all
a\in\mathscr{A}
, we have

\left[|D|^{-q},a\right]\in\mathscr{L}_{(q+1)^{-1}p,\infty}.

In fact, there is a constant
C_{D,q}>0
such that

(1.14)

\left\|\left[|D|^{-q},a\right]\right\|_{(q+1)^{-1}p,\infty}\leq C_{D,q}\left\|\left[D,a\right]\right\|\qquad\forall a\in\mathscr{A}.

The above Lemma subsumes and improves various commutator results for spectral triples (see, e.g.,
[
8
,
39
,
59
,
135
,
159
,
172
,
200
]
). It yields as sharp Schatten properties as it can be established by means of pseudodifferential techniques in the cases where suitable pseudodifferential calculi are available (see, e.g.,
[
134
]
). However, it holds in far greater generality and requires very little regularity for the elements of
\mathscr{A}
. For instance, for Dirac spectral triples over closed Riemannian spin manifolds, Lemma
1.9
holds for all Lipschitz functions. In addition, the type of estimate provided by (
1.14
) is usually not reachable by pseudodifferential techniques.

The other two new technical results provide refinements for spectral triples of deep results of Connes-Sukochev-Zanin
[
61
, Lemma 5.3]
and
Sukochev-Zanin
[
193
, Theorem 5.4.2]
.

Lemma 1.10
.

Given any
\alpha\in(0,1]
and
s>1
, for every
a\in{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
, we have

(1.15)

\left(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{s}-|D|^{-\alpha s}a^{s}\in\mathscr{L}_{(\alpha s+1-\epsilon)^{-1}p,\infty}\qquad\forall\epsilon\in(0,1).

Lemma 5.3 of
[
61
]
was stated without proof by Connes
[
53
, Lemma IV.3.11]
. This result implies that for
0<q<p
the differences
a^{p/2}|D|^{-p}a^{p/2}-(a^{1/2}|D|^{-q}a^{1/2})^{p/q}
,
a\in\mathscr{A}_{++}
, are in
(\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty})_{0}
. In particular, for
q=2
(which is needed for getting the semiclassical Weyl law of
[
137
]
) the condition
q<p
yields the restriction
p>2
.

In contrast, Lemma
1.10
above allows us to deal with differences
(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-1}a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{q}-a^{\frac{1}{2q}}|D|^{-q}a^{\frac{1}{2q}}
for
all values

q>0
and we obtain better Schatten properties (e.g., for
q=p
we get membership in ideals
\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty}
with
r<1
, which implies that the difference is trace-class). Incidentally, Lemma
1.10
is instrumental in removing the restriction
p>2
from the approach of
[
137
]
.

In fact, we prove Lemma
1.10
for all
s\in\mathbb{C}
with
\Re s>1
. An elaboration of the arguments of its proof further shows that if we set
c=\max(1,\alpha^{-1}(q^{-1}p-1))
, then, for every
a\in\mathscr{A}_{++}
, we have

\left(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{s}-|D|^{-\alpha s}a^{s}\in\operatorname{Hol}\left(\Re s>c;\mathscr{L}_{q}\right).

This can be interpreted as a holomorphic version of Lemma
1.10
(see Lemma
6.8
). As we shall see this implies our 3rd new technical lemma.

Lemma 1.11
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1]
be such that
\delta:=\min(1,p-\alpha)>0
. Given any
a\in{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
, the function

\operatorname{Tr}\big[\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)^{s}\big]-\operatorname{Tr}\big[|D|^{-\alpha s}a^{s}\big],\qquad\Re s>\alpha^{-1}p,

has an analytic extension to the half-plane
\Re s>\alpha^{-1}(p-\delta)
.

In the setup of (unital) spectral triples, Lemma
1.11
is a refinement of
[
193
, Theorem 5.4.2]
. The latter result is proved for
p>2
under some strong regularity assumptions (their result actually holds for non-unital spectral triples). A weaker version for (unital) Lipschitz regular spectral triples of summability degree
p>2
is given in
[
137
]
.

All the above regularity conditions are removed in Lemma
1.11
above. More importantly, our result does not involve any restriction on the degree of summability, since we always can find
\alpha\in(0,1]
small enough so that
\min(1,p-\alpha)>0
.

As in
[
137
]
the approach relies on the integral representation given in
[
61
,
193
]
for differences
(A^{1/2}BA^{1/2})^{s}-B^{s}A^{s}
,
\Re s>1
, where
A
and
B
are arbitrary positive operators. However, we make a few observations that simplify matters significantly and lead to stronger results. In particular, degree summability issues disappear and we do not need to appeal to the difficult and deep results of
[
106
,
160
]
that were used in
[
137
]
. Thus, even if we get stronger results, with respect to the approaches of
[
61
,
137
,
193
]
, our approach remains fairly elementary.

In addition, it is instrumental for our approaches to the proofs of the key results Proposition
1.1
and Theorem
1.7
to establish the above results for the fractional powers
|D|^{\alpha}
and the commutators
[|D|^{-\alpha},a]
,
a\in\mathscr{A}
, as
\alpha
ranges over
all

(0,1]
, and not just for
\alpha=1
. This also comes into play the proof of Lemma
1.9
.

The main simplifications provided by our approach occur from the following factorization lemma.

Lemma 1.12

(Factorization Lemma)
.

Let
\alpha\in[0,1]
and
\beta,\gamma\in[0,1]
be such that, either
\beta+\gamma<\alpha+1
, or
\beta+\gamma=\alpha+1
with
\max(\beta,\gamma)<1
.

(i)

There is a bounded linear operator
\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}^{\alpha}:\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})\rightarrow\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
such that, for all
a\in\mathscr{A}
, we have

(1.16)

\displaystyle\left[|D|^{-\alpha},a\right]=|D|^{-\beta}\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}^{\alpha}\left([D,a]\right)|D|^{-\gamma}+|D|^{-(\alpha+1)}F[D,a]\Pi_{0}+\Pi_{0}[D,a]F|D|^{-(\alpha+1)},

where
F=D|D|^{-1}
is the sign of
D
and
\Pi_{0}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\ker D
.

(ii)

Suppose that
\beta+\gamma<\alpha+1
. If
\max(\beta,\gamma)<1
, then
\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}^{\alpha}
gives rise to a continuous linear operator,

\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}^{\alpha}:\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty},\qquad\text{with}\ r=(1+\alpha-\beta-\gamma)^{-1}p.

If
\max(\beta,\gamma)=1
, then this results holds for any
r>(1+\alpha-\beta-\gamma)^{-1}p
. In both cases the range of
\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}^{\alpha}
is contained in the ideal of compact operators.

The operator
\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}^{m}
in (
1.16
) is defined in terms of double operator-integrals (DOIs) in the sense of Birman-Solomyak
[
20
,
23
,
33
]
(see Eq. (
5.21
)). Factorization results for commutators and differences of functions of positive operators in terms of DOIs are not new (see, e.g.,
[
31
,
32
,
85
,
124
,
126
,
159
]
). The proof of our factorization result is actually a mere adaptation to our setup of the approach of DOI representation of differences of fractional powers of positive operators in
[
31
]
.
However, the type of factorization provided by (
1.16
) in such a wide setting does not seem to have appeared elsewhere.

Lemma
1.9
is a consequence of the above factorization lemma and some standard induction argument (see Chapter
3
).

As mentioned above, the proofs of Lemma
1.10
and Lemma
1.11
rely on the integral representations in
[
61
,
193
]
for
(A^{1/2}BA^{1/2})^{s}-B^{s}A^{s}
,
\Re s>1
. In the case
A=a\in\mathscr{A}_{++}
and
B=|D|^{-\alpha}
,
\alpha\in(0,1]
the integrands in this formula involve commutators
[|D|^{-\alpha},a^{z}]
,
z\in\mathbb{C}
. By Lemma
1.9
these commutators lies in the weak Schatten class
\mathscr{L}_{(|\alpha|+1)^{-1}p,\infty}
. Weak Schatten classes
\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty}
are Banach spaces for
r>1
, but for
r\leq 1
they are only quasi-Banach spaces, and hence are not locally convex spaces. This results in serious technical difficulties to deal with integration and analyticity of maps with values in
\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty}
,
r\geq 1
. This is in a nutshell the main technical hurdle that prevent us to extend to summability degree
p\leq 2
the approaches of
[
61
,
137
,
193
]
.

Our main idea is to use Lemma
1.12
to “factorize out” powers of
|D|^{-1}
and get new integrands depending on the bounded commutators
[D,a^{z}]
. Thereby, we trade integrals with integrands in weak Schatten classes for integrals whose integrands are compact operators, for which standard Bochner integration techniques apply. The Schatten properties stated by Lemma
1.10
and Lemma
1.11
then become consequences of Hölder’s inequality for weak Schatten classes. In particular, degree summability issues become irrelevant. We refer to Chapter
6
for the full proofs of Lemma
1.10
and Lemma
1.11
.

1.1.8.
Sketch of proof of Proposition
1.1

We observe that Lemma
1.9
enables us to reduce the proof of Proposition
1.1
as follows. First, we need only to prove the result for
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
, i.e.,

(1.17)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\tau\left[|a|^{\frac{p}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}}\qquad\forall a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}.

Second, the above spectral asymptotics are equivalent to

(1.18)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a^{-\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-q}a^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)=\tau\left[|a|^{\frac{p}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}}\qquad\forall a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}.

These kind of reductions are not new, but here they follow from elementary arguments (compare
[
137
,
192
]
).

The main new idea in the proof of Proposition
1.1
is using Lemma
1.10
, along with Lemma
1.9
and the perturbation theory of Birman-Solomyak
[
21
,
29
]
), to show in substance that if the spectral asymptotics (
1.18
) hold for
some

q_{0}>0
, then they hold for
all

q>0
. Note that for achieving this it is crucial to the allow exponent of
|D|^{-\alpha}
in Lemma
1.10
to range over all
(0,1]
instead of restricting ourselves to
\alpha=1
(compare
[
137
]
).

As it turns out, Condition (W) is actually equivalent to the spectral asymptotics (
1.18
) for
q=2
(see Lemma
3.8
). This allows us to complete the proof of Proposition
1.1
(see Chapter
3
for the full details).

1.1.9.
Sketch of proof of Theorem
1.7

The proof of Theorem
1.7
clarifies the relationships between the conditions of this monograph and the Tauberian condition of
[
137
]
(see also Condition
4.2
for the statement of that condition). In
[
137
]
the condition is required to hold for all
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
. This is arguably too strong a requirement. We introduce a weaker condition (Condition (
\textup{Z}_{0}
); see Chapter
4
). This condition is required to hold for all
a\in\mathscr{A}_{++}
only.

For one thing, we use Lemma
1.11
, along with the well-known Ikehara’s Tauberian theorem, to show that Condition (
\textup{Z}_{0}
) implies Condition (W). The role of Lemma
1.11
in this proof is paramount. For the other thing, we show that Condition (Z) and Condition (H) of Theorem
1.7
both imply Condition (
\textup{Z}_{0}
), and hence they imply Condition (W). This establishes Theorem
1.7
.

We refer to Chapter
4
for the full proof of Theorem
1.7
.

1.2.
Bounded Euclidean Domains

In Chapter
7
, we explain how the main results of this monograph allows us to recover well known semiclassical Weyl laws for fractional Schrödinger operators associated with boundary value problems on Euclidean domains with smooth boundary. We also obtain an integration formula in this setting, which seems to be a new result.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
n\geq 2
, with smooth boundary
\partial\Omega
. We also let
\Delta_{\Omega}
be either the Dirichlet Laplacian or the Neumann Laplacian on
\Omega
. In either case we get an essentially selfadjoint strongly elliptic 2nd order differential operator whose spectrum is non-negative and purely discrete. To get a spectral triple we consider the
*
-algebra,

\dot{C}^{\infty}(\Omega):=\mathbb{C}1+C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega).

That is,
\dot{C}^{\infty}(\Omega)
is the algebra of
C^{\infty}
-functions on
\Omega
that are constant near the boundary
\partial\Omega
.
This is a
*
-subalgebra of the
C^{*}
-algebra of bounded continuous functions on
\Omega
, and so it is represented in
L^{2}(\Omega)
by bounded multiplication operators. Its norm closure is the
C^{*}
-algebra,

\dot{C}(\Omega):=\mathbb{C}+C_{0}(\Omega),

where
C_{0}(\Omega)
is the (closed) ideal of continuous functions on
\Omega
that converges to
0
near the boundary
\partial\Omega
. In other words,
\dot{C}(\Omega)
is the space of continuous functions on
\overline{\Omega}
that are constant on the boundary
\partial\Omega
.

The square-root Laplacian
\Delta_{\Omega}^{1/2}
is defined by Borel functional calculus. Results of Seeley
[
175
,
177
]

and Grubb
[
95
, §4.4]
) allow us to show that we get a spectral triple,

\left(\dot{C}^{\infty}(\Omega),L^{2}(\Omega),\sqrt{\Delta_{\Omega}}\right).

The above spectral triple satisfies Condition (W). In fact, old results of Carleman
[
41
]
and Minakshisundaram
[
146
]
(see also
[
176
]
) allow us to check that Condition (Z) holds. Alternatively, it follows from results of Grubb
[
95
, §4.5]
that, for all
0<f\in\dot{C}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, as
j\rightarrow\infty
we have the Weyl law,

(1.19)

\lambda_{j}\big(f\Delta_{\Omega}f\big)\sim j^{\frac{2}{n}}\left(c(n)\int_{\Omega}f(x)^{-n}dx\right)^{-\frac{2}{n}},\qquad c(n):=(2\pi)^{-n}|\mathbb{B}^{n}|.

In any case, Condition (W) holds with

\tau(f)=c(n)\int_{\Omega}f(x)dx,\qquad f\in\dot{C}^{\infty}(\Omega).

Furthermore, Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
) is a consequence of well known Cwikel-type estimates. It holds as follows:

(i)

For
r>1
, with
\mathscr{V}_{r}=L_{r}(\Omega)
(Rozenblum
[
163
,
164
]
).

(ii)

For
r<1
, with
\mathscr{V}_{r}=L^{1}(\Omega)
(Birman-Solomyak
[
24
]
).

(iii)

For
r=1
, with
\mathscr{V}_{1}=L\!\log\!L(\Omega)
if
n
is even (Rozenblum-Shargorodsky
[
168
]
, Solomyak
[
185
,
186
]
), or
\mathscr{V}_{1}=L_{r^{\prime}}(\Omega)
,
r^{\prime}>1
, if
n
is odd (Birman-Solomyak
[
24
]
).

Here
L\!\log\!L(\Omega)
is the space of
L\!\log\!L
-Orlicz functions on
\Omega
(see Chapter
7
for its definition). Note that
L^{r^{\prime}}(\Omega)\subsetneq L\!\log\!L(\Omega)\subsetneq L^{1}(\Omega)
for all
r^{\prime}>1
.

In this setting, the spectral asymptotics (
1.2
)–(
1.3
) and the semiclassical Weyl law (
1.11
) are well known (see Birman-Solomyak
[
21
,
24
]
, Rozenblum
[
163
,
164
]
, Rozenblum-Shargorodsky
[
168
]
, Solomyak
[
186
]
). The only novelty here is the observation that thanks to the spectral theoretic considerations of these monographs these results can be deduced from the results of Carleman and Minakshisundaram mentioned above. In particular, we recover the following semiclassical result.

Semiclassical Weyl Law

(
[
21
,
24
,
163
,
164
,
168
,
186
]
)
.

Assume we are in one the following situations:

(i)

q\neq n/2
and
V(x)
is a real-valued potential in
L^{r}(\Omega)
with
r=\max(n(2q)^{-1},1)
.

(ii)

q=n/2
and
V(x)
is a real-valued potential in
L\!\log\!L(\Omega)
(
n
even) or is in
L_{r^{\prime}}(\Omega)
with
r^{\prime}>1
(
n
odd).

Then, for every energy level
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
we have

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{n}N\left(h^{2q}\Delta_{\Omega}^{q}+V;\lambda\right)=c(n)\int_{\Omega}(V(x)-\lambda)_{-}^{\frac{n}{2q}}dx.

In this setting, the integration formulas (
1.12
)–(
1.13
) do not seem to have been appeared elsewhere, and so, in this respect, there are new. Namely, we have the following result.

Integration Formulas

(Theorem
7.2
)
.

Assume that, either
n
is even and
f\in L\!\log\!L(\Omega)
, or
n
is odd and
f\in L_{r}(\Omega)
with
r>1
. Then, the operators

\Delta_{\Omega}^{-n/4}f\Delta_{\Omega}^{-n/4}
and
|\Delta_{\Omega}^{-n/4}f\Delta_{\Omega}^{-n/4}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\Delta_{\Omega}^{-\frac{n}{4}}f\Delta_{\Omega}^{-\frac{n}{4}}=c(n)\int_{\Omega}f(x)dx,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\left|\Delta_{\Omega}^{-\frac{n}{4}}f\Delta_{\Omega}^{-\frac{n}{4}}\right|=c(n)\int_{\Omega}|f(x)|dx.

1.3.
Closed Riemannian Manifolds

Chapter
8
has two main parts. In the first part, we explain how the main results of this monograph enables us to recover well known semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas for closed manifolds, as well as Weyl laws for Steklov eigenvalue problems on compact manifolds with boundary.

In the second part, we
explain that in the case of
spectral triples associated with algebras of zeroth order
\Psi
DOs, Proposition
1.1
provides us with a new proof of the Weyl laws of Birman-Solmyak for negative order
\Psi
DOs. In fact, we shall obtain a more general result for suitable closures the spaces of (negative order)
\Psi
DOs. This ulitmately leads to a far-reaching refinement of Connes’ trace theorem
[
52
]
and of its version in
[
113
]
.

In what follows,
(M^{n},g)
is a closed Riemannian manifold, and
E
is a Hermitian vector bundle over
M
.

1.3.1.
Semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas

Let
D_{E}:C^{\infty}(M,E)\rightarrow C^{\infty}(M,E)
be a selfadjoint first order elliptic
\Psi
DO such that
D_{E}^{2}
has same principal symbol as the Laplacian, i.e.,

\sigma_{2}\left(D_{E}^{2}\right)(x,\xi)=|\xi|_{g}^{2}\operatorname{id}_{E_{x}},\qquad(x,\xi)\in T^{*}M\setminus 0.

We then get an
n
-summable spectral triple,

(1.20)

\left(C^{\infty}\left(M,\operatorname{End}(E)\right),L^{2}(M,E),D_{E}\right).

This set-up encapsulates the following examples:

(i)

E
is the trivial line bundle, and
D_{E}=\sqrt{\Delta_{g}}
is the square-root Laplacian.

(ii)

M
is spin,
E=\not{S}
is the spinor bundle, and
D_{E}=\not{D}_{g}
is the Dirac operator.

(iii)

\nabla^{E}
is a Hermitian connection on
E
and
D_{E}=\sqrt{(\nabla^{E})^{*}\nabla^{E}}
is the square root of the connection Laplacian
(\nabla^{E})^{*}\nabla^{E}
.

(iv)

M=\partial X
is the boundary of a compact manifold
\overline{X}
,
E
is the trivial line bundle, and
D_{E}=\Lambda_{g}
is the Dirichlet-to-Neuman operator (see
[
119
,
198
]
).

Condition (W) holds. Namely, for every positive section
u\in C^{\infty}(M)
, as
j\rightarrow\infty
we have

(1.21)

\lambda_{j}(uD_{E}u)\sim j^{\frac{2}{n}}\left(c(n)\int_{M}\operatorname{tr}_{E_{x}}\left[u(x)^{-n}\right]d\nu_{g}(x)\right)^{-\frac{2}{n}},\quad c(n):=(2\pi)^{-n}|\mathbb{B}^{n}|,

where
\nu_{g}(x)
is the Riemannian measure. This can be deduced from esults of Minakshisundaram-Pleijel
[
147
]
, which were established about 75 years ago. Those results show that Condition (Z) holds, and hence Condition (W) is satisfied thanks to Theorem
1.7
.
Alternatively, the Weyl law (
1.21
) is a special case of the Weyl law for positive elliptic
\Psi
DOs on closed manifolds (see Chapter
8
).

As with bounded Euclidean domains, Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
) is the consequence of various Cwikel-type estimates. Namely, Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
) holds as follows:

(i)

For
r>1
with
\mathscr{V}_{r}=L^{r}(M,E)
(see
[
64
]
).

(ii)

For
r<1
with
\mathscr{V}_{r}=L^{1}(M,E)
(see
[
25
]
).

(iii)

For
r=1
with
\mathscr{V}_{1}=L\!\log\!L(M,E)
(see
[
185
,
186
,
191
]
; see also
[
156
,
165
,
168
]
). Here
L\!\log\!L(M,E)
is the space of
L\!\log\!L
-Orlicz sections of
E
(see Chapter
8
for its definition).

Applying Proposition
1.4
in this setting shows that if
q>0
and
r=nq^{-1}
, then, for any
u\in\mathscr{V}_{r}
, we have

(1.22)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{n}}\mu_{j}\big(|D_{E}|^{-\frac{q}{2}}u|D_{E}|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\left(c(n)\int_{M}\operatorname{tr}_{E_{x}}\big[|u(x)|^{\frac{n}{q}}\big]d\nu_{g}(x)\right)^{\frac{q}{n}},

(1.23)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{n}}\lambda_{j}^{\pm}\big(|D_{E}|^{-\frac{q}{2}}u|D_{E}|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\left(c(n)\int_{M}\operatorname{tr}_{E_{x}}\big[u_{\pm}(x)^{\frac{n}{q}}\big]d\nu_{g}(x)\right)^{\frac{q}{n}}\quad(\text{if $u(x)^{*}=u(x)$}).

In the case of smooth sections the above spectral asymptotics are also special cases of the Weyl laws for negative order
\Psi
DOs of Birman-Solomyak
[
26
,
27
,
28
]
. They can be extended to sections in
\mathscr{V}_{r}
by using the Cwikel-type estimates mentioned in (i)–(iiii) above. In the scalar case the spectral asymptotics (
8.4
) for
q=n
are proved in
[
165
,
191
]
. Both sets of spectral asymptotics (
8.3
)–(
8.4
) for
q=n
are mentioned in the above generality in
[
156
]
.

Our point is is that the spectral asymptotics (
1.22
)–(
1.23
), along with the semiclassical Weyl law (
1.24
) and the integration formulas (
1.25
)–(
1.26
) below, can be deduced from old results of Minakshisundaram-Pleijel
[
147
]
by using the spectral theoretic considerations of this monograph.

Applying Theorem
1.2
allows us to recover the following semiclassical Weyl laws.

Semiclassical Weyl Law

(
[
21
,
163
,
164
,
165
,
186
]
)
.

Assume that
q\neq n/2
and
V(x)=V(x)^{*}\in L^{r}(M,E)
with
r=\max(n(2q)^{-1},1)
, or
q=n/2
and
V(x)=V(x)^{*}\in L\!\log\!L(M,E)
. Then, for any energy level

\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

(1.24)

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{n}N\left(h^{2q}|D_{E}|^{2q}+V;\lambda\right)=c(n)\int_{M}\operatorname{tr}_{E_{x}}\big[\left(V(x)-\lambda\right)_{-}^{\frac{n}{2q}}\big]d\nu_{g}(x).

For
q\neq n/2
, the semiclassical Weyl laws (
1.24
) go back to
[
21
,
47
,
163
,
164
]
(see also
[
64
,
180
]
). In the critical case
q=n/2
this is a more recent result
[
165
,
186
]
(see also
[
156
,
168
]
).

Finally, by applying Theorem
1.3
we obtain the following extensions of Connes’ integration formula.

Integration Formulas

(
[
156
,
165
]
; see also
[
192
]
)
.

For every
u\in L\!\log\!L(M,E)
, the operators
|D_{E}|^{-n/2}u|D_{E}|^{-n/2}
and

||D_{E}|^{-n/2}u|D_{E}|^{-n/2}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

(1.25)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int|D_{E}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}u|D_{E}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}=c(n)\int_{M}\operatorname{tr}_{E_{x}}[u(x)]d\nu_{g}(x),

(1.26)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\left||D_{E}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}u|D_{E}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}\right|=c(n)\int_{M}\operatorname{tr}_{E_{x}}\left[|u(x)|\right]d\nu_{g}(x).

The integration formula (
8.6
) in the form stated above for
L\!\log\!L
-functions was established by Rozenblum
[
165
]
. The integration formulas (
8.6
)–(
8.7
) in the bundle case appeared in
[
156
]
. In the scalar case with
D_{E}=(1+\Delta_{g})^{-1/2}
the integration formula (
8.6
) for
L\!\log\!L
-functions is not stated explicitly in
[
192
]
, but it’s a corollary of the main results of that paper. Both sets of spectral asymptotics (
8.3
)–(
8.4
) for
q=n
are stated in the bundle setting in
[
156
]
.

1.3.2.
Steklov eigenvalues

The example of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator is of special interest because it is closely related to Steklov eigenvalue problems. Assume that
M
is the boundary of a compact manifold
(\overline{X}^{n+1},\overline{g})
with interior
X
such that
\overline{g}_{|TM}=g
on
M
. Given any real-valued function
\gamma(x)
in
L^{n}(M)
(
n\geq 2
) or in
L\!\log\!L(M)
(
n=1
), the
weighted
Steklov problem associated with
\gamma(x)
is the eigenvalue problem,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Delta_{\overline{g}}u=0&\text{on $X$},\\
\partial_{\nu}u=\sigma\gamma u&\text{on $M$},\end{array}\right.

where
\partial_{\nu}
is the outward normal derivative. (The ordinary Steklov problem correspond to the constant weight
\gamma=1
.)

As is well known, the positive (resp., negative) Steklov eigenvalues agree with the positive (resp., negative) eigenvalue of the Birman-Schwinger operator
\Lambda_{g}^{-1/2}\gamma\Lambda_{g}^{-1/2}
, where
\Lambda_{g}
is the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator (see also Chapter
8
). As a result, the spectral asymptotics (
1.23
) for
D_{E}=\Lambda_{g}
and
q=1
are equivalent to Weyl laws for the positive/negative Steklov eigenvalues. This allows us to recover results of Agranovich
[
2
]
and Suslina
[
194
]
(
n\geq 1
) and Rozenblum
[
165
]
(
n=1
) (see Proposition
8.10
for the precise statement).

Note also that Weyl laws for Steklov eigenvalues associated the weights in
L^{n}(M)
(
n\geq 2
) or in
L\!\log\!L(M)
(
n=1
) imply Weyl law for ordinary Steklov eigenvalues on surfaces with Lipschitz boundaries (see
[
109
,
166
]
).

In addition, the integration formula (
1.25
) for
D_{E}=\Lambda_{g}
enables us to reformulate the Steklov Weyl laws in terms of the NC integral (see Proposition
8.12
).

1.3.3.
Birman-Solomyak’s Weyl laws and Connes’ trace theorem

Suppose now that
D_{E}=\sqrt{1+(\nabla^{E})^{*}\nabla^{E}}
, where
\nabla^{E}
is some Hermitian connection on
E
. In what follows, for
m\in\mathbb{R}
we denote by

\Psi^{m}(M,E)
the space of
\Psi
DOs of order
m
acting on sections of
E
.

We observe that, as the principal symbol of
(D_{E})^{2}
is scalar, we still get a spectral triple if in (
1.20
) we replace the algebra
C^{\infty}(M,\operatorname{End}(E))
by the /larger algebra
\Psi^{0}(M,E)
of zeroth order
\Psi
DOs acting on sections of
E
. That is, we have an
n
-summable spectral triple,

(1.27)

\left(\Psi^{0}(M,\operatorname{End}(E)),L^{2}(M,E),D_{E}\right).

The Weyl law for positive elliptic
\Psi
DOs ensures that the above spectral triple satisfies Condition (W), with

\tau(A):=\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\iint_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\left[\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)\right]|\xi|_{g}^{-n}dxd\xi,\qquad A\in\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)},

where
\sigma_{0}:\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}\rightarrow C(S^{*}M,\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E))
is the unique continuous extension to
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}
of the principal symbol map on
\Psi^{0}(M,E)
.

In this setting Proposition
1.1
ensures that, given any
q>0
and
A\in\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}
, we have the spectral asymptotics,

(1.28)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{n}}\mu_{j}\big((D_{E})^{-\frac{q}{2}}A(D_{E})^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\bigg[\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[|\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)|^{\frac{n}{q}}\big]|\xi|_{g}^{-n}dxd\xi\bigg]^{\frac{q}{n}},

(1.29)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{n}}\lambda^{\pm}_{j}\big((D_{E})^{-\frac{q}{2}}A(D_{E})^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\bigg[\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)_{\pm}^{\frac{n}{q}}\big]|\xi|_{g}^{-n}dxd\xi\bigg]^{\frac{q}{n}}\ (\textup{if}\ A^{*}=A).

Moreover, by applying Theorem
1.3
we obtain the following integration formulas.

Integration Formulas

(Theorem
8.24
)
.

For every
A\in\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}
, the operators
A(D_{E})^{-n}
,
(D_{E})^{-n/2}A(D_{E})^{-n/2}
, and
|(D_{E})^{-n/2}A(D_{E})^{-n/2}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

(1.30)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int A(D_{E})^{-n}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int(D_{E})^{-\frac{n}{2}}A(D_{E})^{-\frac{n}{2}}=\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)\big]|\xi|_{g}^{-n}dxd\xi,

(1.31)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\big|(D_{E})^{-\frac{n}{2}}A(D_{E})^{-\frac{n}{2}}\big|=\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[|\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)|\big]|\xi|_{g}^{-n}dxd\xi.

The integration formula (
1.31
) is new. The integration formula (
1.30
) is a refinement of the recent integration formula of Kodyukov-Suckochev-Zanin
[
113
, Theorem 1.5]
(see Remark
8.26
). In particular, we obtain spectral measurability, whereas the focus in
[
113
]
is on strong measurability.

The spectral asymptotics (
1.28
)–(
1.29
) and the integration formulas (
1.30
)–(
1.31
) have equivalent reformulations in terms of suitable closures of the spaces
\Psi^{m}(M,E)
,
m<0
.

For
s\geq 0
, we let
\mathscr{H}^{(s)}
be the Sobolev space with Bessel potential
|D_{E}|^{s}=(1+(\nabla^{E})^{*}\nabla^{E})^{s/2}
. We define
\mathscr{H}^{(-s)}
to be its antilinear dual. If
m<0
, then every
P\in\Psi^{m}(M,E)
uniquely extends to a continuous linear operator
P:\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)}\rightarrow\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)}
. We define

\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}
to be the closure of
\Psi^{m}(M,E)
in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)},\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)})
. The adjoint map
P\rightarrow P^{*}
uniquely extends to an isometric antilinear involution on
\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}
. In addition, the principal symbol map on
\Psi^{m}(M,E)
uniquely extends to a continuous linear map
\sigma_{m}:\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}\rightarrow C(S^{*}M,\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E))
(see Chapter
8
).

Let
\Phi:\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)},\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)})\rightarrow\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(M,E))
be the Banach space isomorphism,

\Phi(T)=|D_{E}|^{|m|/2}T|D_{E}|^{|m|/2},\qquad T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)},\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)}).

This induces an a Banach space isomorphism
\Phi:\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}\rightarrow\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}
. This isomorphism then provides a one-to-one correspondence between for operators in
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}
and equivalent results for operators in
\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}
.

Under this correspondence, the spectral asymptotics (
1.28
)–(
1.29
) are equivalent to the following extension of the Weyl law for negative order
\Psi
DOs of Birman-Solomyak
[
26
,
27
,
28
]
.

Spectral Asymptotics

(Theorem
8.33
)
.

Let
A\in\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}
,
m<0
, and set
q=|m|
. We have

(1.32)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{n}}\mu_{j}\big(P)=\bigg[\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[|\sigma_{m}(P)(x,\xi)|^{\frac{n}{q}}\big]dxd\xi\bigg]^{\frac{q}{n}},

(1.33)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{n}}\lambda^{\pm}_{j}(P)=\bigg[\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[\sigma_{m}(P)(x,\xi)_{\pm}^{\frac{n}{q}}\big]|\xi|_{g}^{-n}dxd\xi\bigg]^{\frac{q}{n}}\ (\textup{if}\ P^{*}=P).

We recover the original spectral asymptotics of Birman-Solomyak
[
28
]
for closed manifolds by specializing the above result to operators in
\Psi^{m}(M,E)
. Note that Birman-Solomyak’s perturbation theory also allows us to extend to operators in
\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}
the original spectral asymptotics of
[
28
]
(see Remark
8.34
). The main takeaway here is a new proof of Birman-Solomyak’s spectral asymptotics as a consequence of Propositon
1.1
.

As for the integration formulas (
1.30
)–(
1.31
), they are equivalent to the following Connes’ trace theorem
[
52
]
.

Trace Theorem

(Theorem
8.36
)
.

For every
P\in\overline{\Psi^{-n}(M,E)}
, the operators
P
and
|P|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

(1.34)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int P=\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[\sigma_{-n}(P)(x,\xi)\big]dxd\xi,

(1.35)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int|P|=\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[|\sigma_{-n}(P)(x,\xi)|\big]dxd\xi.

Finally, for sake of completeness, we add an appendix where it is explained that if we’re only interested in strong measurability, then the part for the operator
P
in the above trace theorem can be deduced from a mere elaboration of the original arguments of Connes
[
52
]
. In particular, this yields an elementary derivation of the integration formula of
[
113
, Theorem 1.5]
(see Remark
8.39
).

1.4.
Open Manifolds with Conformally Cusp Metrics

In Chapter
9
, we apply the main results of this monograph to get semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas for some class of open Riemannian manifolds. In particular, the semiclassical Weyl laws that we obtain complement recent semiclassical Weyl laws for complete Riemanian manifolds by Braverman
[
36
]
and Dai-Yan
[
65
]
(see Remark
9.3
).

We work in the following geometric setup. We let
X^{n}
be an open manifold, which is the interior of a compact manifold
\overline{X}
with closed boundary
M^{n-1}=\partial\overline{X}
and boundary defining function
x
. We assume that
X
is endowed with a Riemannian metric of the form,

(1.36)

g=x^{2r}g_{0},\qquad r>\frac{1}{n},

where
g_{0}
is a cusp metric on
\overline{X}
(see Chapter
9
for the precise definition). We also denote by
h_{0}
the Riemannian metric induced by
g_{0}
on the boundary
M=\partial X
. The condition
r>1/n
ensures that
(X,g)
has finite volume. Moreover,
(X,g)
is a complete Riemannian manifold if and only if
r\leq 1
.

Examples of manifolds with conformally cusp metrics include hyperbolic metrics of finite volume (in which case
r=1
; see
[
150
]
). Another class of examples is provided by metric horns in the sense of Cheeger
[
42
]
(see also
[
120
]
) for which
r>1
.

1.4.1.
Dirac Operators

Assume further that
\overline{X}
is spin. We denote by
\not{D}_{g}
the Dirac operator of
X
acting on the sections of the spinor bundle
\not{S}_{X}
. In addition, we denote by
\not{D}_{h_{0}}
the Dirac operator of
M=\partial X
with respect to the metric (
1.36
). We make the following assumptions:

•

g_{0}
is an exact cusp metric in the sense of Melrose
[
141
]
(the definition is recalled in Chapter
9
).

•

\ker\not{D}_{h_{0}}=\{0\}
, i.e., there are no (non-zero) harmonic spinors on
M
.

Under the above conditions it has been shown by Moroianu
[
150
]
that the operator
\not{D}_{g}
with domain
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
is essentially selfadjoint on
L_{g}^{2}(X)
and has compact resolvent, and hence its spectrum consists of isolated real eigenvalues with finite multiplicity. In the same way as with bounded Euclidean domains, in order to get a spectral triple we use the
*
-algebra,

\dot{C}^{\infty}(X)=\mathbb{C}1+C_{c}^{\infty}(X).

That is,
\dot{C}^{\infty}(X)
is the algebra of
C^{\infty}
-functions on
X
that are constant near the boundary
M=\partial X
. It is represented in
L_{g}^{2}(X,\not{S}_{X})
by bounded multiplication operators. Its norm closure is the
C^{*}
-algebra,

\dot{C}(X):=\mathbb{C}1+C_{0}(X),

where
C_{0}(X)
is the ideal of continuous functions on
X
that converges to
0
near the boundary
M
. Thus,
\dot{C}(X)
consists of continuous functions on
\overline{X}
that are constant on the boundary. If
f
is such a function, then we denote by
f_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
its boundary value.

By using the results of
[
150
]
it can be shown that we have an
n
-summable spectral triple,

\left(\dot{C}^{\infty}(X),L^{2}_{g}(X,\not{S}_{X}),\not{D}_{g}\right).

The results
[
150
]
further enable us to show that Condition (Z) holds, and hence Condition (W) is satisfied. Namely, if
f\in\dot{C}^{\infty}(X)
is positive on
X
and have positive boundary value, then as
j\rightarrow\infty
we have the Weyl law,

\lambda_{j}\big(f\not{D}_{g}^{2}f\big)\sim j^{\frac{2}{n}}\left(\not{c}(n)\int_{X}f(y)^{-n}d\nu_{g}(y)\right)^{-\frac{2}{n}},\qquad\not{c}(n):=2^{\left[\frac{n}{2}\right]}(2\pi)^{-n}|\mathbb{B}^{n}|,

As Condition (W) is satisfied we may apply Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.3
. We obtain the following results.

Semiclassical Weyl Law

(Theorem
9.1
)
.

Given any
q>0
and any real-valued potential
V\in\dot{C}(X)
, for every energy level
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{n}N\left(h^{2q}|\not{D}_{g}|^{2q}+V;\lambda\right)=\not{c}(n)\int_{X}(V(y)-\lambda)_{-}^{\frac{n}{2q}}d\nu_{g}(y).

Integration Formulas

(Theorem
9.2
)
.

For every
f\in\dot{C}(X)
, the operators
f|\not{D}_{g}|^{-n}
,
|\not{D}_{g}|^{-n/2}f|\not{D}_{g}|^{-n/2}
, and
||\not{D}_{g}|^{-n/2}f|\not{D}_{g}|^{-n/2}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int f|\not{D}_{g}|^{-n}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int|\not{D}_{g}|^{-n/2}f|\not{D}_{g}|^{-n/2}=\not{c}(n)\int_{X}f(y)d\nu_{g}(y),

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\big||\not{D}_{g}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}f|\not{D}_{g}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}\big|=\not{c}(n)\int_{X}|f(y)|d\nu_{g}(y).

1.4.2.
Magnetic Laplacians

We drop the spin assumption for
X
. The ordinary Laplacian of
(X,g)
need not have discrete spectrum (see, e.g.,
[
82
]
). However, as observed by Golénia-Moroianu
[
92
]
, the results of
[
150
]
for the Dirac operators have analogues if we couple the Laplacian with a suitable magnetic potentials, i.e., for magnetic Laplacians.

More precisely, let
B
be a magnetic field of the form
B=dA
, where the vector potential
A
is a cusp real 1-form, i.e., a smooth real 1-form on
X
which near
M
is of the form,

A=a_{0}(x,y)x^{-2}dx+\omega,\qquad\iota_{\partial_{x}}\omega=0.

where
a_{0}(x,y)
and
\omega
are smooth up to the boundary. The
magnetic Laplacian
is the differential operator on
X
defined by

\Delta_{A}:=d_{A}^{*}d_{A},\qquad d_{A}=d+iA\wedge\cdot,

where
d:C_{c}^{\infty}(X)\rightarrow C_{c}^{\infty}(X,T^{*}X)
is the de Rham differential. We also denote by
\Delta_{A_{0}}^{0}
the magnetic Laplacian on
M
associated with the metric
h_{0}
and the
1
-form
A_{0}:=\omega_{|M}
.

Golénia-Moroianu
[
92
]
observed that under a trapping condition (the definition is recalled in Chapter
9
) the results of
[
150
]
applies
verbatim
to the magnetic Laplacian. Thus, the operator
\Delta_{A}
with domain
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
is essentially selfadjoint on
L_{g}^{2}(X)
and has compact resolvent, and so its spectrum consists of isolated real eigenvalues with finite multiplicity. Moreover, as with the Dirac operator, the results
[
150
]
show that we have an
n
-summable spectral triple,

\left(\dot{C}^{\infty}(X),L^{2}_{g}(X),\sqrt{\Delta_{A}}\right).

As with the Dirac operator, Condition (Z) holds, and so Condition (W) is satisfied. Namely, if
f\in\dot{C}^{\infty}(X)
is positive on
X
and have positive boundary value, then as
j\rightarrow\infty
we have

\lambda_{j}\big(f\Delta_{A}f\big)\sim j^{\frac{2}{n}}\left(c(n)\int_{X}f(y)^{-n}d\nu_{g}(y)\right)^{-\frac{2}{n}},\qquad c(n):=(2\pi)^{-n}|\mathbb{B}^{n}|.

Therefore, we may apply Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.3
. This gives the following results.

Semiclassical Weyl Law

(Theorem
9.4
)
.

Assume that the
1
-form
A
is trapping. Given any
q>0
and any real-valued potential
V\in\dot{C}(\overline{X})
, we have.

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{n}N\left(h^{2q}\Delta_{A}^{q}+V;\lambda\right)=c(n)\int_{X}(V(y)-\lambda)_{-}^{\frac{n}{2q}}d\nu_{g}(y).

Theorem 1.13

(Theorem
9.7
)
.

Assume that the
1
-form
A
is trapping. For every function
f\in\dot{C}(X)
,
the operators
f\Delta_{A}^{-n/2}
,
\Delta_{A}^{-n/4}f\Delta_{A}^{-n/4}
, and
|\Delta_{A}^{-n/4}f\Delta_{A}^{-n/4}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int f\Delta_{A}^{-\frac{n}{2}}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\Delta_{A}^{-\frac{n}{4}}f\Delta_{A}^{-\frac{n}{4}}=c(n)\int_{X}f(y)d\nu_{g}(y),

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\big|\Delta_{A}^{-\frac{n}{4}}f\Delta_{A}^{-\frac{n}{4}}\big|=c(n)\int_{X}|f(y)|d\nu_{g}(y).

As further observed by Golénia-Moroianu
[
93
]
, the results of
[
92
,
150
]
continues to hold for the Hodge Laplacian on
k
-forms, provided the Betti numbers of
M
vanish in degrees
k-1
and
k
. This leads to semiclassical Weyl laws for Schrödinger operators associated with this operators, as well as integration formulas (see Remark
9.9
).

1.5.
Quantum Tori

In Chapter
10
, we apply the main results of this chapter to quantum tori to get new semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas. We look at spectral triples associated with the square-root Laplacian and the Dirac operator. For the square root Laplacian the semiclassical Weyl laws were conjectured by Edward McDonald and the author in
[
132
]
. The integration formulas that we obtain are refinements of previous integration formulas
[
132
,
136
,
155
]
.

1.5.1.
Quantum tori

Let
\theta=(\theta_{jk})
be a real anti-symmetric
n\times n
-matrix. The
quantum torus

\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta}
is meant to be the noncommutative space
represented by a
C^{*}
-algebra
C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
generated by
n
-unitaries
U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}
and subject to the relations,

(1.37)

U_{k}U_{j}=e^{2i\pi\theta_{jk}}U_{j}U_{k},\qquad j,k=1,\ldots,n.

For
\theta=0
we recover the
C^{*}
-algebra
C(\mathbb{T}^{n})
of continuous functions on the ordinary torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}=(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{n}
with
U_{j}=e^{ix_{j}}
.

In general, a complete independent set of
C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
is provided by the unitaries
U^{m}:=U^{m_{1}}\cdots U^{m_{n}}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.
The
standard trace

\tau_{0}:C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is given by

(1.38)

\tau_{0}(1)=1,\qquad\tau_{0}\big(U^{m}\big)=0\quad\mathrm{if}\ m\neq 0.

This is a faithful positive trace. We then define the space
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
as the Hilbert space completion of
C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
with respect to the inner product,

(1.39)

\left\langle x|y\right\rangle=\tau_{0}\big[y^{*}x\big],\qquad x,y\in C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta}).

Note that
\{U^{m};\ m\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
. Moreover, the action of
C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
on itself uniquely extends to a
*
-representation of
C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
; this is just the GNS representation associated with
\tau
. More generally, for any
r\in[1,\infty)
we use the trace
\tau_{0}
to define the NC
L^{r}
-space
L_{r}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
. This possible since
\tau_{0}
extends to a continuous trace on the von Neuman algebra
L_{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
generated by
U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}
(see Chapter
10
).

We have a natural
C^{*}
-action of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
on
C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
whose infinitesimal generators are the
canonical derivations

\partial_{1},\ldots,\partial_{n}
given by

(1.40)

\partial_{j}\big(U_{j}\big)=iU_{j},\qquad\partial_{j}\big(U_{k}\big)=0,\quad k\neq j.

The
smooth quantum torus

C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
is precisely the
*
-subalgebra of smooth vectors for this action. Equivalently,

(1.41)

C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})=\left\{u=\sum u_{m}U^{m};(u_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{Z}^{n})\right\},

where
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{Z}^{n})
is the space of complex-valued sequences with rapid decay. For
\theta=0
we recover the description of smooth functions on
\mathbb{T}^{n}
in terms of the rapid decay of their Fourier coefficients.

1.5.2.
Semiclassical analysis

The (flat) Laplacian is the differential operator
\Delta:C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
defined by

\Delta:=-(\partial_{1}^{2}+\cdots+\partial_{n}^{2}).

This is an essentially selfadjoint operator with nonnegative and discrete spectrum. It is isospectral to the Laplacian on the ordinary torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}
. In particular, the partial inverse
\Delta^{-1}
is in the weak Schatten class
\mathscr{L}_{n/2,\infty}
. In fact, we have an
n
-summable spectral triple,

(1.42)

\left(C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta}),L_{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta}),\sqrt{\Delta}\right).

A big impetus for this paper is proving the following semiclassical Weyl law for (flat) quantum tori, which was conjectured by Edward McDonald and the author in
[
132
]

Conjecture 1.14

(
[
132
, Conjecture 8.8]
)
.

Let
q>0
, set
r=2nq^{-1}
, and suppose that either
r\neq 1
and
r^{\prime}=\max(r,1)
, or
r=1<r^{\prime}
. Given any
V=V^{*}\in L_{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
,
for every energy level
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{n}N\big(h^{2q}\Delta^{q}+V;\lambda\big)=\hat{c}(n)\tau_{0}\big[(V-\lambda)_{-}^{\frac{n}{2q}}\big],\qquad\hat{c}(n):=|\mathbb{B}^{n}|.

A special case of this conjecture was proved in
[
137
]
for
n\geq 3
,
q=1
and potentials
V=V^{*}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
. We stress that the approach of

n\geq 3
does not apply to quantum 2-tori due to the fact that it requires
p
-summability with
p>2
. This excludes quantum 2-tori from that approach, since in this case the spectral triple (
1.42
) is 2-summable. Once again being able to include 2-dimensional examples is one of the main motivation for the results of this paper.

As it turns out, the conjecture is now an easy consequence of the main results of this paper and the Cwikel-type estimates for (flat) quantum tori of
[
132
,
134
]
. It can be shown that Condition (H) holds. In fact, for all
a\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
, we have

\operatorname{Tr}\big[ae^{-t\Delta}\big]=\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}t^{-\frac{n}{2}}\left[\tau_{0}(a)+\operatorname{O}\big(e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}}{t}}\big)\right]\qquad\text{as}\ t\rightarrow 0^{+}.

This heat-trace asymptotic is an elementary consequence of Poisson’s summation formula. It follows that Condition (W) is satisfied with

(1.43)

\tau(a):=\frac{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+1\right)}\tau_{0}(a)=\hat{c}(n)\tau_{0}(a).

Moreover, the Cwikel-type estimates of
[
132
,
134
]
ensure us that Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
) is satisfied, with

(1.44)

\mathscr{V}_{r}=L_{r}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})\ (r>1),\qquad\mathscr{V}_{r}=L_{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})\ (r=1<r^{\prime}),\qquad\mathscr{V}_{r}=L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})\ (r<1).

It follows that the assumptions of Theorem
1.5
hold. Conjecture
1.14
then follows from a direct application of that result.

We may also apply Theorem
1.6
. This gives the following integration formulas.

Integration Formulas

(Theorem
10.8
)
.

For every
x\in L_{r}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
,
r>1
, the operators
\Delta^{-n/4}x\Delta^{-n/4}
and
|\Delta^{-n/4}x\Delta^{-n/4}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

(1.45)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\Delta^{-\frac{n}{4}}x\Delta^{-\frac{n}{4}}=\hat{c}(n)\tau_{0}\big[x\big],

(1.46)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\big|\Delta^{-\frac{n}{4}}x\Delta^{-\frac{n}{4}}\big|=\hat{c}(n)\tau\big[|x|\big].

In the above level of generality the integration formula (
1.45
) is known (see
[
133
, Corollary 5.3]
; see also
[
136
,
155
]
). The improvement is getting a stronger form of measurability. In
[
133
,
136
,
155
]
the focus is on strong measurability. The above result provides spectral measurability, which is a stronger property.

The integration formula (
1.46
) is new. Note that, even for
x\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
, it does not follow from the trace theorems for
\Psi
DOs in
[
136
,
155
]
, since
|\Delta^{-n/4}x\Delta^{-n/4}|
need not be a
\Psi
DO.

1.5.3.
Dirac spectral triple

The above results apply
verbatim
to the Dirac spectral triple of
[
87
, §§12.3]
.

Recall that the spinor bundle of ordinary torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}
is trivial with typical fiber the spinor space
\not{S}_{n}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}
, where
N:=2^{[n/2]}
. The Dirac operator
\not{D}:C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{N}\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{N}
is then defined by

\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\partial_{j}\otimes\gamma_{j},

where
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}
are the canonical Clifford matrices in
M_{N}(\mathbb{C})
with relations
\gamma_{j}\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{k}\gamma_{j}=-2\delta_{jk}
and
\gamma_{j}^{*}=-\gamma_{j}
. We then get the spectral triple,

\left(C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta}),L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n};\not{S}_{n}),\not{D}\right).

As
\not{D}^{2}=\Delta\otimes 1
, the results for the square-root Laplacian show that Condition (W) holds with

\tau(a):=\not{\hat{c}}(n)\tau_{0}(a),\qquad\not{\hat{c}}(n):=N|\mathbb{B}^{n}|=2^{\left[\frac{n}{2}\right]}|\mathbb{B}^{n}|.

In addition, Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
) hold exactly as with the spectral triple associated with the square-root Laplacian
\sqrt{\Delta}
. Therefore, we may apply Theorem
1.5
and Theorem
1.6
. We get the following semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas.

Theorem 1.15

(Theorem
10.16
)
.

Given any
q>0
and
V=V^{*}\in L_{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
, for every energy level
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{n}N\big(h^{2q}|\not{D}|^{2q}+V;\lambda\big)=\not{\hat{c}}(n)\tau_{0}\big[(V-\lambda)_{-}^{\frac{n}{2q}}\big].

Integration Formulas

(Theorem
10.17
)
.

For every
x\in L_{r}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
,
r>1
,
the operators
|\not{D}|^{-n/2}x|\not{D}|^{-n/2}
and
||\not{D}|^{-n/2}x|\not{D}|^{-n/2}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int|\not{D}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}x|\not{D}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}=\not{\hat{c}}(n)\tau_{0}\big[x\big],

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\big||\not{D}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}x|\not{D}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}\big|=\not{\hat{c}}(n)\tau\big[|x|\big].

1.6.
Sub-Riemannian Geometry

In Chapter
11
, we apply the main results of the monograph to get semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas in sub-Riemannian geometry. These results connect nicely with a string of recent results on spectral asymptotics and quantum limits in sub-Riemannian geometry (see, e.g.,
[
11
,
48
,
50
,
75
,
76
,
77
,
78
,
121
,
138
]
). In addition, in the contact case we obtain stronger forms of the integration formulas of
[
114
,
153
]
.

In what follows, we let
(M,H,g_{H})
be a closed sub-Riemannian manifold, where
M
is an
n
-dimensional closed manifold,
H
is a Lie-bracket generating subbundle of the tangent bundle
TM
of rank
m
and
g_{H}
is a sub-Riemannian metric on
H
, i.e., a smooth field of positive-definite inner products on its fibers. The Lie-bracket generating condition
H
then ensures that, at every point
x_{0}\in M
, we have a filtration,

(1.47)

H(x_{0})=:H_{1}(x_{0})\subseteq H_{2}(x_{0})\subseteq\cdots\subseteq H_{r}(x_{0})=TM(x_{0}),

where
H_{w}(x_{0})
,
w\geq 2
, is the subspace spanned by iterated Lie brackets,

[X_{i_{1}},[X_{i_{2}},\cdots[X_{i_{k-1}},X_{i_{k}}]\cdots]](x_{0}),\qquad k\leq w,

with
X_{i_{1}},\ldots,X_{i_{k}}
ranging over all
H
-valued vector fields near
x_{0}
.

Sub-Riemannian structures and Lie-bracket generating distributions naturally occur in a variety of settings. This includes control theory of non-holonomic systems of vector fields as those that comes into play in computer vision and image processing, Asian option in finance, dynamics of polymer, and distributions of clusters in space in astronomy (see
[
35
]
). They also appear on (graded) nilpotent Lie groups and their quotients by lattices (i.e., nilmanifolds).

In addition, Sub-Riemannian structures appear in the setting of parabolic geometry
[
38
]
, including boundary-geometry of symmetric spaces of rank
\geq 2
. Contact manifolds (e.g., cosphere bundles of positive line bundles) and their various generalizations yield additional examples. A related class of examples is provided by boundaries of complex domains (e.g., odd-dimensional Euclidean spheres), and more generally finite-type Cauchy-Riemann manifolds.

We further assume that
M
is equipped with a smooth measure
\nu
; it allows us to define an inner product on
L^{2}(M)
. In this setting, the analogue of the Laplacian is provided by the
sub-Laplacian

\Delta_{H}:C^{\infty}_{c}(M)\rightarrow C^{\infty}_{c}(M)
. We refer to Chapter
11
for its precise definition. This is a formally selfadjoint 2nd order differential operator. In the Riemannian setting, i.e.,
H=TM
, in which case
g_{H}
is a Riemannian metric, we recover the Laplacian associated with
\nu
and this Riemannian metric. In general, if
X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}
is a local orthonormal frame of
H
, then, locally, we have

\Delta_{H}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq m}X_{j}^{*}X_{j}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq m}\left(-X_{j}^{2}+\operatorname{div}_{\nu}(X_{j})X_{j}\right).

where
\operatorname{div}_{\nu}
is the divergence of vector fields with respect to the measure
\nu
. In particular, if
m<n
, then
\Delta_{H}
