is not elliptic. However, by a celebrated result of Hörmander
[
104
]
, the Lie-bracket generating condition ensures that
\Delta_{H}
and the heat operator
\Delta_{H}+\partial_{t}
on
M\times\mathbb{R}
are both hypoelliptic with loss of derivatives.

As the manifold
M
is closed, the hypoellipticity of
\Delta_{H}
ensures it is essentially selfadjoint and has a non-negative and disrete spectrum. This spectrum then can be arranged as a non-decreasing sequence,

0\leq\lambda_{0}(\Delta_{H})\leq\lambda_{1}(\Delta_{H})\leq\lambda_{2}(\Delta_{H})\leq\cdots,

where each eigenvalue is repeated according to multiplicity.

We shall further assume that the sub-Riemannian structure is
equiregular
. This means that in (
1.47
) the step
r
can be chosen independently of
x_{0}
and the dimensions
\dim H_{w}(x_{0})
,
w\geq 2
, do not vary with
x_{0}
. This condition is satisfied in many examples of sub-Riemannian structures.
For
j=2,\ldots,r
, the vector spaces
H_{j}(x_{0})
,
x_{0}\in M
, can then be organized as smooth subbundles
H_{w}\subseteq TM
. We thus get a filtration by subbundles,

\{0\}=H_{0}\subseteq H_{1}=H\subseteq H_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq H_{r}=TM.

This filtration is compatible with the Lie bracket of vector fields, i.e.,
[H_{j},H_{k}]\subseteq H_{j+k}
. Manifolds equipped with such filtrations of their tangent bundles are called
filtered manifolds
(see, e.g.,
[
38
]
). The
sub-Riemannian dimension
(or
sR-dimension
) of
M
then is defined by

N:=\sum_{1\leq j\leq r}j\dim\left(H_{j}/H_{j-1}\right).

This is also the Hausdorff dimension with respect to the Carathéodory metric defined by
(H,g_{H})
(see, e.g.,
[
1
]
).

Under the equiregularity assumption, Métivier
[
145
]
obtained a Weyl law for the sub-Laplacian
\Delta_{H}
. Namely, as
j\rightarrow\infty
we have

(1.48)

\lambda_{j}(\Delta_{H})\sim j^{\frac{2}{N}}\left(\int_{M}\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)d\nu(x)\right)^{-\frac{2}{N}},

where
\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)
is some positive smooth function on
M
(see also
[
143
,
144
]
in the contact case). In particular, the order of the r.h.s. is provided by the sR-dimension
N
, and not
\dim M
as we have in the Riemannian case.

Alternatively, the heat kernel
k_{t}(x,y)
of
\Delta_{H}
as a short-time expansion along the diagonal (see
[
16
,
49
,
67
,
117
,
195
]
; see also
[
14
]
for the step
2
case). Namely, as
t\rightarrow 0^{+}
and uniformly on
M
, we have

(1.49)

k_{t}(x,x)\sim t^{-\frac{N}{2}}\sum_{j\geq 0}t^{\frac{j}{2}}a_{j}(\Delta_{H})(x),

where the coefficients
a_{j}(\Delta_{H})(x)
are smooth. This shows that Condition (Z) holds and provide some computable formula for the Weyl’s density
\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)
(see Chapter
11
).

In the step 2 case (i.e.,
r=2
) the relevant pseudodifferential calculus to study sub-Laplacians is the Heisenberg calculus of Beals-Greiner
[
13
]
and Taylor
[
197
]
. A version of this calculus for arbitrary filtered manifolds was presented by Melin in the unpublished preprint
[
140
]
. As shown by van Erp-Yuncken
[
199
]
this can understood in terms of pseudodifferential calculi on Lie groupoids (see also
[
6
]
for an extension of this approach to singular Lie filtrations). In what follows shall refer the operators of this pseudodifferential calculus as
\Psi_{H}
DOs.

Results of Dave-Haller
[
67
,
68
]
(see also
[
154
]
in the step 2 case) ensure that the square root
\sqrt{\Delta_{H}}
is a
\Psi_{H}
DO of order
1
. As a result we get a spectral triple,

\left(C^{\infty}(M),L_{\nu}^{2}(M),\sqrt{\Delta_{H}}\right),

where
C^{\infty}(M)
acts by bounded multiplication operators on
L_{\nu}^{2}(M)
. The Weyl law (
1.48
) implies that the above spectral triple is
N
-summable. Moreover, Condition (W) holds with

\tau(f):=\int_{M}f(x)\gamma(\Delta_{H})d\nu(x),\qquad f\in C(M).

In this setting Proposition
1.1
yields the following spectral asymptotics.

Spectral Asymptotics

(Proposition
11.1
)
.

Given any
q>0
and any function
f\in C(M)
, we have the spectral asymptotics,

(1.50)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{N}}\mu_{j}\big(\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{q}{4}}f\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{q}{4}}\big)=\left(\int_{M}|f(x)|^{\frac{N}{q}}\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)d\nu(x)\right)^{\frac{q}{N}},

(1.51)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{N}}\lambda_{j}^{\pm}\big(\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{q}{4}}f\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{q}{4}}\big)=\left(\int_{M}f_{\pm}(x)^{\frac{N}{q}}\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)d\nu(x)\right)^{\frac{q}{N}}\quad(\mathrm{if}\ f\ \mathrm{is\ real\ valued}).

It is conjectured in
[
157
, Remark 6.11]
and
[
131
, §2.5]
that there are analogues for negative order
\Psi_{H}
DOs of Weyl laws of Birman-Solomyak for negative order pseudodifferential operators (see (
1.32
)–(
1.33
)). The above result shows that this conjecture holds for the Birman-Schwinger operators
\Delta_{H}^{-q/4}f\Delta_{H}^{-q/4}
,
q>0
. In fact, it continuous to hold if we replace
\Delta_{H}
by any power of a positive Rockland differential operator (
cf
. Remark
11.2
).

Applying Theorem
1.2
yields the following semiclassical result.

Semiclassical Weyl Law

(Theorem
11.3
)
.

Let
q>0
. Given any potential
V\in C(M,\mathbb{R})
, for every energy level
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

(1.52)

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{N}N\big(h^{2q}\Delta_{H}^{q}+V;\lambda\big)=\int_{M}(V(x)-\lambda)_{-}^{\frac{N}{2q}}\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)d\nu(x).

Using a different approach, Fischer-Mikkelsen
[
78
]
recently obtained semiclassical Weyl laws in the special setting of nilmanifolds. Note that for nilmanifolds the density
\gamma(\Delta_{H})
is constant (see Remark
11.11
). We also refer to
[
138
]
for a semiclassical Weyl law on graded nilpotent Lie group of sub-Riemannian dimension
N\geq 4
(see also
[
63
,
122
]
for related results). The semiclassical Weyl law (
1.52
) seems to be the first instance of such a result for sub-Riemannian manifolds that are not locally equivalent to graded nilpotent Lie groups.

Applying Theorem
1.3
yields integration formulas in sub-Riemannian geometry. In particular, we have the following result.

Integration Formulas

(Theorem
11.6
)
.

For every function
f\in C(M)
, the operators
f\Delta_{H}^{-N/2}
,
\Delta_{H}^{-N/4}f\Delta_{H}^{-N/4}
, and
|\Delta_{H}^{-N/4}f\Delta_{H}^{-N/4}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

(1.53)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int f\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{N}{2}}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{N}{4}}f\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{N}{4}}=\int_{M}f(x)\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)d\nu(x),

(1.54)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\left|\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{N}{4}}f\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{N}{4}}\right|=\int_{M}|f(x)|\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)d\nu(x).

The integration formulas (
1.53
)–(
1.54
) are new. In the step 2 case the integration formula (
1.54
) is known (see
[
153
]
; see also
[
114
]
for the contact case). However, even in that case, the above result provides a stronger form of measurability. In fact, in
[
114
, p. 18]
it is conjectured that we should have strong measurability and spectral asymptotics of the form (
1.50
). Therefore, our results establish these conjectures in the far more general setting of (equiregular) sub-Riemannian geometry. Moreover, our approach bypasses the technical considerations in
[
114
]
inherent to the
C^{*}
-approach to the principal symbol that is used there.

We refer to §
11.6
of Chapter
11
for a comparison of the integration formulas (
1.53
)–(
1.54
) with the version for
\Psi_{H}
DOs of Connes’ trace theorem that recently appeared in
[
131
]
.

In the contact case, the Weyl density
\gamma(\Delta_{H})
can be computed explicitly thanks to the results of
[
14
]
(see also
[
197
]
). This gives explicit semiclassical Weyl law and integration formulas for contact manifolds (see Theorem
11.14
and Theorem
11.15
for the precise statements). In particular, this provides a refinement of the integration formulas of
[
114
,
153
]
.

The above results actually hold in far greater generality than stated above. First, the results of
[
67
,
68
,
154
]
actually apply to all positive selfadjoint Rockland operators on general filtered manifolds (see Remark
11.12
). Therefore, our approach remains valid if we replace
\Delta_{H}
by anu such operator (see Remark
11.12
). Second, in the light of the results of
[
6
,
50
]
, it is expected that our results can be extended to a large class of singular sub-Riemannian manifolds, including the Baouendi-Grushin example (see the discussion on this in §
11.5
of Chapter
11
).

Finally, we refer to §
11.6
of Chapter
11
for a comparison of the integration formulas (
1.53
)–(
1.54
) with the extension of Connes’ trace theorem to
\Psi_{H}
DOs in the recent preprint of McDonald
[
131
]
(which appeared near the end of the completion of this manuscript).

1.7.
Final comments

The focus of this memoir is on
unital
spectral triples. It would be of upmost importance to extend the results of this memoir to
non-unital
spectral triples. This would allow us to include in our framework Euclidean spaces, infinite volume geometries, and quantum Euclidean spaces. Condition (W) is really specific to unital spectral triples. However, the equivalent condition (
3.7
) and the Tauberian conditions (Z) and (H) continue to make sense in the non-unital case with minor modifications. In addition, it is clear that the considerations on double operator-integrals of this memoir hold in much greater generality.

Condition (W) is stated in terms of
classical
Weyl laws. However, in a number of examples we actually have
nonclassical
Weyl laws in the sense of Barry Simon
[
181
]
. This means Weyl laws involving logarithmic of the spectral parameter, instead of sole powers. For instance, if Riemann Hypothesis holds and we let
\mathscr{D}
be the Dirac operator whose eigenvalues are the imaginary parts of the zeros of Riemann’s zeta function (see
[
54
,
60
]
), then by the Riemann-van Managoldt formula the counting function of
\mathscr{D}^{2}
satisfies a
nonclassical
Weyl law of the form,

N\big(\mathscr{D}^{2};\lambda^{2}\big)=\frac{\lambda}{\pi}\left(\log\left(\frac{\lambda}{2\pi}\right)-1\right)+\operatorname{O}(\log\lambda)\qquad\text{as}\ \lambda\rightarrow\infty.

In fact, in a number examples arising from manifolds with infinite volumes or with singularities the corresponding geometric operators may satisfy nonclassical Weyl laws.

From the point of view of spectral triples this amounts to replace summability in terms of weak Schatten classes by summability in terms of weak Lorentz ideals
\mathscr{L}_{g}
of the form,

(1.55)

\mathscr{L}_{g}:=\left\{T;\ \mu_{j}(T)=\operatorname{O}\left(g(j)\right)\right\},\qquad g(t)=t^{-p}(\log(t+1))^{q},\quad p,q>0.

Therefore, a proper extension of the results of this memoir to non-unital spectral triples should include spectral triples that are summable with respect to the above types of weak Lorentz ideals.

A careful study of the relationships between Connes’ integration in weak Lorentz ideals and nonclassical Weyl laws is given in the forthcoming paper
[
158
]
(see also
[
84
,
90
,
125
]
). It also seems likely that, at least for unital spectral triples, the considerations on double operator-integrals of this memoir hold
verbatim
if we replace weak Schatten classes by powers of any weak Lorentz ideal (
1.55
) with
p=1
. We will need to allow poles of higher order in Condition (Z) and incorporate logarithmic terms in the heat trace expansion in Condition (H).

Finally, the main results of this memoir for a given spectral triple
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D)
involves fractional powers of
|D|
, rather than of
D
, or, equivalently fractional powers of
D^{2}
. As pointed out to the author by Magnus Goffeng
[
88
]
, it would be interesting to extend the results of this memoir to generalized spectral triples
like those considered by Goffeng-Mesland
[
89
, Appendix A]
, or for a variant of the definition there. For instance, we could consider triples

(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D)
that are like spectral triples, for which the condition on the boundedness of the commutators
[D,a]
,
a\in\mathscr{A}
, is replaced by the boundedness of the operators
(1+D^{2})^{(1-m)/4m}[D,a](1+D^{2})^{(1-m)/4m}
,
a\in\mathscr{A}
, for some number
m>0
. For instance, if
(M^{n},g)
is a closed Riemannian manifold and
\mathscr{A}=C^{\infty}(M)
, the above condition is satisfied by
D=\Delta_{g}
for
m=2
. This would bypass some of the technical difficulties to checking in some examples that fractional powers of natural operators give rise to (ordinary) spectral triples.

1.8.
Organization of the paper

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Chapter
2
, we review the main background on weak Schatten ideals, Connes’ integration and their relationships with Weyl laws and the Birman-Schwinger principle. In Chapter
3
, we prove Proposition
1.1
(assuming Lemma
1.10
and Lemma
1.12
) and we deduce from it Theorem
1.2
through Theorem
1.6
. In Chapter
4
, we prove Theorem
1.7
assuming Lemma
1.10
. In Chapter
5
, we use double integral operators techniques to prove Lemma
1.12
. In Chapter
6
, we prove Lemma
1.10
and Lemma
1.11
.

In the remaining chapters we apply the main results of the monograph to various settings. In Chapter
7
, we look at the semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas for powers of the Dirichlet and Neumann Laplacians on bounded Euclidean domains. In Chapter
8
, we deal with the results for closed Riemanian manifolds. Chapter
9
is devoted to proving semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas for open manifolds with conformally cusp metrics. In Chapter
10
the focus is on quantum tori. In particular, we prove Conjecture
1.14
. In Chapter
11
, we prove semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas in sub-Riemannian geometry.

Chapter 2
Weak Schatten Classes, Connes’ Integration, and Weyl laws

In this chapter, we review the main facts regarding Connes’ integration and its relationship with Birman-Solmyak’s perturbation theory. The presentation follows closely that of
[
157
]
.

Throughout this chapter we let
\mathscr{H}
be a (separable) Hilbert space, and we denote by
\mathscr{K}
the (closed) ideal of compact operators on
\mathscr{H}
.

2.1.
Weak Schatten Classes

We recall here the main definition and properties regarding weak Schatten classes. We refer to
[
91
,
182
]
for more detailed accounts on this topic.

Given any operator
T\in\mathscr{K}
, we denote by
(\mu_{j}(T))_{j\geq 0}
its sequence of
singular values
, i.e.,
\mu_{j}(T)
is the
(j+1)
-th eigenvalue counted with multiplicity of the absolute value
|T|=\sqrt{T^{*}T}
. Recall that by the
min-max principle
we have

\displaystyle\mu_{j}(T)

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\|T_{|E^{\perp}}\|;\ \dim E=j\right\},

(2.1)

\displaystyle=\min\{\|T-R\|;\ R\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}),\ \operatorname{rk}(R)\leq j\}.

Note that
\mu_{0}(T)=\|T\|
. We record the following properties of the singular values (see, e.g.,
[
91
,
182
]
),

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mu_{j}(T)=\mu_{j}(T^{*})=\mu_{j}(|T|),

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mu_{j+k}(S+T)\leq\mu_{j}(S)+\mu_{k}(T),

(2.4)

\displaystyle\mu_{j}(ATB)\leq\|A\|\mu_{j}(T)\|B\|,\quad\mu_{j}(U^{*}TU)=\mu_{j}(T),

where
A,B\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
and
U\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
is unitary.

By definition the
Schatten class

\mathscr{L}_{p}
,
p>0
, consist of operators
T\in\mathscr{K}
such that
|T|^{p}
is trace-class. It is equipped with the quasi-norm,

\|T\|_{p}:=\operatorname{Tr}\big(|T|^{p}\big)^{\frac{1}{p}}=\bigg(\sum_{j\geq 0}\mu_{j}(T)^{p}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}},\qquad T\in\mathscr{L}_{p}.

We obtain a quasi-Banach ideal. For
p\geq 1
the above quasi-norm is actually a norm, and so in this case
\mathscr{L}_{p}
is a Banach ideal.

The
weak Schatten class

\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
,
p>0
, is defined by

\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}:=\left\{T\in\mathscr{K};\ \mu_{j}(T)=\operatorname{O}\big(j^{-\frac{1}{p}}\big)\right\}.

This is a two-sided ideal. We equip it with the quasi-norm,

(2.5)

\|T\|_{p,\infty}:=\sup_{j\geq 0}\;(j+1)^{\frac{1}{p}}\mu_{j}(T),\qquad T\in\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}.

With this quasi-norm
\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
is a quasi-Banach ideal. In particular, we have

\|ATB\|_{p,\infty}\leq\|A\|\|T\|_{p,\infty}\|B\|\qquad A,B\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}),\ T\in\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}.

For
p>1
the above quasi-norm is equivalent to an actual norm. Thus, in this case
\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
is a Banach ideal.

We denote by
(\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty})_{0}
the closure in
\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
of the ideal of finite-rank operators. Equivalently,

\big(\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}\big)_{0}=\left\{T\in\mathscr{K};\ \mu_{j}(T)=\operatorname{o}\big(j^{-\frac{1}{p}}\big)\right\}.

We have the following (strict) continuous inclusions,

(2.6)

\mathscr{L}_{p}\subsetneq\big(\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}\big)_{0}\subsetneq\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}\subsetneq\mathscr{L}_{q},\qquad 0<p<q.

We mention the following version of Hölder’s inequality for weak Schatten classes.

Proposition 2.1

(Hölder’s Inequality; see
[
91
,
182
,
190
]
)
.

Suppose that
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=r^{-1}
.

(1)

If
S\in\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
and
T\in\mathscr{L}_{q,\infty}
, then
ST\in\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty}
, and we have

(2.7)

\|ST\|_{r,\infty}\leq C_{pq}\|S\|_{p,\infty}\|T\|_{q,\infty}.

where
C_{pq}=p^{-\frac{1}{q}}q^{-\frac{1}{p}}(p+q)^{\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}}
and the inequality is sharp.

(2)

If in addition
S\in(\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty})_{0}
or
T\in(\mathscr{L}_{q,\infty})_{0}
, then

ST\in(\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty})_{0}
.

We will also need the following version of the BKS inequality of Birman-Koplenko-Solomyak.

Proposition 2.2

(BKS Inequality; see
[
19
,
31
]
)
.

Let
A
and
B
be positive operators on
\mathscr{H}
such that
A-B\in\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
,
p>0
. Given any
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the difference

A^{\alpha}-B^{\alpha}
is in
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha^{-1}p,\infty}
, and we have

\big\|A^{\alpha}-B^{\alpha}\big\|_{\alpha^{-1}p,\infty}\leq C_{p\alpha}\|A-B\|_{p,\infty}^{\alpha},

where the constant
C_{p\alpha}
depends only on
p
and
\alpha
. If in addition,
A-B\in(\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty})_{0}
, then
A^{\alpha}-B^{\alpha}
is actually in
(\mathscr{L}_{\alpha^{-1}p,\infty})_{0}
.

Remark 2.3
.

The last part in the statement above is the contents of
[
19
, Theorem 3]
.

2.2.
Connes’ integration

One of the main goals of noncommutative geometry
[
53
]
is to translate into the Hilbert space formalism of quantum mechanics the main tools of differential geometry. In this framework the notion of integral corresponds to positive traces on the weak trace class
\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}
. Such traces are annihilated by finite rank operators (see, e.g.,
[
129
]
) and are always continuous (see, e.g.,
[
155
, Remark 2.3]
), and so they are annihilated by
(\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty})_{0}
.

There is a whole zoo of positive traces on
\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}
(see, e.g.,
[
129
,
130
]
and the references therein). One important class of such traces is provided by Dixmier traces
\operatorname{Tr}_{\omega}:\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
(see
[
69
]
; see also
[
53
,
129
,
157
]
). We then say that an operator
A\in\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}
is
measurable
if the value
\operatorname{Tr}_{\omega}(A)
does not depend on of the choice of the Dixmier trace. Equivalently (see
[
129
,
157
]
), the operator
A
is measurable if and only if it satisfies the following Tauberian condition,

(2.8)

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int A:=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\log(N+1)}\sum_{j<N}\lambda_{j}(A)\quad\text{exists}.

Here
(\lambda_{j}(A))_{j\geq 0}
is any eigenvalue sequence of
A
in such a way that

|\lambda_{0}(A)|\geq|\lambda_{1}(A)|\geq\cdots\geq|\lambda_{j}(A)|\geq\cdots\geq 0.

where each eigenvalue is repeating according to its (algebraic) multiplicity. The limit
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int A
is then called the
NC integral
of
A
.

There are numerous positive traces on
\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}
that are not Dixmier traces (see, e.g.,
[
178
]
). Therefore, it stands for reason to consider a stronger notion of measurability. We say that an operator
A\in\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}
is
strongly measurable
(or
positively measurable
) if all positive normalized trace take on the same value on
A
. Here a trace
\varphi
on
\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}
is called normalized if

\big(A\geq 0\ \text{and}\ \lambda_{j}(A)=(j+1)^{-1}\big)\ \Longrightarrow\ \varphi(A)=1.

The Dixmier traces are positive normalized traces. Thus, if
A\in\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}
is strongly measurable, then
A
is measurable and, for any positive normalized trace
\varphi
on
\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}
, we have

\varphi(A)=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int A=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\log(N+1)}\sum_{j<N}\lambda_{j}(A).

We refer to
[
178
, Section 7]
for a characterization of strongly measurable operators in terms of their eigenvalue sequences, as well as for examples of measurable operators that are not strongly measurable.

As an example, let
\mathscr{H}=L^{2}(M)
, where
(M^{n},g)
is a closed Riemannian manifold. Denote by
\Delta_{g}
the corresponding Laplacian acting on (smooth) functions. This operator is essentially selfadjoint and has pure discrete non-negative spectrum. The partial inverse
\Delta_{g}^{-1}
is in the weak Schatten class
\mathscr{L}_{n/2,\infty}
. A well known result of Connes
[
52
]
asserts that, for every
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, the operator
f\Delta_{g}^{-n/2}
is measurable, and we have

(2.9)

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int f\Delta_{g}^{-\frac{n}{2}}=c(n)\int_{M}f(x)d\nu_{g}(x),\qquad c(n):=(2\pi)^{-n}|\mathbb{B}^{n-1}|,

where
d\nu_{g}(x)
is the Riemannian measure. The above formula is often called
Connes’ integration formula
. It shows that the NC integral recaptures the Riemannian density. In particular,
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int\Delta_{g}^{-n/2}=c(n)\operatorname{Vol}_{g}(M)
.

It was further shown in
[
108
]
that the above formula continues to hold for functions in
L^{2}(M)
and the operators
f\Delta_{g}^{-\frac{n}{2}}
are actually strongly measurable. Recently, versions of the formula for functions in the Orlicz
L\!\log\!L
-space have been established (see
[
165
,
192
]
; see also
[
156
]
).
We will address further extensions of Connes’ integration formula in the Riemannian case in Chapter
8
.

2.3.
NC integration and Weyl laws

An important class of strongly measurable operators is provided by operators that satisfy some form of Weyl law. Given any
A=A^{*}\in\mathscr{K}
we denote by
\pm\lambda_{j}^{\pm}(A)
its positive and negative eigenvalues in such a way that

\lambda_{0}^{\pm}(A)\geq\lambda_{1}^{\pm}(A)\geq\cdots>0,

where each eigenvalue is repeated according to multiplicity. If
A\geq 0
, then we set
\lambda_{j}(A)=\lambda_{j}^{+}(A)
.

Let
p>0
. We say that
A=A^{*}\in\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
,
p>0
, is a
Weyl operator
if
\lim j^{1/p}\lambda_{j}^{\pm}(A)
exist. In this case we set

\Lambda^{\pm}(A)=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{1}{p}}\lambda_{j}^{\pm}(A).

If
A\geq 0
, then we just set
\Lambda(A)=\Lambda^{+}(A)
.

If
A\in\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
is not selfadjoint, we call it a
Weyl operator
if its real part
\Re A:=\frac{1}{2}(A+A^{*})
and its imaginary part
\Im A:=\frac{1}{2i}(A-A^{*})
are both Weyl operators. In this case we define

\Lambda^{\pm}(A)=\Lambda^{\pm}\big(\Re A)+i\Lambda^{\pm}\big(\Im A).

We denote by
\mathscr{W}_{p,\infty}
the class of Weyl operators in
\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
.

We also denote by
|\mathscr{W}|_{p,\infty}
the class of operators
A\in\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
such that its absolute value
|A|
is a Weyl operator. Thus,

A\in|\mathscr{W}|_{p,\infty}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{1/p}\mu_{j}(A)\ \text{exists}.

Note that if
A\in|\mathscr{W}|_{p,\infty}
, then the above limit is just
\Lambda(|A|)
.

Proposition 2.4

(Birman-Solomyak
[
21
,
29
]
)
.

Let
p>0
. The following hold.

(1)

\mathscr{W}_{p,\infty}
and
|\mathscr{W}|_{p,\infty}
are both closed subsets of
\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}
containing
(\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty})_{0}
.

(2)

The maps
A\rightarrow\Lambda^{\pm}(A)
and
A\rightarrow\Lambda(|A|)
are continuous on
\mathscr{W}_{p,\infty}
and
|\mathscr{W}|_{p,\infty}
, respectively.

(3)

If
A\in\mathscr{W}_{p,\infty}
and
B\in(\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty})_{0}
, then
A+B\in\mathscr{W}_{p,\infty}
and
\Lambda^{\pm}(A+B)=\Lambda^{\pm}(A)
.

(4)

If
A\in|\mathscr{W}|_{p,\infty}
and
B\in(\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty})_{0}
, then
A+B\in|\mathscr{W}|_{p,\infty}
and
\Lambda(|A+B|)=\Lambda(|A|)
.

Remark 2.5
.

The last two parts go back to Fan
[
73
]
(see also
[
91
]
).

For
p=1
, it follows from (
2.8
) that any Weyl operator in
\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}
is measurable. We actually have a stronger result.

Proposition 2.6

(see
[
157
]
)
.

If
A\in\mathscr{W}_{1,\infty}
, then
A
is strongly measurable, and we have

(2.10)

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int A=\Lambda^{+}(A)-\Lambda^{-}(A).

In particular, if
A=A^{*}\in\mathscr{W}_{1,\infty}
, then

(2.11)

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int A=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\lambda_{j}^{+}(A)-\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\lambda_{j}^{-}(A).

In addition, if
A\in|\mathscr{W}|_{1,\infty}
(i.e.,
|A|\in\mathscr{W}_{1,\infty}
), then

(2.12)

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int|A|=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\mu_{j}(A).

The above proposition leads to the following definition.

Definition 2.7
.

The operators in
\mathscr{W}_{1,\infty}
are called
spectrally measurable
.

Proposition
2.6
thus asserts that any spectrally measurable operator is strongly measurable. Thus spectral measurability is even stronger form of measurability than strong measurability. Moreover, if
A\in\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}
is spectrally measurable, then from (
2.10
)–(
2.11
) we get

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int A=\left[\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\lambda_{j}^{+}(\Re A)-\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\lambda_{j}^{-}(\Re A)\right]+i\left[\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\lambda_{j}^{+}(\Im A)-\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\lambda_{j}^{-}(\Im A)\right].

2.4.
Connes’ integration and Birman-Schwinger principle

Let
H_{0}
be an unbounded selfadjoint operator with non-negative spectrum. We assume that
0
is not in the essential spectrum of
H_{0}
, i.e., it is either not
in
\operatorname{Sp}(H_{0})
or is an isolated eigenvalue with finite multiplicity. These conditions are automatically satisfied if
H_{0}
has compact resolvent. In any case, this allows us to define the (partial) inverse
H_{0}^{-1}
in the usual way.

Let
V
be a selfdjoint operator which is
H_{0}
-form compact, i.e.,
\operatorname{dom}(V)\supseteq\operatorname{dom}(1+H_{0})^{1/2}
and
(1+H_{0})^{-1/2}V(1+H_{0})^{-1/2}
is a compact operator. We then define the operator
H_{V}:=H_{0}+V
as a form-sum (see, e.g.,
[
183
]
). This operator is selfadjoint, bounded from below, and has same essential spectrum as
H_{0}
. This implies that the negative part of its spectrum consists of finitely isolated eigenvalues. Moreover, if
H_{0}
has a compact resolvent, then
H_{V}
has a compact resolvent as well (
loc. cit.
).

For
h>0
, denote by
N^{-}(h^{2}H_{0}+V)
the number of negative eigenvalues of the Schrödinger operator
h^{2}H_{0}+V
counted with multiplicity. In the case of Schrödinger operators on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
this is precisely the number of bound states. We are interested in the behaviour of
N^{-}(h^{2}H_{0}+V)
under the semiclassical limit
h\rightarrow 0^{+}
. We shall use the following form of the Birman-Schwinger principle.

Proposition 2.8
.

If
H_{0}^{-1/2}VH_{0}^{-1/2}
is in
\mathscr{W}_{p,\infty}
,
p>0
, then

(2.13)

\displaystyle\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{2p}N^{-}\big(h^{2}H_{0}+V\big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\lambda_{j}^{-}\left(H_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}VH_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{p}

(2.14)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\big(H_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}VH_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)_{-}^{p}

Remark 2.9
.

The equality (
2.13
) is a well known consequence of the Birman-Schwinger principle in its abstract form given in
[
30
, Lemma 1.4]
(see also
[
22
, Theorem 10.1]
).
In fact, the equality holds whenever any of the limit exists. The equality (
2.14
) is a direct consequence of Proposition
2.6
.
For the reader’s convenience a proof of Proposition
2.8
is included in
[
157
]
.

Chapter 3
Spectral asymptotics, Semiclassical Weyl laws, and Integration Formulas

In this chapter, after presenting Condition (W) and some of its implications, we prove Lemma
1.9
and Proposition
1.1
, assuming Lemma
1.10
and Lemma
1.12
whose proofs are postponed to Chapter
5
and Chapter
6
, respectively. We shall then prove the main semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas stated in Introduction (i.e., Theorem
1.2
through Theorem
1.6
) as consequences of Proposition
1.1
.

3.1.
Spectral triples

In the framework of noncommutative geometry, noncommutative manifolds are represented by spectral triples. We shall use the following definition of spectral triples.

Definition 3.1
.

A
spectral triple
is a triple
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D)
, where
\mathscr{A}
is a unital
*
-algebra represented by bounded operators on the (separable) Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}
and
D
is a selfadjoint unbounded operator on
\mathscr{H}
with compact resolvent such that

a\left(\operatorname{dom}(D)\right)\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(D)\quad\text{and}\quad[D,a]\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})\qquad\forall a\in\mathscr{A}.

We further say that
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D)
is
p
-summable
, with
p>0
, if the partial inverse
D^{-1}
is in
\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
.

The prototype of a spectral triple is given by the Dirac spectral triple
(C^{\infty}(M),L^{2}(M,\not{S}),\not{D})
, where
M
is a closed Riemannian spin manifold,
L^{2}(M,\not{S})
is the Hilbert space of
L^{2}
-spinors on
M
and
\not{D}
is the Dirac operator of
M
acting on spinors. Another example is the square-root Laplacian spectral triple
(C^{\infty}(M),L^{2}(M),\sqrt{\Delta_{g}})
, where
\Delta_{g}
is the (positive) Laplacian of
(M,g)
acting on functions. Both spectral triples are
p
-summable with
p=\dim M
.

Some authors also require some regularity properties for the spectral triple. A spectral triple
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D)
is called
Lipschitz regular
if

\big[|D|,a\big]\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})\qquad\forall a\in\mathscr{A}.

In other words,
\mathscr{A}
is contained in the domain of the derivation
\delta(T):=[|D|,T]
. We also say that

(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D)
is
QC^{\infty}
-
regular
if

\text{$a$ and $[D,a]$ are in}\ \bigcap_{j\geq 1}\operatorname{dom}\delta^{j}\ \text{for all $a\in\mathscr{A}$}.

For instance, the Dirac spectral triple
(C^{\infty}(M),L^{2}(M,\not{S}),\not{D})
and the square-root Laplacian spectral triple
(C^{\infty}(M),L^{2}(M),\sqrt{\Delta_{g}})
are
QC^{\infty}
-regular.

Throughout the rest of this chapter, we let
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D)
be a
p
-summable spectral triple with
p>0
. We denote by
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
the closure of
\mathscr{A}
in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
, and let
\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
be its cone of positive elements (i.e., selfadjoint elements with non-negative spectrum). We also denote by
\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
the open cone of its invertible elements (i.e., selfadjoint elements with positive spectrum). The cone
{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
is then defined be to
\mathscr{A}\cap\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
, i.e., it consists of elements of
\mathscr{A}
that are positive and invertible in
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
.

3.2.
Condition (W)

Let
\Pi_{0}
be the orthogonal projection onto
\ker D
. This is a finite rank operator, since
D
has compact resolvent. Let
D^{-1}
be the partial inverse of
D
, so that
D^{2}D^{-2}=1-\Pi_{0}
on
\mathscr{H}
and
D^{-2}D^{2}=1-\Pi_{0}
on
\operatorname{dom}(D^{2})
. If
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
, then the action of
a
on
\mathscr{H}
is an isomorphism. Thus,
aD^{2}a
is a selfadjoint densily defined operator on
\mathscr{H}
with non-negative spectrum whose domain is
a^{-1}(\operatorname{dom}(D^{2}))
. In particular,
\operatorname{dom}(aD^{2}a)\supseteq a^{-1}(\operatorname{ran}D^{2})
, and so on
\mathscr{H}
we have

(3.1)

\big(aD^{2}a\big)\big(a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1}\big)=aD^{2}D^{-2}a^{-1}=1-a\Pi_{0}a^{-1}.

Likewise, on
\operatorname{dom}(aD^{2}a)=a^{-1}(\operatorname{dom}(D^{2}))
we have

(3.2)

(a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1})(aD^{2}a)=1-a^{-1}\Pi_{0}a.

This shows that
aD^{2}a
is invertible modulo finite rank operators, and so this is a Fredholm operator. In particular, it has pure discrete spectrum, and we can list its
positive
eigenvalues as a sequence,

(3.3)

0<\lambda_{0}\big(aD^{2}a\big)\leq\lambda_{1}\big(aD^{2}a\big)\leq\lambda_{2}\big(aD^{2}a\big)\leq\cdots,

where each eigenvalue is repeated according to multiplicity.

The spectral condition on which the approach relies is the following.

Condition (W)
.

For every
a\in{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
, we have

(3.4)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{-\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(aD^{2}a\big)=\tau\big[a^{-p}\big]^{-\frac{2}{p}},

where
\tau:\overline{\mathscr{A}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a given non-zero positive linear map.

Remark 3.2
.

For
a=1
the Weyl law (
3.4
) gives

\lambda_{j}(|D|)=\sqrt{\lambda(D^{2})}\sim j^{1/p}\tau[1]^{-1/p}\qquad\text{as}\ j\rightarrow\infty.

This automatically implies that
|D|^{-1}\in\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
, i.e., the spectral triple
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D)
is
p
-summable.

Remark 3.3
.

The assumption on the linearity of
\tau
is not necessary. In the main examples the linearity is immediate. Moreover, the map
\tau
will be later identified with a linear map (see Remark
3.21
). Therefore, there is no harm in assuming it beforehand.

Remark 3.4
.

The fact that
\tau
is a non-zero positive linear map on
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
ensures that
\tau
is continuous and
\tau(a)>0
for all
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
.

Remark 3.5
.

If
A=A^{*}\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
and we set
D_{A}=D+A
, then
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D_{A})
is a
p
-summable spectral triple. It can also be shown that if
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D_{A})
satisfies Condition (W), then
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D_{A})
still satisfies this condition with the same map
\tau
(see Lemma
3.10
below). Thus, Condition (W) is invariant under bounded perturbations
D\rightarrow D+A
. This includes the inner fluctuations of the metric in the sense of Connes (see, e.g.,
[
55
]
).

It is useful to relate the Weyl laws (
3.4
) to Weyl laws for the compact operators
aD^{-2}a
,
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}
.

Lemma 3.6
.

For every
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
, we have

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\left(a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1}\right)=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\left(aD^{-2}a\right)^{-1},

provided any the above limits exists in
\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{\infty\}
. That is, if one limit exists, then the other limit exists and the limits agree.

Proof.

Let
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
, and denote by
\Pi_{1}
the orthogonal projection onto
\ker(aD^{2}a)=a^{-1}(\ker D)
. By using (
3.2
) we get

\displaystyle a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1}

\displaystyle=a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1}(1-\Pi_{1})+(a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1})\Pi_{1}

\displaystyle=(a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1})(aD^{2}a)(aD^{2}a)^{-1}+(a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1})\Pi_{1}

\displaystyle=(1-a^{-1}\Pi_{0}a)(aD^{2}a)^{-1}+(a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1})\Pi_{1}

\displaystyle=(aD^{2}a)^{-1}-(a^{-1}\Pi_{0}a)(aD^{2}a)^{-1}+(a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1})\Pi_{1}.

It then follows that the operator
R:=(aD^{2}a)^{-1}-a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1}
has finite rank.

Set
N=\operatorname{rk}(R)
. The min-max principle (
2.1
) implies that
\mu_{j}(R)=0
for
j\geq N
. Thus, if
j\geq N
, then by using (
2.3
) we get

\lambda_{j}\big(\left(aD^{2}a\right)^{-1}\big)=\mu_{j}\big(\left(aD^{2}a\right)^{-1}\big)\leq\mu_{j-N}\left(a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1}\right)+\mu_{N}(R)=\lambda_{j-N}\left(a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1}\right).

Similarly, we have

\lambda_{j}\big(\left(aD^{2}a\right)^{-1}\big)=\mu_{j}\big(\left(aD^{2}a\right)^{-1}\big)\geq\mu_{j+N}\left(a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1}\right)-\mu_{N}(R)=\lambda_{j+N}\left(a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1}\right).

With our index conventions
\lambda_{j}[(a^{-1}D^{2}a^{-1})^{-1}]=\lambda_{j}(a^{-1}D^{2}a^{-1})^{-1}
. Thus, for all
j\geq N
, we have

\lambda_{j+N}\left(a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1}\right)\leq\lambda_{j}\left(aD^{2}a\right)^{-1}\leq\lambda_{j-N}\left(a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1}\right).

It then follows that

(3.5)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\left(aD^{2}a\right)^{-1}=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\left(a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1}\right),

provided any of these limits exists. This gives the result.
∎

In what follows, we set

(3.6)

\mathscr{A}_{++}^{q}:=\left\{a^{q};\ a\in{\mathscr{A}}_{++}\right\},\qquad q\neq 0.

If
\mathscr{A}
is closed under holomorphic functional calculus, then
\mathscr{A}_{++}^{q}=\mathscr{A}_{++}
, but in general we don’t have an equality for
q\neq 1
.

We will need the following lemma.

Lemma 3.7

(compare
[
137
, Remark 3.5]
)
.

If
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
, then, given any
q\neq 0
, we always can find a sequence
(a_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 0}\subseteq\mathscr{A}_{++}^{q}
such that
(a_{\ell})^{r}\rightarrow a^{r}
in
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
for all
r>0
. In particular,
\mathscr{A}_{++}^{q}
is a dense subset of
\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
.

Proof.

Note that if
a_{\ell}\rightarrow a
in
\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
, then
f(a_{\ell})\rightarrow f(a)
for every continuous function
f:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
. This is true for polynomials. The result then can be extended to all continuous functions on
[0,\infty)
by using Stone-Weierstrass theorem. In particular, the result holds for all power functions
t\rightarrow t^{q}
,
q>0
. Therefore, in order to prove the lemma it is enough to show that

\mathscr{A}_{++}^{q}
is a dense subset of
\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
for all
q\neq 0
.

We first prove the result for
q=1
. Let
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
, and let
(b_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 0}\subseteq\mathscr{A}_{++}
be such that
b_{\ell}\rightarrow\sqrt{a}
. Set
a_{\ell}=b_{\ell}^{*}b_{\ell}+(\ell+1)^{-1}
. Then
a_{\ell}\in\mathscr{A}_{++}
and
a_{\ell}\rightarrow(\sqrt{a})^{*}\sqrt{a}=a
as
\ell\rightarrow\infty
. This shows that
\mathscr{A}_{++}
is dense in
\mathscr{A}_{++}
.

Let
q\neq 0
and
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
. The density of
\mathscr{A}_{++}
in
\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
ensures there is a sequence
(b_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 0}\subseteq\mathscr{A}_{++}
such that
b_{\ell}\rightarrow b^{1/q}
. As mentioned above this implies that
b_{\ell}^{q}\rightarrow(b^{1/q})^{q}=b
. This shows that
\mathscr{A}_{++}^{q}
is dense in
\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
. As
\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
is dense in
\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
, we deduce that
\mathscr{A}_{++}^{q}
is dense in
\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
, completing the proof.
∎

We have the following characterizations of Condition (W).

Lemma 3.8
.

The following are equivalent:

(i)

The Weyl laws (
3.4
) hold for all
a\in{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
, i.e., Condition (W) is satisfied.

(ii)

The Weyl laws (
3.4
) hold for all
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
.

(iii)

For all
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
, we have

(3.7)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(aD^{-2}a\big)=\tau\big[a^{p}\big]^{\frac{2}{p}}.

Proof.

It is immediate that (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i). Moreover, if (iii) holds, then by Lemma
3.6
, for all
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
, we have

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\left(aD^{-2}a\right)=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1}\big)^{-1}=\tau\big[a^{-p}\big]^{\frac{2}{p}}.

This shows that (iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii).

It remains to show that (i)
\Rightarrow
(iii). Suppose that (i) holds. Lemma
3.6
then ensures that, for all
a\in\mathscr{A}_{++}
, we have

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a^{-1}D^{-2}a^{-1}\big)=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\left(aD^{-2}a\right)^{-1}\tau\big[a^{-p}\big]^{\frac{2}{p}}.

This shows that the Weyl laws (
3.7
) hold for all
a\in\mathscr{A}_{++}^{-1}
.

In general, if
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
, then by Lemma
3.7
there is a sequence
(a_{\ell})\subseteq{\mathscr{A}}_{++}^{-1}
such that
a_{\ell}\rightarrow a

and
(a_{\ell})^{p}\rightarrow a^{p}
in
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
. In particular,
a_{\ell}D^{-2}a_{\ell}\rightarrow aD^{-2}a
in
\mathscr{L}_{p/2,\infty}
. Combining this with Proposition
2.4
and using the continuity of
\tau
we then get

(3.8)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(aD^{-2}a\big)=\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a_{\ell}D^{-2}a_{\ell}\big)=\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}\tau\big[a_{\ell}^{p}\big]^{\frac{2}{p}}=\tau\big[a^{p}\big]^{\frac{2}{p}}.

That is,
aD^{-2}a
satisfies the Weyl law
3.7
, and so (iii) is satisfied. This proves that (i)
\Rightarrow
(iii). The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

The spectral asymptotics (
3.7
) identifies on
\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
the map
\tau
with the functional,

\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}\ni a\longrightarrow\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{1}{p}}D^{-2}a^{\frac{1}{p}}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}=\Lambda\big(a^{\frac{1}{p}}D^{-2}a^{\frac{1}{p}}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}.

This functional is continuous on
\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
thanks to Proposition
2.4
. Moreover, the density of
{\mathscr{A}}_{++}^{-p}
in
\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
implies that the above functional is uniquely determined by its restriction to
{\mathscr{A}}_{++}^{-p}
. It follows that the Weyl laws (
3.4
) uniquely define a continuous functionality on
\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
.

We are now in a position to prove the following perturbation result (which was mentioned in Remark
3.5
).

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
A=A^{*}\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
, and set
D_{A}=D+A
. If Condition
(W)
is satisfied by the spectral triple
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D)
, then it is also satisfied by the spectral triple

(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D_{A})
.

Proof.

As
A
is bounded,
\operatorname{dom}(D_{A})=\operatorname{dom}(D)
. We then have the resolvent formula,

D_{A}^{-1}=D^{-1}-D^{-1}(D_{A}-D)D_{A}^{-1}=D^{-1}-D^{-1}AD_{A}^{-1}.

As
D^{-1}\in\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
, it follows that
D_{A}^{-1}\in\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
, and hence

D_{A}^{-1}-D^{-1}=-D^{-1}AD_{A}^{-1}\in\mathscr{L}_{p/2,\infty}.

Thus,

D^{-2}_{A}-D^{-2}=D^{-1}_{A}\left(D_{A}^{-1}-D^{-1}\right)+\left(D_{A}^{-1}-D^{-1}\right)D^{-1}\in\mathscr{L}_{p/3,\infty}.

It follows that, for all
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
, we have

aD^{-2}_{A}a=aD^{-2}a\qquad\bmod\big(\mathscr{L}_{\frac{p}{2},\infty}\big)_{0}.

Thus, if Condition (W) holds, then by using Proposition
2.4
and Lemma
3.8
we get

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(aD_{A}^{-2}a\big)=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(aD^{-2}a\big)=\tau\big[a^{p}\big]^{\frac{2}{p}}.

Using Lemma
3.8
once more then shows that

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{-\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(aD_{A}^{2}a\big)=\tau\big[a^{-p}\big]^{\frac{2}{p}}\qquad\forall a\in\mathscr{A}_{++}.

That is, the spectral triple
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D_{A})
satisfies Condition (W). The proof is complete.
∎

3.3.
Weak Schatten properties of fractional commutators – Proof of Lemma
1.9

In what follows given any
q>0
we define
|D|^{-q}
by means of the Borel functional calculus for
D
with the convention that
|D|^{-q}=0
on
\ker A
.
Thus, if
(\xi_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 0}
is any orthonormal eigenbasis of
\mathscr{H}
with
D\xi_{\ell}=\lambda_{\ell}\xi_{\ell}
for all
\ell\geq 0
, then

|D|^{-q}\xi_{\ell}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}|\lambda_{\ell}|^{-q}\xi_{\ell}&\text{if}\ \lambda_{\ell}>0,\\
0&\text{if}\ \lambda_{\ell}=0.\end{array}\right.

Proof of Lemma
1.9
.

In what follows we denote by
C_{pq}
and
C_{D,q}
positive constants depending only on the data
(p,q)
or
(D,q)
which may vary from line to line.

Assume that
q\in(0,1]
, and let
a\in\mathscr{A}
. Applying Lemma
1.12
with

\beta=\gamma=0
shows there is a bounded operator
\Phi^{(q)}:\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})\rightarrow\mathscr{L}_{(q+1)^{-1}p,\infty}
depending only on
D
and
q
such that

(3.9)

\displaystyle\left[|D|^{-q},a\right]=

\displaystyle\Phi^{(q)}\left([D,a]\right)+|D|^{-(q+1)}F[D,a]\Pi_{0}+\Pi_{0}[D,a]F|D|^{-(q+1)},

where
F=D|D|^{-1}
is the sign of
D
, and
\Pi_{0}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\ker D
.

Set
r=(q+1)^{-1}p
. The continuity of
\Phi^{(q)}
ensures us that

\big\|\Phi^{(q)}\left([D,a]\right)\big\|_{r,\infty}\leq C_{D,q}\left\|\left[D,a\right]\right\|.

Moreover, as
|D|^{-(q+1)}\in\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty}
, we also get

\left\||D|^{-(q+1)}F[D,a]\Pi_{0}\right\|_{r,\infty}\leq\big\||D|^{-(q+1)}\big\|_{r,\infty}\left\|F[D,a]\Pi_{0}\right\|\leq C_{D,q}\left\|\left[D,a\right]\right\|.

We have a similar estimate for
\Pi_{0}[D,a]F|D|^{-(q+1)}
. Combining all those estimates with (
3.9
) yields the estimates (
1.14
) for all
q\in(0,1]
. In particular, for
q=1
we get

(3.10)

\big\|\big[|D|^{-1},a\big]\big\|_{2p,\infty}\leq C_{D}\left\|\left[D,a\right]\right\|\qquad\forall a\in\mathscr{A}.

Suppose now that the estimate (
1.14
) holds for some
q>0
. Set
r=(q+1)^{-1}p
and
r_{1}=(q+2)^{-1}p
. By Leibniz’s rule, we have

(3.11)

\big[|D|^{-(q+1)},a\big]=|D|^{-1}\big[|D|^{-q},a\big]+\big[|D|^{-1},a\big]|D|^{-q}.

Here
|D|^{-1}\in\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
and
r^{-1}+p^{-1}=r_{1}^{-1}
. Thus, by using (
1.14
) and Hölder’s inequality (Proposition
2.1
) we get

\big\||D|^{-1}\left[|D|^{-q},a\right]\big\|_{r_{1},\infty}\leq C_{pq}\big\||D|^{-1}\big\|_{p,\infty}\left\|\big[|D|^{-q},a\big]\right\|_{r,\infty}\leq C_{D,q}\left\|\left[D,a\right]\right\|.

In addition,
|D|^{-q}\in\mathscr{L}_{q^{-1}p,\infty}
and
2p^{-1}+qp^{-1}=r_{1}^{-1}
. Therefore, by using (
3.10
) and Hölder’s inequality we also get

\big\|\big[|D|^{-1},a\big]|D|^{-q}\big\|_{r_{1},\infty}\leq C_{pq}\left\||D|^{-q}\right\|_{q^{-1}p,\infty}\big\|\big[|D|^{-1},a\big]\big\|_{2p,\infty}\leq C_{D,q}\left\|\left[D,a\right]\right\|.

Combining the previous two estimates with (
3.11
) yields the estimate (
1.14
) for
q+1
. As the estimate (
1.14
) holds for all
q\in(0,1]
, an induction then shows that it holds for all
q
in intervals
(0,k]
,
k=1,2,\ldots
, and hence it holds for all
q>0
. This completes the proof of Lemma
1.9
.
∎

3.4.
Proof of Proposition
1.1

We will need the following consequences of Lemma
1.9
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
q>0
. For all
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}
, we have

\left[|D|^{-q},a\right]\in\big(\mathscr{L}_{q^{-1}p,\infty}\big)_{0}.

Proof.

Thanks to Proposition
2.1
, for all
\beta,\gamma\geq 0
, the map
a\rightarrow|D|^{-\beta}a|D|^{-\gamma}
is a continuous linear map from
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
to
\mathscr{L}_{(\beta+\gamma)^{-1}p,\infty}
. Thus,
a\rightarrow[|D|^{-q},a]
is at least a continuous linear map from
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
to
\mathscr{L}_{q^{-1}p,\infty}
.
By Lemma
1.9
, if
a\in\mathscr{A}
, then
[|D|^{-q},a]
is contained in
\mathscr{L}_{(q+1)^{-1}p,\infty}\subseteq(\mathscr{L}_{q^{-1}p,\infty})_{0}
. As
\mathscr{A}
is dense in
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
, it follows that
[|D|^{-q},a]\in(\mathscr{L}_{q^{-1}p,\infty})_{0}
for all
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}
. The result is proved.
∎

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
q>0
. For all
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
, we have

\displaystyle|D|^{-q}a

\displaystyle=|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\quad\bmod\big(\mathscr{L}_{q^{-1}p,\infty}\big)_{0},

(3.12)

\displaystyle=a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-q}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\qquad\bmod\big(\mathscr{L}_{q^{-1}p,\infty}\big)_{0}.

In particular,

(3.13)

|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}-a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-q}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\in\big(\mathscr{L}_{q^{-1}p,\infty}\big)_{0}\qquad\forall a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}.

Proof.

Let
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
. We have

\displaystyle|D|^{-q}a

\displaystyle=|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}+|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big[|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}},a\big],

\displaystyle=a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-q}a^{\frac{1}{2}}+\big[|D|^{-q},a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big]a^{\frac{1}{2}}.

It follows from Lemma
3.11
that
[|D|^{-q},a^{1/2}]\in(\mathscr{L}_{q^{-1}p,\infty})_{0}
. This gives the 2nd equality in (
3.12
). Thanks to Lemma
3.11
we also know that

[|D|^{-q/2},a]\in(\mathscr{L}_{2q^{-1}p,\infty})_{0}
. As
|D|^{-q/2}\in\mathscr{L}_{2q^{-1}p,\infty}
, the 2nd part of Proposition
2.1
ensures that
|D|^{-q/2}[|D|^{-q/2},a]\in(\mathscr{L}_{2q^{-1}p,\infty})_{0}
. This gives the 1st equality in (
3.12
) and completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.13
.

The first equality in (
3.12
) actually holds for all
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}
. If
a\in\mathscr{A}
, then it actually holds modulo
\mathscr{L}_{(q+1)^{-1}p,\infty}
. This is also true for the 2nd equality provided that
a\in\mathscr{A}_{+}
and
a^{1/2}\in\mathscr{A}
.

Lemma 3.14

(see also
[
137
,
192
]
)
.

For any
q>0
and
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}
, we have

(3.14)

\displaystyle\big||D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big|

\displaystyle-|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}|a||D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\in\big(\mathscr{L}_{q^{-1}p,\infty}\big)_{0},

(3.15)

\displaystyle\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)_{\pm}

\displaystyle-|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a_{\pm}|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\in\big(\mathscr{L}_{q^{-1}p,\infty}\big)_{0}\quad(\text{if}\ a^{*}=a).

Proof.

Let
q>0
and
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}
. We have

\displaystyle\big||D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big|^{2}-\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}|a||D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big(a^{*}|D|^{-q}a-|a||D|^{-q}|a|\big)|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}

(3.16)

\displaystyle=|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a^{*}\big[|D|^{-q},a\big]|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}-|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}|a|\big[|D|^{-q},|a|\big]|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}.

By Lemma
3.11
the commutators
[|D|^{-q},a]
and
[|D|^{-q},|a|]
are contained in
(\mathscr{L}_{q^{-1}p,\infty})_{0}
. Proposition
2.1
then ensures that both summands

|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a^{*}[|D|^{-q},a]|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}
and
|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}|a|[|D|^{-q},|a|]|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}
are in
(\mathscr{L}_{2q^{-1}p,\infty})_{0}
, and so the difference

||D|^{-q/2}a|D|^{-q/2}|^{2}-(|D|^{-q/2}|a||D|^{-q/2})^{2}
is in
(\mathscr{L}_{2q^{-1}p,\infty})_{0}
. Applying Proposition
2.2
then gives (
3.14
).

Suppose that
a^{*}=a
. We have

\displaystyle\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\big||D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big|\pm|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\right),

\displaystyle|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a_{\pm}|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\left(|a|\pm a\right)|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}.

Thus,

\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)_{\pm}-|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a_{\pm}|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\big||D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big|-|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}|a||D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\Big).

Combining this with (
3.14
) then gives (
3.15
). The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 3.15
.

If
a\in\mathscr{A}
is invertible, we actually can get an even stronger result. In this case
|a|\in\mathscr{A}
, and so by using the first part of Lemma
1.9
we see that the lower r.h.s of (
3.16
) is in
\mathscr{L}_{(2q+1)^{-1}p}
.
Proposition
2.2
then ensures that the relation (
3.14
) holds in the smaller ideal
\mathscr{L}_{(q+1/2)^{-1}p,\infty}
rather than in
(\mathscr{L}_{pq^{-1},\infty})_{0}
. Likewise, the relation (
3.15
) holds in
\mathscr{L}_{(q+1/2)^{-1}p,\infty}
.

Remark 3.16
.

A version of (
3.15
) is established in
[
137
, Lemma 1.15]
(see also
[
192
, Lemma 5.10]
). The approach in
[
137
,
192
]
relies on a deep result of
[
106
]
which extends the BKS inequality to
q
-convexifications of fully symmetric normed ideals. As the above proof shows, the original version of the BKS inequality as stated by Proposition
2.2
is enough for our purpose.

We are now in a position to prove Proposition
1.1

Proof of Proposition
1.1
.

We need to show that, given any
q>1
, for all
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}
, we have

(3.17)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\mu_{j}\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\tau\left[|a|^{\frac{p}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}},

(3.18)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\lambda_{j}^{\pm}\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\tau\left[\big(a_{\pm}\big)^{\frac{p}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}}\quad(\text{if}\ a^{*}=a).

Let
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}
. It follows from (
3.14
) and Proposition
2.4
that

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\mu_{j}\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}|a||D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big),

provided any of these limits exists. Likewise, if
a^{*}=a
, then by using (
3.15
) and Proposition
2.4
we see that

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\lambda_{j}^{\pm}\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\mu_{j}\left(\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)_{\pm}\right)=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a_{\pm}|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big).

Therefore, in order to establish the spectral asymptotics (
3.17
)–(
3.18
) it is enough to show that

(3.19)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\tau\left[a^{\frac{p}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}}\qquad\forall a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}.

Let
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
. If we use (
3.13
) and Proposition
2.4
, then we get

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-q}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big),

provided any of these limits exists. It follows that (
3.19
) is equivalent to

(3.20)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-q}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)=\tau\left[a^{\frac{p}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}}\qquad\forall a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}.

This reduces the proof of Proposition
1.1
to establishing (
3.20
).

Let
a\in\mathscr{A}_{++}
. If
\alpha\in(0,1]
and
s>1
, then it follows from Lemma
1.10
that

\left(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{s}-|D|^{-\alpha s}a^{s}\in\big(\mathscr{L}_{(\alpha s)^{-1}p,\infty}\big)_{0}.

Moreover, by (
3.13
) we have

|D|^{-\alpha s}a^{s}-a^{\frac{s}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha s}a^{\frac{s}{2}}\in\big(\mathscr{L}_{(\alpha s)^{-1}p,\infty}\big)_{0}.

Thus,

\left(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{s}-a^{\frac{s}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha s}a^{\frac{s}{2}}\in\big(\mathscr{L}_{(\alpha s)^{-1}p,\infty}\big)_{0}.

Combining this with Proposition
2.4
we then get

(3.21)

\left[\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{\alpha}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)\right]^{s}=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{\alpha s}{p}}\lambda_{j}\left(\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)^{s}\right)=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{\alpha s}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{s}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha s}a^{\frac{s}{2}}\big),

provided any of those limits exists.

Assume now that Condition (W) holds. Lemma
3.8
then ensures that, for all
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
, we have

(3.22)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}D^{-2}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)=\tau\left[a^{\frac{p}{2}}\right]^{\frac{2}{p}}.

This proves (
3.20
) for
q=2
.

Let
q\leq 1
and
a\in\mathscr{A}_{++}
. Specializing (
3.21
) to
\alpha=q
and
s=2q^{-1}
(so as to have
\alpha s=2
), and using (
3.22
) we obtain

(3.23)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-q}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)=\left[\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{1}{q}}D^{-2}a^{\frac{1}{q}}\big)\right]^{\frac{q}{2}}=\tau\left[a^{\frac{p}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}}.

This shows that the spectral asymptotic (
3.20
) holds for
a\in\mathscr{A}_{++}
. Using Proposition
2.4
and Lemma
3.7
and arguing as in the proof of Lemma
3.8
shows that (
3.20
) holds for all
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
if
q\leq 1
. In particular, for
q=1
we get

(3.24)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{1}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-1}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)=\tau\left[a^{p}\right]^{\frac{1}{p}}\qquad\forall a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}.

It remains to deal with the case
q>1
. In this case, given any
a\in\mathscr{A}_{++}
, in (
3.21
) we may take
\alpha=1
and
s=q
and use (
3.24
) to get

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{q}{2}}|D|^{-q}a^{\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\left[\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{1}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-1}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)\right]^{q}=\tau\left[a^{p}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}}.

This shows that the spectral asymptotic (
3.20
) is satisfied by all
a\in\mathscr{A}_{++}^{q}
. Using Lemma
3.7
and Proposition
2.4
as above shows that it is satisfied
by all
a\in\mathscr{A}_{+}
. This completes the proof of Proposition
1.1
.
∎

Remark 3.17
.

For general values
q>0
, the proof of Proposition
1.1
relies on Lemma
1.10
.
However, as the proof of Proposition
1.1
shows, for
q=2
we get the spectral asymptotics (
3.17
)–(
1.3
) directly from Lemma
1.9
, which is a much easier result. Therefore, Lemma
1.10
is not needed if we are interested in proving (
3.17
)–(
3.18
) only for
q=2
.

3.5.
Semiclassical Weyl law – Proof of Theorem
1.2

For
q>0
and
V=V^{*}\in\mathscr{A}
, we consider the Schrödinger operators,

(3.25)

H_{V}^{(q)}(h):=h^{2q}\left(D^{2}\right)^{q}+V,\qquad h>0.

Here
H_{V}^{(q)}(h)
with domain
\operatorname{dom}(D^{2})
is a selfadjoint operator which is bounded from below and has pure discrete spectrum.

We are interested in the behavior of the spectrum of
H_{V}^{(q)}(h)
under the semiclassical limit
h\rightarrow 0^{+}
. We list the eigenvalues of
H_{V}^{(q)}(h)
as a sequence,

(3.26)

\lambda_{0}\big(H_{V}^{(q)}(h)\big)\leq\lambda_{1}\big(H_{V}^{(q)}(h)\big)\leq\cdots,

where each eigenvalue is repeated according to multiplicity. Given any energy level
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we then define

(3.27)

N\big(H_{V}^{(q)};\lambda\big):=\#\big\{j;\ \lambda_{j}\big(H_{V}^{(q)}\big)<\lambda\big\}.

In particular, in the notation of Proposition
2.8
we have
N^{-}(H_{V}^{(q)})=N(H_{V}^{(q)};0)
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Assume Condition (W) holds. Let
q>0
and
V^{*}=V\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}
. We have to determine the limit of
h^{p}N(H_{V}^{(q)}(h);\mu,\lambda)
under the semiclassical limit
h\rightarrow 0^{+}
.

Combining Proposition
1.1
with the Birman-Schwinger principle (
2.13
) for
H_{0}=(D^{2})^{q}
gives

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{p}N\big(H_{V}^{(q)}(h);\infty,0\big)=\left[\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2q}{p}}\lambda_{j}^{-}\left(|D|^{-q}V|D|^{-q}\right)\right]^{\frac{p}{2q}}=\tau\Big[V_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}\Big].

Given any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, by substituting
V-\lambda
for
V
above we then get

(3.28)

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{p}N\big(H_{V}^{(q)}(h);\lambda\big)=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{p}N\big(H_{V-\lambda}^{(q)}(h);0\big)=\tau\Big[\left(V-\lambda\right)_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}\Big].

This proves the result.
∎

Remark 3.18
.

The most important case for the semiclassical Weyl law (
3.28
) is
q=1
. In this case, the asymptotic is a consequence of the spectral asymptotics (
1.3
) for
q=2
. As mentioned in Remark
3.17
, for
q=2
that spectral asymptotics can be deduced from Lemma
1.9
without using Lemma
1.10
. Therefore, for
q=1
we have a much simpler proof of the semiclassical Weyl law (
3.28
).

Remark 3.19
.

It’s a routine argument to deduce from (
3.28
) semiclassical Weyl laws for the number of eigenvalues in bands
(\mu,\lambda)
,
\mu<\lambda
. Given
\mu<\lambda
, set

N\big(H_{V}^{(q)}(h);\mu,\lambda\big):=\#\left\{j;\ \lambda_{j}\big(H_{V}^{(q)}(h)\big)\in(\mu,\lambda)\right\}.

For all
\epsilon\in(0,\lambda-\mu)
, we have

h^{p}N\big(H_{V}^{(q)};\lambda\big)-h^{p}N\big(H_{V}^{(q)};\mu-\epsilon\big)\leq h^{p}N\big(H_{V}^{(q)};\mu,\lambda\big)\leq h^{p}N\big(H_{V}^{(q)};\lambda\big)-h^{p}N\big(H_{V}^{(q)};\mu-\epsilon\big).

Combining this with the semiclassical Weyl law (
3.28
) gives

\displaystyle\limsup_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{p}N\big(H_{V}^{(q)};\mu,\lambda\big)\leq\tau\Big[\left(V-\lambda\right)_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}\Big]-\tau\Big[\left(V-\mu-\epsilon\right)_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}\Big],

\displaystyle\liminf_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{p}N\big(H_{V}^{(q)};\mu,\lambda\big)\geq\tau\Big[\left(V-\lambda\right)_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}\Big]-\tau\Big[\left(V-\mu+\epsilon\right)_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}\Big].

We claim that

(3.29)

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\tau\Big[\left(V-\mu\pm\epsilon\right)_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}\Big]=\tau\Big[\left(V-\mu\right)_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}\Big].

This would imply the semiclassical Weyl law,

(3.30)

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{p}N\big(H_{V}^{(q)}(h);\mu,\lambda\big)=\tau\Big[\left(V-\lambda\right)_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}\Big]-\tau\Big[\left(V-\mu\right)_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}\Big].

To get (
3.29
), set
x=V-\mu
and
r=p(2q)^{-1}
. Using the positivity of
\tau
and the uniform continuity of the function
t\rightarrow t_{-}^{r}
on the compact set
\operatorname{Sp}(x)
we get

\displaystyle\left|\tau\left[(x\pm\epsilon)_{-}^{r}\right]-\tau\left[x_{-}^{r}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\tau(1)\left\|(x\pm\epsilon)_{-}^{r}-x_{-}^{r}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\tau(1)\sup_{t\in\operatorname{Sp}(x)}\left|(t\pm\epsilon)_{-}^{r}-t_{-}^{r}\right|\longrightarrow 0\qquad\text{as $\epsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}$}.

This gives (
3.29
) and proves the semiclassical Weyl law (
3.30
).

3.6.
Integration formulas – Proof of Theorem
1.3

As a further consequence of Proposition
1.1
we prove Theorem
1.3
.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Let
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}
and
q>0
. The spectral asymptotics (
3.17
) for
q=p
gives

(3.31)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\mu_{j}\left(|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}\right)=\tau\left[|a|\right].

Combining this with the 3rd part of Proposition
2.6
shows that the operator
||D|^{-p/2}a|D|^{-p/2}|
is spectrally measurable, and we have

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int\big||D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}\big|=\tau\left[|a|\right].

Likewise, if
a^{*}=a
, then by (
1.3
) we have

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\lambda_{j}^{\pm}\left(|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}\right)=\tau\left[a_{\pm}\right].

It then follows from Proposition
2.6
that the operator
|D|^{-p/2}a|D|^{-p/2}
is spectrally measurable, and we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\lambda_{j}^{+}\big(|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}\big)-\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\lambda_{j}^{-}\big(|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}\big)

(3.32)

\displaystyle=\tau\big[a_{+}\big]-\tau\big[a_{-}\big]

\displaystyle=\tau\big[a\big].

If
a
is not selfadjoint, then the selfadjoint part of
|D|^{-p/2}a|D|^{-p/2}
is equal to

\frac{1}{2}\left(|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}+|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}a^{*}|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}\right)=|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}(\Re a)|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}.

Likewise, its imaginary part is equal to
|D|^{-p/2}(\Im a)|D|^{-p/2}
. Therefore, the real and imaginary parts both are spectrally measurable, and so

|D|^{-p/2}a|D|^{-p/2}
is spectrally measurable. Moreover, by using (
3.32
) we get

\displaystyle\int|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int\Re\left(|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}\right)+i\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int\Im\left(|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\tau\left[\Re a\right]+i\tau\left[\Im a\right]

\displaystyle=\tau[a].

Finally, by (
3.13
) and Remark
3.13
we have

a|D|^{-p}=|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}\quad\bmod\big(\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty})_{0}.

It then follows from Proposition
2.4
that
a|D|^{-p}
is spectrally measurable. Moreover,
as the operators in
(\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty})_{0}
annihilate the NC integral, we have

(3.33)

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int a|D|^{-p}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}=\tau\big[a\big].

This completes the proof of Theorem
1.3
.
∎

Remark 3.20
.

Under the Tauberian condition (H) the integration formulas (
3.33
) can also be deduced from
[
189
, Theorem 1.5]
. We also can deduce them from a slightly stronger form of Condition (Z) by using
[
188
, Theorem 1.4]
(see also
[
193
, Theorem 1.2.7]
).

Remark 3.21
.

The formula (
3.33
) identifies
\tau
with the functional
a\rightarrow\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int a|D|^{-p}
. Thus, given any
q>0
and
V^{*}=V\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}
, we may rewrite the semiclassical Weyl law (
3.28
) in the form,

\displaystyle\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{p}N^{-}\big(H_{V}^{(q)}(h);\lambda\big)=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int\left(V-\lambda\right)_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}|D|^{-p},\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{R}.

Remark 3.22
.

By using the spectral asymptotics (
1.2
)–(
1.3
) and arguing as in the proof of Theorem
1.3
above, it can be further shown that, for any
q>0
,
q\neq p
, the operators
||D|^{-q/2}a|D|^{-q/2}|^{p/q}
and
(|D|^{-q/2}a|D|^{-q/2})_{\pm}^{p/q}
(if
a^{*}=a
), are spectrally measurable, and we have

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int\big||D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big|^{\frac{p}{q}}=\tau\left[|a|^{\frac{p}{q}}\right],\qquad\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}a|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)_{\pm}^{\frac{p}{q}}=\tau\left[(a_{\pm})^{\frac{p}{q}}\right].

3.7.
Extension to Unbounded Potentials – Proofs of Theorem
1.5
and Theorem
1.6

In this section, we shall prove Proposition
1.4
and use it to obtain Theorem
1.5
and Theorem
1.6
. These results provide a general paradigm for extending to suitable classes of unbounded potentials the spectral asymptotics of Proposition
1.1
and the
semiclassical Weyl law and integration formulas provided by Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.3
.

The approach of this section is a mere elaboration of the well-known approach of Birman-Solomyak (see, e.g.,
[
24
]
) and Simon
[
180
]
to semiclassical Weyl laws for
L^{p}
-potentials. The only technical difference is the fact that, due to the noncommutativity of our general setup, we need to work with noncommutative
L^{p}
-spaces.

3.7.1.
Cwikel-type estimates for spectral triples

As mentioned in the Introduction (Chapter
1
), on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
or bounded domains in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the extensions to
L^{r}
-potentials can be obtained by combing the perturbation theory of Birman-Solomyak (see Proposition
2.4
) and with deep estimates of Cwikel
[
64
]
and its generalizations (see, e.g.,
[
124
,
165
,
168
,
186
,
191
]
and the references therein). In the setup of spectral triples we can formulate Cwikel-type estimates for spectral triples as follows.

For
s>0
denote by
\mathscr{H}^{(s)}
the Sobolev space with Bessel potential
(1+D^{2})^{s/2}
, i.e., the Hilbert space consisting of
\operatorname{dom}(1+D^{2})^{s/2}
equipped with the Hilbert norm
\xi\rightarrow\|(1+D^{2})^{s/2}\xi\|
. We denote by
\mathscr{H}^{(-s)}
its antilinear dual, i.e., the Hilbert space of continuous antilinear forms on
\mathscr{H}^{(s)}
.
Any operator
V\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(s)},\mathscr{H}^{(-s)})
defines a quadratic form on
\mathscr{H}^{(s)}
by

Q_{V}(\xi,\eta)=\left\langle V\xi,\eta\right\rangle,\qquad\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{H}^{(s)},

where
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle:\mathscr{H}^{(-s)}\times\mathscr{H}^{(s)}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is the duality pairing.
The adjoint
V^{*}\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(s)},\mathscr{H}^{(-s)})
is given by

\left\langle V^{*}\xi,\eta\right\rangle=\overline{\left\langle V\eta,\xi\right\rangle},\qquad\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{H}^{(s)}.

In particular,
Q_{V^{*}}=\overline{Q_{V}}
, and so
Q_{V}
is symmetric if and only if
V^{*}=V
. Note also that any
T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
induces an operator
T:\mathscr{H}^{(s)}\rightarrow\mathscr{H}^{(s)}
such that

\left\langle T\xi,\eta\right\rangle=\left\langle T\xi|\eta\right\rangle\qquad\forall\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{H}.

This yields a continuous
*
-embedding of
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
into
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(s)},\mathscr{H}^{(-s)})
, and so this induces a continuous
*
-embedding of
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
into
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(s)},\mathscr{H}^{(-s)})
.

Given any
q>0
, the Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}^{(q)}
is contained in the domain of the quadratic form of
(D^{2})^{q}
. In addition,

|D|^{-q}
maps continuously
\mathscr{H}
to
\mathscr{H}^{(q)}
. It also extends to a bounded operator
|D|^{-q}:\mathscr{H}^{(-q)}\rightarrow\mathscr{H}
given by

\left\langle|D|^{-q}\xi|\eta\right\rangle=\left\langle\xi,|D|^{-q}\eta\right\rangle,\qquad\xi\in\mathscr{H}^{(-q)},\ \eta\in\mathscr{H}.

Thus, if
V\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(q)},\mathscr{H}^{(-q)})
, then the operator
|D|^{-q}V|D|^{-q}
is bounded on
\mathscr{H}
. This operator is selfdajoint (resp., compact) if
V
is selfadjoint (resp., compact).

It follows from this that if
V=V^{*}\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(q)},\mathscr{H}^{(-q)})
is compact, then the symmetric quadratic form
Q_{V}
is
(D^{2})^{q}
-form compact. This allows us to define the operator
H_{V}^{(q)}:=(D^{2})^{q}+V
as a form sum, i.e., as the selfadjoint operator whose quadratic form is
Q_{(D^{2})^{q}}+Q_{V}
. This operator is bounded from below and has compact resolvent, and hence it has pure discrete spectrum (see, e.g.,
[
183
]
).

Assume now that Condition (W) holds and
\tau
is the restriction of a positive faithful trace
\tau:\mathscr{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, where
\mathscr{M}\subseteq\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
is the von Neumann algebra generated by
\mathscr{A}
, i.e., its closure with respect to the weak operator topology. As
\tau
is finite, for any
r\in(0,\infty)
we may define the noncommutative
L^{r}
-space
L_{r}(\mathscr{M})
1
1

1

Throughout this monograph we shall use subscripts for the exponents of NC
L^{p}
-spaces.
as the closure of
\mathscr{M}
with respect to the quasi-norm,

\|x\|_{r}=\left(\tau\big[|x|^{r}\big]\right)^{\frac{1}{r}},\qquad x\in\mathscr{M}.

For
r\geq 1
, we get a Banach space, since in this case
\|\cdot\|_{r}
is a norm.
We refer to
[
72
,
116
]
for more background on noncommutative
L^{p}
-spaces. For instance, if
(X,\mu)
is a finite measured space, then we may take
\mathscr{M}=L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
and
\tau(f)=\int_{X}f(x)d\mu(x)
, and in this case
L_{r}(\mathscr{M})=L^{r}(X,\mu)
.

We note that for
r^{\prime}\leq r
we have a continuous inclusion of
L_{r}(\mathscr{M})
into
L_{r^{\prime}}(\mathscr{M})
. Moreover, the map
x\rightarrow x^{*}
uniquely extends to an antilinear isometric map of
L_{r}(\mathscr{M})
onto itself. It is a non-trivial fact that, for
r\geq 1
, the absolute value map
x\rightarrow|x|
uniquely extends to continuous map on
L_{r}(\mathscr{M})
(see
[
115
]
).

Condition 3.23

(Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
))
.

Let
r>0
, and set
\hat{r}=\max(r,1)
. There is a continuous
*
-invariant norm
\|\cdot\|_{(r)}
on
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
such that

(i)

The inclusion of
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
into
L_{\hat{r}}(\mathscr{M})
is continuous with respect to the
\|\cdot\|_{(r)}
-topology.

(ii)

There is a constant
C_{r}>0
such that

(3.34)

\big\|(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{p}{4r}}a(1+D^{2})^{-\frac{p}{4r}}\big\|_{r,\infty}\leq C_{r}\|a\|_{(r)}\qquad\forall a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}.

Remark 3.24
.

In (i) we use the space
L_{\hat{r}}(\mathscr{M})
instead of
L_{r}(\mathscr{M})
to ensure the continuity of the absolute value map
x\rightarrow|x|
on
L_{\hat{r}}(\mathscr{M})
(
cf
. proof of Proposition
1.4
below). In various examples, for
r<1
the best
\|\cdot\|_{(r)}
-norm actually agrees with the
L_{1}
-norm
\|\cdot\|_{1}
(see, e.g.,
[
24
,
132
]
).

Remark 3.25
.

We refer to
[
124
]
for extensions of the Cwikel estimates to a large class of noncommutative
L^{p}
-spaces. This was the main impetus for the Cwikel estimates for noncommutive tori in
[
132
,
133
]
(see also Chapter
10
). It would be interesting to use the framework of
[
124
]
to get further examples of spectral triples satisfying Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
).

3.7.2.
Spectral asymptotics

If Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
) holds, then we denote by
\mathscr{V}_{r}
the Banach space completion of
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
with respect to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{(r)}
. We have a continuous inclusion of
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
into
\mathscr{V}_{r}
, as well as a continuous inclusion of
\mathscr{V}_{r}
into
L_{\hat{r}}(\mathscr{M})
thanks to (i). Moreover, the involution
a\rightarrow a^{*}
of
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
uniquely extend to an isometric antilinear involution of
\mathscr{V}_{r}
.

Set
\Lambda=(1+D^{2})^{1/2}
and
q=p/2r
. The estimate (
3.34
) is an abstract analogue of Cwikel’s estimates. It implies that

\overline{\mathscr{A}}\ni a\rightarrow\Lambda^{-q}a\Lambda^{-q}\in\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty}
uniquely extends to a continuous linear map on
\mathscr{V}_{r}
. In particular, this provides us with a continuous inclusion of
\mathscr{V}_{r}
into the closed subspace of compact operators in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(q)},\mathscr{H}^{(-q)})
. Here any
x\in\mathscr{V}_{r}
is identified with the operator given by

\left\langle x\xi,\eta\right\rangle=\big\langle(\Lambda^{-q}x\Lambda^{-q})(\Lambda^{q}\xi)|\Lambda^{q}\eta\big\rangle,\qquad\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{H}^{(q)}.

We are now in a position to prove Proposition
1.4
.

Proof of Proposition
1.4
.

Suppose that Condition (W) is satisfied. Let
q>0
, and assume further that Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
) holds with
r:=pq^{-1}
. We have to prove, for every
x\in\mathscr{V}_{r}
, the following spectral asymptotics holds:

(3.35)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\mu_{j}\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}x|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\tau\left[|x|^{\frac{p}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}},

(3.36)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\lambda_{j}^{\pm}\big(|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}x|D|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\tau\left[\big(x_{\pm}\big)^{\frac{p}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}}\quad(\text{if}\ x^{*}=x).

By the first part of Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
) we have a continuous inclusion of
\mathscr{V}_{r}
into
L_{\hat{r}}(\mathscr{M})
. As
\hat{r}\geq 1
, the map
x\rightarrow|x|
is continuous from
L_{\hat{r}}(\mathscr{M})
to itself.
We also have a continuous inclusion of
L_{\hat{r}}(\mathscr{M})
into
L_{r}(\mathscr{M})
, since
\hat{r}\geq r
. Therefore, the absolute value map
x\rightarrow|x|
induces a continuous map from
\mathscr{V}_{r}
to
L_{r}(\mathscr{M})
. Moreover, if we denote by
\mathscr{V}_{r}^{\textup{sa}}
the closed
real
subspace of selfadjoint elements of
\mathscr{V}_{r}
, we also get continuous maps

x\rightarrow x_{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}(|x|\pm x)
from
\mathscr{V}_{r}^{\textup{sa}}
to
L_{r}(\mathscr{M})
. Composing all those maps with the quasi-norm
\|x\|_{r}=\tau[|x|^{p/q}]^{q/p}
we then get continuous functions,

\mathscr{V}_{r}\ni x\longrightarrow\tau\left[|x|^{\frac{p}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}},\qquad\mathscr{V}_{r}^{\textup{sa}}\ni x\longrightarrow\tau\left[(x_{\pm})^{\frac{p}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}}.

Bearing this in mind, let
x=x^{*}\in\mathscr{V}_{r}
and
(x_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 0}\subseteq\overline{\mathscr{A}}
be such that
x_{\ell}^{*}=x_{\ell}\rightarrow V
in
\mathscr{V}_{r}
.
The estimate (
3.34
) ensures that
|D|^{-q/2}x_{\ell}|D|^{-q/2}\rightarrow|D|^{-q/2}x|D|^{-q/2}
in
\mathscr{L}_{q^{-1}p,\infty}
.
Therefore, by using Proposition
2.4
and Proposition
1.1
and the continuity the function

x\rightarrow\tau[(x_{\pm})^{p/q}]^{q/p}
on
\mathscr{V}_{r}
we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\lambda^{\pm}_{j}\big(|D|^{-q/2}x|D|^{-q/2}\big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{p}}\lambda^{\pm}_{j}\big(|D|^{-q/2}x_{\ell}|D|^{-q/2}\big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}\tau\left[(x_{\ell})_{\pm}^{\frac{p}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}}=\tau\left[(x)_{\pm}^{\frac{p}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{p}}.

This proves (
3.36
). The spectral asymptotics (
3.35
) is proved similarly. This completes the proof of Proposition
1.4
.
∎

3.7.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.5
and Theorem
1.6

Let
q>0
, and set
r=p(2q)^{-1}
. If we assume Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
), then, as mentioned above, we have a continuous inclusion of

\mathscr{V}_{r}
into the space of compact operators in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(q)},\mathscr{H}^{(-q)})
. Given any
V=V^{*}\in\mathscr{V}_{r}
this allows us to define the Schrödinger operators
H_{V}^{(q)}(h):=h^{2q}(D^{2})^{q}+V
,
h>0
