, as form sums as above. As mentioned above, we get bounded from below selfadjoint operator with pure discrete spectrum. We list its eigenvalues as a non-decreasing sequence as in (
3.26
). We then define the counting functions
N(H_{V}^{(q)};\mu,\lambda)
as in (
3.27
).

Granted this preparation we are ready to prove Theorem
1.5
.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Let
q>0
, set
r=p(2q)^{-1}
, and assume Condition (W) and Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
) are both satisfied. In addition, let
V=V^{*}\in\mathscr{V}_{r}
. Given any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, by using Proposition
1.4
and the Birman-Schwinger principle in its version of Proposition
2.8
and by arguing along the same lines as in the proof of Theorem
1.2
we get

(3.37)

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{p}N\big(H_{V}^{(q)}(h);\lambda\big)=\tau\Big[\left(V-\lambda\right)_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}\Big].

This gives the result.
∎

Remark 3.26
.

Like with (selfadjoint) potentials in
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
(
cf
. Remark
3.19
), the semiclassical Weyl law (
3.37
) implies semiclassical Weyl laws for the number of eigenvalues in bands
(\mu,\lambda)
,
\mu<\lambda
, for any potential
V\in\mathscr{V}_{r}^{\textup{sa}}
. Indeed, as mentioned in the proof of Proposition
1.4
, the map
x\rightarrow\tau[(x_{-})^{{p}/{2q}}]^{2q/p}
is continuous on
\mathscr{V}_{r}^{\textup{sa}}
. It follows that

(3.38)

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\tau\Big[\left(V-\mu\pm\epsilon\right)_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}\Big]=\tau\Big[\left(V-\mu\right)_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}\Big].

We then can argue along the same lines as that of Remark
3.19
to see that, for all
V\in\mathscr{V}_{r}^{\textup{sa}}
, we have

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{p}N\big(H_{V}^{(q)}(h);\mu,\lambda\big)=\tau\Big[\left(V-\lambda\right)_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}\Big]-\tau\Big[\left(V-\mu\right)_{-}^{\frac{p}{2q}}\Big].

Finally, we prove Theorem
1.6
.

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

The proof follows the same outline as that of the proof of Theorem
1.3
. Assume that Condition (W) and Condition (
\textup{C}_{1}
) hold. Let
x\in\mathscr{V}_{1}
. As with (
3.31
), specializing the spectral asymptotic (
3.35
) to
q=p
yields

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\mu_{j}\left(|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}x|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}\right)=\tau\left[|x|\right].

As in the proof of Theorem
1.3
, Proposition
2.6
then ensures that
||D|^{-p/2}x|D|^{-p/2}|
is spectrally measurable, and we have

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int\big||D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}x|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}\big|=\tau\left[|x|\right].

Suppose that
x^{*}=x
. The spectral asymptotics (
3.36
) for
q=p
then gives

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\lambda_{j}^{\pm}\left(|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}x|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}\right)=\tau\left[x_{\pm}\right].

In the same way as in the proof of Theorem
1.3
, combining this with Proposition
2.6
shows that

|D|^{-p/2}x|D|^{-p/2}
is spectrally measurable, and we have

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}x|D|^{-\frac{p}{2}}=\tau\big[x_{+}\big]-\tau\big[x_{-}\big]==\tau\big[x\big].

Arguing as in the proof of Theorem
1.3
shows that properties actually hold for all
x\in\mathscr{V}_{1}
. This completes the proof of Theorem
1.6
.
∎

Chapter 4
Tauberian Conditions – Proof of Theorem
1.7

This chapter is devoted to proving Theorem
1.7
, which provides Tauberian conditions that give Condition (W). As a byproduct of the approach of this chapter, we will even get a stronger version for spectral triples of the Tauberian theorem of
[
137
]
without any extra regularity or summability conditions (see Remark
4.5
).

Throughout this chapter we let
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D)
be a
p
-summable spectral triple, with
p>0
arbitrary. We keep on using the notation of the previous chapters.

4.1.
Complex powers of positive operators

In what follows, if
X
is a positive compact operator, then we define
X^{z}
,
\Re z\geq 0
, by standard continuous or Borel functional calculus. Thus, if
(\xi_{\ell})_{j\geq 0}
is an orthonormal eigenfamily such that
X\xi_{\ell}=\lambda_{\ell}\xi_{\ell}
for all
\ell\geq 0
(where
\lambda_{0}\geq\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots
are the eigenvalues of
X
counted with multiplicity), then

(4.1)

X^{z}\xi_{\ell}=\lambda_{\ell}^{z}\xi_{\ell}\quad\ell\geq 0,\qquad X^{z}=0\ \text{on}\ \ker X.

Similarly, if
A
is a positive operator on
\mathscr{H}
with compact resolvent, we define its powers
A^{z}
,
z\in\mathbb{C}
, by using the Borel functional calculus for
A
with the convention that
A^{z}=0
on
\ker A
. That is,
A^{z}=f_{z}(A)
with
f_{z}(\lambda)=\mathbbm{1}_{[c,\infty)}(t)t^{z}
, where
0<c<\operatorname{dist}(0,\operatorname{Sp}(P)\setminus 0)
. Equivalently, if
(\xi_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 0}
is any orthonormal eigenbasis of
\mathscr{H}
with
A\xi_{\ell}=\lambda_{\ell}\xi_{\ell}
for all
\ell\geq 0
, then

A^{z}\xi_{\ell}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\lambda_{\ell}^{z}\xi_{\ell}&\text{if}\ \lambda_{\ell}>0,\\
0&\text{if}\ \lambda_{\ell}=0.\end{array}\right.

For
\Re z\leq 0
the operator
A^{z}
is bounded. For
\Re z>0
this an unbounded selfadjoint operator whose domain is

\operatorname{dom}(A^{z})=\left\{\xi\in\mathscr{H};\ \sum|\lambda_{\ell}(A)|^{2\Re z}\left|\left\langle\xi_{\ell}|\xi\right\rangle\right|^{2}<\infty\right\}.

In any case we have,

A^{0}=1-\Pi_{0}(A),\qquad A^{z_{1}+z_{2}}=A^{z_{1}}A^{z_{2}},\quad z_{j}\in\mathbb{C}.

Here
\Pi_{0}(A)
is the orthogonal projection onto
\ker A
. In particular,
A^{-1}
is the partial inverse of
A
, and in view of (
4.1
) we have

A^{-z}=(A^{-1})^{z}\qquad\text{if}\ \Re z\geq 0.

Note also that with the above convention
A^{it}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, is not a unitary operator unless
\ker A=0
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
X
be a (non-zero) positive compact operator on
\mathscr{H}
. The following holds.

(i)

The family
X^{z}
,
\Re z>0
, is a holomorphic family of compact operators.

(ii)

Assume further that
X\in\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
. Given any
c>0
, the family
X^{z}
,
\Re z>c
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{L}_{c^{-1}p}
.

Proof.

The above result is standard. We include a proof for the reader’s convenience. We may assume
X\neq 0
, since otherwise the result is trivially true.
Let
(\xi_{j})_{j\geq 0}
be an orthonormal eigenfamily of
X
such that
X\xi_{j}=\lambda_{j}(X)\xi_{j}
for all
j\geq 0
, where

\lambda_{0}(X)\geq\lambda_{1}(X)\geq\cdots
are the eigenvalues of
X
. It follows from (
4.1
) that

X^{z}=\sum_{\lambda_{j}(X)>0}\lambda_{j}(X)^{z}|\xi_{j}\rangle\langle\xi_{j}|,\qquad\Re z>0,

where the series converges in
\mathscr{K}
uniformly on each halfspace
\Re z>c>0
. This ensures that
X^{z}
,
\Re z>0
, is a holomorphic family of compact operators.

Suppose now that
X\in\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
. Let
c>0
and
\epsilon\in(0,c)
. Then
X^{z}=X^{c+\epsilon}X^{z-c-\epsilon}
,
\Re z>c+\epsilon
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{L}_{c^{-1}p}
, since
X^{c+\epsilon}
is an operator in
\mathscr{L}_{(c+\epsilon)^{-1}p,\infty}\subseteq\mathscr{L}_{c^{-1}p}
and
X^{z-c-\epsilon}
,
\Re z>c+\epsilon
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{K}
by the first part. As this is true for all
\epsilon\in(0,c)
, it follows that
X^{z}
,
\Re z>c
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{L}_{c^{-1}p}
.
The proof is complete.
∎

4.2.
Condition (
\textup{Z}_{0}
)

In our setting, the Tauberian condition of
[
137
]
is formulated as follows.

Condition 4.2

(Tauberian Condition of
[
137
]
)
.

For every
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
, the function

\operatorname{Tr}\big[a^{s}|D|^{-s}\big]-p\tau\left[a^{p}\right](s-p)^{-1},\qquad\Re s>p,

has a unique continuous extension to the closed half-plane
\Re s\geq p
.

It is a too strong a requirement to have the condition to hold for all positive elements of the
C^{*}
-algebra
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
. In fact, in examples where
\mathscr{A}
is an algebra of
\Psi
DOs it may be technically cumbersome to check Condition
4.2
for all elements of
\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
, whereas it is comparatively straightforward to check Condition (W).

In addition, as mentioned in Introduction, it was asked by Alain Connes to relate Condition
4.2
to more standard Tauberian conditions involving the zeta functions
\operatorname{Tr}[a|D|^{-s}]
,
a\in\mathscr{A}
, instead of the functions
\operatorname{Tr}[a^{s}|D|^{-s}]
,
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{+}
. He also pointed out that closeness under holomorphic functional calculus could come into play.

In fact, what is relevant in our setting is a weakening of Condition
4.2
provided by Condition (
\textup{Z}_{0}
) below. For one thing, that condition implies Condition (W) (see Lemma
4.4
below). For the other things, it will be shown that Condition (
\textup{Z}_{0}
) is implied by both Condition (Z) and Condition (H) (see Lemma
4.10
and Lemma
4.14
below). As a result, this will show Condition (Z) and Condition (H) both imply Condition (W), thereby proving Theorem
1.7
.

Condition 4.3

(Condition (
\textup{Z}_{0}
))
.

For every
a\in{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
, the function

\operatorname{Tr}\big[a^{s}|D|^{-s}\big]-p\tau\left[a^{p}\right](s-p)^{-1},\qquad\Re s>p,

has a unique continuous extension to the closed half-plane
\Re s\geq p
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Condition
\textup{(Z}_{0}\textup{)}
implies Condition
(W)
.

Proof.

Assume Condition
\textup{(Z}_{0}\textup{)}
holds. Pick some
\alpha\in(0,1]
such that
\delta:=\min(1,p-\alpha)>0
, and
and let
a\in{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
. Define

N(\lambda):=\#\left\{j;\ \lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)>\lambda^{-1}\right\},\qquad\lambda>0.

Alternatively,
N(\lambda^{-1})
is the counting function of
a^{1/2}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{1/2}
. In particular,
N(\lambda)
is a non-negative non-decreasing step function such that
N(\lambda)=0
for
\lambda<\lambda_{0}(a^{1/2}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{1/2})^{-1}
. As
dN(\lambda)=\sum\delta_{\lambda_{j}(a^{1/2}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{1/2})^{-1}}
, for
\Re s>\alpha^{-1}p
we have

\operatorname{Tr}\left[\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)^{s}\right]=\sum_{j\geq 0}\left(\lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)^{-1}\right)^{-s}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{-s}dN(\lambda).

Moreover, the fact that
N(\lambda^{-1})
is the counting function of
a^{1/2}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{1/2}
further implies that

(4.2)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)^{\frac{p}{\alpha}}=\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\lambda^{\frac{p}{\alpha}}N(\lambda^{-1})=\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow\infty}\lambda^{-\frac{p}{\alpha}}N(\lambda),

provided any of these limits exists.

Bearing this in mind, for
\Re s>\alpha^{-1}p
set

\displaystyle F(s)=\operatorname{Tr}\left[\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)^{s}\right]-\operatorname{Tr}\big[a^{s}|D|^{-\alpha s}\big],

\displaystyle G(s)=\operatorname{Tr}\big[a^{s}|D|^{-\alpha s}\big]-\alpha^{-1}p\tau\big[a^{\frac{p}{\alpha}}\big](s-\alpha^{-1}p)^{-1}.

We then have

(4.3)

\operatorname{Tr}\left[\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)^{-s}\right]=\alpha^{-1}p\tau\big[a^{\frac{p}{\alpha}}\big](s-\alpha^{-1}p)^{-1}+F(s)+G(s).

By Lemma
1.11
the function
F(s)
has an analytic extension to the half-plane
\Re s>\alpha^{-1}(p-\delta)
with
\delta
as above. Moreover, by assumption

G(s)=\operatorname{Tr}[(a^{1/\alpha})^{\alpha s}|D|^{-\alpha s}]-p\tau[(a^{1/\alpha})^{p}](\alpha s-p)^{-1}
has a continuous extension to the half-plane
\Re s\geq\alpha^{-1}p
. It follows that the function

\operatorname{Tr}\left[\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)^{s}\right]-\alpha^{-1}p\tau\big[a^{\frac{p}{\alpha}}\big](s-\alpha^{-1}p)^{-1},\qquad\Re s>\alpha^{-1}p,

has a continuous extension to the half-plane
\Re s\geq\alpha^{-1}p
. Combining this with (
4.3
) and Ikehara’s Tauberian theorem (see, e.g.,
[
179
, Theorem 14.1]
), and using (
4.2
), we then obtain

(4.4)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j\lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)^{\frac{p}{\alpha}}=\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow\infty}\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha}{p}}N(\lambda)=\tau\big[a^{\frac{p}{\alpha}}\big].

In a similar way as we got (
3.23
), specializing (
3.21
) to
s=2\alpha^{-1}
and using (
4.4
) yields

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}D^{-2}a^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\big)=\bigg[\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{\alpha}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)\bigg]^{\frac{2}{\alpha}}=\tau\big[a^{\frac{p}{\alpha}}\big]^{\frac{2}{p}}.

Combining this with Lemma
3.6
we then get

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{-\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}D^{2}a^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\big)=\left[\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{2}{p}}\lambda_{j}\big(a^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}D^{-2}a^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\big)\right]^{-1}=\tau\big[a^{\frac{p}{\alpha}}\big]^{-\frac{2}{p}}

This shows that the Weyl law (
3.4
) holds for all
a\in{\mathscr{A}}_{++}^{-1/\alpha}
, where
{\mathscr{A}}_{++}^{-1/\alpha}
is defined as in (
3.6
). Lemma
3.8
then ensures us that Condition (W) is satisfied. The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

The equality (
4.4
) is merely the contents of the Tauberian theorem of
[
137
]
in its version for spectral triples (i.e.,
[
137
, Theorem 1.4]
). In
[
137
]
the result is obtained by assuming Lipschitz regularity and
p
-summability with
p>2
. Both assumptions are unnecessary in our approach.

4.3.
Condition (Z)

We may replace Condition (
\textup{Z}_{0}
) by a more standard Tauberian condition, which is easier to check in practice. Incidentally, Condition (Z) is the type of Tauberian condition sought for by Alain Connes.

In what follows by a
Fréchet subalgebra
of
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
we shall mean a subalgebra
\mathscr{B}
which is endowed with a Fréchet algebra topology which is stronger than the topology of
\mathscr{A}
. Recall that if
\mathscr{B}
is a unital Fréchet algebra, then the inverse map
x\rightarrow x^{-1}
is continuous on its set of invertible elements
\mathscr{B}^{-1}
(see
[
152
,
203
]
). Therefore, if
\mathscr{B}
is a Fréchet subalgebra of
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
containing the unit, then it is holomorphically closed (i.e., it is closed under holomorphic functional calculus) if and only if it is inverse closed (i.e.,
\mathscr{B}^{-1}=\overline{\mathscr{A}}^{-1}\cap\mathscr{B}
).

Considering Fréchet subalgebras ensures that Banach-Steinhaus theorem holds. The need for holomorphic closedness stems from the following lemma.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be a holomorphically closed Fréchet subalgebra of
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
containing
\mathscr{A}
. For every
a\in\mathscr{A}_{++}
, the family
a^{s}
,
s\in\mathbb{C}
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{B}
.

Proof.

Let
a\in\mathscr{A}_{++}
. If
\mathscr{B}=\overline{\mathscr{A}}
, then the result is an immediate consequence of the power series expansion,

a^{s}=e^{s\log a}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{k!}s^{k}(\log a)^{k},

since the above series converges normally in
\mathscr{B}
uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}
.

In general, we proceed as follows. As
a
has positive spectrum, its spectrum is contained in some interval
[c_{1},c_{2}]
with
0<c_{1}<c_{2}
. Let
\Gamma
be a direct oriented piecewise
C^{1}
contour in the halfplane
\Re\lambda>0
whose interior contains
[c_{1},c_{2}]
. We then have

a^{s}=\frac{1}{2i\pi}\int_{\Gamma}\lambda^{s}(\lambda-a)^{-1}d\lambda,\qquad s\in\mathbb{C}.

Note that
\lambda\rightarrow(\lambda-a)^{-1}
is a continuous map from
\Gamma
to
\mathscr{B}
. This allows us to regard the above integral as a
\mathscr{B}
-valued integral.

For
\lambda\in\Gamma
and
s\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

\lambda^{s}-1-s\log\lambda=\sum_{k\geq 2}\frac{1}{k!}s^{k}(\log a)^{k}=s^{2}h(s,\lambda),

where we have set

h(\lambda,s)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{(k+2)!}s^{k}(\log a)^{k+2},\qquad(\lambda,s)\in\Gamma\times\mathbb{C}.

Note that
h(\lambda,s)
is a continuous function on
\Gamma\times\mathbb{C}
. We then have

\displaystyle a^{s}-1-s\log a

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2i\pi}\int_{\Gamma}(\lambda^{s}-1-s\log\lambda)(\lambda-a)^{-1}d\lambda

\displaystyle=\frac{s^{2}}{2i\pi}\int_{\Gamma}h(\lambda,s)(\lambda-a)^{-1}d\lambda.

The integrand
h(\lambda,s)(\lambda-a)^{-1}
is a continuous map from
\Gamma\times\mathbb{C}
to
\mathscr{B}
, and hence it is bounded in
\mathscr{B}
on
\Gamma\times\overline{D(0,1)}
, where
\overline{D(0,1)}
is the closed unit disk. Therefore, given any continuous semi-norm
\mathfrak{p}
on
\mathscr{B}
, there is a constant
C_{\mathfrak{p}\Gamma}>0
such that

\mathfrak{p}\left(a^{s}-1-s\log a\right)\leq\frac{|s|^{2}}{2\pi}\int_{\Gamma}\mathfrak{p}\left(h(\lambda,s)(\lambda-a)^{-1}\right)|d\lambda|\leq C_{\mathfrak{p}\Gamma}|s|^{2}.

Thus, near
s=0
, we have

a^{s}-1-s\log a=\operatorname{O}(|s|^{2})\qquad\text{in}\ \mathscr{B}.

This shows that the
\mathscr{B}
-valued family
a^{s}
,
s\in\mathbb{C}
, is holomorphic at
s=0
(with respect to the
\mathscr{B}
-topology). The 1-parameter group property
a^{s}=a^{s_{0}}a^{s-s_{0}}
further ensures it is holomorphic at every
s_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
. This proves the result.
∎

We have a continuous embedding
T\rightarrow\varphi_{T}
of
\mathscr{L}_{1}
into
\overline{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
given by

(4.5)

\varphi_{T}(a)=\operatorname{Tr}\big[aT\big],\qquad a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}},\quad T\in\mathscr{L}_{1}.

In particular, as
|D|^{-s}
,
\Re s>p
, is a holomorphic family of trace-class operators (
cf
. Lemma
4.1
), we get a
holomorphic family
\varphi_{s}
,
\Re s>p
, in
\overline{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
given by

\varphi_{s}(a):=\varphi_{|D|^{-s}}(a)=\operatorname{Tr}\big[a|D|^{-s}\big],\qquad a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}},\quad\Re s>p.

If
\mathscr{B}
is any Fréchet subalgebra of
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
with strong dual
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
, then, as we have a continuous embedding of
\overline{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
into
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
, we see that

(\varphi_{s})_{|\mathscr{B}}
,
\Re s>p
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
.

In what follows we denote by
\Omega_{p}
the open halfplane
\{\Re z>p\}
with closure
\overline{\Omega}_{p}:=\{\Re z\geq p\}
.

Condition 4.7

(Condition (Z))
.

There is a holomorphically closed Fréchet subalgebra
\mathscr{B}\subseteq\overline{\mathscr{A}}
containing
\mathscr{A}
such that, for every
a\in\mathscr{B}
,
the function

(4.6)

\Omega_{p}\ni s\longrightarrow\varphi_{s}(a)-p\tau(a)(s-p)^{-1}

extends continuously to
\overline{\Omega}_{p}
.

Remark 4.8
.

In practice we often may take
\mathscr{B}=\overline{\mathscr{A}}
, or
\mathscr{B}=\mathscr{A}
if
\mathscr{A}
is itself a holomorphically closed Fréchet subalgebra.

Remark 4.9
.

Condition (Z) means that, given any
a\in\mathscr{B}
, for all
s\in\partial\Omega_{p}
, we have a limit

\psi_{s}(a):=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\rightarrow s\\
z\in\Omega_{p}\end{subarray}}\psi_{z}(a),

in such a way that the function
\overline{\Omega}_{p}\ni s\rightarrow\psi_{s}(a)
is continuous. By Banach-Steinhaus theorem each
\psi_{s}
,
s\in\partial\Omega
, is a continuous linear functional on
\mathscr{B}
. It follows that
(\psi_{s})_{s\in\overline{\Omega}_{p}}
is a continuous family in
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}_{\textup{weak}}
.

We have the following relationship between Condition (Z) and Condition
\textup{(Z}_{0}\textup{)}
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Condition
(Z)
implies Condition
\textup{(Z}_{0}\textup{)}
.

Proof.

As Condition (Z) holds, it follows from Remark
4.9
that there is a holomorphically closed Fréchet subalgebra
\mathscr{B}\subseteq\overline{\mathscr{B}}
and a continuous family
(\psi_{s})_{s\in\overline{\Omega}_{p}}
in
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}_{\textup{weak}}
such that

(4.7)

\varphi_{s}(a)=p\tau(a)(s-p)^{-1}+\psi_{s}(a)\qquad\forall a\in\mathscr{B}\quad\forall s\in\Omega_{p}.

Let
a\in{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
. We have

(4.8)

a^{s}-a^{p}=a^{p}\big(e^{s\log a}-1\big)=a^{p}\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n!}(s-a)^{n}(\log a)^{n}=(s-p)b(s),

where we have set

b(s)=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{1}{(n+1)!}(s-p)^{n}a^{p}(\log a)^{n+1},\qquad s\in\mathbb{C}.

In particular,
b(s)
,
s\in\mathbb{C}
, is an analytic family in
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
.

Combining (
4.7
) with (
4.8
) shows that, for
\Re s>p
, we have

(4.9)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}\big[a^{s}|D|^{-s}\big]=\varphi_{s}(a^{s})

\displaystyle=p\tau\big[a^{s}\big](s-p)^{-1}+\psi_{s}\big[a^{s}\big]

(4.10)

\displaystyle=p\tau\big[a^{p}\big](s-p)^{-1}+p\tau\big[b(s)\big]+\psi_{s}\big[a^{s}\big].

The continuity of
\tau
ensures that
\tau[b(s)]
is an entire function on
\mathbb{C}
. Moreover, we know from Lemma
4.6
that
a^{s}
,
s\in\mathbb{C}
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{B}
. Let
s_{0}\in\overline{\Omega}_{p}
and
K
a compact neighborhood of
s
. As
(\psi_{s})_{s\in\overline{\Omega}_{p}}
is a continuous family in
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}_{\textup{weak}}
, its restriction
(\psi_{s})_{s\in K}
is weakly bounded, and hence is equicontinuous by the Banach-Steinhaus theorem. This means there is continuous seminorm
\mathfrak{p}
on
\mathscr{B}
such that

|\psi_{s}(x)|\leq\mathfrak{p}(x)\qquad\forall x\in\mathscr{B}\quad\forall s\in K.

Thus, for all
s\in K
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\psi_{s}(a^{s})-\psi_{s_{0}}(a^{s_{0}})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\psi_{s}(a^{s}-a^{s_{0}})\right|+\left|\psi_{s}(a^{s_{0}})-\psi_{s_{0}}(a^{s_{0}})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{p}(a^{s}-a^{s_{0}})+\left|\psi_{s}(a^{s_{0}})-\psi_{s_{0}}(a^{s_{0}})\right|.

As
\psi_{s}(a^{s_{0}})\rightarrow\psi_{s_{0}}(a^{s_{0}})
and
a^{s}\rightarrow a^{s_{0}}
in
\mathscr{B}
as
s\rightarrow s_{0}
, we then deduce that
\psi_{s}(a^{s})\rightarrow\psi_{s_{0}}(a^{s_{0}})
as
s\rightarrow s_{0}
. This shows that the function
s\rightarrow\psi_{s}(a^{s})
is continuous on
\overline{\Omega}_{p}
.

It follows from all this that, for every
a\in\mathscr{A}_{++}
, the function

\Omega_{p}\ni s\longrightarrow\operatorname{Tr}\big[a^{s}|D|^{-s}\big]-p\tau\left[a^{p}\right](s-p)^{-1}

extends continuously to
\overline{\Omega}_{p}
. That is, Condition
\textup{(Z}_{0}\textup{)}
is satisfied, proving the result.
∎

4.4.
Condition (H)

There is a closed relationship between singularities of zeta functions and short-time behaviours of traces of heat semigroups (see, e.g.,
[
97
]
). In various examples the latter are often simpler to study. Therefore, it stands for reason to seek for a heat semigroup version of Condition
(Z)
.

Lemma 4.11
.

The heat semigroup
e^{-tD^{2}}
,
t>0
, yields a continuous family of trace-class operators such that

(4.11)

\displaystyle\big\|(1-\Pi_{0})e^{-tD^{2}}\big\|_{1}=\operatorname{O}\big(e^{-t\lambda_{0}(D^{2})}\big)\quad\text{as $t\rightarrow\infty$},

(4.12)

\displaystyle\big\|e^{-tD^{2}}\big\|_{1}=\operatorname{O}\big(t^{-\frac{p}{2}}\big)\quad\text{as $t\rightarrow 0^{+}$}.

Proof.

This result is standard. We sketch a proof for the reader’s convenience. Let
(\xi_{j})_{j\geq 0}
be an orthonormal family in
\mathscr{H}
such that
D^{2}\xi_{j}=\lambda_{j}(D^{2})\xi_{j}
. For all
t>0
, we have

(4.13)

e^{-tD^{2}}=\Pi_{0}+\sum_{j\geq 0}e^{-t\lambda_{j}(D^{2})}|{\xi_{j}}\rangle\langle\xi_{j}|,

where the series converges in
\mathscr{K}
. As
D^{-1}\in\mathscr{L}_{p,\infty}
, setting
C=\|D^{-1}\|_{p,\infty}
we get

\lambda_{j}\big(D^{2}\big)=\mu_{j}(D)^{-2}\geq C(j+1)^{\frac{2}{p}}\quad\text{for all $j\geq 0$}.

Thus, given any
b>0
, for all
j\geq 0
and
t\geq b
, we have

(4.14)

\big\|e^{-t\lambda_{j}(D^{2})}|{\xi_{j}}\rangle\langle\xi_{j}|\big\|_{1}=e^{-t\lambda_{j}(D^{2})}\leq e^{-bC(j+1)^{\frac{2}{p}}}.

This shows that the Schmidt series
\sum_{j\geq 0}e^{-t\lambda_{j}(D^{2})}|{\xi_{j}}\rangle\langle\xi_{j}|
converges normally in the Banach space
C_{0}([b,\infty);\mathscr{L}_{1})
for all
b>0
. It then follows that

e^{-tD^{2}}
,
t>0
, is a continuous family in
\mathscr{L}_{1}
.

Note that
\|(1-\Pi_{0})e^{-tD^{2}}\|=e^{-t\lambda_{0}(D^{2})}
for all
t>0
. Thus, for all
t>1
, by using the semigroup property
e^{-tD^{2}}=e^{-(t-1)D^{2}}e^{-D^{2}}
, we get

\displaystyle\big\|(1-\Pi_{0})e^{-tD^{2}}\big\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\big\|(1-\Pi_{0})e^{-(t-1)D^{2}}e^{-D^{2}}\big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|(1-\Pi_{0})e^{-(t-1)D^{2}}\|\|e^{-D^{2}}\big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|e^{-D^{2}}\big\|_{1}e^{-(t-1)\lambda_{0}(D^{2})}.

This gives (
4.11
).

Moreover, given any
t>0
, by using (
4.13
) and (
4.14
) we get

\big\|e^{-tD^{2}}\big\|_{1}\leq\|\Pi_{0}\|_{1}+\sum_{j\geq 0}\big\|e^{-t\lambda_{j}(D^{2})}|{\xi_{j}}\rangle\langle\xi_{j}|\big\|_{1}\leq\dim\ker D+\sum_{j\geq 0}e^{-bC(j+1)^{\frac{2}{p}}}.

Note that

\sum_{j\geq 0}e^{-bC(j+1)^{\frac{2}{p}}}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-bCx^{\frac{2}{p}}}dx=(Ct)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-x^{\frac{2}{p}}}dx.

This gives (
4.12
). The proof is complete.
∎

As
e^{-tD^{2}}
,
t>0
, is a continuous family in
\mathscr{L}^{1}
, it gives rise to a continuous family
\theta_{t}
,
t>0
, in
\overline{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
given by

(4.15)

\theta_{t}(a)=\operatorname{Tr}\big[ae^{-tD^{2}}\big],\qquad a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}},\quad t>0.

In particular, if
\mathscr{B}
is any Fréchet subalgebra of
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
, then
(\theta_{t})_{|\mathscr{B}}
,
t>0
, is a continuous family in
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
.

The heat semigroup version of Condition
(Z)
is provided by the following condition.

Condition 4.12

(Condition
(H)
)
.

There are
\delta>0
and a holomorphically closed Fréchet subalgebra
\mathscr{B}\subseteq\overline{\mathscr{A}}
containing
\mathscr{A}
such that, for all
a\in\mathscr{B}
, as
t\rightarrow 0^{+}
we have

\theta_{t}(a)=\Gamma\left(1+\frac{p}{2}\right)t^{-\frac{p}{2}}\left(\tau(a)+\operatorname{O}\big(t^{\delta}\big)\right).

Remark 4.13
.

Equivalently, Condition (H) means that the family of linear functionals,

\varepsilon_{t}(a):=t^{-\delta}\left[\theta_{t}(a)-\Gamma\left(1+\frac{p}{2}\right)\tau(a)\right],\qquad a\in\mathscr{B},\quad t>0,

is weakly bounded in
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
on every interval
(0,c]
,
c>0
. Note also that
(\varepsilon_{t})_{t>0}
is a continuous family in
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 4.14
.

Condition
(H)
implies Condition
(Z)
.

Proof.

Assume Condition
(H)
holds. For
\Re s>0
, we have

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\frac{s}{2}\right)|D|^{-s}=\Gamma\left(\frac{s}{2}\right)\left(D^{2}\right)^{-\frac{s}{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{s}{2}}\left(1-\Pi_{0}\right)e^{-tD^{2}}\frac{dt}{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{s}{2}}e^{-tD^{2}}\frac{dt}{t}-\frac{1}{s}\Pi_{0}+R(s),

where we have set

R(s)=\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{s}{2}}\left(1-\Pi_{0}\right)e^{-tD^{2}}\frac{dt}{t}.

We have also used the fact that

\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{s}{2}}\Pi_{0}e^{-tD^{2}}\frac{dt}{t}=\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{s}{2}}\Pi_{0}\frac{dt}{t}=\frac{2}{s}\Pi_{0}.

The above integrals converges in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
. However, for
\Re s>p
, Lemma
4.11
ensures that
t^{-1+s/2}\left(1-\Pi_{0}\right)e^{-tD^{2}}
,
t>0
, is an
L^{1}
-family in
\mathscr{L}_{1}
, and so the integrals actually converges in
\mathscr{L}_{1}
. Thus, for all
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}
, we have

\Gamma\left(\frac{s}{2}\right)\operatorname{Tr}\big[a|D|^{-s}\big]=\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{s}{2}}\operatorname{Tr}\big[ae^{-tD^{2}}\big]\frac{dt}{t}-\frac{2}{s}\operatorname{Tr}\big[a\Pi_{0}\big]+\operatorname{Tr}\big[aR(s)\big],\quad\Re s>p.

Equivalently, with the notation of (
4.5
), (
4.6
) and (
4.15
) we have

(4.16)

\Gamma\left(\frac{s}{2}\right)\varphi_{s}(a)=\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{s}{2}}\theta_{t}(a)\frac{dt}{t}-\frac{2}{s}\operatorname{Tr}\big[a\Pi_{0}\big]+\varphi_{R(s)}(a),\quad\Re s>p.

Note that
(s,t)\rightarrow t^{-1+s/2}(1-\Pi_{0})e^{-tD^{2}}
is a continuous map from
\mathbb{C}\times[1,\infty)
to
\mathscr{L}_{1}
. Moreover, thanks to (
4.11
), for any compact
K\subseteq\mathbb{C}
, there are
\beta_{K}\in\mathbb{R}
and
C_{K}>0
such that, for all
s\in K
and
t\geq 1
, we have

\big\|t^{-1+s/2}(1-\Pi_{0})e^{-tD^{2}}\big\|_{1}\leq t^{\beta_{K}}\big\|(1-\Pi_{0})e^{-tD^{2}}\big\|_{1}\leq C_{K}t^{\beta_{K}}e^{-t\lambda_{0}(D^{2})}.

As
t^{\beta_{K}}e^{-t\lambda_{0}(D^{2})}\in L^{1}[1,\infty)
, it follows that
R(s)
,
s\in\mathbb{C}
, is a continuous family in
\mathscr{L}_{1}
, and so

\varphi_{R(s)}
,
s\in\mathbb{C}
, is a continuous family in
\overline{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
.

As Condition (H) holds, we know from Remark
4.13
, there are
\delta>0
, a holomorphically closed Fréchet subalgebra
\mathscr{B}\subseteq\overline{\mathscr{A}}
, and a weakly bounded continuous family
(\varepsilon_{t})_{0<t\leq 1}
in
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
such that, for all
a\in\mathscr{B}
, we have

\theta_{t}(a)=\Gamma\left(1+\frac{p}{2}\right)t^{-\frac{p}{2}}\left[\tau(a)+t^{\delta}\varepsilon_{t}(a)\right],\qquad 0<t\leq 1.

Thus, if
a\in\mathscr{B}
, then, for
\Re s>p
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{s}{2}}\theta_{t}(a)\frac{dt}{t}

\displaystyle=\Gamma\left(1+\frac{p}{2}\right)\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{s-p}{2}}\tau(a)\frac{dt}{t}+\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{s-p}{2}}t^{\delta-1}\varepsilon_{t}(a)dt

(4.17)

\displaystyle=p\Gamma\left(\frac{p}{2}\right)\tau(a)(s-p)^{-1}+\hat{\varepsilon}_{s}(a),

where we have set
\hat{\varepsilon}_{s}(a):=\int_{0}^{1}t^{(s-p+\delta)/2}\varepsilon_{t}(a)t^{-1}dt
. As
t^{\delta-1}\varepsilon_{t}(a)\in L^{1}((0,1])
, we see that

\hat{\varepsilon}_{s}(a)
is a holomorphic function on the half-plane
\Re s>p-\delta
.

Combining (
4.16
) and (
4.17
) shows that, for all
a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}
, we have

\Gamma\left(\frac{s}{2}\right)\varphi_{s}(a)=p\Gamma\left(\frac{p}{2}\right)\tau(a)(s-p)^{-1}+\psi_{s}(a),\qquad\Re s>p.

where we have set

\psi_{s}(a):=\hat{\varepsilon}_{s}(a)-\frac{2}{s}\operatorname{Tr}\big[a\Pi_{0}\big]+\varphi_{R(s)}(a),\qquad\Re s>p-\delta.

Note that
s\rightarrow\psi_{s}(a)
is a holomorphic function on the halfplane
\Re s\geq p-\delta
.

In addition, as
\Gamma(s/2)^{-1}
is a holomorphic function on the half-plane
\Re s>0
, we may write

\Gamma\left(\frac{s}{2}\right)^{-1}=\Gamma\left(\frac{p}{2}\right)^{-1}+(s-p)u(s),

where
u(s)
is a holomorphic function on the half-plane
\Re s>0
. Thus, for
\Re s>p
, we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{s}(a)

\displaystyle=p\Gamma\left(\frac{s}{2}\right)^{-1}\Gamma\left(\frac{p}{2}\right)\tau(a)(s-p)^{-1}+\Gamma\left(\frac{s}{2}\right)^{-1}\psi_{s}(a)

\displaystyle=p\tau(a)(s-p)^{-1}+pu(s)\Gamma\left(\frac{p}{2}\right)\tau(a)+\Gamma\left(\frac{s}{2}\right)^{-1}\psi_{s}(a).

Equivalently,

\varphi_{s}(a)-p\tau(a)(s-p)^{-1}=pu(s)\Gamma\left(\frac{p}{2}\right)\tau(a)+\Gamma\left(\frac{s}{2}\right)^{-1}\psi_{s}(a).

As the r.h.s. is a holomorphic function on the halfplane
\Re s>p-\delta
, this shows that the l.h.s. has a holomorphic extension to that halfplane for all
a\in\mathscr{B}
. This ensures that Condition
(Z)
holds, proving the result.
∎

Remark 4.15
.

The proof above shows that Condition (H) implies a slightly stronger form of Condition (Z), since we obtain an analytic continuation of the functions (
4.6
) to the halfplane
\{\Re z>p-\delta\}
.

4.5.
Proof of Theorem
1.7

We know from Lemma
4.4
that Condition
\textup{(Z}_{0}\textup{)}
implies Condition (W). By Lemma
4.10
and Lemma
4.14
Condition (Z) and Condition (H) both imply Condition
\textup{(Z}_{0}\textup{)}
, and so they imply Condition (W). This completes the proof of Theorem
1.7
.
\Box

Chapter 5
Double Integral Operators – Proof of Lemma
1.12

This chapter is devoted to proving Lemma
1.12
. This lemma provides factorization formulas for fractional commutators. These formulas play crucial roles in the proof of Lemma
1.9
in Chapter
3
, and in the proofs of Lemma
1.10
and Lemma
1.11
in Chapter
6
. Those lemmas are the key ingredients in the proofs of the main results of this monograph (see Chapter
3
and Chapter
4
).

The proof of Lemma
1.12
relies on results on basic results for double operator-integrals (DOIs). These results were established by Birman and Solomyak in the 60s and 70s.

5.1.
The transformers
\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)

The proof of Lemma
1.12
relies on expressing commutators in terms of operators of the form,

(5.1)

\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1+\alpha}|D|^{\delta}\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1}T|D|^{\delta^{\prime}}\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1}d\mu,\qquad T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}),

where
\alpha\in(-2,2)
and
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in[0,2]
satisfy one of the following two conditions:

(i)

\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}<2
, or

(ii)

\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}=2
and
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in(0,2)
.

These operators (when they make sense) are special instances of double operator integrals (DOIs) in the sense of Birman-Solomyak
[
20
,
23
,
33
]
(see also
[
184
]
). In fact, in the case (ii), up to change of variable and scaling, the operator
\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)
is the operator
\Theta^{A_{0},A_{1}}_{\frac{1}{2}\delta,\frac{1}{2}\delta^{\prime}}(T)
with
A_{0}=A_{1}=D^{2}
from
[
31
, Section 1]
. Therefore, at least in the case
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}=2
, the properties of our operators
\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)
follow from standard properties of DOIs as described in
[
20
,
23
,
33
,
184
]
.

For our purpose we only need the boundedness of DOIs on
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
. Moreover, it is important to incorporate condition (i) as well, even though it is simpler. For these reasons, we provide a unified account that focuses on boundedness on
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
under our two conditions, and includes the original arguments of
[
23
,
33
]
for our class of DOIs under condition (ii).

Actually, the main issue at stake is to give sense of the integrals appearing in (
5.1
). To ease the exposition we introduce some notation. For
0\leq\delta\leq 2
, set

R_{\delta}(\mu)=|D|^{\delta}\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1},\qquad\mu\geq 0.

with the convention that
|D|^{0}=1-\Pi_{0}
. Note that
R_{\delta}(\mu)
is a bounded operator which is compact for
0\leq\delta<2
. For
0\leq\delta,\delta^{\prime}\leq 2
, we also define

\displaystyle\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T;\mu):=

\displaystyle R_{\delta}(\mu)TR_{\delta^{\prime}}(\mu)

(5.2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|D|^{\delta}\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1}T|D|^{\delta^{\prime}}\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1},\quad\mu\geq 0,\ T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}).

Note that
\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T;\mu)
is always a bounded operator and is compact whenever
\min(\delta,\delta^{\prime})<2
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\alpha\in(-2,2)
and
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in[0,2]
. The following hold.

(i)

If
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}<2
, then there is
C_{\alpha\delta\delta^{\prime}}>0
such that

(5.3)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1+\alpha}\left\|\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T;\mu)\right\|d\mu\leq C_{\alpha\delta\delta^{\prime}}\|T\|\qquad\forall T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}).

In particular, the map
\mu\rightarrow\mu^{1+\alpha}\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T;\mu)
is integrable in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
in Bochner’s sense.

(ii)

If
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}=2
and
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in(0,2)
, then there is
C_{\delta\delta^{\prime}}>0
such that

(5.4)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1+\alpha}\left|\big\langle\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T;\mu)\xi|\eta\big\rangle\right|d\mu\leq C_{\delta\delta^{\prime}}\|T\|\|\xi\|\|\eta\|\qquad\forall T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}),\ \forall\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{H}.

In particular, the map
\mu\rightarrow\mu^{1+\alpha}\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T;\mu)
is integrable with respect to the weak operator topology of
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
.

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

Let
T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
. We have

(5.5)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1+\alpha}\left\|\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T;\mu)\right\|d\mu\leq\|T\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1+\alpha}\|R_{\delta}(\mu)\|\|R_{\delta^{\prime}}(\mu)\|d\mu.

Moreover, given any
\mu>0
and
\delta\in[0,2]
, we have

\left\|R_{\delta}(\mu)\right\|=\mu^{\delta-2}\big\||(\mu^{-1}D)|^{\delta}\big((\mu^{-1}D)^{2}+1\big)^{-1}\big\|\leq\mu^{-2+\delta}\max_{t\geq 0}t^{\delta}(t^{2}+1)^{-1}.

In particular, we see that

(5.6)

\left\|R_{\delta}(\mu)\right\|=\operatorname{O}\left(\mu^{-2+\delta}\right)\qquad\text{as}\ \mu\rightarrow\infty.

If
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}<2
, then (
5.6
) ensures that
\mu^{1+\alpha}\|R_{\delta}(\mu)\|\|R_{\delta^{\prime}}(\mu)\|=\operatorname{O}(\mu^{r})
, where

r=(1+\alpha)+(-2+\delta)+(-2+\delta^{\prime})=-1-[2-(\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime})]<-1.

Together with the fact that
\alpha>-2
, i.e.,
1+\alpha>-1
, we deduce that

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1+\alpha}\|R_{\delta}(\mu)\|\|R_{\delta^{\prime}}(\mu)\|d\mu<\infty.

Combining this with (
5.5
) gives the estimate (
5.3
).

To prove the estimate (
5.4
) we follow the arguments of Birman-Solomyak
[
23
,
33
]
. Suppose that
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}=2
and
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in(0,2)
. Let
\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{H}
. We have

\left|\big\langle\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T;\mu)\xi|\eta\big\rangle\right|=\left|\big\langle TR_{\delta^{\prime}}(\mu)\xi|R_{\delta}(\mu)\eta\big\rangle\right|\leq\|T\|\|R_{\delta^{\prime}}(\mu)\xi\|\|R_{\delta}(\mu)\eta\|.

Moreover, as
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}=2
, we have
1+\alpha=3-(\delta+\delta^{\prime})
. Thus,

(5.7)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1+\alpha}\left|\big\langle\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T;\mu)\xi|\eta\big\rangle\right|d\mu\leq\|T\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{3-(\delta+\delta^{\prime})}\|R_{\delta^{\prime}}(\mu)\xi\|\|R_{\delta}(\mu)\eta\|d\mu\\
\leq\|T\|\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{3-2\delta^{\prime}}\|R_{\delta^{\prime}}(\mu)\xi\|^{2}d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{3-2\delta}\|R_{\delta}(\mu)\eta\|^{2}d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Bearing this in mind, set
c=\min\{|\lambda|;\ \lambda\in\operatorname{Sp}(D)\setminus 0\}
. Note that
c>0
. Denote by
\chi(\lambda)
the characteristic function of

(-\infty,-c]\cup[c,\infty)
, and, for
0\leq\delta\leq 2
, set

r_{\delta}(\lambda;\mu)=\chi(\lambda)|\lambda|^{\delta}\left(\lambda^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1},\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{R},\ \mu\in\mathbb{R}.

We then have
R_{\delta}(\mu)=r_{\delta}(D;\mu)
, i.e.,

R_{\delta}(\mu)=\int_{0}^{\infty}r_{\delta}(\lambda;\mu)dE(\lambda),\qquad\mu\in\mathbb{R},

where
E(\lambda)
is the spectral measure of
D
. Therefore, given any
\xi\in\mathscr{H}
, we have

\left\|R_{\delta}(\mu)\xi\right\|^{2}=\left\langle R_{\delta}(\mu)^{2}\xi|\xi\right\rangle=\int r_{\delta}(\lambda;\mu)^{2}d\left\langle E(\lambda)\xi|\xi\right\rangle=\int\chi(\lambda)|\lambda|^{2\delta}\left(\lambda^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-2}d\left\langle E(\lambda)\xi|\xi\right\rangle.

Thus,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{3-2\delta}\|R_{\delta}(\mu)\xi\|^{2}d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mu^{3-2\delta}\chi(\lambda)\left(\lambda^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-2}d\left\langle E(\lambda)\xi|\xi\right\rangle d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi(\lambda)|\lambda|^{2\delta}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{3-2\delta}\left(\lambda^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-2}d\mu\right)d\left\langle E(\lambda)\xi|\xi\right\rangle.

Note that, for
\lambda\neq 0
, we have

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{3-2\delta}\left(\lambda^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-2}d\mu=|\lambda|^{-2\delta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{3-2\delta}\left(1+\mu^{2}\right)^{-2}d\mu.

Therefore, we get

(5.8)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{3-2\delta}\|R_{\delta}(\mu)\xi\|^{2}d\mu=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{3-2\delta}\left(1+\mu^{2}\right)^{-2}d\mu\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi(\lambda)d\left\langle E(\lambda)\xi|\xi\right\rangle.

We observe that

(5.9)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi(\lambda)d\left\langle E(\lambda)\xi|\xi\right\rangle=\left\langle\chi(D)\xi|\xi\right\rangle=\left\langle(1-\Pi_{0})\xi|\xi\right\rangle\leq\|\xi\|^{2}

Moreover, the integral
I(\delta):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{3-2\delta}\left(1+\mu^{2}\right)^{-2}d\mu
is finite if and only if the following two conditions are satisfied:

•

3-2\delta>-1
, i.e.,
\delta<2
.

•

(3-2\delta)-4=-1-2\delta<-1
, i.e.,
\delta>0
.

Therefore, if
\delta\in(0,2)
, then
I(\delta)<\infty
, and so by using (
5.8
) and (
5.9
) we get

(5.10)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{3-2\delta}\|R_{\delta}(\mu)\xi\|^{2}d\mu\leq I(\delta)\|\xi\|^{2}<\infty.

It follows from this that, if
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}=2
and
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in(0,2)
, then by (
5.7
) and (
5.10
) we have

\left|\big\langle\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T;\mu)\xi|\eta\big\rangle\right|\leq\sqrt{I(\delta)I(\delta^{\prime})}\|T\|\|\xi\|\|\eta\|<\infty.

This yields the estimate (
5.4
). The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

The arguments of the proof of the estimate (
5.3
) are reminiscent of standard arguments that are used to show that spectral triples gives rise to bounded Fredholm modules (see, e.g.,
[
8
,
39
,
59
,
172
]
).

Remark 5.3
.

The estimate (
5.4
) is a special case of an estimate for a larger class of DOIs for which we have a separation of variables
[
33
,
184
]
. Once again, the proof of this estimate given above reproduces the original arguments of Birman-Solomyak
[
23
,
33
]
.

It follows from Lemma
5.1
that in (
5.1
) the integral converges in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
in Bochner’s sense if
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}<2
, and it converges in the weak operator topology’s sense if
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}=2
and
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in(0,2)
. In the latter case, this means that
\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)
is defined by

(5.11)

\left\langle\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)\xi|\eta\right\rangle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1+\alpha}\big\langle\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T;\mu)\xi|\eta\big\rangle d\mu\qquad\forall\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{H}.

Moreover, the estimates (
5.3
)–(
5.4
) ensure there is
C_{\alpha\delta\delta^{\prime}}>0
such that

\left\|\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)\right\|\leq C_{\alpha\delta\delta^{\prime}}\|T\|\qquad\forall T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}).

That is,
T\rightarrow\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)
is a bounded linear operator from
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
to itself.

In addition, as
\alpha>-2
, the condition
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}<2
ensures that
\delta+\delta^{\prime}<2-\alpha<4
, and hence
\min(\delta,\delta^{\prime})<2
. In this case one of the operators
R_{\delta}(\mu)
or
R_{\delta^{\prime}}(\mu)
is compact, and so
\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T;\mu)
is a compact operator for all
\mu\geq 0
. It follows that in this case the integral (
5.1
) actually converges in
\mathscr{K}
, and so we get a bounded operator
\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}:\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})\rightarrow\mathscr{K}
.

We will need the following factorization properties.

Lemma 5.4
.

Suppose that
\alpha\in(-2,2)
and
\delta,\delta^{\prime},\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in[0,2]
are such that, either
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime}<2
, or

\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime}=2
with
\delta+\epsilon
and
\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}
in
(0,2)
. Then, we have

(5.12)

\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)=|D|^{-\epsilon}\Psi_{\delta+\epsilon,\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)|D|^{-\epsilon^{\prime}}\qquad\forall T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}).

Proof.

Let
T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
. Note that
R_{\delta}(\mu)=|D|^{-\epsilon}R_{\delta+\epsilon}(\mu)
. Likewise,
R_{\delta^{\prime}}(\mu)=|D|^{-\epsilon^{\prime}}R_{\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}}(\mu)=R_{\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}}(\mu)|D|^{-\epsilon^{\prime}}
. Thus,

(5.13)

\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T;\mu)=|D|^{-\epsilon}\Delta_{\delta+\epsilon,\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}}(T;\mu)|D|^{-\epsilon^{\prime}},\qquad\mu\geq 0.

If
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime}<2
, then
\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)
and
\Psi_{\delta+\epsilon,\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)
are both given by Bochner integrals, and so we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1+\alpha}|D|^{-\epsilon}\Delta_{\delta+\epsilon,\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}}(T;\mu)|D|^{-\epsilon^{\prime}}d\mu

\displaystyle=|D|^{-\epsilon}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1+\alpha}\Delta_{\delta+\epsilon,\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}}(T;\mu)d\mu\right)|D|^{-\epsilon^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=|D|^{-\epsilon}\Psi_{\delta+\epsilon,\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)|D|^{-\epsilon^{\prime}}.

Suppose now that
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime}=\alpha
with
\delta+\epsilon
and
\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}
in
(0,2)
. Let
\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{H}
. In view of (
5.11
) we have

\displaystyle\big\langle|D|^{-\epsilon}\Psi_{\delta+\epsilon,\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)|D|^{-\epsilon^{\prime}}\xi|\eta\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle\Psi_{\delta+\epsilon,\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)|D|^{-\epsilon^{\prime}}\xi||D|^{-\epsilon}\eta\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1+\alpha}\big\langle\Delta_{\delta+\epsilon,\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}}(T;\mu)|D|^{-\epsilon^{\prime}}\xi||D|^{-\epsilon}\eta\big\rangle d\mu.

Thanks to (
5.13
) we have

\big\langle\Delta_{\delta+\epsilon,\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}}(T;\mu)|D|^{-\epsilon^{\prime}}\xi||D|^{-\epsilon}\eta\big\rangle=\big\langle|D|^{-\epsilon}\Delta_{\delta+\epsilon,\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}}(T;\mu)|D|^{-\epsilon^{\prime}}\xi|\eta\big\rangle=\big\langle\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T;\mu)\xi|\eta\big\rangle.

Thus,

\big\langle|D|^{-\epsilon}\Psi_{\delta+\epsilon,\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)|D|^{-\epsilon^{\prime}}\xi|\eta\big\rangle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1+\alpha}\big\langle\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T;\mu)\xi|\eta\big\rangle d\mu=\big\langle\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)\xi|\eta\big\rangle.

This gives the equality (
5.12
). The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

As
|D|^{0}=1-\Pi_{0}
, the factorization (
5.12
) for
\epsilon=\epsilon^{\prime}=0
means that, for all
T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
, we have

(1-\Pi_{0})\Psi^{\alpha}_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T)(1-\Pi_{0})=\Psi^{\alpha}_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T).

That is,
\ker D\subseteq\ker(\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T))
and
\operatorname{ran}(\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T))\subseteq(\ker D)^{\perp}=\overline{\operatorname{ran}D}
.

Recall that
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D)
is
p
-summable, and so
|D|^{-q}\in\mathscr{L}_{q^{-1}p,\infty}
for all
q>0
. We then have the following consequence of Lemma
5.4
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Suppose that
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}<2
.

(i)

If
\max(\delta,\delta^{\prime})<2
, then
\Psi^{\alpha}_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}
gives rise to a bounded operator,

\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}:\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty},\qquad\textup{with}\ r=(2-\alpha-\delta-\delta^{\prime})^{-1}p.

(ii)

If
\max(\delta,\delta^{\prime})=2
, then the same result holds for any
r>(2-\alpha-\delta-\delta^{\prime})^{-1}p
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\max(\delta,\delta^{\prime})<2
. The function
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})\rightarrow\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime}
is continuous and maps
[0,2-\delta)\times[0,2-\delta^{\prime})
to
[\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime},\alpha+2)
. As the latter interval contains
\alpha+2
, we always can find
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})\in[0,2]
such that
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime}=2
such that
\max(\delta+\epsilon,\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime})<2
. By Lemma
5.4
we then have

\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)=|D|^{-\epsilon}\Psi_{\delta+\epsilon,\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)|D|^{-\epsilon^{\prime}},\qquad T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}).

Here
\Psi_{\delta+\epsilon,\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}}^{\alpha}:\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})\rightarrow\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
is bounded since
\max(\delta+\epsilon,\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime})<2
. Moreover, the operators
|D|^{-\epsilon}
and
|D|^{-\epsilon^{\prime}}
are contained in
\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon^{-1}p,\infty}
and
\mathscr{L}_{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}p,\infty}
, respectively. By using Hólder’s inequality (Proposition
2.1
) we then deduce that
\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}
maps continuously
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
to
\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty}
, with

r=(\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}p=(2-\alpha-\delta-\delta^{\prime})^{-1}p
. This proves the first part.

Suppose that
\max(\delta,\delta^{\prime})=2
. Assume that
\delta^{\prime}=2
. The inequality
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}<2
then means that
\alpha+\delta<0
. Let
\epsilon\in(0,-(\alpha+\delta)]
, and set
\epsilon_{1}=-(\alpha+\delta)-\epsilon
. Then
\epsilon_{1}\in[0,-(\alpha+\delta))\subseteq[0,2)
and
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon_{1}=2-\epsilon<2
. Therefore, by Lemma
5.4
we have

\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T)=|D|^{-\epsilon_{1}}\Psi_{\delta+\epsilon,\delta^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}}^{\alpha}(T),\qquad T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}).

As
|D|^{-\epsilon_{1}}\in\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon_{1}^{-1},\infty}
, in the same way as above we see that

\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}
maps continuously
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
to
\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty}
, with

r=\epsilon_{1}^{-1}p=(-\alpha-\delta-\epsilon)^{-1}p=(2-\alpha-\delta-\delta^{\prime}-\epsilon)^{-1}p
. Here
\epsilon
ranges over
(0,-(\alpha+\delta)]
, and so
r
ranges
over
(2-\alpha-\delta-\delta^{\prime})^{-1}p,\infty)
. The same results holds in case
\delta=2
. This gives the 2nd part and completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

Suppose that
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}=2
and
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in(0,2)
. A well known result of the theory of DOI (see
[
23
,
33
]
) asserts that DOIs like
\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}
induce bounded operators on
\mathscr{L}_{1}
. By interpolation, we then obtain the boundedness of
\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}
on all weak Schatten classes
\mathscr{L}_{q,\infty}
with
q>1
(see
[
31
,
33
,
184
]
).

Remark 5.8
.

Suppose that
\alpha+\delta+\delta^{\prime}<2
. By using Remark
5.7
and arguing as in the proof of Lemma
5.6
we obtain that if
\max(\delta,\delta^{\prime})<2
, then, given any
q>1
, the operator
\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}
induces a bounded operator,

\Psi_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}:\mathscr{L}_{q,\infty}\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty},\qquad r^{-1}=q^{-1}+(2-\alpha-\delta-\delta^{\prime})p^{-1}.

If
\max(\delta,\delta^{\prime})=2
, the same result holds for any
r>(q^{-1}+(2-\alpha-\delta-\delta^{\prime})p^{-1})^{-1}
.

5.2.
Proof of Lemma
1.12

Throughout this section we let
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D)
be a spectral triple. We also set

c(\alpha)=\frac{2}{\pi}\sin\left(\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\right),\qquad\alpha\geq 0.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\alpha\in[0,1]
. Given any
a\in\mathscr{A}
, we have

(5.14)

(1-\Pi_{0})\left[|D|^{-\alpha},a\right](1-\Pi_{0})=-c(\alpha)F\Psi_{1,0}^{-\alpha}\left([D,a]\right)-c(\alpha)\Psi_{0,1}^{-\alpha}\left([D,a]\right)F.

Proof.

If
\alpha=0
then
c(\alpha)=0
, and so the r.h.s. of (
5.14
) vanishes. The l.h.s. vanishes as well, since, as
|D|^{0}=1-\Pi_{0}
, we have

(1-\Pi_{0})\left[|D|^{0},a\right](1-\Pi_{0})=(1-\Pi_{0})\left[(1-\Pi_{0}),a\right](1-\Pi_{0})=0.

Thus, Eq. (
5.14
) holds trivially for
\alpha=0
.

Suppose that
0<\alpha<1
. If
x>0
and
0<\beta<1
, then

x^{-\beta}=\frac{\sin(\pi\beta)}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{-\beta}(x+\mu)^{-1}d\mu.

This formula is well known. Recall it follows from the identities,

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{-\beta}(1+\mu)^{-1}d\mu=\int_{0}^{1}(1-\lambda)^{-\beta}\lambda^{\beta-1}d\lambda=\Gamma(1-\beta)\Gamma(\beta)=\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi\beta)},

the last identity being Euler’s reflection formula. Thus, if
x\neq 0
, and we take
\alpha=\beta/2
, then

|x|^{-\alpha}=(x^{2})^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}=\frac{\sin(\pi\alpha/2)}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}(x^{2}+\mu)^{-1}d\mu=c(\alpha)\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1-\alpha}(x^{2}+\mu^{2})^{-1}d\mu.

It then follows that

|D|^{-\alpha}=c(\alpha)\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1-\alpha}(1-\Pi_{0})\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1}d\mu.

Thus,

\displaystyle(1-\Pi_{0})\left[|D|^{-\alpha},a\right](1-\Pi_{0})

\displaystyle=c(\alpha)\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1-\alpha}(1-\Pi_{0})\big[(1-\Pi_{0})\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1}\!\!,a\big](1-\Pi_{0})d\mu

(5.15)

\displaystyle=c(\alpha)\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1-\alpha}(1-\Pi_{0})\left[\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1},a\right](1-\Pi_{0})d\mu.

Given any
\mu>0
, by
[
39
, Lemma 2.3]
we have

\displaystyle\big[\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1},a\big]=

\displaystyle-D\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1}[D,a]\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle-\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1}[D,a]D\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1}.

Note that, as it is not assumed that
a(\operatorname{dom}(D^{2}))\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(D^{2})
, a bit of care is needed in order to get the above equality (see
[
39
]
). Thus, in terms of the operators
\Delta_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(T;\mu)
defined in (
5.2
) (and recalling that
|D|^{0}=1-\Pi_{0}
) we get

\displaystyle(1-\Pi_{0})\left[a,\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1}\right](1-\Pi_{0})=

\displaystyle\ F|D|\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1}[D,a]\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1}|D|^{0}

\displaystyle+|D|^{0}\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1}[D,a]F|D|\left(D^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ F\Delta_{1,0}\left([D,a];\mu\right)+\Delta_{0,1}\left([D,a];\mu\right)F.

Combining this with (
5.15
) then gives

\displaystyle(1-\Pi_{0})\left[|D|^{-\alpha},a\right](1-\Pi_{0})=

\displaystyle\ -c(\alpha)\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1-\alpha}F\Delta_{1,0}\left([D,a];\mu\right)d\mu

(5.16)

\displaystyle-c(\alpha)\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu^{1-\alpha}\Delta_{0,1}\left([D,a];\mu\right)d\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ -c(\alpha)F\Psi_{1,0}^{-\alpha}\left([D,a]\right)-c(\alpha)\Psi_{0,1}^{-\alpha}\left([D,a]\right)F,

where the integrals converge in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
in Bochner’s sense (
cf
. Lemma
5.1
). This proves the result for
0<\alpha<1
. The proof is complete.
∎

We are now in a position to prove Lemma
1.12
.

Proof of Lemma
1.12
.

Assume that the spectral
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D)
is
p
-summable with
p>0
arbitrary. Let
\alpha\in[0,1]
. We have to show that if
\beta,\gamma\in[0,1]
are such that, either
\beta+\gamma<\alpha+1
, or
\beta+\gamma=\alpha+1
with
\max(\beta,\gamma)<1
, then there is a bounded operator
\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}^{\alpha}:\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})\rightarrow\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
such that, for all
a\in\mathscr{A}
, we have

(5.17)

\left[|D|^{-\alpha},a\right]=|D|^{-\beta}\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}^{\alpha}\left([D,a]\right)|D|^{-\gamma}+|D|^{-(\alpha+1)}F[D,a]\Pi_{0}+\Pi_{0}[D,a]F|D|^{-(\alpha+1)}.

Let
a\in\mathscr{A}
. As
\Pi_{0}|D|^{-\alpha}=|D|^{-\alpha}\Pi_{0}=0
, we have

\displaystyle\left[|D|^{-\alpha},a\right]

\displaystyle=(1-\Pi_{0})\left[|D|^{-\alpha},a\right](1-\Pi_{0})+\Pi_{0}\left[|D|^{-\alpha},a\right]+(1-\Pi_{0})\left[|D|^{-\alpha},a\right]\Pi_{0}

(5.18)

\displaystyle=(1-\Pi_{0})\left[|D|^{-\alpha},a\right](1-\Pi_{0})+|D|^{-\alpha}a\Pi_{0}-\Pi_{0}a|D|^{-\alpha}.

Note that

(5.19)

|D|^{-\alpha}a\Pi_{0}=|D|^{-(\alpha+1)}FDa\Pi_{0}=|D|^{-(\alpha+1)}F[D,a]\Pi_{0}.

Likewise, we have

-\Pi_{0}a|D|^{-\alpha}=-\Pi_{0}aDF|D|^{-(\alpha+1)}=\Pi_{0}[D,a]F|D|^{-(\alpha+1)}.

Thus,

(5.20)

\left[|D|^{-\alpha},a\right]=(1-\Pi_{0})\left[|D|^{-\alpha},a\right](1-\Pi_{0})+|D|^{-(\alpha+1)}F[D,a]\Pi_{0}+\Pi_{0}[D,a]F|D|^{-(\alpha+1)}.

By Lemma
5.9
we have

(1-\Pi_{0})\left[|D|^{-\alpha},a\right](1-\Pi_{0})=-c(\alpha)F\Psi_{1,0}^{-\alpha}\left([D,a]\right)-c(\alpha)\Psi_{0,1}^{-\alpha}\left([D,a]\right)F.

Let
\beta,\gamma\in[0,1]
be such that, either
\beta+\gamma<\alpha+1
, or
\beta+\gamma=\alpha+1
with
\max(\beta,\gamma)<1
. Then either
-\alpha+(\beta+1)+\gamma<2
, or
-\alpha+(\beta+1)+\gamma=2
with
\max(1+\beta,\gamma)<2
. In either case by Lemma
5.4
we have

\Psi^{-\alpha}_{1,0}(T)=|D|^{-\beta}\Psi^{-\alpha}_{\beta+1,\gamma}(T)|D|^{-\gamma},\qquad T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}).

Likewise, we have

\Psi^{-\alpha}_{0,1}(T)=|D|^{-\beta}\Psi^{-\alpha}_{\beta,\gamma+1}(T)|D|^{-\gamma},\qquad T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}).

Therefore, we may write

(1-\Pi_{0})\left[|D|^{-\alpha},a\right](1-\Pi_{0})=|D|^{-\beta}\Phi^{\alpha}_{\beta,\gamma}\left([D,a]\right)|D|^{-\gamma},

where
\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}^{\alpha}:\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})\rightarrow\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
is the bounded linear operator defined by

(5.21)

\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}^{\alpha}(T)=-c(\alpha)F\Psi^{-\alpha}_{\beta+1,\gamma}(T)-c(\alpha)\Psi^{-\alpha}_{\beta,\gamma+1}(T)F,\qquad T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}).

Combining this with (
5.20
) yields the equality (
5.17
).

Finally, to get the 2nd part of Lemma
1.12
suppose that
\beta+\gamma<\alpha+1
, i.e.,
-\alpha+\beta+\gamma+1<2
. If
\max(\beta,\gamma)<1
, then
\max(\beta+1,\gamma)
and
\max(\beta,\gamma+1)
are
<2
. Lemma
1.12
then ensures that
\Psi^{-\alpha}_{\beta+1,\gamma}
and
\Psi^{-\alpha}_{\beta,\gamma+1}
both maps continuously
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
to
\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty}
with
r=(2+\alpha-\beta-\gamma-1)^{-1}p=(1+\alpha-\beta-\gamma)^{-1}p
, and so
\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}^{\alpha}
is a continuous operator from
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
to
\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty}
as well. If
\max(\beta,\gamma)=1
, then the same result holds for any

r>(1+\alpha-\beta-\gamma)^{-1}p
. This gives the 2nd part. The proof of Lemma
1.12
is complete.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

Lemma
1.12
is stated in much more generality than what is needed for this monograph. In fact, for our purpose we only need the result in the following cases:

(i)

0<\alpha\leq 1
and
\beta=\gamma=0
.

(ii)

0<\alpha\leq 1
,
\gamma=|m|
and
0<\beta<1
.

The first case is used in the proof of Lemma
1.9
. The second case is needed in the proof of Lemma
1.10
, for which it is absolutely crucial to be able to take
\gamma=\alpha
, including for
\alpha=1
(see Eqs. (
6.10
) and (
6.12
) and Remark
6.7
on this point).

Chapter 6
Proofs of Lemma
1.10
and Lemma
1.11

This chapter is devoted to proving Lemma
1.10
and Lemma
1.11
, which are two the main key technical lemmas used
the proof of Proposition
1.1
in Chapter
3
and in the proof of Theorem
1.7
in Chapter
4
. Those results lead to the main results of this monograph.

Throughout this chapter we let
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{H},D)
be a
p
-summable spectral with
p>0
arbitrary. We shall keep on using the notation of the previous chapters.

6.1.
Preliminary lemmas

In this section, we prove several lemmas that will be used in the proofs of Lemma
1.10
and Lemma
1.11
.

The following lemma is a version of Lemma
4.1
for imaginary powers.

Lemma 6.1
.

If
X
is a (non-zero) positive compact operator on
\mathscr{H}
, then
X^{it}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, is a bounded strongly continuous family in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
.

Proof.

As with Lemma
4.1
, the above result is standard. We include a proof for the reader’s convenience.
Let
(\xi_{j})_{j\geq 0}
be an orthonormal eigenfamily of
X
such that
X\xi_{j}=\lambda_{j}(X)\xi_{j}
for all
j\geq 0
, where

\lambda_{0}(X)\geq\lambda_{1}(X)\geq\cdots
are the eigenvalues of
X
. The family
X^{it}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, then is a strongly continuous family in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
, since, for every
\xi\in\mathscr{H}
, we have

X^{it}\xi=\sum_{\lambda_{j}(X)>0}\lambda_{j}(X)^{it}\left\langle\xi_{j}|\xi\right\rangle\xi_{j},\qquad t\in\mathbb{R},

where the series converges in
\mathscr{H}
uniformly on compact sets of
\mathbb{R}
. Note also that
\|X^{it}\|=1
, since
|\lambda_{j}(X)^{it}|=1
if
\lambda_{j}(X)>0
(such an eigenvalue exists, since
X\neq 0
). In particular, the family
X^{it}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, is bounded in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
. The result is proved.
∎

In what follows, given a Banach space
(\mathscr{E},\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{E}})
, we denote by
C^{(m)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{E})
,
m\in\mathbb{R}
, the space of continuous maps
F:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
for which there is a constant
C>0
such that

\|F(t)\|_{\mathscr{E}}\leq C\left(1+|t|\right)^{m}\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}.

This is a Banach space with respect to the norm,

\left\|F\right\|^{(m)}_{\mathscr{E}}:=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}(1+|t|)^{-m}\|F(t)\|_{\mathscr{E}},\qquad F(t)\in C^{(m)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{E}).

Lemma 6.2

(compare
[
61
]
)
.

Suppose that
X
and
Y
are positive compact operators on
\mathscr{H}
.

(i)

If
F(t)\in C^{(m)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})
,
m<-1
, then
X^{it}F(t)Y^{-it}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, is an
L^{1}
-family in
\mathscr{K}
.

(ii)

We define a continuous linear operator
\Theta_{X,Y}:C^{(m)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})\rightarrow\mathscr{K}
by

(6.1)

\Theta_{X,Y}(F)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}X^{it}F(t)Y^{-it}dt,\qquad F(t)\in C^{(m)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K}),

where the integral converges in
\mathscr{K}
in the Bochner’s sense.

Proof.

Let
F(t)\in C^{(m)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})
,
m<-1
. We know from Lemma
6.1
that the families
X^{it}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and
Y^{-it}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, are bounded strongly continuous in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
. Thus,
X^{it}F(t)Y^{-it}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, is a bounded strongly continuous family in
\mathscr{K}
. In particular, it is continuous with respect to the weak operator topology, i.e., it is a continuous with respect to the topology defined by the semi-norms
T\rightarrow\left\langle T\xi|\eta\right\rangle
,
\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{H}
. Equivalently, for every finite rank operator
R
, the map
t\rightarrow\operatorname{Tr}[RX^{it}F(t)Y^{-it}]
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}
. Here
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\simeq\mathscr{L}_{1}
and finite-rank operators are dense in
\mathscr{L}_{1}
.
Combining this with the boundedness of the family
X^{it}F(t)Y^{-it}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we deduce this family is continuous with respect to the weak topology of
\mathscr{K}
, and hence it is weakly measurable. As
\mathscr{K}
is separable, Pettis theorem (see, e.g.,
[
103
]
) then ensures that the family is measurable with respect to the norm topology of
\mathscr{K}
.

Moreover, as
m<-1
and
\|X^{it}\|=\|Y^{-it}\|=1
, we have

(6.2)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\|X^{it}F(t)Y^{-it}\right\|dt\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\|F(t)\right\|dt\leq C\|F\|^{(m)},

where we have set
C:=\int(1+|t|)^{m}dt<\infty
. This shows that
X^{it}F(t)Y^{-it}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, is an
L^{1}
-family in
\mathscr{K}
in Bochner’s sense. The above inequality further ensures that

\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}}X^{it}F(t)Y^{-it}dt\right\|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\|X^{it}F(t)Y^{-it}\right\|dt\leq C\|F\|^{(m)}\qquad\forall F\in C^{(m)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K}).

This shows that (
6.1
) defines a bounded operator
\Theta_{X,Y}:C^{(m)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})\rightarrow\mathscr{K}
. The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

A small bit of extra work shows that
\Theta_{X,Y}
induces a bounded operator
\Theta_{X,Y}:C^{(m)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}_{1})\rightarrow\mathscr{L}_{1}
, and so by interpolation we get bounded operators
\Theta_{X,Y}:C^{(m)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}_{q,\infty})\rightarrow\mathscr{L}_{q,\infty}
for all
q>1
.

As mentioned above, the assumption that
\mathscr{A}
is closed under holomorphic functional calculus ensures that, if
a\in{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
, then, the powers
a^{z}
,
z\in\mathbb{C}
, are s in
\mathscr{A}
, and so the commutators
[D,a^{z}]
are bounded. We actually have the following result.

Lemma 6.4
.

If
a\in{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
, then
[D,a^{z}]
,
z\in\mathbb{C}
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
. Moreover, for any closed vertical strip
\Sigma=\{c_{1}\leq\Re z\leq c_{2}\}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
, there is a constant
C_{\Sigma}(a)>0
, such that

(6.3)

\left\|[D,a^{z}]\right\|\leq C_{\Sigma}(a)\left(1+|\Im z|\right)\qquad\forall z\in\Sigma.

Proof.

Given
a\in{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
, let
\Gamma
be an outward-oriented rectifiable closed contour in the half-plane
\Re\lambda>0
whose interior contains
\operatorname{Sp}(a)
.
For all
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

a^{z}=\frac{1}{2i\pi}\int_{\Gamma}\lambda^{z}(\lambda-a)^{-1}d\lambda.

Thus,

\displaystyle[D,a^{z}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2i\pi}\int_{\Gamma}\lambda^{z}\left[D,(\lambda-a)^{-1}\right]d\lambda

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2i\pi}\int_{\Gamma}\lambda^{z}(\lambda-a)^{-1}\left[D,a\right](\lambda-a)^{-1}d\lambda.

In the last integral the integrand
(\lambda,z)\rightarrow\lambda^{z}(\lambda-a)^{-1}[D,a](\lambda-a)^{-1}
is a continuous map from

\Gamma\times\mathbb{C}
to
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
which is holomorphic with respect to
z
.
This ensures that
[D,a^{z}]
,
z\in\mathbb{C}
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
. Indeed, given any
z\in\mathbb{C}
, as
h\rightarrow 0
we have

\displaystyle[D,a^{z+h}]-[D,a^{z}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2i\pi}\int_{\Gamma}(\lambda^{z+h}-\lambda^{z})(\lambda-a)^{-1}\left[D,a\right](\lambda-a)^{-1}d\lambda

\displaystyle=\frac{h}{2i\pi}\int_{\Gamma}(\log\lambda)\lambda^{z}(\lambda-a)^{-1}\left[D,a\right](\lambda-a)^{-1}d\lambda+\operatorname{o}(|h|).

The above property implies that, for any compact
K\subseteq\mathbb{C}
, the family
[D,a^{z}]
,
z\in K
, is bounded in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
. Moreover, if
z=x+iy
with
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, then

\big[D,a^{z}]=\big[D,a^{x}]a^{iy}+a^{x}\big[D,a^{iy}].

We observe that if
x
remains in a closed interval
[\alpha,\beta]

and
y
varies over
\mathbb{R}
, then
a^{x}
and
a^{iy}
remains in bounded subsets of
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
and
[D,a^{x}]
remains in a bounded subset of
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
. Therefore, in order to prove the estimates (
6.3
) for closed vertical stripes it is enough to do it for the imaginary line
\Sigma=i\mathbb{R}
.

As
i[-1,1]
is a compact subset of
\mathbb{C}
, there is
C>0
such that

\left\|[D,a^{it}]\right\|\leq C\qquad\forall t\in[-1,1].

If
y\geq 1
and we write
y=t+\ell
with
t\in[0,1]
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\displaystyle[D,a^{iy}]

\displaystyle=a^{it}[D,a^{i\ell}]+[D,a^{it}]a^{i\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq j\leq\ell-1}a^{i(t+j)}[D,a^{i\ell}]a^{i(\ell-j)}+[D,a^{it}]a^{i\ell}.

Thus,

\left\|[D,a^{iy}]\right\|\leq\ell\left\|[D,a^{i}]\right\|+\left\|[D,a^{it}]\right\|\leq C(\ell+1)\leq C(|y|+1).

Likewise, if
x\leq-1
, then
\|[D,a^{iy}]\|\leq C(1+|y|)
. This proves (
6.3
) for
\Sigma=i\mathbb{R}
. The proof is complete.
∎

In what follows for
\Re s>1
we let
g_{s}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the function defined by

(6.4)

g_{s}(t)=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\sinh\left[(s-2)t/2\right]}{\sinh(t/2)\cosh\left[(s-1)t/2\right]},\quad t\neq 0,\qquad g_{s}(0)=-\frac{1}{2}(s-2).

Lemma 6.5

(compare
[
137
,
193
]
)
.

The family
g_{s}(t)
,
\Re s>1
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

Set
\Omega=\{\Re s>1\}
. Note that each function
g_{s}(t)
,
s\in\Omega
, is even. In addition, let
h:\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be the function defined by

h(z)=\frac{\sinh(z)}{z},\quad z\neq 0,\qquad h(0)=1.

This is an entire function on
\mathbb{C}
which is
>0
on
\mathbb{R}
and has
i\pi\mathbb{Z}
as zero-set. Moreover, we have

g_{s}(t)=-\frac{1}{2}(s-2)\frac{h\left[(s-2)t/2\right]}{h(t/2)\cosh\left[(s-1)t/2\right]},\qquad\Re s>1,\ t\in\mathbb{R}.

As
\cosh(z)
is a non-vanishing holomorphic function on the half-plane
\Re z>0
, it follows that
(s,t)\rightarrow g_{s}(t)
is a
C^{\infty}
-function on
\Omega\times\mathbb{R}
which is holomorphic with respect to
s
. This ensures that
g_{s}(t)
,
s\in\Omega
, is at least a holomorphic family in
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.

In view of (
6.4
), for
t>0
and
s\in\Omega
, we have

g_{s}(t)=\frac{e^{-(s-2)t/2}-e^{(s-2)t/2}}{e^{st/2}(1-e^{-t})(1+e^{-(s-1)t})}=\frac{e^{-(s-1)t}-e^{-t}}{1-e^{-t}-e^{-(s-1)t}+e^{-st}}

An induction then shows that, for
\ell=0,1,\ldots
we may write

\partial_{t}^{\ell}g_{s}(t)=\frac{e^{-t}u_{s}^{(\ell)}(t)+e^{-(s-1)t}v_{s}^{(\ell)}(t)}{(1+f_{s}(t))^{-(\ell+1)}},\qquad t>0,\ s\in\Omega,

where we have set
f_{s}(t)=e^{-st}-e^{-t}-e^{-(s-1)t}
and
u_{s}^{(\ell)}(t)
and
v_{s}^{(\ell)}(t)
are polynomials in
s
,
e^{-t}
and
e^{-(s-1)t}
. It follows from this (and the fact that
g_{s}(t)
is an even function) that, given any
N\geq 1
and compact
K\subseteq\Omega
, there is a constant
C_{\ell KN}>0
such that

\left|\partial_{t}^{\ell}g_{s}(t)\right|\leq C_{\ell KN}|t|^{-N}\qquad\forall(s,t)\in K\times(\mathbb{R}\setminus(-1,1)).

These estimates together with the fact that
g_{s}(t)
,
s\in\Omega
, is a holomorphic family in
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
ensure that
g_{s}(t)
,
s\in\Omega
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
. The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 6.6
.

As
g_{s}(t)
,
\Re s>1
, is a Schwartz-class function, we may define its Fourier transform by

(6.5)

\hat{g}_{s}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}g_{s}(\xi)e^{-it\xi}d\xi,\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}.

The family
\hat{g}_{s}(t)
,
\Re s>1
, then is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
.

6.2.
Proof of Lemma
1.10

We shall actually prove a more general result. Namely, given any
a\in{\mathscr{A}}_{++}
and
\alpha\in(0,1]
, we seek for showing that, for
\Re s>1
, we have

\left(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{s}-|D|^{-\alpha s}a^{s}\in\mathscr{L}_{(\alpha s+1-\epsilon)^{-1}p,\infty}\qquad\forall\epsilon\in(0,1).

Specializing this to
s\in(1,\infty)
will give Lemma
1.10
. As we shall see, an elaboration of the arguments of the proof this more general version of Lemma
1.10
, will lead to a holomorphic version of this lemma (see Lemma
6.8
below). Lemma
1.11
will then be a by-product of that lemma.

For
\Re s>1
, set

T(s):=\big(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)^{s}-|D|^{-\alpha s}a^{s}.

We then need to show that

(6.6)

T(s)\in\mathscr{L}_{(\alpha\Re s+1-\epsilon)^{-1}p,\infty}\qquad\forall\epsilon\in(0,1).

Set
X=|D|^{-\alpha}
and
Y=a^{1/2}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{1/2}
. As mentioned above, our approach relies on the integral representation for differences

(A^{1/2}BA^{1/2})^{z}-B^{z}A^{z}
,
\Re z>1
, established by Connes-Sukochev-Zanin
[
61
, Lemma 5.2]
and Sukochev-Zanin
[
193
, Theorem 5.2.1]
. Applying this formula to
A=a
and
B=X
gives

(6.7)

T(s)=F_{s}(0)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{g}_{s}(t)X^{it}F_{s}(t)Y^{-it}dt,\qquad\Re s>1,

where
F_{s}(t)=F_{1,s}(t)+F_{2,s}(t)
, with

F_{1,s}(t):=X^{s-1}\big[X,a^{s-\frac{1}{2}+it}\big]a^{\frac{1}{2}},\qquad F_{2,s}(t):=\big[X,a^{\frac{1}{2}+it}\big]a^{\frac{1}{2}}Y^{s-1}.

In
[
61
,
193
]
the integral in (
6.7
) is meant in the sense of the weak operator topology. However, the arguments below show that the integral actually converges in
\mathscr{K}
in the Bochner’s sense.

Bearing this in mind, let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Applying Lemma
1.12
for
\beta=1-\epsilon
and
\gamma=\alpha
shows there is a bounded operator
\Phi^{(\alpha)}_{\epsilon}=\Phi^{-\alpha}_{1-\epsilon,\alpha}:\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})\rightarrow\mathscr{K}
such that, for all
b\in\mathscr{A}
, we have

\displaystyle[X,b]

\displaystyle=\big[|D|^{-\alpha},b\big]

(6.8)

\displaystyle=|D|^{-\beta}\Phi^{(\alpha)}_{\epsilon}\left([D,b]\right)|D|^{-\alpha}+|D|^{-(1+\alpha)}F[D,b]\Pi_{0}+\Pi_{0}[D,b]F|D|^{-(1+\alpha)},

where
F=D|D|^{-1}
is the sign of
D
and
\Pi_{0}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\ker D
. More precisely, in the notation of (
5.21
) we have

\Phi^{(\alpha)}_{\epsilon}=-c(\alpha)F\Psi^{-\alpha}_{2-\epsilon,\alpha}(T)-c(\alpha)F\Psi^{-\alpha}_{1-\epsilon,\alpha+1}(T)F,\qquad T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}),

where
\Psi^{-\alpha}_{2-\epsilon,\alpha}(T)
and
\Psi^{-\alpha}_{1-\epsilon,\alpha+1}(T)
are DOIs given by (
5.1
). As
|D|^{-1}=X^{1/\alpha}
and
|D|^{-\alpha}=a^{-1/2}Ya^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, we may rewrite the above formula in the form,

(6.9)

\displaystyle[X,b]=X^{-\beta/\alpha}\Phi^{(\alpha)}_{\epsilon}\left([D,b]\right)a^{-1/2}Ya^{-\frac{1}{2}}+X^{1+(1/\alpha)}F[D,b]\Pi_{0}+\Pi_{0}[D,b]FX^{1+(1/\alpha)}.

By using (
6.9
) and the vanishing of
X^{s-1}
on
\operatorname{ran}\Pi_{0}=\ker D
we get

(6.10)

F_{1,s}(t)=X^{(\beta/\alpha)+s-1}\Phi^{(\alpha)}_{\epsilon}\big([D,a^{s-\frac{1}{2}+it}]\big)a^{-\frac{1}{2}}Y+X^{(1/\alpha)+s}F[D,a^{s-\frac{1}{2}+it}]\Pi_{0}a^{\frac{1}{2}}.

It is convenient to rewrite this in the form,

(6.11)

F_{1,s}(t)=X^{(\beta/\alpha)+s-1}G_{1,s}(t)Y+X^{(1/\alpha)+s}H_{1,s}(t),

where we have set

G_{1,s}(t)=\Phi^{(\alpha)}_{\epsilon}\big([D,a^{s-\frac{1}{2}+it}]\big)a^{-\frac{1}{2}},\qquad H_{1,s}(t)=F[D,a^{s-\frac{1}{2}+it}]\Pi_{0}a^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Similarly, we obtain

\displaystyle F_{2,s}(t)=

\displaystyle\ X^{(\beta/\alpha)}\Phi^{(\alpha)}_{\epsilon}\big([D,a^{\frac{1}{2}+it}]\big)a^{-\frac{1}{2}}Y^{s}+X^{(1/\alpha)}F[D,a^{\frac{1}{2}+it}]\Pi_{0}a^{\frac{1}{2}}Y^{s-1}

(6.12)

\displaystyle+\Pi_{0}[D,b]FX^{1+(1/\alpha)}a^{\frac{1}{2}}Y^{s-1}.

Equivalently,

(6.13)

F_{2,s}(t)=\tilde{F}_{2,s}(t)+\Pi_{0}R_{s}(t)Y^{s-1},\qquad\tilde{F}_{2,s}(t):=X^{(\beta/\alpha)}G_{2}(t)Y^{s}+X^{(1/\alpha)}H_{2}(t)Y^{s-1},

where we have set

G_{2}(t)=\Phi^{(\alpha)}_{\epsilon}\big([D,a^{\frac{1}{2}+it}]\big)a^{-\frac{1}{2}},\quad H_{2}(t)=F[D,a^{\frac{1}{2}+it}]\Pi_{0}a^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad R_{s}(t)=[D,a^{\frac{1}{2}+it}]FX^{1+(1/\alpha)}a^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Note that as
X^{it}\Pi_{0}=0
, we have

(6.14)

X^{it}F_{2,s}(t)Y^{-it}=X^{it}\tilde{F}_{2,s}(t)Y^{-it}.

Given any
z\in\mathbb{C}
, Lemma
6.4
implies that that
[D,a^{z+it}]
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, is a family in
C^{(1)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}))
. As
\Pi_{0}
is bounded and has finite-rank, and hence is compact, we deduce that
H_{1,s}(t)
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and
H_{2}(t)
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, are families in
C^{(1)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})
. As
\hat{g}_{s}(t)
is in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
, we further see that

\hat{g}_{s}(t)H_{1,s}(t)
and
\hat{g}_{s}(t)H_{2}(t)
are in
C^{(m)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})
for any
m<-1
. Lemma
6.2
then ensures that

\hat{g}_{s}(t)X^{it}H_{1,s}(t)Y^{-it}
and
\hat{g}_{s}(t)X^{it}H_{2}(t)Y^{-it}
are in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})
. It follows that

\hat{g}_{s}(t)X^{it}X^{(1/\alpha)+s}H_{1,s}(t)Y^{-it}
is in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})
and, using the notation of Lemma
6.2
, we have

(6.15)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{g}_{s}(t)X^{it}\big(X^{(1/\alpha)+s}H_{1,s}(t)\big)Y^{-it}dt=X^{(1/\alpha)+s}\Theta_{X,Y}\big[\hat{g}_{s}H_{1,s}\big].

Likewise,
\hat{g}_{s}(t)X^{it}X^{1+(1/\alpha)}H_{2}(t)Y^{s-1}Y^{-it}
is in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})
, and we have

(6.16)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{g}_{s}(t)X^{it}\big(X^{1+(1/\alpha)}H_{2}(t)Y^{s-1}\big)Y^{-it}dt=X^{1+(1/\alpha)}\Theta_{X,Y}\big[\hat{g}_{s}H_{2}\big]Y^{s-1}.

In addition, as
\Phi^{(\alpha)}_{\epsilon}
is a bounded operator from
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
to
\mathscr{K}
, we see that
\Phi^{(\alpha)}_{\epsilon}([D,a^{z+it}])
is a family in
C^{(1)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})
, and hence
G_{1,s}(t)
and
G_{2}(t)
are families in
C^{(1)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})
. As above this implies that

\hat{g}_{s}(t)X^{it}X^{(\beta/\alpha)+s-1}G_{1,s}(t)YY^{-it}
is in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})
, and we have

(6.17)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{g}_{s}(t)X^{it}\big(X^{(\beta/\alpha)+s-1}G_{1,s}(t)Y\big)Y^{-it}dt=X^{(\beta/\alpha)+s-1}\Theta_{X,Y}\big[\hat{g}_{s}G_{1,s}\big]Y.

Similarly,
\hat{g}_{s}(t)X^{it}X^{\beta/\alpha}G_{2}(t)Y^{s}Y^{-it}
is in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})
, and we have

(6.18)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{g}_{s}(t)X^{it}\big(X^{\beta/\alpha}G_{2}(t)Y^{s}\big)Y^{-it}dt=X^{\beta/\alpha}\Theta_{X,Y}\big[\hat{g}_{s}G_{2}\big]Y^{s}.

Combining (
6.11
) with (
6.15
) and (
6.17
) gives

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{g}_{s}(t)X^{it}F_{1,s}(t)Y^{-it}dt=X^{(\beta/\alpha)+s-1}\Theta_{X,Y}\big[\hat{g}_{s}G_{1,s}\big]Y+X^{(1/\alpha)+s}\Theta_{X,Y}\big[\hat{g}_{s}H_{1,s}\big].

Similarly, by combining (
6.13
)–(
6.14
) with (
6.16
) and (
6.18
) we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{g}_{s}(t)X^{it}F_{2,s}(t)Y^{-it}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{g}_{s}(t)X^{it}\tilde{F}_{s}(t)Y^{-it}dt

\displaystyle=X^{\beta/\alpha}\Theta_{X,Y}\big[\hat{g}_{s}G_{2}\big]Y^{s}+X^{1+(1/\alpha)}\Theta_{X,Y}\big[\hat{g}_{s}H_{2}\big]Y^{s-1}.

As
F_{s}(t)=F_{1,s}(t)+F_{2,s}(t)
, we then obtain

(6.19)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{g}_{s}(t)X^{it}F_{s}(t)Y^{-it}dt=X^{\beta/\alpha}A(s)Y+X^{1+(1/\alpha)}B(s),

where we have set

A(s)=X^{s-1}\Theta_{X,Y}\big[\hat{g}_{s}G_{1,s}\big]+\Theta_{X,Y}\big[\hat{g}_{s}G_{2}\big]Y^{s-1},\quad B(s)=X^{s-1}\Theta_{X,Y}\big[\hat{g}_{s}H_{1,s}\big]+\Theta_{X,Y}\big[\hat{g}_{s}H_{2}\big]Y^{s-1}.

Moreover, setting
t=0
in (
6.11
) and (
6.13
) gives

\displaystyle F_{1,s}(0)=X^{(\beta/\alpha)+s-1}G_{1,s}(0)Y+X^{(1/\alpha)+s}H_{1,s}(0),

\displaystyle F_{2,s}(0)=X^{(\beta/\alpha)}G_{2}(0)Y^{s}+X^{(1/\alpha)}H_{2}(0)Y^{s-1}+\Pi_{0}R_{s}(0)Y^{s-1}.

Thus,

\displaystyle F_{s}(0)=F_{1,s}(0)+F_{2,s}(0)

\displaystyle=X^{\beta/\alpha}\left(X^{s-1}G_{1,s}(0)+G_{2}(0)Y^{s-1}\right)Y

(6.20)

\displaystyle+X^{1+(1/\alpha)}\left(X^{s-1}H_{1,s}(0)+H_{2}(0)Y^{s-1}\right)+\Pi_{0}R_{s}(0)Y^{s-1}.

For
\Re s>1
, let
\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}:C^{(1)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})\rightarrow\mathscr{K}
be the operator defined by

(6.21)

\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}[K]=K(0)-{\Theta}_{X,Y}[\hat{g}_{s}K],\qquad K(t)\in C^{(1)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K}).

Combining (
6.7
) with (
6.19
) and (
6.20
) gives

\displaystyle T(s)

\displaystyle=F(0)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{g}_{s}(t)X^{it}F_{s}(t)Y^{-it}dt

(6.22)

\displaystyle=X^{\beta/\alpha}\tilde{A}(s)Y+X^{1+(1/\alpha)}\tilde{B}(s)+\Pi_{0}R(s),

where we have set

(6.23)

\displaystyle\tilde{A}(s):=X^{s-1}\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}\big[G_{1,s}\big]+\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}\big[G_{2}\big]Y^{s-1},

(6.24)

\displaystyle\tilde{B}(s):=X^{s-1}\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}\big[H_{1,s}\big]+\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}\big[H_{2}\big]Y^{s-1},

(6.25)

\displaystyle R(s):=R_{s}(0)Y^{s-1}=[D,a^{\frac{1}{2}}]FX^{1+(1/\alpha)}a^{\frac{1}{2}}Y^{s-1}.

Set
c=\Re s
. The operators
\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}[G_{1,s}]
and
\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}\big[G_{2}\big]
are both bounded. Likewise,

\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}[H_{1,s}]
and
\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}\big[H_{2}\big]
are bounded operators. Moreover, as
X
and
Y
are operators in
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha^{-1}p,\infty}
, the powers
X^{s-1}
and
Y^{s-1}
are both operators in
\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha-\alpha)^{-1}p,\infty}
. It then follows that
\tilde{A}(s)
and
\tilde{B}(s)
are operators in
\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha-\alpha)^{-1}p,\infty}
.

The fact that
X
is in
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha^{-1}p,\infty}
further implies that
X^{\beta/\alpha}\in\mathscr{L}_{\beta^{-1}p,\infty}
. As

\tilde{A}(s)\in\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha-\alpha)^{-1}p,\infty}
and
Y\in\mathscr{L}_{\alpha^{-1}p,\infty}
, by using Hölder’s inequality (Proposition
2.1
) we then get

(6.26)

X^{\beta/\alpha}\tilde{A}(s)Y\in\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha+\beta)^{-1}p,\infty}.

Similarly, the operator
X^{1+(1/\alpha)}
is in
\mathscr{L}_{(\alpha+1)^{-1}p,\infty}
. As
\tilde{B}(s)\in\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha-\alpha)^{-1}p,\infty}
, by using Hölder’s inequality we get

(6.27)

X^{1+(1/\alpha)}\tilde{B}(s)\in\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha+1)^{-1}p,\infty}\subseteq\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha+\beta)^{-1}p,\infty}.

In addition, the operator
\Pi_{0}R(s)
is a finite-rank bounded operator, and hence is contained in
\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha+\beta)^{-1}p,\infty}
. Combining this with (
6.22
) and
(
6.26
)–(
6.27
) we then see that

T(s)\in\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha+\beta)^{-1}p,\infty}.

As
\beta=1-\epsilon
this gives (
6.6
). This completes the proof of Lemma
1.10
.
\Box

Remark 6.7
.

Eq. (
6.10
) and Eq. (
6.12
) are two of the main steps in the proof of Lemma
1.10
above. For getting these results it was crucial to be able to factor out
|D|^{-\alpha}
in the factorization (
6.8
) for
[|D|^{-\alpha},b]
.

6.3.
Holomorphic version of Lemma
1.10

Prior to proving Lemma
1.10
, we shall explain that an elaboration of the arguments of the proof of Lemma
1.10
leads to the following holomorphic version of that result. Namely, in this section we shall prove the following result.

Lemma 6.8
.

If we set
c:=\max(1,\alpha^{-1}(q^{-1}p-1))
, then

T(s)\in\operatorname{Hol}\left(\Re s>c;\mathscr{L}_{q}\right).

Prior to proving Lemma
6.8
we will need the following lemma.

Lemma 6.9
.

The following holds.

(i)

\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}[G_{1,s}]
,
\Re s>1
, and
\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}[G_{2}]
,
\Re s>1
, are
holomorphic families of compact operators.

(ii)

The same holds for the families
\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}[H_{1,s}]
,
\Re s>1
, and
\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}[H_{2}]
,
\Re s>1
,

Proof.

Suppose that
K_{s}
,
\Re s>1
, is a holomorphic family in
C^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})
. We know from Lemma
6.5
and Remark
6.6
that

\hat{g}_{s}(t)
,
\Re s>1
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
.
Therefore,
\hat{g}_{s}K_{s}
,
\Re s>1
, is a holomorphic family in
C^{(m)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})
for any
m<-1
.
As Lemma
6.2
asserts that
\Theta_{X,Y}:C^{(m)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})\rightarrow\mathscr{K}
is a continuous linear operator, we see that
