\Theta_{X,Y}[\hat{g}_{s}K_{s}]
,
\Re s>1
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{K}
.
Clearly,
K_{s}(0)
,
\Re s>1
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{K}
, and so in view of (
6.21
) we see that
\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}[K_{s}]
,
\Re s>1
, is a holomorphic family of compact operators.

Applying the above observation to
K_{s}=G_{2}
and
K_{s}=H_{2}
shows that
\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}[G_{2}]
,
\Re s>1
, and
\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}[H_{2}]
,
\Re s>1
, are holomorphic families of compact operators. Moreover, in order to prove the claim for
\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}[G_{1,s}]
and
\tilde{\Theta}_{X,Y}^{(s)}[H_{1,s}]
it is enough to show that
G_{1,s}
,
\Re s>1
, and
H_{1,s}
,
\Re s>1
, are holomorphic families in
C^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})
.

Set
\Omega=\{\Re s>1\}
. Lemma
6.4
asserts that
[D,a^{z}]
,
z\in\mathbb{C}
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
, and, for any (closed) vertical strip
\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{C}
, there is
C_{\Sigma}(a)>0
such that

\big\|[D,a^{z}]\big\|\leq C_{\Sigma}(a)\left(1+|\Im z|\right)\qquad\forall z\in\Sigma.

Therefore, if we set
V_{s}(t)=[D,a^{s-1/2+it}]
,
\Re s>1
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, then
V_{s}(t)
,
\Re s>1
, is a holomorphic family in
C(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}))
, and, for any compact
L\subseteq\Omega
, there is
C_{L}(a)>0
such that

\big\|V_{s}(t)\big\|\leq C_{L}(a)(1+|t|)\qquad\forall(s,t)\in L\times\mathbb{R}.

This ensures that
V_{s}(t)
,
\Re s>1
, is a holomorphic family in
C^{(1)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}))
.

By definition
H_{1,s}(t)=FV_{s}(t)\Pi_{0}a^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Here
\Pi_{0}
is a bounded finite-rank operator, and hence is compact, and so we see that
H_{1,s}
,
\Re s>1
, is a holomorphic family in
C^{(1)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})
. Moreover, as

\Phi_{\epsilon}^{(\alpha)}
is a continuous linear operator from
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H})
to
\mathscr{K}
and
G_{1,s}=\Phi^{(\alpha)}_{\epsilon}[V_{s}(t)]a^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, we see that

G_{1,s}
,
\Re s>1
, is a holomorphic families in
C^{(1)}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{K})
as well. This completes the proof.
∎

We are now in a position to prove Lemma
6.8

Proof of Lemma
6.8
.

Let
c\geq 1
. As
X
is a positive operator in
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha^{-1}p,\infty}
, Lemma
4.1
ensures that
X^{z}
,
\Re z>c-1
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha-\alpha)^{-1}p,\infty}
, i.e.,
X^{s-1}
,
\Re s>c
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha-\alpha)^{-1}p,\infty}
.
Likewise,
Y^{s-1}
,
\Re s>c
, is holomorphic family in
\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha-\alpha)^{-1}p,\infty}
. Combining this with (
6.23
)–(
6.24
) and the claim above shows that

(6.28)

\tilde{A}(s)\in\operatorname{Hol}\left(\Re s>c;\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha-\alpha)^{-1}p,\infty}\right)\quad\textup{and}\quad\tilde{B}(s)\in\operatorname{Hol}\left(\Re s>c;\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha-\alpha)^{-1}p,\infty}\right).

If
r_{1}^{-1}+r_{2}^{-1}=r^{-1}
, then Hölder’s inequality implies we have a continuous bilinear map from
\mathscr{L}_{r_{1},\infty}\times\mathscr{L}_{r_{2},\infty}
to
\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty}
. Thus, if
Z_{1}\in\mathscr{L}_{r_{1},\infty}
and
Z_{2}(s)
,
\Re s>c
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{L}_{r_{2},\infty}
, then
Z_{1}Z_{2}(s)
is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{L}_{r,\infty}
. Using this remark and the fact that
X^{\beta/\alpha}\in\mathscr{L}_{\beta^{-1}p,\infty}
and
Y\in\mathscr{L}_{\alpha^{-1}p,\infty}
together with (
6.28
) shows that

(6.29)

X^{\beta/\alpha}\tilde{A}(s)Y\in\operatorname{Hol}\left(\Re s>c;\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha+\beta)^{-1}p,\infty}\right).

Similarly, as
X^{1+(1/\alpha)}\in\mathscr{L}_{(\alpha+1)^{-1}p,\infty}
we see that

(6.30)

X^{1+(1/\alpha)}\tilde{B}(s)\in\operatorname{Hol}\left(\Re s>c;\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha+1)^{-1}p,\infty}\right).

Likewise,
R(s)=[D,a^{\frac{1}{2}}]FX^{1+(1/\alpha)}a^{\frac{1}{2}}Y^{s-1}
,
\Re s>c
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha+1)^{-1},p}
, and hence

(6.31)

\Pi_{0}R(s)\in\operatorname{Hol}\left(\Re s>c;\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha+1)^{-1}p,\infty}\right).

Combining (
6.22
) with (
6.29
)–(
6.31
), and recalling that
\beta=1-\epsilon
, we then obtain

T(s)\in\operatorname{Hol}\left(\Re s>c;\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha+1-\epsilon)^{-1}p}\right)\qquad\forall\epsilon\in(0,1).

Let
q>0
, and set
c=\max(1,\alpha^{-1}(q^{-1}p-1))
. The inequality
\alpha^{-1}(q^{-1}p-1)\leq c
ensures that
(c\alpha+1)^{-1}p\leq q
. Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Substituting
c+2\epsilon\alpha^{-1}
for
c
shows that

T(s)
,
\Re s>c+2\epsilon\alpha^{-1}
, is a holomorphic family in
\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha+1+\epsilon)^{-1}p,\infty}
. As
(c\alpha+1+\epsilon)^{-1}p<(c\alpha+1)^{-1}p\leq q
, we have a continuous inclusion of
\mathscr{L}_{(c\alpha+1+\epsilon)^{-1}p,\infty}
into

\mathscr{L}_{q}
. This allows us to regard
T(s)
,
\Re s>c+2\epsilon\alpha^{-1}
, as a holomorphic family in

\mathscr{L}_{q}
. As this is true for all
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, we deduce that

T(s)\in\operatorname{Hol}\left(\Re s>c;\mathscr{L}_{q}\right).

This gives proves Lemma
1.10
.
∎

6.4.
Proof of Lemma
1.11

Let
a\in\mathscr{A}_{++}
. It follows from Lemma
4.1
that
(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{s}
and
|D|^{-\alpha s}a^{s}
are holomorphic families of trace-class operators for
\Re s>\alpha^{-1}p
. Therefore, the zeta functions
\operatorname{Tr}[(a^{1/2}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{1/2})^{s}]
and
\operatorname{Tr}[a^{s}|D|^{-\alpha s}]
are holomorphic functions on the half-plane
\Re s>\alpha^{-1}p
.

In the special case
q=1
Lemma
1.10
shows that, if we set
c=\max(1,\alpha^{-1}(p-1))
, then we have

(6.32)

\left(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{s}-|D|^{-\alpha s}a^{s}\in\operatorname{Hol}\left(\Re s>c;\mathscr{L}_{1}\right).

Assume now that
\delta:=\min(1,p-\alpha)>0
. We then have

c=\alpha^{-1}p+\max\left(1-\alpha^{-1}p,-\alpha^{-1}\right)=\alpha^{-1}p-\alpha^{-1}\min\left(p-\alpha,1\right)=\alpha^{-1}(p-\delta).

Thus,

(6.33)

\left(a^{\frac{1}{2}}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{s}-|D|^{-\alpha s}a^{s}\in\operatorname{Hol}\left(\Re s>\alpha^{-1}(p-\delta);\mathscr{L}_{1}\right).

It then follows that the function
s\rightarrow\operatorname{Tr}[(a^{1/2}|D|^{-\alpha}a^{1/2})^{s}]-\operatorname{Tr}[a^{s}|D|^{-\alpha s}]
is holomorphic on the half-plane
\Re s>\alpha^{-1}(p-\delta)
. This completes the proof of Lemma
1.11
.
\Box

Chapter 7
Boundary Value Problems on Bounded Euclidean Domains

In this chapter, we explain how the main results of this monograph enable us to recover well known spectral asymptotics and semiclassical Weyl laws for Laplacians on bounded domains in Euclidean space. Incidentally, we shall also obtain integrations, which seem to be new in this setting.

The semiclassical Weyl laws mentioned above were essentially established in the 60s and 70s. The only novelty here (if any) is that the spectral theoretic considerations of this monograph enables us to recover these results from well-known results of Carleman
[
41
]
and Minakshisundaram
[
146
]
that appeared the 30s and 40s.

Throughout this chapter we let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
n\geq 2
, with smooth boundary
\partial\Omega
. We also let
\Delta_{\Omega}
be either the
Dirichlet Laplacian
or
Neumann Laplacian
on
\Omega
. We get an essentially selfadjoint strongly elliptic 2nd order differential operator with non-negative and purely discrete spectrum.

To get a spectral triple we consider the
*
-algebra,

\dot{C}^{\infty}(\Omega):=\mathbb{C}1+C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega).

That is,
\dot{C}^{\infty}(\Omega)
is the algebra of
C^{\infty}
-functions on
\Omega
that are constant near the boundary
\partial\Omega
. This is a
*
-subalgebra of the
C^{*}
-algebra of bounded continuous functions on
\Omega
, and so it is represented in
L^{2}(\Omega)
by bounded multiplication operators. Its norm closure is the
C^{*}
-algebra,

\dot{C}(\Omega):=\mathbb{C}+C_{0}(\Omega),

where
C_{0}(\Omega)
is the (closed) ideal of continuous functions on
\Omega
that converges to
0
near the boundary
\partial\Omega
. In other words,
\dot{C}(\Omega)
is the space of continuous functions on
\overline{\Omega}
that are constant on the boundary
\partial\Omega
.

We define the square-root Laplacian
\Delta_{\Omega}^{1/2}
by Borel functional calculus. As pointed out to the authors by Rozenblum
[
167
]
results of Seeley
[
175
,
177
]

and Grubb
[
95
, §4.4]
) show that

\Delta_{\Omega}^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\big(\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}+G,

where
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{-1/2}
is the inverse square-root Laplacian on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(i.e., the multiplication by
|\xi|^{-1}
in the Fourier variable) and

G:C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
is a generalized singular Green operator in the sense of
[
95
]
. In particular, the singular support of the Schwartz kernel of
G
is contained in
\partial\Omega\times\partial\Omega
. This ensures that if
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
, then
G\psi
and
\psi G
both have smooth Schwartz kernels.

If
u\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
satisfies the Dirichlet or Neumann condition, then we have

\big(\sqrt{\Delta_{\Omega}}\big)u=\big(\Delta_{\Omega}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)\Delta u=\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\big(\sqrt{\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\big)\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}u+G\Delta u.

Here
\sqrt{\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}
is the multiplication by
|\xi|
in the Fourier variable. Therefore, if
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
and
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
is such that
\chi=1
near
\operatorname{supp}\psi
, then we have

\left[\sqrt{\Delta_{\Omega}},\psi\right]=\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\left[\sqrt{\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}},\psi\right]\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}+(G\chi)\Delta\psi-(\psi G)\Delta.

The operator
\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}[\sqrt{\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}},\psi]\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\Omega)
, since
[\sqrt{\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}},\psi]
maps continuously

L^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
to
L^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Moreover, as

(G\chi)\Delta\psi
and
(\psi G)\Delta
are given by Schwartz kernels that are smooth on
\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{\Omega}
, they both give rise to bounded operators on
L^{2}(\Omega)
. This ensures that the commutator
[\sqrt{\Delta_{\Omega}},\psi]
is bounded. It then follows that we have a spectral triple,

(7.1)

\left(\dot{C}^{\infty}(\Omega),L^{2}(\Omega),\sqrt{\Delta_{\Omega}}\right).

Bearing this in mind, the fact that Condition (Z) is satisfied can be traced back to well known results of Carleman
[
41
]
and Minakshisundaram
[
146
]
from the 30s and 40s (see also
[
176
]
). Namely, let
(\xi_{j})_{j\geq 0}\subseteq C(\overline{\Omega})
be an orthonormal family such that
\Delta_{\Omega}\xi_{j}=\lambda_{j}(\Delta_{\Omega})\xi_{j}
for all
j\geq 0
. The series

(7.2)

\rho_{s}(x):=\sum_{j\geq 0}\lambda_{j}\left(\Delta_{\Omega}\right)^{-\frac{s}{2}}\left|\xi_{j}(x)\right|^{2},\qquad\Re s>n,

converges uniformly on
\overline{\Omega}
and has a meromorphic extension to
\mathbb{C}
(as a
C(\overline{\Omega})
-valued map)
with at worst simple poles on
n-\mathbb{N}_{0}
. At
s=n
the residue is given by

\operatorname{Res}_{s=n}\rho_{s}(x)=(4\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\frac{2}{\Gamma(n/2)}=nc(n),\qquad c(n):=(2\pi)^{-n}|\mathbb{B}^{n}|.

Given any
f\in\dot{C}(\Omega)
, we have

(7.3)

\operatorname{Tr}\big[f\Delta_{\Omega}^{-\frac{s}{2}}\big]=\sum_{j\geq 0}\big\langle f\Delta_{\Omega}^{-\frac{s}{2}}\xi_{j}|\xi_{j}\big\rangle=\sum_{j\geq 0}\lambda_{j}\left(\Delta_{\Omega}\right)^{-\frac{s}{2}}\left\langle f\xi_{j}|\xi_{j}\right\rangle=\int_{\Omega}f(x)\rho_{s}(x)dx.

Therefore, we see that the function

\operatorname{Tr}\big[f\Delta_{\Omega}^{-\frac{s}{2}}\big]-nc(n)(s-n)^{-1}\int_{\Omega}f(x)dx,\qquad\Re s>n,

has a continuous extension to the closed half-plane
\Re s\geq n
. This shows that Condition (Z) and Condition (W) hold with

\tau(f)=c(n)\int_{\Omega}f(x)dx,\qquad f\in\dot{C}^{\infty}(\Omega).

Note that the approach of
[
146
]
is based on the heat equation, and so it also yields Condition (H).

Alternatively, if
0<f\in\dot{C}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, then
f\Delta_{\Omega}f
is a 2nd order elliptic differential operator whose principal symbol is
f(x)^{2}|\xi|^{2}
. It agrees up to a positive constant multiple with
\Delta_{\Omega}
outside a compact subset of
\Omega
. Therefore, the results of
[
95
, §4.5]
apply, and so we directly get the Weyl law,

\lambda_{j}\big(f\Delta_{\Omega}f\big)\sim j^{\frac{2}{n}}\left(c(n)\int_{\Omega}f(x)^{-n}dx\right)^{-\frac{2}{n}}\qquad\text{as $j\rightarrow\infty$}.

This also gives Condition (W).

In addition, Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
) holds as follows:

(i)

For
r>1
with
\mathscr{V}_{r}=L_{r}(\Omega)
(see
[
163
,
164
]
).

(ii)

For
r<1
with
\mathscr{V}_{r}=L^{1}(\Omega)
(see
[
24
]
).

(iii)

For
r=1
with
\mathscr{V}_{1}=L\!\log\!L(\Omega)
if
n
is even (see
[
168
,
185
,
186
]
) and
\mathscr{V}_{1}=L_{r^{\prime}}(\Omega)
for any
r^{\prime}>1
if
n
is odd (see
[
24
]
).

Here
L\!\log\!L(\Omega)
is the space of
L\!\log\!L
-Orlicz functions on
\Omega
, i.e., measurable functions
f(x)
such that

\int_{\Omega}|f(x)|\log(1+|f(x)|)dx<\infty.

In particular,
L^{r^{\prime}}(\Omega)\subsetneq L\!\log\!L(\Omega)\subsetneq L^{1}(\Omega)
for all
r^{\prime}>1
.

For the spectral triple (
7.1
) the spectral asymptotics (
1.9
)–(
1.10
) and the semiclassical Weyl law (
1.11
) are well known
(see
[
21
,
24
,
163
,
164
,
168
,
186
]
). The only bit of novelty here is the observation that these results can be deduced from the results of Carleman and Minakshisundaram mentioned above. In particular, we have the following semiclassical Weyl law.

Proposition 7.1

(
[
21
,
24
,
163
,
164
,
168
,
186
]
)
.

Assume we are in one the following situations:

(i)

q\neq n/2
and
V(x)
is a real-valued potential in
L^{r}(\Omega)
with
r=\max(n(2q)^{-1},1)
.

(ii)

q=n/2
and
V(x)
is a real-valued potential in
L\!\log\!L(\Omega)
(
n
even) or is in
L_{r^{\prime}}(\Omega)
with
r^{\prime}>1
(
n
odd).

Then, for any energy level
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{n}N\left(h^{2q}\Delta_{\Omega}^{q}+V;\lambda\right)=c(n)\int_{\Omega}(V(x)-\lambda)_{-}^{\frac{n}{2q}}dx.

In this setting the integration formulas (
1.12
)–(
1.13
) do not seem to have been mentioned elsewhere, and so, in this sense, there are new.

Theorem 7.2
.

Assume that, either
n
is even and
f\in L\!\log\!L(\Omega)
, or
n
is odd and
f\in L_{r^{\prime}}(\Omega)
with
r^{\prime}>1
. Then, the operators

\Delta_{\Omega}^{-n/4}f\Delta_{\Omega}^{-n/4}
and
|\Delta_{\Omega}^{-n/4}f\Delta_{\Omega}^{-n/4}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\Delta_{\Omega}^{-\frac{n}{4}}f\Delta_{\Omega}^{-\frac{n}{4}}=c(n)\int_{\Omega}f(x)dx,\qquad\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\left|\Delta_{\Omega}^{-\frac{n}{4}}f\Delta_{\Omega}^{-\frac{n}{4}}\right|=c(n)\int_{\Omega}|f(x)|dx.

Remark 7.3
.

The results of this chapter should hold for more general local boundary conditions. At least for continuous potentials, they should hold on compact manifolds with boundary conditions as those considered in
[
95
]
, since in this case the above mentioned results of
[
95
, §4.4–5]
apply. This includes the spectral triple of Levy-Iochum
[
107
]
, which is using the Dirac operator under a (local) chiral boundary condition (see also
[
12
]
for a generalization for this construction).

Chapter 8
Closed Riemannian Manifolds

The goal of this chapter two-fold. In Section
8.1
, we explain how the main results of this monograph enable us to recover various known results regarding semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas on closed Riemannian manifolds. We work in a general setup with spectral triples associated with algebras of sections of endomorphism bundles and 1st order
\Psi
DOs whose squares are “generalized Laplacians”. In particular, in the example of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator this allows us to recover Weyl laws for Steklov eigenvalue problems (see Section
8.2
).

These results are not new. As in the previous chapter, the main aim is to show that these well-known results can be recovered by combining the main results of this monograph with old results of Minakshisundaram-Pleijel
[
147
]
, which predate the aforementioned results.

In Section
8.3
, we shall show that by using algebras of zeroth order
\Psi
DOs we still get spectral triples satisfying Condition (W). This leads to a new proof of the celebrated Weyl laws for negative order
\Psi
DOs of Birman-Solomyak
[
26
,
27
,
28
]
. In fact, we will obtain these results for suitable completions of the spaces of
\Psi
DOs. This implies a far-reaching extension of Connes’ trace theorem to this class of operators. In the scalar case this turns to be equivalent to a stronger form for the integration formula of Kordyukov-Sukochev-Zanin
[
113
]
. The
C^{*}
-extension of compact operators provided by the principal symbol map also plays a crucial role in this approach.

Finally, for sake of completeness, we explain in an appendix how an elaboration of the original arguments of Connes
[
52
]
enables us to get weaker version of our integration formula. In the scalar case this is actually enough to recover the integration formula of
[
113
]
.

Throughout this chapter, we let
(M^{n},g)
,
n\geq 1
, be a closed Riemannian manifold, and let
E
be a Hermitian vector bundle over
M
.

8.1.
Semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas

Let
D_{E}:C^{\infty}(M,E)\rightarrow C^{\infty}(M,E)
be a selfadjoint first order elliptic
\Psi
DO such that
(D_{E})^{2}
has same principal symbol as the Laplacian, i.e.,

(8.1)

\sigma_{2}\left((D_{E})^{2}\right)(x,\xi)=|\xi|_{g}^{2}\operatorname{id}_{E_{x}},\qquad(x,\xi)\in T^{*}M\setminus 0.

We then get an
n
-summable spectral triple,

(8.2)

\left(C^{\infty}\left(M,\operatorname{End}(E)\right),L^{2}(M,E),D_{E}\right).

It is more usual to use the algebra
C^{\infty}(M)
rather than
C^{\infty}(M,\operatorname{End}(E))
. However, using the latter allows us to consider Schrödinger operators with matrix-valued potentials (see below).

This set-up encapsulates the following examples:

(i)

M
is spin,
E=\not{S}
is the spinor bundle, and
D_{E}=\not{D}_{g}
is the Dirac operator.

(ii)

E
is the trivial line bundle, and
D_{E}=\sqrt{\Delta_{g}}
is the square-root Laplacian.

(iii)

\nabla^{E}
is a Hermitian connection on
E
and
D_{E}=\sqrt{(\nabla^{E})^{*}\nabla^{E}}
is the square root of the connection Laplacian
(\nabla^{E})^{*}\nabla^{E}
.

(iv)

M=\partial X
is the boundary of a compact manifold
\overline{X}
,
E
is the trivial line bundle, and
D_{E}=\Lambda_{g}
is the Dirichlet-to-Neuman operator (see
[
119
,
198
]
).

At least in the case of the square-root Laplacian spectral triple
(C^{\infty}(M),L^{2}(M),\sqrt{\Delta_{g}})
, the fact that Condition (Z) holds can be traced back to well known results of Minakshisundaram-Pleijel
[
147
]
from the 40s (see also
[
174
,
179
]
). The results also hold for the square root
\sqrt{\Delta_{E}}
of the connection Laplacian
\Delta_{E}:=(\nabla^{E})^{*}\nabla^{E}
associated with any Hermitian connection
\nabla^{E}
on
E
. More precisely, let
(\xi_{j})\subseteq C^{\infty}(M,E)
be an orthonormal family such that
\Delta_{E}\xi_{j}=\lambda_{j}(\Delta_{E})\xi_{j}
for all
j\geq 0
. As with the series (
7.2
), for
\Re s>n
, the series

\rho_{s}(x)=\sum_{j\geq 0}\lambda_{j}\!\left(\Delta_{E}\right)^{-\frac{s}{2}}\left\langle\ \cdot\ |\xi_{j}(x)\right\rangle_{\!x}\xi_{j}(x)\in\operatorname{End}(E_{x}),\qquad x\in M,

converges uniformly on
M
and has a meromorphic extension to
\mathbb{C}
(as an
C(M,\operatorname{End}(E))
-valued family) with at worst simple pole singularities on
-n+\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Here
\left\langle\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,\right\rangle_{x}
is the Hermitian inner product on
E_{x}
. At
s=n
the residue is given by

\operatorname{Res}_{s=n}\rho_{s}(x)=nc(n)\mathrm{id}_{E_{x}},\qquad c(n):=(2\pi)^{-n}|\mathbb{B}^{n}|.

Denote by
\nu_{g}(x)
the Riemannian measure. As in (
7.3
), given any
u\in C(M,\operatorname{End}(E))
, we have

\operatorname{Tr}\big[u\Delta_{E}^{-\frac{s}{2}}\big]=\sum_{j\geq 0}\lambda_{j}\left(\Delta_{E}\right)^{-\frac{s}{2}}\left\langle u\xi_{j}|\xi_{j}\right\rangle=\int_{M}\operatorname{tr}_{E_{x}}\left[u(x)\rho_{s}(x)\right]d\nu_{g}(x).

Therefore, we see that the function

\operatorname{Tr}\big[u\Delta_{E}^{-\frac{s}{2}}\big]-nc(n)(s-n)\int_{M}\operatorname{tr}_{E_{x}}\left[u(x)\right]d\nu_{g}(x),\quad\Re s>n,

has a continuous extension to the closed half-plane
\Re s\geq n
. This shows that the spectral triple
(C^{\infty}(M),L^{2}(M,E),\sqrt{\Delta_{E}})
satisfies Condition (Z), and hence Condition (W) holds, with

\tau(u)=c(n)\int_{M}\operatorname{tr}_{E_{x}}\left[u(x)\right]d\nu_{g}(x),\qquad u\in C\left(M,\operatorname{End}(E)\right).

This also gives the result for the spectral triple
(C^{\infty}(M),L^{2}(M,E),D_{E})
. Indeed, as
D_{E}
and
\sqrt{\Delta_{E}}
are 1st order
\Psi
DOs that have same principal symbol, their difference is a zeroth order
\Psi
DO, and hence is bounded. As Condition (W) is invariant under bounded perturbations (
cf
. Lemma
3.10
), we deduce that
(C^{\infty}(M),L^{2}(M,E),D_{E})
too satisfies Condition (W) with the same functional
\tau
.

Alternatively, Condition (W) can be checked directly by using the Wey’s law for positive elliptic
\Psi
DOs, since
u(D_{E})^{2}u
is positive elliptic 2nd order
\Psi
DO with principal symbol
\sigma_{2}(x,\xi)=|\xi|_{g}^{2}u(x)^{2}
(see, e.g.,
[
70
,
98
]
).

As with bounded domains, Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
) is a consequence of several Cwikel-type estimates. More precisely, it holds as follows:

(i)

For
r>1
with
\mathscr{V}_{r}=L^{r}(M,E)
(see
[
64
]
).

(ii)

For
r<1
with
\mathscr{V}_{r}=L^{1}(M,E)
(see
[
25
]
).

(iii)

For
r=1
with
\mathscr{V}_{1}=L\!\log\!L(M,E)
(see
[
185
,
186
,
191
]
; see also
[
156
,
165
,
168
]
).

Here
L\!\log\!L(M,E)
is the space of
L\!\log\!L
-Orlicz sections of
E
, i.e., measurable sections
u(x)
such that

\int_{M}\|u(x)\|_{E_{x}}\log\left(1+\|u(x)\|_{E_{x}}\right)d\nu_{g}(x)<\infty.

Applying Proposition
1.1
yields the following spectral asymptotics.

Proposition 8.1
.

Assume that
0<q\neq n
and
u(x)^{*}\in L^{r}(M,E)
with
r=\max(nq^{-1},1)
, or
q=n
and
u(x)\in L\!\log\!L(M,E)
. Then, we have

(8.3)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{n}}\mu_{j}\big(|D_{E}|^{-\frac{q}{2}}u|D_{E}|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\left(c(n)\int_{M}\operatorname{tr}_{E_{x}}\big[|u(x)|^{\frac{n}{q}}\big]d\nu_{g}(x)\right)^{\frac{q}{n}},

(8.4)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{n}}\lambda_{j}^{\pm}\big(|D_{E}|^{-\frac{q}{2}}u|D_{E}|^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\left(c(n)\int_{M}\operatorname{tr}_{E_{x}}\big[u_{\pm}(x)^{\frac{n}{q}}\big]d\nu_{g}(x)\right)^{\frac{q}{n}}\quad(\text{if $u(x)^{*}=u(x)$}).

Remark 8.2
.

The above spectral asymptotics are not new in the sense that, for smooth sections
u(x)
they are mere special cases of the Weyl laws for negative order
\Psi
DOs of Birman-Solomyak
[
26
,
27
,
28
]
) (see also Theorem
8.13
below). For
q\neq n
, their extensions to sections in
L^{r}(M,E)
then follows from Birman-Solomyak’s perturbation theory (i.e., Proposition
2.4
) and the Cwikel-type estimates of
[
25
,
64
]
mentioned above. For
q=n
the extensions of the spectral asymptotics to sections in
L\!\log\!L(M,E)
is a consequence of the Cwikel estimates of
[
185
,
186
,
191
]
. In the scalar case the spectral asymptotics (
8.4
) for
q=n
are proved in
[
165
,
191
]
. Both sets of spectral asymptotics (
8.3
)–(
8.4
) for
q=n
are extended to
L\!\log\!L(M,E)
in the bundle setting in
[
156
]
.

Applying Theorem
1.2
enables us to recover the following semiclassical Weyl laws.

Proposition 8.3

(
[
21
,
163
,
164
,
165
,
186
]
)
.

Assume that
q\neq n/2
and
V(x)=V(x)^{*}\in L^{r}(M,E)
with
r=\max(n(2q)^{-1},1)
, or
q=n/2
and
V(x)=V(x)^{*}\in L\!\log\!L(M,E)
. Then, for any energy level

\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

(8.5)

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{n}N\left(h^{2q}|D_{E}|^{2q}+V;\lambda\right)=c(n)\int_{M}\operatorname{tr}_{E_{x}}\big[\left(V(x)-\lambda\right)_{-}^{\frac{n}{2q}}\big]d\nu_{g}(x).

Remark 8.4
.

For
q\neq n/2
, the semiclassical Weyl law (
8.5
) goes back to
[
21
,
163
,
164
]
(see also
[
64
,
180
]
). In those references the authors consider operators on Euclidean spaces, but the results extend to closed manifolds. In the critical case
q=n/2
this is a more recent result
[
165
,
186
]
(see also
[
156
,
168
]
).

Remark 8.5
.

For
q=1
, with
D_{E}=\sqrt{\Delta_{g}}
and smooth scalar potentials
V(x)
, it seems like the earliest available proof of the semiclassical Weyl law (
8.5
) on closed manifolds is due to Colin de Verdière
[
47
, Théorème 4.4]
(see also, e.g.,
[
99
, Chapter 10]
and
[
204
, Theorem 14.11]
for more recent proofs). Note that thanks to the Birman-Schwinger principle this asymptotic can also deduced from the Weyl laws for negative order
\Psi
DOs on closed manifolds of Birman-Solomyak
[
26
,
27
,
28
]
. However, the approach in
[
47
]
actually provides a more precise asymptotic with a lower order reminder term.

Finally, by applying Theorem
1.3
we get the following extension of Connes’ integration formula (
2.9
).

Proposition 8.6

(
[
156
,
165
]
; see also
[
192
]
)
.

For every section
u\in L\!\log\!L(M,E)
, the operators
|D_{E}|^{-n/2}u|D_{E}|^{-n/2}
and

||D_{E}|^{-n/2}u|D_{E}|^{-n/2}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

(8.6)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int|D_{E}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}u|D_{E}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}=c(n)\int_{M}\operatorname{tr}_{E_{x}}[u(x)]d\nu_{g}(x),

(8.7)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\left||D_{E}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}u|D_{E}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}\right|=c(n)\int_{M}\operatorname{tr}_{E_{x}}\left[|u(x)|\right]d\nu_{g}(x).

Remark 8.7
.

The integration formula (
8.6
) in the form stated above for
L\!\log\!L
-functions was established by Rozenblum
[
165
]
. The integration formulas (
8.6
)–(
8.7
) in the bundle case appeared in
[
156
]
. In the scalar case with
D_{E}=(1+\Delta_{g})^{-1/2}
the integration formula (
8.6
) for
L\!\log\!L
-functions is not stated explicitly in
[
192
]
, but it is implied that we have such a formula.
as a consequence of the version of the spectral asymptotics (
8.4
) established there.

Remark 8.8
.

The integration formula (
8.6
) need not hold for sections in
L^{1}(M,E)
(see
[
108
]
).

Remark 8.9
.

The spectral asymptotics (
8.3
)–(
8.4
) in the critical case
q=n/2
and the integration formulas (
8.6
)–(
8.7
) for
L\!\log\!L
-potentials are special cases of more general results for operators of the form
Q^{*}uP
, where where
P
and
Q
are
\Psi
DOs of order
-n/2
(see
[
156
,
165
,
168
]
). These results actually hold for an even larger class of potentials of the form
u=v\mu
where
\mu
is an Alfhors-regular measure supported on a regular submanifold
\Sigma\subseteq M
and

v
is an
L\!\log\!L
-section over
\Sigma
(see
[
165
,
168
]
).

8.2.
Steklov eigenvalues

The example of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator is of special interest due to its relationship with Steklov eigenvalue problems (see
[
46
,
86
]
for recent surveys).

Suppose that
M
is the boundary of a compact manifold
(\overline{X}^{n+1},\overline{g})
with interior
X
such that
\overline{g}_{|TM}=g
on
M
. Let
\gamma(x)
be a real-valued function in
L^{n}(M)
(if
n\geq 2
) or in
L\!\log\!L(M)
(if
n=1
). The associated weighted Steklov problem is the eigenvalue problem,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Delta_{\overline{g}}u=0&\text{on $X$},\\
\partial_{\nu}u=\sigma\gamma u&\text{on $M$},\end{array}\right.

where
\partial_{\nu}
is the outward normal derivative. For
\gamma=1
this is the ordinary Steklov problem. In terms of the Dirichlet-to-Neuman operator
\Lambda_{g}
this is equivalent to the eigenvalue problem,

\Lambda_{g}v=\sigma\gamma v\quad\text{on $M$}.

For
\sigma\neq 0
, this is further equivalent to

w=\sigma\Lambda_{g}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\gamma\Lambda_{g}^{-\frac{1}{2}}w,\qquad\int_{M}w(x)d\nu_{g}(x)=0.

Thus, if we let
\sigma_{j}^{\pm}(\gamma)
,
j\geq 0
, be the positive/negative Steklov eigenvalues, then we have

\sigma_{j}^{\pm}(\gamma)=\lambda_{j}^{\pm}\big(\Lambda_{g}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\gamma\Lambda_{g}^{-\frac{1}{2}}),\qquad j\geq 0.

It immediately follows that the Weyl law (
8.4
) for
\Lambda_{g}^{-1/2}\gamma\Lambda_{g}^{-1/2}
yields a Weyl law for the Steklov eigenvalues

\sigma_{j}^{\pm}(M,\gamma)
. More precisely, we have the following result.

Proposition 8.10

(
[
2
,
165
,
194
]
)
.

For any real-valued weight
\gamma(x)
in
L^{n}(M)
(if
n\geq 2
) or in
L\!\log\!L(M)
(if
n=1
), we have

(8.8)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{-\frac{1}{n}}\sigma_{j}^{\pm}(\gamma)=\left(c(n)\int_{M}\gamma_{\pm}(x)^{n}d\nu_{g}(x)\right)^{\frac{1}{n}}.

Remark 8.11
.

For
n\geq 2
the above result goes back to Suslina
[
194
]
and Agranovich
[
2
]
. For
n=1
it was established by Rozenblum
[
165
]
. It is shown in
[
109
]
that this implies a Weyl law for ordinary Steklov eigenvalues on surfaces with Lipschitz boundaries. This was extended to higher dimension by Rozenblum
[
166
]
.

The integration formula (
8.6
) enables us to reformulate the Steklov Weyl law (
8.8
) in terms of the NC integral. Namely, we have the following statement.

Proposition 8.12
.

For any real-valued weight
\gamma(x)
such that
|\gamma(x)|^{n}\in L\!\log\!L(M)
, we have

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{-1}\sigma_{j}^{\pm}(\gamma)^{n}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\Lambda_{g}^{-\frac{n}{2}}\gamma_{\pm}(x)^{n}\Lambda_{g}^{-\frac{n}{2}}.

8.3.
Birman-Solomyak’s Weyl laws and Connes’ trace theorem

We shall now explain how the main results of this monograph, and more precisely Proposition
1.1
, enable us to get a new proof of the Weyl laws of Birman-Solomyak for negative order pseudodifferential operators. This will lead to a far-reaching refinements of Connes’ trace theorem
[
52
]
and
its version in
[
113
]
.

In what follows we denote by
\Psi^{m}(M,E)
,
m\in\mathbb{R}
, the space of classical
\Psi
DOs of order
m
on
M
acting on the sections of
E
. We let

\sigma_{m}:\Psi^{m}(M,E)\longrightarrow C^{\infty}\left(M;\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E)\right)

be the
principal symbol map
on
\Psi^{m}(M,E)
. Here
S^{*}M=(T^{*}M\setminus 0)/\mathbb{R}^{*}_{+}
is the cosphere bundle of
M
and
\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E)
is the pullback of the algebra bundle
\operatorname{End}(E)
by the canonical submersion
\pi:S^{*}M\rightarrow M
. We recall that

\displaystyle\sigma_{m_{1}+m_{2}}(P_{1}P_{2})=\sigma_{m_{1}}(P_{1})\sigma_{m_{2}}(P_{2}),\qquad P_{j}\in\Psi^{m_{j}}(M,E),

\displaystyle\sigma_{m}(P^{*})=\sigma(P)^{*},\qquad P\in\Psi^{m}(M,E).

In addition, we a have an exact sequence,

(8.9)

0\longrightarrow\Psi^{m-1}(M,E)\longrightarrow\Psi^{m}(M,E)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma_{m}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C^{\infty}\left(M;\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E)\right)\longrightarrow 0.

The result of Birman-Solomyak that we seek to re-prove is the following.

Theorem 8.13

(Birman-Solomyak
[
26
,
27
,
28
]
)
.

Let
P\in\Psi^{m}(M,E)
,
m<0
, and set
q=|m|
.

(1)

We have

(8.10)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{n}}\mu_{j}(P)=\bigg[\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[|\sigma(P)(x,\xi)|^{\frac{n}{q}}\big]dxd\xi\bigg]^{\frac{q}{n}}.

(2)

If
P
is selfadjoint, then

(8.11)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{n}}\lambda^{\pm}_{j}(P)=\bigg[\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[\sigma(P)(x,\xi)_{\pm}^{\frac{n}{q}}\big]dxd\xi\bigg]^{\frac{q}{n}}.

Remark 8.14
.

In
[
26
,
27
]
Birman-Solomyak established the Weyl laws above for compactly supported pseudodifferential operators on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
under very low regularity assumptions on the symbols. Furthermore, the symbols are allowed to be anisotropic. This was extended to classical
\Psi
DOs on closed manifolds in
[
28
]
. A “soft” proof of Theorem
8.13
is given in
[
157
]
. We refer to
[
3
,
4
,
5
,
34
,
66
,
96
,
165
,
168
]
, and the references therein, for various generalizations and applications of Birman-Solomyak’s asymptotics.

In what follows we let
\nabla^{E}
be a Hermitian connection on
E
, and set

D_{E}:=\sqrt{1+(\nabla^{E})^{*}\nabla^{E}}.

Thus,
D_{E}
is a first order elliptic
\Psi
DO such that
\sigma_{1}(D_{E})(x,\xi)=|\xi|_{g}\operatorname{id}_{E_{x}}
. In addition, it has a positive spectrum, and hence is invertible. In fact, for all
m\in\mathbb{R}
, the power
D_{E}^{m}
is a
\Psi
DO of order
m
with

\sigma_{m}(D_{E}^{m})(x,\xi)=\sigma(D_{E})(x,\xi)^{m}=|\xi|^{m}_{g}\operatorname{id}_{E_{x}}.

For
s\geq 0
, we let
\mathscr{H}^{(s)}=W^{s,2}(M,E)
be the
W^{s,2}
-Sobolev space of sections of
E
. Equivalently, this is the Sobolev space with Bessel potential
(D_{E})^{s}=(1+(\nabla^{E})^{*}\nabla^{E})^{s/2}
, i.e.,

\mathscr{H}^{(s)}:=\left\{u\in L^{2}(M,E);\ (D_{E})^{s}u\in L^{2}(M,E)\right\}.

We then equip
\mathscr{H}^{(s)}
with the Hilbert norm,

(8.12)

\|u\|_{(s)}=\left\|(D_{E})^{s}u\right\|,\qquad u\in\mathscr{H}^{(s)}.

Note that
\mathscr{H}^{(0)}=L^{2}(M,E)
. In addition, the closure of
D_{E}
is a selfadjoint operator on
L^{2}(M,E)
whose domain is
\mathscr{H}^{(1)}
.

For
s<0
, we define
\mathscr{H}^{(s)}
to be the antilinear dual of
\mathscr{H}^{(-s)}
. Note that we have a continuous embedding
L^{2}(M,E)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{H}^{(s)}
given by

(8.13)

\left\langle u,v\right\rangle=\left\langle u|v\right\rangle,\qquad u\in L^{2}(M,E),\quad v\in\mathscr{H}^{(-s)},

where
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle:\mathscr{H}^{(s)}\times\mathscr{H}^{(-s)}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is the duality pairing.

Recall that if
P\in\Psi^{m}(M,E)
,
m\in\mathbb{R}
, then, for every
s\in\mathbb{R}
, it uniquely extends to a continuous linear operator,

(8.14)

P:\mathscr{H}^{(s+m)}\longrightarrow\mathscr{H}^{(s)}.

In particular, we see that
\Psi^{0}(M,E)
is a unital
*
-subalgebra of
\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(M,E))
.

Our approach is partly based on the following observation.

Lemma 8.15
.

The following triple

\left(\Psi^{0}(M,E),L^{2}(M,E),D_{E}\right)

is an
n
-summable spectral triple.

Proof.

This result is a mere consequence of the principal symbol of
D_{E}
being scalar. We only need to show that if
A\in\Psi^{0}(M,E)
, then
A(\operatorname{dom}(D_{E}))\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(D_{E})
, and the commutator
[D_{E},A]
is bounded. Here
\operatorname{dom}(D_{E})=\mathscr{H}^{(1)}
, and so it follows from (
8.14
) for
s=1
and
m=0
that
A(\operatorname{dom}(D_{E}))\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(D_{E})
.

It remains to show that
[D_{E},A]=D_{E}A-AD_{E}
is bounded. Here
D_{E}A
is a first order
\Psi
DO with principal symbol,

\sigma_{1}(D_{E}A)(x,\xi)=\sigma_{1}(D_{E})(x,\xi)\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)=|\xi|_{g}\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi).

Likewise,
AD_{E}
is a first order
\Psi
DO with principal symbol,

\sigma_{1}(AD_{E})(x,\xi)=\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)\sigma_{1}(D_{E})(x,\xi)=|\xi|_{g}\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi).

In particular, we see that
\sigma_{1}(D_{E}A)=\sigma_{1}(AD_{E})
. Therefore, if we regard the commutator
[A,D_{E}]=AD_{E}-D_{E}A
has a
\Psi
DO of order
\leq 1
, then
we have
\sigma_{1}([D_{E},A])=\sigma_{1}(D_{E}A)-\sigma_{1}(AD_{E})=0
. The exactness of the sequence (
8.9
) then ensures that
[D_{E},A]
has order
\leq 0
, and hence is bounded. This completes the proof.
∎

In what follows we denote by
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}
the closure of
\Psi^{0}(M,E)
in
\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(M,E))
. This is a unital
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(M,E))
. Note also that
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}
contains the closed ideal
\mathscr{K}
of compact operators on
L^{2}(M,E)
, since any such operator is the norm-limit of finite-rank smoothing operators.

As in
[
113
]
, another key ingredient of our approach is the following well-known result.

Lemma 8.16
.

The principal symbol map
\sigma_{0}:\Psi^{0}(M,E)\rightarrow C^{\infty}\left(M;\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E)\right)
uniquely extends to a unital
C^{*}
-algebra map,

\sigma_{0}:\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}\longrightarrow C\left(M;\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E)\right),

where
C\left(M;\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E)\right)
is the
C^{*}
-algebra of continuous sections of
\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E)
. This fits into the following exact sequence of
C^{*}
-algebras,

(8.15)

0\longrightarrow\mathscr{K}\longrightarrow\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C\left(M;\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E)\right)\longrightarrow 0.

Remark 8.17
.

The above result is stated by Atiyah-Singer in
[
7
, p. 512]
. It follows from a more general result of Hörmander
[
105
, Theorem 3.3]
(see also
[
111
, Corollary 6.1]
and
[
173
, §11.1]
). The result in
[
105
]
is stated for scalars
\Psi
DOs on an open of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. However, as pointed out by Hörmander the result continues to hold for
\Psi
DOs acting on sections of vector bundles over manifolds (see
[
105
, Remark, p. 159]
).

Remark 8.18
.

Recently, Kordyukov-Sukochev-Zanin
[
113
]
constructed a
C^{*}
-subalgebra
\Pi_{M}
of
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M)}
that provides a
C^{*}
-extension of
C(M)
by the compact operators of the same form as (
8.15
(see Theorem 1.4 in
[
113
]
). It is not shown in
[
113
]
that
\Pi_{M}=\overline{\Psi^{0}(M)}
, but this follows from a straightforward application of the Five Lemma.

Remark 8.19
.

As
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}
is a unital
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(M,E))
, the spectrum in
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}
of any
A\in\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}
agrees with its spectrum in
\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(M,E))
. We shall denote either spectrum by
\operatorname{Sp}(A)
.

The fact that
\sigma_{0}:\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}\rightarrow C(M;\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E))
is a unital
C^{*}
-algebra map has the following consequences.

Lemma 8.20
.

Let
A\in\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}
. The following hold.

(i)

For all
(x,\xi)\in S^{*}M
, we have

(8.16)

\operatorname{Sp}\left(\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)\right)\subseteq\operatorname{Sp}\left(\sigma_{0}(A)\right)\subseteq\operatorname{Sp}(A).

(ii)

Given any function
f\in C(\operatorname{Sp}(A))
, for all
(x,\xi)\in S^{*}M
, we have

(8.17)

\sigma_{0}\left(f(\sigma_{0}(A))\right)(x,\xi)=f(\sigma_{0}(A))(x,\xi)=f(\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)).

Remark 8.21
.

The first inclusion in (
8.16
) and the first equality (
8.17
) are direct consequences of the symbol map
\sigma_{0}
being a
C^{*}
-algebra map. In particular, it increases spectra and is compatible with the continuous functional calculus. The other inclusion and the other equality follow from the fact that the evaluation map
\sigma\rightarrow\sigma(x,\xi)
is a
C^{*}
-algebra map as well.

Proposition 8.22
.

Given any
A\in\Psi^{0}(M,E)_{++}
, as
j\rightarrow\infty
we have

(8.18)

\lambda_{j}\left(A(D_{E})^{2}A\right)\sim j^{\frac{2}{n}}\left[\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\iint_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\left[\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)^{-n}\right]|\xi|_{g}^{-n}dxd\xi\right]^{-\frac{2}{n}}.

Proof.

By assumption
A
is an invertible positive element of
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}
, and so this is a positive invertible operator in
\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(M,E))
. The fact that
A
is selfadjoint ensures that
\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)
is a Hermitian endomorphism of
E_{x}
for all
(x,\xi)\in S^{*}M
. Moreover, as
A
is a positive invertible operator, its spectrum is positive, and so there is
c>0
such that
\operatorname{Sp}(A)\subseteq[c,\infty)
. Combining this with (
8.16
) shows that
\operatorname{Sp}(\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi))\subseteq[c,\infty)
. It then follows that
\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)\geq c
for all
(x,\xi)\in S^{*}M
, and hence
A
is elliptic.

Bearing this in mind, we see that
A(D_{E})^{2}A
is positive operator on
L^{2}(M,E)
. This is also an operator in
\Psi^{2}(M,E)
whose principal symbol satisfies

\sigma_{2}\left(A(D_{E})^{2}A\right)(x,\xi)=\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)^{2}|\xi|_{g}^{2}\geq c^{2}|\xi|_{g}^{2}.

In particular,
\sigma_{2}\left(A(D_{E})^{2}A\right)(x,\xi)
is positive-definite for
\xi\neq 0
, and so
A(D_{E})^{2}A
is a positive elliptic
\Psi
DO. It then satisfies the classical Weyl law for such operators (see, e.g.,
[
70
,
98
]
). Namely, as
j\rightarrow\infty
we have

\lambda_{j}\left(A(D_{E})^{2}A\right)\sim j^{\frac{2}{n}}\left[\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\iint_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\left[\sigma_{2}(A(D_{E})^{2}A)(x,\xi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\right]dxd\xi\right]^{-\frac{2}{n}}.

As
\sigma_{2}(A(D_{E})^{2}A)(x,\xi)=\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)^{2}|\xi|^{2}_{g}
we get the Weyl law (
8.18
). The proof is complete.
∎

In what follows, we let
\hat{\tau}:C(S^{*}M,\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E))\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be the linear form defined by

(8.19)

\hat{\tau}(u):=\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\iint_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\left[u(x,\xi)\right]|\xi|_{g}^{-n}dxd\xi,\qquad u\in C(S^{*}M,\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E)).

This a positive linear form on
C(S^{*}M,\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E))
. Moreover, if
A\in\Psi^{0}(M,E)_{++}
, then by using (
8.17
) we can reformulate the Weyl law (
8.18
) in the following form,

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{-\frac{2}{n}}\lambda_{j}\left(A(D_{E})^{2}A\right)=\hat{\tau}\left(\sigma_{0}(A)^{-n}\right)=\hat{\tau}\left(\sigma_{0}(A^{-n})\right).

This shows that Condition (W) is satisfied with

\tau(A):=\hat{\tau}\left(\sigma_{0}(A)\right),\qquad A\in\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}.

As Condition (W) holds, we may apply the main results of this monograph. In this setting, Proposition
1.1
leads to the following result.

Proposition 8.23
.

Let
A\in\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}
. For every
q>0
, the following hold.

(1)

We have

(8.20)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{n}}\mu_{j}\big((D_{E})^{-\frac{q}{2}}A(D_{E})^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\bigg[\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[|\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)|^{\frac{n}{q}}\big]|\xi|_{g}^{-n}dxd\xi\bigg]^{\frac{q}{n}}.

(2)

If
A
is selfadjoint, then

(8.21)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{n}}\lambda^{\pm}_{j}\big((D_{E})^{-\frac{q}{2}}A(D_{E})^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\bigg[\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)_{\pm}^{\frac{n}{q}}\big]|\xi|_{g}^{-n}dxd\xi\bigg]^{\frac{q}{n}}.

Proof.

By applying Proposition
1.1
we get

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{n}}\mu_{j}\big((D_{E})^{-\frac{q}{2}}A(D_{E})^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\tau\left[|A|^{\frac{n}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{n}},

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{n}}\lambda^{\pm}_{j}\big((D_{E})^{-\frac{q}{2}}A(D_{E})^{-\frac{q}{2}}\big)=\tau\left[\big(A_{\pm}\big)^{\frac{n}{q}}\right]^{\frac{q}{n}}\quad(\text{if}\ A^{*}=A).

Combining these spectral asymptotics with (
8.17
) gives the result.
∎

Moreover, applying Theorem
1.3
in this setting yields the following integration formulas.

Theorem 8.24
.

For every
A\in\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}
, the operators
A(D_{E})^{-n}
,
D_{E}^{-n/2}AD_{E}^{-n/2}
, and
|D_{E}^{-n/2}AD_{E}^{-n/2}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

(8.22)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int A(D_{E})^{-n}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int(D_{E})^{-\frac{n}{2}}A(D_{E})^{-\frac{n}{2}}=\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)\big]|\xi|_{g}^{-n}dxd\xi,

(8.23)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\big|(D_{E})^{-\frac{n}{2}}A(D_{E})^{-\frac{n}{2}}\big|=\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[|\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)|\big]|\xi|_{g}^{-n}dxd\xi.

Remark 8.25
.

The integration formula (
8.23
) seems to be new in this level of generality.

Remark 8.26
.

The integration formula (
8.22
) is a refinement of
[
113
, Theorem 1.5]
. For one thing, the focus in
[
113
]
is on strong measurability and scalar
\Psi
DOs, whereas here we obtain spectral measurability for
\Psi
DOs acting on vector bundles. For the other thing, the result in
[
113
]
is stated for the
C^{*}
-subalgebra
\Pi_{M}\subseteq\overline{\Psi^{0}(M)}
, and the integration formula is stated in the following form,

(8.24)

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int A(D_{E})^{-n}=c_{n}\iint_{T^{*}M}\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)e^{-|\xi|^{2}_{g}}dxd\xi,

where
c_{n}
is an unspecified constant and
dxd\xi
is the Liouville measure of
T^{*}M
. As mentioned in Remark
8.18
the
C^{*}
-subalgebra
\Pi_{M}
actually agrees with
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M)}
. Moreover, by definition of the Liouville measure of
S^{*}M=(T^{*}M\setminus 0)/\mathbb{R}^{*}_{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\iint_{T^{*}M}\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)e^{-|\xi|^{2}_{g}}dxd\xi

\displaystyle=\iint_{S^{*}M}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}\sigma_{0}(A)(x,t\xi)e^{-t^{2}|\xi|^{2}_{g}}t^{n-1}dt\bigg)dxd\xi

\displaystyle=\iint_{S^{*}M}\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{n-1}e^{-t^{2}|\xi|^{2}_{g}}dt\bigg)dxd\xi.

As
\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{n-1}e^{-t^{2}|\xi|^{2}_{g}}dt=|\xi|_{g}^{-n}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{n-1}e^{-t^{2}}dt=|\xi|_{g}^{-n}\Gamma(n)=(n-1)!|\xi|_{g}^{-n}
, we get

\iint_{T^{*}M}\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)e^{-|\xi|^{2}_{g}}dxd\xi=(n-1)!\iint_{S^{*}M}\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)|\xi|_{g}^{-n}dxd\xi.

Therefore, in the scalar case the r.h.s. of (
8.22
) agrees with the r.h.s of (
8.24
) with
c_{n}=\frac{1}{n!}(2\pi)^{-n}
.

From now on, we let
m<0
. It follows from (
8.14
) for
s=-m/2
that every operator
P\in\Psi^{m}(M,E)
uniquely extends to a continuous linear operator,

P:\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)}\longrightarrow\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)}.

Therefore, we may regard
\Psi^{m}(M,E)
as a subspace of
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)},\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)})
. This leads to the following definition.

Definition 8.27
.

\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}
is the closure of
\Psi^{m}(M,E)
in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)},\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)})
.

The advantage of taking the closure in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)},\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)})
is that this Banach space has a natural isometric antilinear involution. Namely,
thanks of the (anti-)duality between
\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)}
and
\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)}
, every operator
T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)},\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)})
has an
adjoint
T^{*}
in
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)},\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)})
defined by

(8.25)

\left\langle u,T^{*}v\right\rangle=\overline{\left\langle v,Tu\right\rangle},\qquad u,v\in\mathscr{H}^{(\frac{m}{2})}.

Here
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle:\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)}\times\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is the duality pairing. We then get an antilinear isometric involution on

\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)},\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)})
.

If
P\in\Psi^{m}(M,E)
, then this adjoint agrees with
P^{*}
on
C^{\infty}(M,E)
. Indeed, by using (
8.13
) we see that,
for all
u,v\in L^{2}(M,E)
, we have

\left\langle u,P^{*}v\right\rangle=\left\langle u|P^{*}v\right\rangle=\left\langle Pu|v\right\rangle=\overline{\left\langle v|Pu\right\rangle}=\overline{\left\langle v,Pu\right\rangle}.

Therefore, we get the following result.

Lemma 8.28
.

The antilinear involution
P\rightarrow P^{*}
of
\Psi^{m}(M,E)
uniquely extends to an isometric antilinear involution on
\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}
satisfying (
8.25
).

In addition, the definition (
8.12
) of the norm of
\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)}
implies we have an isometric linear isomorphism,

(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}:\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)}\longrightarrow L^{2}(M,E).

By duality we get an isometric linear isomorphism
(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}:L^{2}(M,E)\rightarrow\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)}
such that

(8.26)

\big\langle(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}u,v\big\rangle=\big\langle u|(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}v\big\rangle,\qquad u\in L^{2}(M,E),\quad v\in\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)}.

As a result a get an isometric linear isomorphism,

\displaystyle\Phi:\mathscr{L}\big(\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)},\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)}\big)\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(M,E)),

\displaystyle\Phi(T):=(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}T(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}},\qquad T\in\mathscr{L}\big(\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)},\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)}\big).

Lemma 8.29
.

\Phi
is a
*
-isomorphism, i.e.,

\Phi(T^{*})=\Phi(T)^{*}\qquad\forall T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)},\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)}).

Proof.

Let
T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)},\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)})
. Given any
u,v\in L^{2}(M,E)
, we have

\left\langle\Phi(T^{*})u|v\right\rangle=\big\langle(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}T^{*}(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}u|v\big\rangle=\overline{\big\langle v|(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}T^{*}(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}u\big\rangle}.

In view of (
8.25
) and (
8.26
) we see that
\left\langle v|(D_{E})^{|m|/2}T^{*}(D_{E})^{|m|/2}\right\rangle
is equal to

\big\langle(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}v,T^{*}(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}u\big\rangle=\overline{\big\langle(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}u,T(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}v\big\rangle}=\overline{\big\langle u|(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}T(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}v\big\rangle}.

As
\left\langle u|(D_{E})^{|m|/2}T(D_{E})^{|m|/2}v\right\rangle=\left\langle u|\Phi(T)v\right\rangle=\left\langle\Phi(T)^{*}u|v\right\rangle
, we get

\left\langle\Phi(T^{*})u|v\right\rangle=\overline{\big\langle v|(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}T^{*}(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}u\big\rangle}=\big\langle\Phi(T)^{*}u|v\big\rangle.

This shows that
\Phi(T^{*})=\Phi(T)^{*}
, proving the result.
∎

If
P\in\Psi^{m}(M,E)
, then
\Phi(P)=(D_{E})^{|m|/2}P(D_{E})^{|m|/2}\in\Psi^{0}(M,E)
. Conversely, if
A
is any operator in
\Psi^{0}(M,E)
, then
A=\Phi(P)
, with
P=(D_{E})^{m/2}A(D_{E})^{m/2}\in\Psi^{m}(M,E)
. Thus,

\Phi(\Psi^{m}(M,E))=\Psi^{0}(M,E).

As
\Phi
is an isometric
*
-isomorphism, it follows that it induces an isometric
*
-isomorphism,

\Phi:\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}\longrightarrow\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}.

At the level of principal symbols, if
P\in\Psi^{m}(M,E)
, then

\sigma_{0}\big((D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}P(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}\big)=\sigma_{1}(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}\sigma_{m}(P)\sigma_{1}(D_{E})^{\frac{|m|}{2}}=\sigma_{m}(P)|\xi|_{g}^{-m}.

That is,

(8.27)

\sigma_{m}(P)(x,\xi)=\sigma_{0}\left(\Phi(P)\right)\!(x,\xi)|\xi|_{g}^{m}.

Combining all this with Lemma
8.16
enables us to get the following result.

Lemma 8.30
.

The principal symbol map
\sigma_{m}:\Psi^{m}(M,E)\rightarrow C^{\infty}(S^{*}M;\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E))
uniquely extends to a continuous
*
-linear map,

\sigma_{m}:\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}\longrightarrow C\left(S^{*}M;\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E)\right),

in such a way that (
8.27
) holds for all
P\in\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}
.

Remark 8.31
.

The fact that
\sigma_{m}\overline{:\Psi^{m}(M,E})\rightarrow C(S^{*}M;\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E))
is a
*
-linear map means that

\sigma_{m}(P^{*})=\sigma(P)(x,\xi)^{*}\qquad\forall P\in\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}.

In particular, if
P=P^{*}\in\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}
, then
\sigma(P)(x,\xi)
is a Hermitian endormorphism of
E_{x}
for all
(x,\xi)\in S^{*}M
.

Remark 8.32
.

Let
\mathscr{K}^{(m)}
be the (closed) subspace of
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}^{(m/2)},\mathscr{H}^{(|m|/2)})
consisting of compact operators. We then have an exact sequence of Banach spaces,

0\longrightarrow\mathscr{K}^{(m)}\longrightarrow\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma_{m}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C\left(S^{*}M;\pi^{*}\operatorname{End}(E)\right)\longrightarrow 0.

This is essentially the exact sequence mentioned by Atiyah and Singer in
[
7
, p. 512]
.

We are now in a position to prove Theorem
8.13
. Actually, as we shall now see, Proposition
8.23
provides an extension of Theorem
8.13
to the closure
\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}
. We then get Theorem
8.13
by specializing that result to the space
\Psi^{m}(M,E)
.

Theorem 8.33
.

Let
P\in\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}
,
m<0
, and set
q=|m|
.

(1)

We have

(8.28)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{n}}\mu_{j}(P)=\bigg[\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[|\sigma_{m}(P)(x,\xi)|^{\frac{n}{q}}\big]dxd\xi\bigg]^{\frac{q}{n}}.

(2)

If
P=P^{*}
, then

(8.29)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{n}}\lambda^{\pm}_{j}(P)=\bigg[\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[\sigma_{m}(P)(x,\xi)_{\pm}^{\frac{n}{q}}\big]dxd\xi\bigg]^{\frac{q}{n}}.

Proof.

Set
A=\Phi(P)
. This is an operator in
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}
such that
P=(D_{E})^{m/2}A(D_{E})^{m/2}
and
\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)=\sigma_{m}(P)(x,\xi)|\xi|_{g}^{-m}
. Therefore, the spectral asymptotic (
8.20
) for
A=\Phi(P)
and
q=|m|
yields the spectral asymptotic (
8.28
).

If in addition
P^{*}=P
, then
A=\Phi(P)
is selfadjoint. The spectral asymptotic (
8.21
) for
A=\Phi(P)
and
q=|m|
then give the spectral asymptotics (
8.29
). The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 8.34
.

Theorem
8.33
extends Theorem
8.13
to operators in the closure
\overline{\Psi^{m}(M,E)}
. This extension can also be deduced directly from Theorem
8.13
by using Lemma
8.30
and the perturbation theory of Birman-Solomyak (i.e., Proposition
2.4
). The main point here is showing that Theorem
8.13
and Theorem
8.33
can be obtained as consequences of Proposition
1.1
.

Remark 8.35
.

We have deduced Theorem
8.33
from Proposition
8.23
. Conversely, we get the asymptotics (
8.20
)–(
8.21
) of Proposition
8.23
by applying Theorem
8.33
to
P=(D_{E})^{m/2}A(D_{E})^{m/2}
. Therefore, Proposition
8.23
and Theorem
8.33
are equivalent statements.

Furthermore, Theorem
8.24
leads to the following extension of Connes’ trace theorem.

Theorem 8.36
.

For every
P\in\overline{\Psi^{-n}(M,E)}
, the operators
P
and
|P|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

(8.30)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int P=\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[\sigma_{-n}(P)(x,\xi)\big]dxd\xi,

(8.31)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int|P|=\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[|\sigma_{-n}(P)(x,\xi)|\big]dxd\xi.

Proof.

Let
P\in\overline{\Psi^{-n}(M,E)}
, and set
A=\Phi(P)
. As in the proof of Theorem
8.33
, the operator
A
is contained in
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}
and is such that
P=(D_{E})^{-n/2}A(D_{E})^{-n/2}
and
\sigma_{0}(A)(x,\xi)=\sigma_{m}(P)(x,\xi)|\xi|_{g}^{n}
. Applying the parts of Theorem
8.24
for the operators
(D_{E})^{-n/2}A(D_{E})^{-n/2}=P
and
|(D_{E})^{-n/2}A(D_{E})^{-n/2}|=|P|
then gives the result.
∎

Remark 8.37
.

We also can recover Theorem
8.24
from Theorem
8.36
by applying the latter to
P=A(D_{E})^{-n}
and
P=(D_{E})^{-n/2}A(D_{E})^{-n/2}
. Thus, Theorem
8.24
and Theorem
8.36
are equivalent statements.

Remark 8.38
.

Theorem
8.36
can also be deduced directly from Theorem
8.33
by using Proposition
2.6
. As mentioned in Remark
8.34
, Theorem
8.33
can be deduced from Birman-Solomyak’s spectral asymptotics (
8.10
)–(
8.11
). Therefore, we see that Theorem
8.36
can be obtained as a consequence of those spectral asymptotics.

8.4.
Appendix

In this appendix, we shall explain that, if we are only interested in showing strong measurability, then the integration formula (
8.30
) can be obtained by a mere elaboration of the original arguments of Connes
[
52
]
to establish his trace theorem. As a byproduct, we will see that the integration formula of
[
113
, Theorem 1.5]
can be deduced from Connes’s original arguments (see Remark
8.39
below).

Proof of (
8.30
) and strong measurability by using Connes’ approach.

Recall that Connes
[
52
]
showed that every operator
P\in\Psi^{-n}(M,E)
is measurable and satisfies the integration formula (
8.30
).

We observe that Connes’ arguments for Dixmier traces apply
verbatim
to any normalized positive trace on
\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}
. Thus, if
\varphi:\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is such a trace, then, for all
P\in\Psi^{-n}(M,E)
, we have

(8.32)

\varphi(P)=\frac{1}{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\iint_{S^{*}M}\operatorname{tr}_{E}\big[\sigma_{-n}(P)(x,\xi)\big]dxd\xi.

It follows from Lemma
8.30
that the r.h.s. of (
8.32
) is a continuous linear form on
\overline{\Psi^{-n}(M,E)}
. Moreover, if
P\in\overline{\Psi^{-n}(M,E)}
, then
P=(D_{E})^{-n/2}\Phi(P)(D_{E})^{-n/2}
. Recall that
\Phi:\overline{\Psi^{-n}(M,E)}\rightarrow\overline{\Psi^{0}(M,E)}
is an isometric isomorphism. Therefore, by using the Hölder’s inequality (
2.7
) we get

\|P\|_{1,\infty}\leq 2^{\frac{2}{n}}\big(\big\|(D_{E})^{-n/2}\big\|_{2,\infty}\big)^{2}\|\Phi(P)\|\leq C_{E}\|P\|_{\overline{\Psi^{-n}(M,E)}},

where the constant
C_{E}
does not depend on
P
. This shows that we have a continuous embedding of
\overline{\Psi^{-n}(M,E)}
into
\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}
. Every positive trace on
\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}
is continuous (see, e.g.,
[
129
, Lemma 2.4.12]
). It then follows that
P\rightarrow\varphi(P)
is a continuous linear form on
\overline{\Psi^{-n}(M,E)}
.

To sum up both sides of (
8.32
) are continuous linear forms on
\overline{\Psi^{-n}(M,E)}
and agree on the dense subspace
\Psi^{-n}(M,E)
. Therefore, they agree on all
\overline{\Psi^{-n}(M,E)}
. This means that every
P\in\overline{\Psi^{-n}(M,E)}
is strongly measurable and satisfies the integration formula (
8.30
). The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 8.39
.

It follows from Remark
8.26
and Remark
8.37
that, in the scalar case, the integration formula (
8.30
) yields the integration formula of
[
113
, Theorem 1.5]
. Therefore, we see that
[
113
, Theorem 1.5]
can be obtained by an easy elaboration of the original arguments of Connes
[
52
]
.

Chapter 9
Open Manifolds with Conformally Cusp Metrics

In this chapter, we use the main results of this monograph to get semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas for some open Riemannian manifolds. More precisely, we consider fractional Schrödinger operators associated with Dirac operators and magnetic Laplacians on manifolds with conformally cusp metrics.

The results of this chapter are new. The semiclassical Weyl laws that we obtain in this chapter complement nicely recent semiclassical Weyl laws for complete Riemanian manifolds by Braverman
[
36
]
and Dai-Yan
[
65
]
(see Remark
9.3
).

9.1.
Conformally Cusp Metrics

Throughout this chapter, we let
X^{n}
be an open manifold, which is the interior of a compact manifold
\overline{X}
with closed boundary
M^{n-1}=\partial\overline{X}
. Let
x
be a boundary defining function, i.e.,
x
is a non-negative smooth function on
\overline{X}
such that
x^{-1}(0)=M
and
dx\neq 0
on
M
. We assume that
X
is endowed with a Riemannian metric of the form,

g=x^{2r}g_{0},\qquad r>\frac{1}{n},

where
g_{0}
is a cusp metric on
\overline{X}
, i.e., a (complete) Riemannian metric which in local coordinates near
M
takes the form,

g_{0}=a_{00}(x,y)x^{-4}dx^{2}+\sum_{1\leq j<n}a_{0j}(x,y)x^{-2}dxdy^{j}+\sum_{1\leq i,j<n}h_{ij}(x,y)dy^{i}dy^{j}.

If in addition
a_{00|M}=1
and
a_{0j|M}=0
for
j=1,\ldots,n
, then (following Melrose
[
141
]
) we say that
g_{0}
is an
exact
cusp metric. Note that the cusp metric
g_{0}
induces on
M
the metric,

(9.1)

h_{0}:=\sum_{1\leq i,j<n}h_{ij}(0,y)dy^{i}dy^{j}.

The condition
r>1/n
is exactly the condition that ensures that
(X,g)
has finite volume. In addition,
(X,g)
is a complete Riemannian manifold if and only if
r\leq 1
(
[
151
]
).

Examples of manifolds with conformally cusp metrics with
r\geq 1/n
include hyperbolic metrics of finite volume (in which case
r=1
; see
[
150
]
). Another class of examples is provided by metric horns in the sense of Cheeger
[
42
]
(see also
[
120
]
) for which
r>1
.

9.2.
Dirac operators

Assume further that
\overline{X}
is spin. We denote by
\not{D}_{g}
the Dirac operator of
X
acting on the sections of the spinor bundle
\not{S}_{X}
. In addition, we denote by
\not{D}_{h_{0}}
the Dirac operator of
M
with respect to the metric (
9.1
). We also make the following assumptions:

•

g_{0}
is an exact cusp metric.

•

\ker\not{D}_{h_{0}}=\{0\}
, i.e., there are no (non-zero) harmonic spinors on
M
.

The second condition holds for instance if
(M,h_{0})
has a non-negative scalar curvature which is non-zero at a point. It also known to hold for generic metrics if
M
either has dimension
n\geq 4
or its fundamental group
\pi_{1}(M)
vanishes or is cyclic of odd order (
cf
. the discussion on this on
[
150
, p. 3]
and the references therein).

We have a natural unitary isomorphism
U:L_{g_{0}}^{2}(X,\not{S}_{X})\rightarrow L_{g}^{2}(X,\not{S}_{X})
given by

(9.2)

U\phi=x^{-\frac{nr}{2}}\phi,\qquad\phi\in L_{g_{0}}(X,\not{S}_{X}).

As observed in
[
150
]
, under these assumptions the conjugate operator
D_{0}:=U^{*}\not{D}_{g}U
is a fully elliptic cusp differential operator of type
(1,r)
. Full ellipticity means that the principal symbol of
D_{0}
is invertible and the normal operator

\mathscr{N}(D_{0})(\xi):=(e^{i\xi/x}D_{0}e^{-i\xi/x})_{|M},\qquad\xi\in\mathbb{R},

is invertible for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
. This ensures the existence of a parametrix for
D_{0}^{2}
in the cusp pseudodifferential calculus of Melrose (see, e.g.,
[
142
, Appendix A]
). Using this
\Psi
DO calculus the following results are established.

(i)

\not{D}_{g}
with domain
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
is essentially selfadjoint on
L_{g}^{2}(X)
and has compact resolvent. Thus, its spectrum consists of isolated real eigenvalues with finite multiplicity (see also
[
10
]
for the case
r=1
).

(ii)

For every
z\in\mathbb{C}
, the power
|\not{D}_{g}|^{z}
is such that
U^{*}|\not{D}_{g}|^{z}U=|D_{0}|^{z}
is a cusp
\Psi
DO of type
(z,rz)
. In particular, it is trace-class for

\Re z<-n
.

(iii)

The zeta function
\zeta_{\not{D}_{g}}(z):=\operatorname{Tr}[|\not{D}_{g}|^{-z}]
,
\Re z>n
, has a meromorphic continuation to
\mathbb{C}
with at worst poles on
(n-\mathbb{N}_{0})\cup(r^{-1}-r^{-1}\mathbb{N}_{0})
.

(iv)

The first pole occurs at
z=n
. This pole is simple and the residue is given by

\operatorname{Res}_{z=n}\zeta_{\not{D}_{g}}(z)=\not{c}(n)\operatorname{Vol}_{g}(X),\qquad\text{where}\ \not{c}(n):=2^{\left[\frac{n}{2}\right]}(2\pi)^{-n}|\mathbb{B}^{n}|.

(v)

By Ikehara’s Tauberian theorem this implies that, as
j\rightarrow\infty
, we have the Weyl law,

\lambda_{j}\big(\not{D}_{g}^{2}\big)\sim j^{\frac{2}{n}}\big(\not{c}(n)\operatorname{Vol}_{g}(X)\big)^{-\frac{2}{n}}

In the same way as in Chapter
7
to get a spectral triple we use the
*
-algebra,

\dot{C}^{\infty}(X)=\mathbb{C}1+C_{c}^{\infty}(X).

Here again
\dot{C}^{\infty}(X)
is the algebra of
C^{\infty}
-functions on
X
that are constant near the boundary
M=\partial X
. It is represented in
L_{g}^{2}(X,\not{S}_{X})
by multiplication operators. Its norm closure is the
C^{*}
-algebra,

\dot{C}(X):=\mathbb{C}1+C_{0}(X),

where
C_{0}(X)
is the ideal of continuous functions on
X
that converges to
0
near the boundary
M
. Thus,
\dot{C}(X)
consists of continuous functions on
\overline{X}
that are constant on the boundary. If
f
is such a function, we denote by
f_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
its boundary value. Note that constant functions act as bounded multiplication operators since
(X,g)
has finite volume.

If
f\in\dot{C}^{\infty}(X)
, then
[\not{D}_{g},f]=c(df)
is a compactly supported section of
\operatorname{End}\not{S}_{X}
, and hence it is bounded on
L_{g}^{2}(X,\not{S}_{X})
. We thus get a spectral triple,

(9.3)

\left(\dot{C}^{\infty}(X),L^{2}_{g}(X,\not{S}_{X}),\not{D}_{g}\right).

This spectral triple is
n
-summable.

Let
f\in\dot{C}^{\infty}(X)
be
>0
on
X
and have positive boundary value. The multiplication by
f
commutes with the unitary isomorphism
U
given by (
9.2
). Thus,

U^{*}(f\not{D}_{g}^{2}f)U=f(U^{*}\not{D}_{g}U)^{2}f=fD_{0}^{2}f.

The operator
fD_{0}^{2}f
is a fully elliptic cusp differential operator of type
(2,2r)
, since its principal symbol is
f^{2}|\xi|_{g_{0}}^{2}
and its normal operator is

f_{0}^{2}\mathscr{N}(D_{0})^{2}
are both positive. Therefore, the results of
[
150
]
apply, and so as
j\rightarrow\infty
we have the Weyl law,

\lambda_{j}\big(f\not{D}_{g}^{2}f\big)\sim j^{\frac{2}{n}}\left(\not{c}(n)\int_{X}f(y)^{-n}d\nu_{g}(y)\right)^{-\frac{2}{n}}.

All this shows that
(\dot{C}^{\infty}(X),L^{2}_{g}(X,\not{S}_{X}),\not{D}_{g})
is a spectral triple satisfying Condition (W). Therefore, Proposition
1.1
and its consequences apply. In particular, Theorem
1.2
yields the following semiclassical Weyl law.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
q>0
, and assume that
g_{0}
is an exact cusp metric and there are no non-zero harmonic spinors on
M
.
Given any real-valued potential
V\in\dot{C}(X)
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{n}N\left(h^{2q}|\not{D}_{g}|^{2q}+V;\lambda\right)=\not{c}(n)\int_{X}(V(y)-\lambda)_{-}^{\frac{n}{2q}}d\nu_{g}(y).

In addition, by using Theorem
1.3
we get the following integration formulas.

Theorem 9.2
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
9.1
, for every
f\in\dot{C}(X)
,
the operators
f|\not{D}_{g}|^{-n}
,
|\not{D}_{g}|^{-n/2}f|\not{D}_{g}|^{-n/2}
, and
||\not{D}_{g}|^{-n/2}f|\not{D}_{g}|^{-n/2}|

are spectrally measurable, and we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int f|\not{D}_{g}|^{-n}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int|\not{D}_{g}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}f|\not{D}_{g}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}=\not{c}(n)\int_{X}f(y)d\nu_{g}(y),

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\big||\not{D}_{g}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}f|\not{D}_{g}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}\big|=\not{c}(n)\int_{X}|f(y)|d\nu_{g}(y).

Remark 9.3
.

As pointed out to the author by Sergiu Moroianu, in the cases
r=1,2
we can replace
\dot{C}^{\infty}(X)
by a larger algebra to get a spectral triple in (
9.3
). For
r=1
(i.e., geometric cusp case) we may use the algebra of functions that are smooth up to the boundary and constant on
M
. For
r=2
we need to further require the normal derivative (i.e.,
\partial f/\partial x
) is constant on
M
as well. In either case we get the same closure in
L^{2}_{g}(X)
as with using
\dot{C}^{\infty}(X)
, and so this does not affect our main results.

9.3.
Magnetic Laplacians

We drop the assumption on
X
being spin. The ordinary Laplacian of
(X,g)
need not have discrete spectrum (see, e.g.,
[
82
]
). However, as observed by Golénia-Moroianu
[
92
]
we get a fully elliptic cusp differential operator with discrete spectrum by coupling the Laplacian with a trapping magnetic field. Such operators appear in the study of the Aharonov-Bohm effect.

More precisely, let
B
be a magnetic field of the form
B=dA
, where the vector potential
A
is a cusp real 1-form, i.e., a smooth real 1-form on
X
which near
M
is of the form,

(9.4)

A=a_{0}(x,y)x^{-2}dx+\omega,\qquad\iota_{\partial_{x}}\omega=0.

where
a_{0}(x,y)
and
\omega
are smooth up to the boundary. Set
A_{0}=\omega_{|M}
. Following
[
92
]
we shall say that
A
is
trapping
on a given connected component
M_{0}\subseteq M
if one of the following two conditions is satisfied:

•

a_{0}(0,y)
is not constant on
M_{0}
.

•

Either
2\pi A_{0}
is not closed or it defines a non-integral cohomology class in
H(M_{0},\mathbb{R})
.

We say that
A
is trapping on
M
if it is trapping on every connected component.

The
magnetic Laplacian
is the differential operator on
X
defined by

\Delta_{A}:=d_{A}^{*}d_{A},\qquad d_{A}=d+iA\wedge\cdot,

where
d:C_{c}^{\infty}(X)\rightarrow C_{c}^{\infty}(X,T^{*}X)
is the de Rham differential. We also denote by
\Delta_{A_{0}}^{0}
the magnetic Laplacian on
M
associated with the metric
h_{0}
and the
1
-form
A_{0}
.

As shown in
[
92
]
, if
A
is trapping, then
\Delta_{A}
is a fully elliptic cusp differential operator of type
(2,2r)
. Thus, in the same way as with the square of the Dirac operator, the operator
\Delta_{A}
with domain
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
is essentially selfadjoint on
L_{g}^{2}(X)
and has compact resolvent. Moreover, we have the Weyl law,

\lambda_{j}\big(\Delta_{A}\big)\sim j^{\frac{2}{n}}\big(c(n)\operatorname{Vol}_{g}(X)\big)^{-\frac{2}{n}},\qquad c(n):=(2\pi)^{-n}|\mathbb{B}^{n}|.

If
f\in\dot{C}^{\infty}(X)
, then
[\sqrt{\Delta_{A}},f]=[\sqrt{\Delta_{A}},f-f_{0}]
. Here
f-f_{0}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(X)
, and so
\sqrt{\Delta_{A}}(f-f_{0})
and

(f-f_{0})\sqrt{\Delta_{A}}
are cusp
\Psi
DOs of type
(1,-\infty)
with same principal symbol and vanishing normal operators. It follows that
[\sqrt{\Delta_{A}},f]

is a cusp
\Psi
DO of type
(0,-\infty)
, and hence is bounded on
L_{g}^{2}(X)
. We thus have a spectral triple,

\left(\dot{C}^{\infty}(X),L^{2}_{g}(X),\sqrt{\Delta_{A}}\right).

As with the Dirac spectral triple (
9.3
), this spectral triple is
n
-summable.

If
f\in\dot{C}^{\infty}(X)
is
>0
on
X
and has a positive boundary value, then
f\Delta_{A}f
is again a fully elliptic cusp differential operator of type
(2,2r)
. Indeed, its principal symbol is
f^{2}|\xi|^{2}_{g}
and its normal operator is
\mathscr{N}(f\Delta_{A}f)(\xi)=f_{0}^{2}\mathscr{N}(\Delta_{A})(\xi)
. Therefore, in the same way as with
\not{D}^{2}_{g}
, as
j\rightarrow\infty
we have the Weyl law,

(9.5)

\lambda_{j}\big(f\Delta_{A}f\big)\sim j^{\frac{2}{n}}\left(c(n)\int_{X}f(y)^{-n}d\nu_{g}(y)\right)^{-\frac{2}{n}}.

It follows from all this that
(\dot{C}^{\infty}(X),L^{2}_{g}(X),\sqrt{\Delta_{A}})
is a spectral triple satisfying Condition (W). Thus,
Proposition
1.1
and its consequences apply. The results are analogous to the results for Dirac operators above. In particular, we have the following semiclassical Weyl law.

Theorem 9.4
.

Let
q>0
, and assume that the
1
-form
A
is trapping. For any real-valued potential
V\in\dot{C}(\overline{X})
, we have

(9.6)

\displaystyle\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{n}N\left(h^{2q}\Delta_{A}^{q}+V;\lambda\right)=c(n)\int_{X}(V(y)-\lambda)_{-}^{\frac{n}{2q}}d\nu_{g}(y).

Remark 9.5
.

We refer to
[
139
,
169
]
for Cwikel-Lieb-Rozenblum inequalities and semiclassical Weyl laws for magnetic Schrödinger operators on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Remark 9.6
.

We also refer to
[
122
]
for a semiclassical Weyl law for Schrödinger operators
h^{2}\Delta_{\text{hyp}}+V
on hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{n}
,
n\geq 3
.

In addition, we obtain the following integration formulas.

Theorem 9.7
.

Suppose that the
1
-form
A
is trapping. For every function
f\in\dot{C}(X)
,
the operators
f\Delta_{A}^{-n/2}
,
\Delta_{A}^{-n/4}f\Delta_{A}^{-n/4}
, and
|\Delta_{A}^{-n/4}f\Delta_{A}^{-n/4}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

(9.7)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int f\Delta_{A}^{-\frac{n}{2}}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\Delta_{A}^{-\frac{n}{4}}f\Delta_{A}^{-\frac{n}{4}}=c(n)\int_{X}f(y)d\nu_{g}(y),

(9.8)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\big|\Delta_{A}^{-\frac{n}{4}}f\Delta_{A}^{-\frac{n}{4}}\big|=c(n)\int_{X}|f(y)|d\nu_{g}(y).

Remark 9.8
.

We refer to
[
125
]
for strong mesurability properties and NC integral formulas for the inverses of a class of magnetic Schrödinger operators
\Delta_{A}+V
on Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Note that in
[
125
]
the measurability is with respect to the weak Lorentz ideal
\mathscr{L}_{g}
with
g(t)=(t+1)^{-1}\log(t+1)
, which falls out of the scope of this monograph.

Remark 9.9
.

Let
\Delta_{g}^{(k)}
be the Hodge Laplacian of
(X,g)
acting on
k
-forms and denote by
\beta_{j}(M)
,
j=0,1,\ldots,n
the Betti numbers of
M
. As shown in
[
93
]
, if
\beta_{k-1}(M)=\beta_{k}(M)=0
, then
\Delta_{g}^{(k)}
is essentially selfadjoint with pure discrete spectrum and the Weyl law (
9.5
) holds
mutatis mutandis
with different constants. By arguing as above it can be shown that the square-root
\Delta_{g}^{(k)}
gives rise to a spectral triple, and so the above results hold
mutatis mutandis
for the Hodge Laplacians
\Delta_{g}^{(k)}
under those conditions. This includes the semiclassical Weyl law (
9.6
) and the integration formulas (
9.7
)–(
9.8
). In those formulas we only have to replace the constants
c(n)
by the constants,

\displaystyle c(n,k):=\binom{n}{k}c(n)=(2\pi)^{-n}\binom{n}{k}\left|\mathbb{B}^{n}\right|.

Remark 9.10
.

Recently, Braverman
[
36
]
and Dai-Yan
[
65
]
derived semiclassical Weyl laws for Shrödinger operators
h^{2}\Delta_{g}+V
on
complete
manifolds with
unbounded
potentials. In contrast, in the semiclassical Weyl laws provided by Theorem
9.1
and Theorem
9.4
the potentials
V
are bounded. Moreover, as mentioned above,
(X,g)
is not a complete for
r>1
.
Therefore, in this case we get instances of semiclassical Weyl laws for a class of
non-complete
Riemanianian manifolds.

Chapter 10
Quantum Tori – Proof of Conjecture
1.14

In this Chapter, we apply the main results of this chapter to quantum tori. We focus on the spectral triples associated with the square-root Laplacian and the Dirac operator. In particular, we shall prove Conjecture
1.14
. The integration formulas that we obtain are refinements of the integration formulas of
[
136
,
132
,
155
]
.

10.1.
Quantum Tori

Let
\theta=(\theta_{jk})
be a real anti-symmetric
n\times n
-matrix. The noncommutative torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta}
is meant to be the noncommutative space
represented by a
C^{*}
-algebra
C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
generated by
n
-unitaries
U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}
and subject to the relations,

U_{k}U_{j}=e^{2i\pi\theta_{jk}}U_{j}U_{k},\qquad j,k=1,\ldots,n.

For
\theta=0
we recover the
C^{*}
-algebra
C(\mathbb{T}^{n})
of continuous functions on the ordinary torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}=(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{n}
with
U_{j}=e^{ix_{j}}
.

In general, a complete independent set of
C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
is provided by the unitaries,

U^{m}:=U^{m_{1}}\cdots U^{m_{n}},\qquad m\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

The standard trace
\tau_{0}:C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is given by

\tau_{0}(1)=1,\qquad\tau_{0}\big(U^{m}\big)=0\quad\text{if $m\neq 0$}.

This is a positive faithful trace. We then define the space
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
as the Hilbert space completion of
C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
with respect to the inner product

\left\langle x|y\right\rangle=\tau_{0}\big[y^{*}x\big],\qquad x,y\in C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta}).

Note that
\{U^{m};\ m\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
. Moreover, the action of
C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
on itself uniquely extends to a
*
-representation of
C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
; this is just the GNS representation associated with
\tau
. More generally, for any
r\in[1,\infty)
we define the NC
L^{r}
-space
L_{r}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
as in Chapter
3
, which is possible since
\tau_{0}
extends to a continuous trace on the von Neuman algebra
L_{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
generated by
U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}
.

We have a natural
C^{*}
-action of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
on
C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
given by

\alpha_{s}(U^{m})=e^{is\cdot m}U^{m},\qquad m\in\mathbb{Z}^{n},\ s\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

For
\theta=0
this is just the action arising from the action of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
on
\mathbb{T}^{n}
by translation. The canonical derivations
\partial_{1},\ldots,\partial_{n}
are the infinitesimals generators of this action with the convention that

\partial_{j}\big(U_{j}\big)=iU_{j},\qquad\partial_{j}\big(U_{k}\big)=0,\quad k\neq j.

The smooth NC torus
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
is precisely the
*
-subalgebra of smooth vectors for this action. Equivalently,

C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})=\left\{u=\sum u_{m}U^{m};(u_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{Z}^{n})\right\},

where
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{Z}^{n})
is the space of complex-valued sequences with rapid decay. For
\theta=0
we recover the description of smooth functions on
\mathbb{T}^{n}
in terms of the rapid decay of their
Fourier coefficients. Note that
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
is closed under holomorphic functional calculus and is a Fréchet algebra with respect to the seminorms,

u\rightarrow\sup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}(1+|m|)^{N}|u_{m}|,\qquad N\geq 0.

In addition, given any
s\geq 0
, the Sobolev space
W_{2}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
is defined by

W_{2}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta}):=\Big\{u=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}u_{m}U^{k}\in L_{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta});\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}(1+|m|^{2})^{s}|u_{m}|^{2}<\infty\Big\}.

We refer to
[
100
,
202
]
, and the references therein, for additional background on quantum tori.

10.2.
Proof of Conjecture
1.14
and Integration Formulas

Our main focus is the Laplacian
\Delta:=-(\partial_{1}^{2}+\cdots+\partial_{n}^{2})
. This is a non-negative selfadjoint operator on
L_{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
with domain
W_{2}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
. We have

(10.1)

\Delta\big(U^{k})=|k|^{2}U^{k},\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

Thus,
\Delta
is isospectral to the Laplacian on the ordinary torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}
. In particular, the partial inverse
\Delta^{-1}
is in the weak Schatten class
\mathscr{L}_{n/2,\infty}
.

We shall prove Conjecture
1.14
as a direct application of Theorem
1.5
.

Proposition 10.1
.

The following

(10.2)

\left(C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta}),L_{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta}),\sqrt{\Delta}\right).

is an
n
-summable spectral triple.

Proof.

This result is somewhat well-known (see, e.g.,
[
87
,
136
]
). The bulk of the proof is showing that the commutators
[\sqrt{\Delta},a]
are bounded as
a
ranges over
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
. We briefly sketch two proofs of this fact for the reader’s convenience.

The first proof relies on the pseudodifferential calculus on NC tori of Connes
[
51
]
and Baaj
[
9
]
. We refer to
[
100
,
101
]
for a detailed account on this
\Psi
DO calculus. The square root
\sqrt{\Delta}
is a
\Psi
DO of order 1 in this pseudodifferential calculus. Its principal symbol is
|\xi|
. Thus, if
a\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
, then
a\sqrt{\Delta}
and
\sqrt{\Delta}a
are
\Psi
DOs of order
1
whose principal symbols are both equal to
|\xi|a
. It follows that their difference, i.e., the commutator
[\sqrt{\Delta},a]
, is a
\Psi
DO of order
0
, and hence is bounded.

This fact can be checked directly without any reference to
\Psi
DOs. First,
\operatorname{dom}(\sqrt{\Delta})=W_{2}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
is a (left-)module over
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
(see, e.g.,
[
187
]
), and so
