a(\operatorname{dom}(\sqrt{\Delta}))\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(\sqrt{\Delta})
. Moreover, if we put
a=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}a_{k}U^{k}
with
(a_{k})\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{Z}^{n})
, then

\displaystyle\big(a\sqrt{\Delta})(U^{m})=|m|aU^{m}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}|m|a_{k}U^{k}U^{m},

\displaystyle\big(\sqrt{\Delta}a)(U^{m})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}a_{k}\sqrt{\Delta}(U^{k}U^{m})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}|m+k|a_{k}U^{k}U^{m}.

Thus,

\big[\sqrt{\Delta},a\big](U^{m})=\rho(m)U^{m},\qquad\text{where}\ \rho(m):=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\big(|m|-|m+k|\big)a_{k}U^{k}.

Here
(a_{k})\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{Z}^{n})
. Thus, given any
N\geq 1
, the triangular inequality
||m+k|-|k||\leq|k|
gives

\displaystyle\big\|(1+\Delta)^{N}\rho(m)\big\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\big||m|-|m+k|\big|\left(1+|k|^{2}\right)^{N}|a_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}|k|\left(1+|k|^{2}\right)^{N}|a_{k}|<\infty.

We then may apply Schur’s test as in the proof of
[
101
, Proposition 10.1]
to check that
[\sqrt{\Delta},a]
is bounded.

Finally, as
\Delta^{-1}\in\mathscr{L}_{n/2,\infty}
, we see that
\Delta^{-1/2}\in\mathscr{L}_{n,\infty}
, and so
(C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta}),L_{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta}),\sqrt{\Delta})
is an
n
-summable spectral triple. This proves the result.
∎

Proposition 10.2
.

Given any
a\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
, as
t\rightarrow 0^{+}
we have

\operatorname{Tr}\big[ae^{-t\Delta}\big]=\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}t^{-\frac{n}{2}}\left[\tau_{0}(a)+\operatorname{O}\big(e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}}{t}}\big)\right].

Proof.

In view of (
10.1
) we have

\operatorname{Tr}\big[ae^{-t\Delta}\big]=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\big\langle ae^{-t\Delta}(U^{m})|U^{m}\big\rangle=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}e^{-t|m|^{2}}\left\langle aU^{m}|U^{m}\right\rangle.

Here
\left\langle aU^{m}|U^{m}\right\rangle=\tau[(U^{m})^{*}aU^{m}]=\tau[aU^{m}(U^{m})^{*}]=\tau_{0}(a)
. Thus,

(10.3)

\operatorname{Tr}\big[ae^{-t\Delta}\big]=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}e^{-t|m|^{2}}\tau_{0}(a)=\vartheta(t)^{n}\tau_{0}(a),\qquad\text{with}\ \vartheta(t):=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-tk^{2}}.

The function
\vartheta(t)
is a Jacobi theta function. By Poisson’s summation formula, we have

\vartheta(t)=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}k^{2}}{t}}=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}+2\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}\sum_{k\geq 1}e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}k^{2}}{t}}.

Note that, for
0<t\leq 1
, we have

\sum_{k\geq 1}e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}k^{2}}{t}}=\sum_{k\geq 1}e^{-\left(\frac{1}{t}-1\right)\pi^{2}k^{2}}\cdot e^{-\pi^{2}k^{2}}\leq e^{-\left(\frac{1}{t}-1\right)\pi^{2}}\sum_{k\geq 1}e^{-\pi^{2}k^{2}}.

Thus,

\vartheta(t)=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}\left(1+\operatorname{O}\big(e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}}{t}}\big)\right)\qquad\text{as $t\rightarrow 0^{+}$}.

Combining this with (
10.3
) gives the result.
The proof is complete.
∎

As
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
is a holomorphically closed Fréchet subalgebra of
C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
, we have the following consequence of Proposition
10.2
.

Corollary 10.3
.

Condition
(H)
and Condition
(W)
hold with

\tau(a):=\frac{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+1\right)}\tau_{0}(a)=\hat{c}(n)\tau_{0}(a),\qquad\text{where}\ \hat{c}(n):=|\mathbb{B}^{n}|.

In addition, we have the following Cwikel-type estimates.

Proposition 10.4

(
[
132
,
134
]
)
.

Suppose that
r\neq 1
and
r^{\prime}=\max(r,1)
, or
r=1<r^{\prime}
. There is
C_{rr^{\prime}}>0
such that

\big\|(1+\Delta)^{-\frac{n}{4r}}a(1+\Delta)^{-\frac{n}{4r}}\big\|_{r,\infty}\leq C_{rr^{\prime}}\|a\|_{r^{\prime}}\qquad\forall a\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}.

It follows from Proposition
10.4
that Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
) is satisfied, with

(10.4)

\mathscr{V}_{r}=L_{r}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})\ (r>1),\qquad\mathscr{V}_{r}=L_{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})\ (r=1<r^{\prime}),\qquad\mathscr{V}_{r}=L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})\ (r<1).

All this shows that the assumptions for Proposition
1.4
, Theorem
1.5
, and Theorem
1.6
are satisfied. In particular, we obtain the following spectral asymptotics.

Proposition 10.5
.

Let
q>0
, set
r=nq^{-1}
, and suppose that, either
r\neq 1
and
r^{\prime}=\max(r,1)
, or
r=1<r^{\prime}
. For every
x\in L_{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
, we have

(10.5)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{n}{q}}\mu_{j}\big(\Delta^{-\frac{q}{4}}x\Delta^{-\frac{q}{4}}\big)=\left(\hat{c}(n)\tau_{0}\big[|x|^{\frac{n}{q}}\big]\right)^{\frac{q}{n}},

(10.6)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{n}{q}}\lambda^{\pm}_{j}\big(\Delta^{-\frac{q}{4}}x\Delta^{-\frac{q}{4}}\big)=\left(\hat{c}(n)\tau_{0}\big[(x_{\pm})^{\frac{n}{q}}\big]\right)^{\frac{q}{n}}\quad(\text{if}\ x^{*}=x).

Proof.

As mentioned above Condition (W) holds with
\tau=\hat{c}(n)\tau_{0}
. Moreover, Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
) holds with
\mathscr{V}_{r}=L_{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
. Applying Proposition
1.4
then gives the result.
∎

We are now in a position to prove Conjecture
1.14
.

Proof of Conjecture
1.14
.

Condition (W) holds with
\tau=\hat{c}(n)\tau_{0}
and Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
) holds with
\mathscr{V}_{r}=L_{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
. Applying
Theorem
1.5
then shows that, given any
q>0
and
V=V^{*}\in L_{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
,
for every energy level
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

(10.7)

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{n}N\big(h^{2q}\Delta^{q}+V;\lambda\big)=\hat{c}(n)\tau_{0}\big[(V-\lambda)_{-}^{\frac{n}{2q}}\big],\qquad\hat{c}(n):=|\mathbb{B}^{n}|.

Conjecture
1.14
is thus proved.
∎

Remark 10.6
.

A proof of the semiclassical Weyl law (
10.7
) for
n\geq 3
and
q=1
with
V\in C(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
is given in
[
137
]
. As mentioned in Chapter
1
, the approach
[
137
]
does not allow to deal with 2-summable spectral triples. As a result, it cannot be used to get semiclassical Weyl laws on quantum 2-tori.

Remark 10.7
.

We refer to
[
133
]
for a
conjectural
“curved” version of the semiclassical Weyl law (
10.7
). In the terminology of
[
133
]
a curved quantum torus is a quantum torus equipped with a Riemannian metric
g
in the sense of
[
102
,
161
]
. In this case the flat Laplacian is replaced by the Laplacian
\Delta_{g}
associated with
g
as in
[
102
]
. We stress that for an arbitrary Riemannian metric the commutators
[\sqrt{\Delta_{g}},a]
,
a\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
, need not be bounded, and so
\sqrt{\Delta_{g}}
does not provide a spectral triple. As a result, although we have Cwikel-type estimates in the curved setting (see
[
133
]
) we cannot expect getting semiclassical Weyl laws in the curved case as a direct application of the results of this monograph.

Finally, applying Theorem
1.6
leads to the following integration formulas.

Theorem 10.8
.

For every
x\in L_{r}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
,
r>1
,
the operators
\Delta^{-n/4}x\Delta^{-n/4}
and
|\Delta^{-n/4}x\Delta^{-n/4}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

(10.8)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\Delta^{-\frac{n}{4}}x\Delta^{-\frac{n}{4}}=\hat{c}(n)\tau_{0}\big[x\big],

(10.9)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\big|\Delta^{-\frac{n}{4}}x\Delta^{-\frac{n}{4}}\big|=\hat{c}(n)\tau\big[|x|\big].

Remark 10.9
.

The integration formula (
10.8
) is a refinement of the integration formulas of
[
136
,
132
,
155
]
. In particular, in
[
136
,
132
,
155
]
the focus is on strong measurability, whereas Theorem
10.8
provides spectral measurability, which is a stronger property. The integration formula
10.9
is new.

Remark 10.10
.

We refer to
[
133
]
(see also
[
155
]
) for curved versions of the integration formula (
10.8
). In
[
133
,
155
]
the focus is on strong measurability. It would be interesting to establish spectral measurability. It would be interesting to get a curved version of the integration formula (
10.9
).

10.3.
Dirac Spectral Triple

The approach to get the above semiclassical Weyl law and integration formulas for the spectral triple (
10.2
) applies
verbatim
to the
Dirac spectral triple of
[
87
, §§12.3]
.

As the ordinary torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}
is parallelizable by the vector fields
\partial_{x_{1}},\ldots,\partial_{x_{n}}
, its spinor bundle is trivial with fiber
\not{S}_{n}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}
, where
N:=2^{[n/2]}
. We then define the
spinor module
of
\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta}
as the free
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
-module,

C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta};\not{S}_{n}):=C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{N}=C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})^{N},

where
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
acts trivially on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
. We equip it with the Hermitian metric,

(a,b):=\sum_{j}b_{j}^{*}a_{j},\qquad a=(a_{j})\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})^{N},\quad b=(b_{j})\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})^{N}.

We then denote by
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta};\not{S}_{n})
the completion of
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta};\not{S}_{n})
with respect to the inner product,

\left\langle x|y\right\rangle:=\tau_{0}\left[(x,y)\right]=\sum_{j}\tau\left[y_{j}^{*}x_{j}\right],\qquad x=(x_{j}),\quad y=(y_{j}).

Note that
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
acts by left multiplication on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta};\not{S}_{n})
.

Let
\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{n}
be the canonical Clifford matrices in
M_{N}(\mathbb{C})
subject to the relations,

\gamma_{j}^{*}=-\gamma_{j},\qquad\gamma_{j}\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{k}\gamma_{j}=-2\delta_{jk},\qquad j,k=1,\ldots,n.

The 2nd set of relations mean that
\gamma_{j}^{2}=-1
and
\gamma_{j}\gamma_{k}=-\gamma_{k}\gamma_{j}
if
j\neq k
. Combining this with the relation
\gamma_{j}^{*}=-\gamma_{j}
show that each matrix
\gamma_{j}
is unitary.

Definition 10.11

(
[
87
]
)
.

The
Dirac operator

\not{D}:C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}_{\theta}^{m};\not{S}_{n})\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}_{\theta}^{m};\not{S}_{n})
is defined by

\not{D}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\partial_{j}\otimes\gamma_{j}.

Remark 10.12
.

For
\theta=0
we recover the Dirac operator on the ordinary torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}
.

Lemma 10.13
.

We have

(10.10)

\displaystyle\not{D}^{*}=\not{D},\qquad\not{D}^{2}=\Delta\otimes 1,

(10.11)

\displaystyle\left[\not{D},a\right]=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\delta_{j}(a)\otimes\gamma_{j}\qquad\text{for all}\ a\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta}).

Remark 10.14
.

The proofs of the above property are the same with the Dirac operator on
\mathbb{T}^{n}
or
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The first equality in (
10.10
) stems from the fact that the derivations
\partial_{j}
and the Clifford matrices
\gamma_{j}
are (formally) skew-adjoint. The 2nd equality is a well-known consequence of the relations
\gamma_{j}\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{k}\gamma_{j}=-2\delta_{jk}
. Finally, for (
10.11
) it is enough to note that

\left[\not{D},a\right]=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}[\partial_{j},a]\otimes\gamma_{j}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\delta_{j}(a)\otimes\gamma_{j}.

The Dirac operator
\not{D}
is unitarily equivalent to the Dirac operator
\not{D}_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}
on the ordinary torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}
. Indeed,
let
\mathscr{U}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n})
be the unitary operator given by

\mathscr{U}(U^{m})=e^{im\cdot x},\qquad m\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

Note that
\mathscr{U}
maps
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
onto
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n})
. We then have

\not{D}=(\mathscr{U}\otimes 1)^{*}\not{D}_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}(\mathscr{U}\otimes 1).

It follows that
\not{D}
is essentially selfadjoint and the domain of its closure is

W_{2}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta};\not{S}_{n}):=W_{2}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{N}.

Note also that by the 2nd equality in (
10.10
) we have
|\not{D}|^{-1}=\Delta^{-1/2}\otimes 1\in\mathscr{L}_{n,\infty}
. Combining this with (
10.11
) we then get the following result.

Proposition 10.15
.

The following triple

\left(C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta}),L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n};\not{S}_{n}),\not{D}\right)

is an
n
-summable spectral triple.

As
\not{D}^{2}=\Delta\otimes 1
, given any
a\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
, we have

\operatorname{Tr}\left[ae^{-\not{D}^{2}}\right]=\operatorname{Tr}\left[\left(ae^{-t\Delta}\right)\otimes 1\right]=N\operatorname{Tr}\left[ae^{-t\Delta}\right].

Combining this with Proposition
10.2
we then deduce that Condition (H) and Condition (W) hold with

\tau(a):=N\frac{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+1\right)}\tau_{0}(a)=\not{\hat{c}}(n)\tau_{0}(a),\qquad\text{where}\ \not{\hat{c}}(n):=N|\mathbb{B}^{n}|=2^{\left[\frac{n}{2}\right]}|\mathbb{B}^{n}|.

In addition, Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
) hold exactly as with the square-root Laplacian
\sqrt{\Delta}
, i.e., as in (
10.4
). This allows us to apply Theorem
1.5
and Theorem
1.6
to get the following semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas.

Theorem 10.16
.

Let
q>0
, set
r=2nq^{-1}
, and suppose that either
r\neq 1
and
r^{\prime}=\max(r,1)
, or
r=1<r^{\prime}
. Given any
V=V^{*}\in L_{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
,
for every energy level
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{n}N\big(h^{2q}|\not{D}|^{2q}+V;\lambda\big)=\not{\hat{c}}(n)\tau_{0}\big[(V-\lambda)_{-}^{\frac{n}{2q}}\big].

Theorem 10.17
.

For every
x\in L_{r}(\mathbb{T}^{n}_{\theta})
,
r>1
,
the operators
|\not{D}|^{-n/2}x|\not{D}|^{-n/2}
and
||\not{D}|^{-n/2}x|\not{D}|^{-n/2}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

(10.12)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int|\not{D}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}x|\not{D}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}=\not{\hat{c}}(n)\tau_{0}\big[x\big],

(10.13)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\big||\not{D}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}x|\not{D}|^{-\frac{n}{2}}\big|=\not{\hat{c}}(n)\tau\big[|x|\big].

Remark 10.18
.

The integration formula for quantum tori of
[
136
]
is actually stated for the above Dirac spectral triple. Therefore, we see that
(
10.12
) is a refinement of the integration formula in
[
136
]
. The integration formula (
10.13
) is new.

Remark 10.19
.

It would be interesting to have a curved version of Theorem
10.17
.

Chapter 11
Sub-Riemannian Manifolds

In this Chapter, we apply the main results of this monograph to get semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas for Schrödinger operators associated with fractional powers of sub-Laplacians on sub-Riemannian manifolds. These results are new and connect nicely with a string of recent results on spectral asymptotics and quantum limits in sub-Riemannian geometry (see, e.g.,
[
11
,
48
,
50
,
75
,
76
,
77
,
78
,
121
,
138
]
). In addition, in the contact case we obtain stronger forms of the integration formulas of
[
114
,
153
]
.

11.1.
Sub-Riemannian manifolds and sub-Laplacians

Throughout this chapter we let
M^{n}
be a manifold equipped with a sub-Riemannian structure
(H^{m},g_{H})
. That is,
H
is a Lie-bracket generating subbundle of the tangent bundle
TM
of rank
m
and
g_{H}
is a sub-Riemannian metric on
H
, i.e., a smooth field of positive-definite inner products on its fibers. The vector fields taking values in
H
are called
horizontal vector fields
. The Lie-bracket generating condition
H
then ensures that, at every point
x_{0}\in M
, we have a filtration,

(11.1)

H(x_{0})=:H_{1}(x_{0})\subseteq H_{2}(x_{0})\subseteq\cdots\subseteq H_{r}(x_{0})=TM(x_{0}),

where
H_{w}(x_{0})
,
w\geq 2
, is the subspace spanned by iterated Lie brackets,

[X_{i_{1}},[X_{i_{2}},\cdots[X_{i_{w-1}},X_{i_{w}}]\cdots]](x_{0})

with
X_{i_{1}},\ldots,X_{i_{w}}
ranging over all horizontal vector fields near
x_{0}
.

Sub-Riemannian structures and Lie-bracket generating distributions naturally occur in a variety of settings. This includes control theory of non-holonomic systems of vector fields as those that comes into play in computer vision and image processing, Asian option in finance, dynamics of polymer, and distributions of clusters in space in astronomy (see
[
35
]
). Sub-Riemannian structures also appear on (graded) nilpotent Lie groups and their quotients by lattices (i.e., nilmanifolds). To a large extent graded nilpotent groups provide “infinitesimal” models for sub-Riemannian manifolds.

Sub-Riemannian structures further appear in the setting of parabolic geometry
[
38
]
, including boundary-geometry of symmetric spaces of rank
\geq 2
. Contact manifolds (e.g., cosphere bundles of positive line bundles) and their various generalizations yield additional examples. A related class of examples is provided by boundaries of complex domains (e.g., odd-dimensional Euclidean spheres), and more generally finite-type Cauchy-Riemann manifolds. In addition, Engel manifolds are well known step 3 examples. We refer to
[
1
,
45
,
149
]
and the various references therein for more background on sub-Riemannian geometry and more substantial discussions of the above examples.

In the sub-Riemannian setting the analogue of the Laplacian is the horizontal sub-Laplacian
\Delta_{H}:C^{\infty}_{c}(M)\rightarrow C^{\infty}_{c}(M)
. This operator is constructed as follows. Let
\nu
be a smooth measure on
M
. In the equiregular case a natural choice is to take
\nu
to be the Popp measure, since this measure only depends on the data
(H,g_{H})
(see, e.g.,
[
1
,
149
]
). However, we shall not be too specific about the choice of the measure. The datum of
\nu
defines an inner product,

(11.2)

\left\langle u|v\right\rangle=\int_{M}u(x)\overline{v(x)}d\nu(x),\qquad u,v\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M).

We denote by
L^{2}_{\nu}(M)
the corresponding
L^{2}
-space. The horizontal “exterior derivative”
d_{H}:C^{\infty}(M)\rightarrow C^{\infty}(M,H^{*})
is defined by

d_{H}u=\left(du\right)_{|H},\qquad u\in C^{\infty}(M).

By duality
g_{H}
defines a (smooth) field
g_{H^{*}}
of positive-definite inner products on the fibers of
H^{*}
, and so we get an inner-product on
C^{\infty}_{c}(M,H^{*})
given by

\left\langle\omega|\eta\right\rangle=\int_{M}g_{{H^{*}}}\!\!\left(\omega(x),\eta(x)\right)d\nu(x),\qquad\omega,\eta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M,H^{*}).

The horizontal sub-Laplacian
\Delta_{H}
is defined by

\left\langle\Delta_{H}u|v\right\rangle=\left\langle d_{H}u|d_{H}v\right\rangle\qquad\forall u,v\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M).

That is,
\Delta_{H}
is the operator of the sequilinear form
(u,v)\rightarrow\left\langle d_{H}u|d_{H}v\right\rangle
.

The operator
\Delta_{H}
is a 2nd order differential operator which is formally selfadjoint with respect to the inner product (
11.2
). In the Riemannian case, i.e.,
H=TM
and
g_{H}=g
is a Riemannian metric, we recover the usual Laplacian associated with the data
(g,\nu)
. In general, if
X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}
is a local orthonormal frame of
H
, then, locally, we have

(11.3)

\displaystyle\Delta_{H}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq m}X_{j}^{*}X_{j}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq m}\left(-X_{j}^{2}+\operatorname{div}_{\nu}(X_{j})X_{j}\right).

where
\operatorname{div}_{\nu}
is the divergence of vector fields with respect to the measure
\nu
. In particular, if
m<n
, then
\Delta_{H}
is not elliptic. However, by a celebrated result of Hörmander
[
104
]
, the Lie-bracket generating condition ensures that
\Delta_{H}
and the heat operator
\Delta_{H}+\partial_{t}
on
M\times\mathbb{R}
are both hypoelliptic.

11.2.
Weyl laws and nilpotent approximation

From now on, we assume that
M
is compact. In this case Hörmander’s result implies that
\Delta_{H}
is essentially selfadjoint and has compact resolvent. Therefore, its spectrum consists of non-negative isolated eigenvalues of finite multiplicity. We thus can list its positive eigenvalues as a non-decreasing sequence
\lambda_{0}(\Delta_{H})\leq\lambda_{1}(\Delta_{H})\leq\cdots
as in (
3.3
).

To get a Weyl law of the form we further assume that the sub-Riemannian structure is
equiregular
. This means that in the filtration (
11.1
) the step
r
can be chosen independently of
x_{0}
and the dimensions
\dim H_{w}(x_{0})
,
w\geq 2
, do not vary with
x_{0}
. Most of the geometric examples of sub-Riemannian manifolds mentioned above are equiregular sub-Riemannian manifolds.

For
w=2,\ldots,r
, the vector spaces
H_{w}(x_{0})
,
x_{0}\in M
, can then be organized as smooth subbundles
H_{w}\subseteq TM
. We thus get a filtration of subbundles,

\{0\}=H_{0}\subseteq H_{1}=H\subseteq H_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq H_{r}=TM.

We make the convention that
H_{r-1}\subsetneq TM
. The above filtration is further compatible with the Lie bracket of vector fields, i.e.,

[H_{w},H_{w^{\prime}}]\subseteq H_{w+w^{\prime}}\qquad\text{if}\ w+w^{\prime}\leq r.

Manifolds equipped with such filtrations are often called
filtered manifolds
(see, e.g.,
[
38
]
).

For
j=1,\ldots,n
, the weight
w_{j}
is defined by

w_{j}=w\quad\textup{if}\ \operatorname{rk}H_{w-1}<j\leq\operatorname{rk}H_{w},\quad 1\leq w\leq r.

The
sR dimension
of
M
then is

N:=\sum_{1\leq w\leq r}w\dim\left(H_{w}/H_{w-1}\right)=w_{1}+\cdots+w_{n}.

This is also the Hausdorff dimension with respect to the Carathéodory metric defined by
(H,g_{H})
(see, e.g.,
[
1
]
).

Under the above assumptions, Métivier
[
145
]
obtained a Weyl law for the sub-Laplacian
\Delta_{H}
. Namely, as
j\rightarrow\infty
we have

(11.4)

\lambda_{j}(\Delta_{H})\sim j^{\frac{2}{N}}\left(\int_{M}\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)d\nu(x)\right)^{-\frac{2}{N}}

where
\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)
is some positive smooth function on
M
(see also
[
143
,
144
]
in the contact case).
Métivier’s approach relies on approximating at every point
x_{0}\in M
the spectral function of
\Delta_{H}
by that of a model operator
\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}
defined on a suitable nilpotent graded Lie group (see below). The idea to use harmonic analysis on nilpotent groups to study sub-Laplacians goes back to E. Stein (see
[
81
,
162
]
).

Alternatively, the hypoellipticity of
\Delta_{H}+\partial_{t}
ensures that the heat semigroup
e^{-t\Delta_{H}}
,
t>0
, has a smooth kernel
k_{t}(x,y)
, i.e.,

e^{-t\Delta_{H}}u(x)=\int_{M}k_{t}(x,y)u(y)d\nu(y),\qquad u\in L^{2}_{\nu}(M).

In particular,

\left(\Delta_{X,x}+\partial_{t}\right)k_{t}(x,y)=0\qquad\text{on}\ M\times\mathbb{R}.

As
t\rightarrow 0^{+}
, and uniformly on
M
, we have a short time asymptotic expansion,

(11.5)

k_{t}(x,x)\sim t^{-\frac{N}{2}}\sum_{j\geq 0}t^{\frac{j}{2}}a_{j}(\Delta_{H})(x),

where the coefficients
a_{j}(\Delta_{H})(x)
are smooth (see
[
16
,
49
,
67
,
117
,
195
]
; see also
[
14
]
for the step
2
case). Combining this with Karamata’s Tauberian theorem (see, e.g.,
[
17
, Theorem 2.42]
) then gives the Weyl law (
11.4
), with

(11.6)

\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)=\Gamma\left(\frac{N}{2}+1\right)^{-1}\!\!\!a_{0}(\Delta_{H})(x).

As in Métivier’s approach, the leading coefficient
a_{0}(\Delta_{H})(x)
is obtained by approximating at any point
x_{0}\in M
the heat kernel
k_{t}(x,y)
by
the heat kernel of the model operator
\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}
, or equivalently, by the fundamental solution of the heat operator
\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}+\partial_{t}
.

The underpinning idea behind the construction of the model operator
\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}
is the fact that for filtered manifolds the natural notion of tangent space at a point is provided by graded nilpotent Lie groups rather than by vector spaces. As observed by Tanaka
[
196
]
(see also
[
38
]
) the Lie bracket of vector fields induces a smooth field of Lie brackets on the graded bundle,

(11.7)

\mathfrak{g}M:=\mathfrak{g}_{1}M\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{r}M,\qquad\text{where}\ \mathfrak{g}_{w}M:=H_{w}/H_{w-1}.

More precisely, if
w+w^{\prime}\leq r
, there is a smooth bilinear bundle map
\mathcal{L}_{w,w^{\prime}}:\mathfrak{g}_{w}M\times_{M}\mathfrak{g}_{w^{\prime}}M\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}_{w+w^{\prime}}M
such that if
x\in M
and
X
(resp.,
Y
) is a smooth section of
H_{w}
(resp.,
H_{w^{\prime}}
) near
x
, and we denote by
\xi
(resp.,
\eta
) the class of
X(x)
(resp.,
Y(x)
) in
\mathfrak{g}_{w}M(x)
(resp.,
\mathfrak{g}_{w^{\prime}}M(x)
), then

(11.8)

\mathcal{L}_{w,w^{\prime}}\left(\xi(x),\eta(x)\right)=\textup{class of $[X,Y](x)$ in $\mathfrak{g}_{w+w^{\prime}}M(x)$}.

That is, the r.h.s. depends only on the classes of
X(x)
and
Y(x)
in
\mathfrak{g}_{w}M(x)
and
\mathfrak{g}_{w^{\prime}}M(x)
, respectively. The field of Lie brackets
[\cdot,\cdot]:\mathfrak{g}M\times_{M}\mathfrak{g}M\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}M
is then such that, if
x\in M
and
(\xi,\eta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{w}M(x)\times\mathfrak{g}_{w^{\prime}}M(x)
, then

(11.9)

[\xi,\eta]=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{L}_{w,w^{\prime}}(\xi,\eta)&\text{if $w+w^{\prime}\leq r$},\\
0&\text{if $w+w^{\prime}>r.$ }\end{array}\right.

In particular, we have

[\mathfrak{g}_{w}M,\mathfrak{g}_{w^{\prime}}M]=\mathfrak{g}_{w+w^{\prime}}M\quad(w+w^{\prime}\leq r),\qquad[\mathfrak{g}_{w}M,\mathfrak{g}_{w^{\prime}}M]=\{0\}\quad(w+w^{\prime}>r).

Therefore,
\mathfrak{g}M
is a smooth bundle of graded nilpotent Lie algebras of step
r
. Note also that the grading (
11.7
) gives rise to Lie algebra isomorphisms

\delta_{t}:\mathfrak{g}M(x)\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}M(x)
,
t>0
, given by

(11.10)

\delta_{t}(\xi)=t^{w}\xi,\qquad\xi\in\mathfrak{g}_{w}M(x).

Let
GM
be the associated Lie group bundle. More precisely, as a manifold
GM
is just
\mathfrak{g}M
. At every point
x\in M
, the group law on
GM(x)
is such that the exponential map
\exp:\mathfrak{g}M(x)\rightarrow GM(x)
is the identity map, i.e., it is given by the Dynkin product,

\displaystyle\xi\cdot\eta

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{(-1)^{n+1}}{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}\\
\alpha_{j}+\beta_{j}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{(|\alpha|+|\beta|)^{-1}}{\alpha!\beta!}(\operatorname{ad}_{\xi})^{\alpha_{1}}(\operatorname{ad}_{\eta})^{\beta_{1}}\cdots(\operatorname{ad}_{\xi})^{\alpha_{n}}(\operatorname{ad}_{\eta})^{\beta_{n}-1}\eta

\displaystyle=\xi+\eta+\frac{1}{2}[\xi,\eta]+\frac{1}{12}\left([\xi,[\xi,\eta]]+[\eta,[\eta,\xi]]\right)-\frac{1}{24}[\eta,[\xi,[\xi,\eta]]]+\cdots.

where
\operatorname{ad}_{\xi}(\eta)=[\xi,\eta]
is the adjoint representation of
\mathfrak{g}M(x)
. The automorphisms (
11.10
) then are Lie group automorphisms of
GM(x)
.

Given any
x_{0}\in M
, let
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
be a local tangent frame near
x_{0}
such that
\{X_{j};w_{j}\leq w\}
is a local frame of
H_{w}
for
w=1,\ldots,r
. We shall call such a frame a (local)
graded tangent frame
. Let
\overline{\xi}_{j}(x)
be the class of
X_{j}(x)
in
\mathfrak{g}_{w_{j}}M(x)=H_{w_{j}}(x)/H_{w_{j}-1}(x)
. Then
\{\xi_{j}(x);w_{j}=w\}
is a local frame of
\mathfrak{g}_{w}M
for
w=1,\ldots,r
, and so
\{\xi_{1}(x),\ldots,\xi_{n}(x)\}
is a local graded frame of
\mathfrak{g}M
near
x_{0}
. We then denote by
X_{j}^{x_{0}}
the left-invariant vector field on
GM(x_{0})
generated by
\xi_{j}(x_{0})
, i.e., the unique left-invariant vector field such that
X_{j}^{x_{0}}(0)=\xi_{j}(x_{0})
in
\mathfrak{g}M(x_{0})=T_{0}(GM(x_{0}))
.

The observation, which goes back to
[
81
,
162
]
, is that
X_{j}^{x_{0}}
is an appropriate approximation of
X_{j}
near
x_{0}
. More precisely, the graded basis
\{\xi_{1}(x_{0}),\ldots,\xi_{n}(x_{0})\}
yields identifications of
\mathfrak{g}M(x_{0})
and
GM(x_{0})
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
equipped with the corresponding Lie algebra and Lie group structures. Under this identification the dilations (
11.10
) are given by

\delta_{t}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\big(t^{w_{1}}x_{1},\ldots,t^{w_{n}}x_{n}\big),\qquad x_{j}\in\mathbb{R},\ t>0.

The vector fields
X_{j}^{x_{0}}
are homogeneous with respect to these dilations. Namely,

\delta_{t}^{*}X_{j}^{x_{0}}=t^{-w_{j}}X_{j}^{x_{0}}\qquad\forall t>0.

As it turns out, we always can find local coordinates centered at
x_{0}
such that, for
j=1,\ldots,n
, we have

(11.11)

\delta_{t}^{*}X_{j}=t^{-w_{j}}X_{j}^{x_{0}}+\operatorname{O}\left(t^{-w_{j}+1}\right)\qquad\text{as}\ t\rightarrow 0^{+},

where
\operatorname{O}(t^{-w_{j}+1})
is meant in the sense of
C^{\infty}
-topology (see, e.g.,
[
43
]
for the precise meaning). In the terminology of
[
44
]
this kind of coordinates are called Carnot coordinates. This a special class of privileged coordinates in the sense of
[
15
,
94
]
. The canonical coordinates of the first type of
[
94
,
162
]
are instances of Carnot coordinates. In fact, any local coordinate system centred at
x_{0}
can be converted into a system of Carnot coordinates by means of an explicit polynomial change of variables (see
[
44
]
). In the contact setting, Darboux coordinates are another instance of Carnot coordinates.

Because of (
11.11
) the vector field
X_{j}^{x_{0}}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
, is called the model vector field at
x_{0}
of
X_{j}
. This approximation of vector fields in Carnot coordinates extends to differential operators. In particular, by using (
11.3
) and (
11.11
) we get

(11.12)

\delta_{t}^{*}\Delta_{H}=t^{-2}\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}+\operatorname{O}\left(t^{-1}\right)\qquad\text{as}\ t\rightarrow 0^{+},

where the model operator
\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}
is the sum of squares,

\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}:=-\left(X_{1}^{x_{0}}\right)^{2}-\cdots-\left(X_{m}^{x_{0}}\right)^{2}.

Note that
\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}
is the (left-invariant) horizontal sub-Laplacian associated with the left-invariant sub-Riemannian structure associated to the left-invariant distribution and sub-Riemannian metric defined by
X_{1}^{x_{0}},\ldots,X_{m}^{x_{0}}
and the Haar measure of
GM(x_{0})
. In particular,
Hörmander’s theorem ensures that
\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}
and the heat operator
\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}+\partial_{t}
are both hypoelliptic.

Note that the heat operator
\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}+\partial_{t}
is a left-invariant differential operator which is homogeneous of degree
-2
with respect to the dilations,

\delta_{s}(\xi,t)=\left(\delta_{s}(\xi),s^{2}t\right),\qquad(\xi,t)\in GM(x_{0})\times\mathbb{R},\ s>0.

Together with its hypoellipticity this ensures it has a unique fundamental solution
K_{\Delta^{x_{0}}_{H}}(x,t)\in C^{\infty}([GM(x_{0})\times\mathbb{R}]\setminus 0)
(see
[
80
]
). That is,

\left(\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}+\partial_{t}\right)K_{\Delta^{x_{0}}_{H}}(\xi,t)=\delta(x,t)\qquad\text{on}\ GM(x_{0})\times\mathbb{R},

where
\delta(x,t)
is the
\delta
-distribution on
GM(x_{0})\times\mathbb{R}
. In particular,

K_{\Delta^{x_{0}}_{H}}(\cdot,t)=e^{-t\Delta^{x_{0}}_{H}}\delta,\qquad t>0

Note that
K_{\Delta^{x_{0}}_{H}}(x,t)
is homogeneous of degree
-N
with respect to the dilations (
11.10
), i.e.,

K_{\Delta^{x_{0}}_{H}}(\delta_{s}(x),s^{2}t)=s^{-N}K_{\Delta^{x_{0}}_{H}}(x,t)\qquad\forall s>0.

In particular, for
x=0
and
t>0
we get

K_{\Delta^{x_{0}}_{H}}(0,t)=K_{\Delta^{x_{0}}_{H}}\circ\delta_{\sqrt{t}}(0,1)=t^{-\frac{N}{2}}K_{\Delta^{x_{0}}_{H}}(0,1).

The approximation result (
11.12
) for
\Delta_{H}
implies an approximation result at the level of the fundamental solutions for the corresponding heat equations. In particular, in Carnot coordinates centred at
x_{0}
, as
t\rightarrow 0^{+}
we have

\displaystyle k_{t}(0,0)\nu(0)

\displaystyle=k_{(\sqrt{t})^{2}}\left(\delta_{\sqrt{t}}(0),\delta_{\sqrt{t}}(0)\right)\nu\left(\delta_{\sqrt{t}}(0)\right)

(11.13)

\displaystyle=t^{-\frac{N}{2}}K_{\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}}(0,1)+\operatorname{O}\left(t^{\frac{1-N}{2}}\right).

Here we denote by
\nu(y)
the (smooth) density of the measure
\nu
with respect to the Lebesgue measure in the local coordinates (i.e.,
\nu=\nu(y)dy
).
Comparing this to (
11.5
) and using (
11.6
) we then obtain

(11.14)

\Gamma\left(\frac{N}{2}+1\right)\gamma(\Delta_{H})(0)\nu(0)=a_{0}(\Delta_{H})(0)\nu(0)=K_{\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}}(0,1).

We refer to
[
16
,
49
,
67
,
195
]
for proper justifications of (
11.13
). The point is that using Carnot coordinates reduces the computation of the spectral density

\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)
to the computation of the fundamental solutions of the model heat operators
\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}+\partial_{t}
,
x_{0}\in M
.

We can give an explicit expression for the fundamental solution
K_{\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}}(0,1)
in terms of the representation theory of
GM(x_{0})
. Let
\widehat{GM(x_{0})}
be the unitary dual of
GM(x_{0})
. Every unitary representation
\pi:GM(x_{0})\rightarrow\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}_{\pi})
gives rise to a representation of the envelopping algebra
d\pi:\mathscr{U}(\mathfrak{g}M(x_{0}))\rightarrow\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}_{\pi}^{\infty})
, where
\mathscr{H}_{\pi}^{\infty}
is the space of smooth vectors of
\pi
. Identifying
\mathscr{U}(\mathfrak{g}M(x_{0}))
with the algebra of left-invariant vector fields on
GM(x_{0})
allows us to define a symbol map,

\sigma\big(\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}};\pi\big):=d\pi\big(\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}\big),\qquad\pi\in\widehat{GM(x_{0})}.

Here
d\pi(\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}})
is regarded as a selfadjoint unbounded operator on
\mathscr{H}_{\pi}
with
\mathscr{H}^{\infty}_{\pi}
as core.

Note that
K_{\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}}(\cdot,t)
,
t>0
, is the convolution kernel of
e^{-t\Delta_{H}}
. Moreover, we have
\sigma(e^{-t\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}};\pi)=e^{-t\sigma(\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}};\pi)}
(see, e.g.,
[
79
, Corollary 4.6]
). Therefore, by using Fourier inversion formula it can be shown (see, e.g.,
[
76
, §4.1]
) that we have

K_{\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}}(x,t)=\int_{\widehat{GM(x_{0})}}\operatorname{Tr}\big[\pi(x)e^{-t\sigma(\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}};\pi}\big]d\mu(\pi),\qquad t>0,

where
\mu
is the Plancherel measure of
\widehat{GM(x_{0})}
. Setting
x=0
and
t=1
then gives

(11.15)

K_{\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}}(0,1)=\int_{\widehat{GM(x_{0})}}\operatorname{Tr}\big[e^{-\sigma\left(\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}};\pi\right)}\big]d\mu(\pi).

In particular, this shows that
K_{\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}}(0,1)
is
>0
, and hence
\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x_{0})
is
>0
thanks to (
11.14
).

11.3.
Semiclassical Weyl law and integration formulas

In the step 2 case the relevant pseudodifferential calculus to study sub-Laplacians is the Heisenberg calculus of Beals-Greiner
[
13
]
and Taylor
[
197
]
. A version of this calculus for arbitrary filtered manifolds was presented by Melin in the unpublished preprint
[
140
]
. A polyhomogeneous version of Melin’s calculus was subsequently constructed by van Erp-Yuncken
[
199
]
in terms of essentially homogeneous distributions on the tangent groupoid of the filtered manifold. In what follows we shall call
\Psi_{H}
DOs the operators in the pseudodifferential calculis of Melin and van Erp-Yuncken.

The square root
\sqrt{\Delta_{H}}
is a
\Psi_{H}
DO of order
1
(see
[
67
,
68
]
; see also
[
154
]
in the step 2 case). If
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, then the model operators at
x_{0}
of
f\sqrt{\Delta_{H}}
and
\sqrt{\Delta_{H}}f
are both equal to
f(x_{0})\sqrt{\Delta_{H}^{x_{0}}}
. Thus, the commutator
[\sqrt{\Delta_{H}},f]
has order
\leq 0
in the
\Psi_{H}
DO calculus, and hence is bounded on
L_{\nu}^{2}(M)
. It follows that we have a spectral triple,

\left(C^{\infty}(M),L_{\nu}^{2}(M),\sqrt{\Delta_{H}}\right).

The Weyl law (
11.4
) implies that the above spectral triple is
N
-summable. Moreover, the heat kernel asymptotics (
11.5
) and (
11.6
) ensures that Condition (H) and Condition (W) holds with

(11.16)

\tau(f)=\int_{M}\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)d\nu(x),\qquad f\in C(M).

This is non-zero positive linear form on
C^{\infty}(M)
. Indeed, by
[
67
, Theorem 1]
the leading coefficient
a_{0}(\Delta_{H})(x)
is
>0
everywhere, and so by (
11.6
) the Weyl density
\gamma(\Delta_{H})
is positive everywhere as well.

In this setting Proposition
1.1
yields the following spectral asymptotics.

Proposition 11.1
.

Given any
q>0
, for every function
f\in C(M)
, we have

(11.17)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{N}}\mu_{j}\big(\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{q}{4}}f\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{q}{4}}\big)=\left(\int_{M}|f(x)|^{\frac{N}{q}}\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)d\nu(x)\right)^{\frac{q}{N}},

(11.18)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}j^{\frac{q}{N}}\lambda_{j}^{\pm}\big(\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{q}{4}}f\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{q}{4}}\big)=\left(\int_{M}f_{\pm}(x)^{\frac{N}{q}}\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)d\nu(x)\right)^{\frac{q}{N}}\quad(\text{if $f$ is real-valued}).

Remark 11.2
.

It is conjectured in
[
157
, Remark 6.11]
and
[
131
, §2.5]
) that spectral asymptotics of the form (
11.17
)– (
11.18
) should hold for negative order
\Psi_{H}
DOs. Proposition
11.1
thus shows that that this conjecture holds for operators of the form

\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{q}{4}}f\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{q}{4}}
,
f\in C(M)
. More generally, this continue to hold if we replace
\Delta_{H}
by any power of a positive Rockland differential operator (
cf
. Remark
11.12
below).

Applying Theorem
1.2
yields the following semiclassical Weyl law in sub-Riemannian geometry.

Theorem 11.3
.

Let
q>0
. Given any potential
V\in C(M,\mathbb{R})
, for any energy level
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

(11.19)

\displaystyle\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{N}N\big(h^{2q}\Delta_{H}^{q}+V;\lambda\big)=\int_{M}(V(x)-\lambda)_{-}^{\frac{N}{2q}}\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)d\nu(x).

Remark 11.4
.

We refer to
[
78
,
138
]
for semiclassical Weyl law on graded nilpotent Lie group of sub-Riemannian dimension
N\geq 4
(see also
[
63
,
122
]
for related results).

Remark 11.5
.

Using a different approach, Fischer-Mikkelsen
[
78
]
recently obtained semiclassical Weyl laws in the setting of nilmanifolds. Note that for nilmanifolds the density
\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)
is constant (see Remark
11.11
below).

In addition, by applying Theorem
1.3
we obtain the following integration formulas.

Theorem 11.6
.

For every function
f\in C(M)
, the operators
f\Delta_{H}^{-N/2}
,
\Delta_{H}^{-N/4}f\Delta_{H}^{-N/4}
, and
|\Delta_{H}^{-N/4}f\Delta_{H}^{-N/4}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

(11.20)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int f\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{N}{2}}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{N}{4}}f\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{N}{4}}=\int_{M}f(x)\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)d\nu(x),

(11.21)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\big|\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{N}{4}}f\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{N}{4}}\big|=\int_{M}|f(x)|\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)d\nu(x).

Remark 11.7
.

The integration formulas (
11.20
)–(
11.21
) are new. In the step 2 case (i.e.,
r=2
) the integration formula (
11.20
) is known (see
[
153
]
; see also
[
114
]
for the contact case). However, even in that case, Theorem
11.6
provides a stronger form of measurability. In fact, in
[
114
, p. 18]
it is conjectured that we should have strong measurability and spectral asymptotics of the form (
11.17
). Therefore, our results establish these conjectures in the far more general setting of (equiregular) sub-Riemannian geometry. Moreover, our approach bypasses the technical considerations in
[
114
]
of the
C^{*}
-approach to the principal symbol. Note that it is really unclear how this approach can be extended to sub-Riemannian manifolds (or more generally filtered manifolds) that not locally equivalent to graded nilpotent Lie groups.

Remark 11.8
.

Unlike in the Riemannian case and Euclidean domain cases (
cf
. Chapter
7
and Chapter
8
), the semiclassical limit (
11.19
) and the NC integrals in (
11.20
)–(
11.21
) are not expressed in terms of the sub-Riemannian volume (i.e., the Popp measure). This is a consequence of the Weyl law (
11.4
), which involves the density
\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)
. Unless in special cases (e.g., contact manifolds or nilmanifolds; see below) this density need not be constant. In the terminology of
[
50
]
the measure
f\rightarrow\int_{M}f(x)\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)d\nu(x)
is called the Weyl’s measure of
(M,H,g_{H})
. Therefore, the integration formula (
11.20
) asserts that the NC integral recaptures the Weyl’s measure.

Remark 11.9
.

If
E
is a Hermitian vector bundle over
M
and
\nabla^{E}:C^{\infty}(M)\rightarrow C^{\infty}(M,H^{*}\otimes E)
is a horizontal connection, then the above results apply
mutatis mutandis
to the connection sub-Laplacian
\Delta_{H,E}:=(\nabla^{E})^{*}\nabla^{E}
. In the above formulas we just need to multiply the spectral density

\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)
by
(\operatorname{rk}E)\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)
, where
\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)
is still given by (
11.14
).

Remark 11.10
.

In the contact case, the density
\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)
can be computed explicitly. This allows us to explicit semiclassical Weyl law and integration formulas for contact manifolds (see Theorem
11.14
and Theorem
11.15
below).

Remark 11.11

(Nilmanifolds)
.

Let
G
be a (simply connected) stratified nilpotent Lie group of step
r
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{r}
(which we identify with
T_{0}G
). Denote by
H
the left-invariant
G
-subbundle of
TG
generated by
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
, and let
g
a left-invariant sub-Riemannian metric on
H
. This defines a left-invariant sub-Riemannian structure on
G
of sR dimension
N=\sum i\dim\mathfrak{g}_{i}
. Note that
g
is uniquely determined by its restriction to
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
and, furthermore, this restriction uniquely defines an inner product on
\mathfrak{g}
which is compatible with its grading (see, e.g.,
[
149
]
). This defines a Lebesgue measure on
\mathfrak{g}
. Pushing-forward it by the exponential map
\exp:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow G
yields a Haar measure
\nu
on
G
, which is precisely its Popp measure in the sense of
[
149
]
.

If
\Gamma\subseteq G
is a co-compact lattice, then
M:=G/\penalty\Gamma
is a compact nilmanifold. The sub-Riemannian structure
(H,g)
on
G
descends to a sub-Riemannian structure on
M
. Moreover, the Popp measure
\nu
on
G
descends to the Popp measure of
M
. We denote by
\Delta_{H}
the corresponding sub-Laplacian. As every point of
M
the model operator of
\Delta_{H}
is the sub-Laplacian
\Delta_{G}
of
G
. It follows that, as in the contact case, the local Weyl density
\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)
is constant and equal to

\gamma_{G}:=\Gamma\left(\frac{N}{2}+1\right)^{-1}K_{\Delta_{G}}(0,1)=\Gamma\left(\frac{N}{2}+1\right)^{-1}\int_{\hat{G}}\operatorname{Tr}\big[e^{-\sigma\left(\Delta_{G};\pi\right)}\big]d\mu(\pi).

Here
K_{G}(x,t)
is the fundamental solution of the heat operator
\Delta_{G}+\partial_{t}
on
G\times\mathbb{R}
and
\mu
is the Plancherel measure of
G
(see also
[
76
,
77
,
78
]
).

Remark 11.12

(Rockland Operators)
.

The results of
[
67
,
68
,
154
]
actually apply to a large class of operators beyond the class of sub-Laplacians, namely, positive Rockland differential operators. In particular, they apply to the Laplacians associated with BGG sequences in parabolic geometry
[
38
]
and their generalizations to filtered manifolds in
[
68
,
171
]
. An instance of such a sequence is the contact complex of Rumin
[
170
]
. As a result, to each generalized BGG sequence we can associate a spectral triple satisfying Condition (H), and so the results of this monograph apply
verbatim
to those spectral triples. The formulas that we obtain are similar to those for sub-Laplacians above. They can be expressed in terms of a spectral density which is computed at each point as in (
11.14
) by using the fundamental solution for the heat equation associated with some model differential operator at that point (see
[
154
]
in the case of the contact complex).

Remark 11.13
.

It is natural to ask for sub-Riemannian versions of the Cwikel-type estimates we have in the Riemannian setting (
cf
. Chapter
8
). A pointer to this direction is provided by the analogues of those estimates for graded nilpotent Lie groups in
[
138
]
. It is expected that a localization argument should allow us to extend these estimates at least to nilmanifolds and contact manifolds, since such manifolds are locally equivalent to graded nilpotent Lie groups. If we were to have such estimates, then Condition (
\textup{C}_{r}
) would be satisfied, with

\mathscr{V}_{r}=L^{r}_{\nu}(M)\ (r>1),\qquad\mathscr{V}_{r}=L\!\log\!L(M)\ (r=1),\qquad\mathscr{V}_{r}=L^{1}_{\nu}(M)\ (r<1).

This would allow us to extend the semiclassical Weyl law (
11.19
) to real-valued potentials in
\mathscr{V}_{r}
with
r=N(2q)^{-1}
. The integration formulas (
11.20
)–(
11.21
) would then hold for all
f\in L\!\log\!L(M)

11.4.
Contact manifolds

It is worth specializing the above results to contact manifolds, since in this case the above considerations are simpler and can be made fully explicit.

From now on we let
(M^{2n+1},\theta)
be a compact (orientable) contact manifold, where
\theta
is a globally defined contact form on
M
, so that the 2-form
d\theta
is non-degenerate on the contact distribution
H=\ker\theta
. Here
H
is a hyperplane bundle, and so we have a step 2 filtration
0\subseteq H\subseteq TM
. In particular, the sR dimension is
N=2n+2
.

Let
J
be a calibrated almost complex structure on
H
, i.e., a smooth section of
\operatorname{End}(H)
such that
J^{2}=-1
and
g_{H}(X,Y)=d\theta(X,JY)
is a sub-Riemannian metric on
H
. We extend
g_{\theta}
to the Riemannian metric on
M
given by

(11.22)

\tilde{g}_{H}(X,Y)=d\theta(X,JY)+\theta(X)\theta(Y),\qquad X,Y\in T_{x}M,\ x\in M.

We denote by
\nu_{\theta}
the corresponding Riemannian measure. This is also the Popp measure associated with
(H,g_{H})
in the sense of
[
149
]
(see Eq. (
11.25
) below). We then let
\Delta_{H}
be the corresponding sub-Laplacian.

In addition, we denote by
X_{0}
the Reeb vector field of
\theta
, i.e., the unique (real) vector field such that
\theta(X_{0})=1
and
\iota_{X_{0}}d\theta=0
.
We also let
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{2n}\}
be a local orthonormal frame of
H
such that
X_{n+j}=JX_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
. This ensures that
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{2n},X_{0}\}
is a graded tangent frame and the corresponding coframe of
T^{*}M
is of the form
\{\theta^{1},\ldots,\theta^{2n},\theta\}
. Note that, for
j,k=1,\ldots,2n
we have
d\theta(X_{j},JX_{k})=g_{H}(X_{j},X_{k})=\delta_{jk}
. As
JX_{j}=X_{n+j}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
, we see that if
1\leq j<k\leq 2n
, then
d\theta(X_{j},X_{k})=-\delta_{k,n+j}
. Combining this with the fact that
\iota_{X_{0}}d\theta=0
we then deduce that

(11.23)

d\theta=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\theta^{j}\wedge\theta^{n+j}.

We thus have

(11.24)

d\theta^{n}\wedge\theta=n!\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{n+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\theta^{n}\wedge\theta^{2n}\wedge\theta.

As
\{\theta^{1},\ldots,\theta^{2n},\theta\}
is an orthonormal coframe with respect to the Riemannian metric (
11.22
) it follows that the measure
\nu_{\theta}
is given by

(11.25)

\nu_{\theta}=\left|\theta^{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\theta^{2n}\wedge\theta\right|=\frac{1}{n!}\left|d\theta^{n}\wedge\theta\right|.

Here we denote by
|\omega|
the smooth (Radon) measure defines by any given
n
-form
\omega
on
M
. Note that
\nu_{\theta}
does not depend on the choice of the almost complex structure
J
.

Bearing this in mind, the graded Lie algebra bundle
\mathfrak{g}M
is just
\mathfrak{g}M=H\oplus(TM/H)
. If
X
and
Y
are horizontal vector fields, then

\displaystyle[X,Y]

\displaystyle=\theta\left([X,Y]\right)X_{0}\quad\bmod H

\displaystyle=-d\theta(X,Y)X_{0}\quad\bmod H.

Therefore, by using (
11.23
) we see that, in the notation of (
11.8
), the bilinear bundle map
\mathcal{L}_{1,1}:H\times H\rightarrow TM/H
(a.k.a. Levi form in the contact setting) is such that, for
1\leq j<k\leq 2n
, we have

\mathcal{L}_{1,1}(X_{j},X_{k})=-\delta_{k,n+j}X_{0}\quad\bmod H.

Thus, given any
x_{0}\in M
, if we set
\xi_{j}=X_{j}(x_{0})\in H(x_{0})
,
j=1,\ldots,2n
, and denote by
\xi_{0}
the class of
X_{0}(x_{0})
in
TM(x_{0})/H(x_{0})
, then the Lie bracket of
\mathfrak{g}M(x_{0})
satisfies

\displaystyle[\xi_{j},\xi_{k}]=-\delta_{j,k-n}\xi_{0},\qquad 0\leq j<k\leq 2n.

That is,
\mathfrak{g}M(x_{0})
agrees with the Heisenberg Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}^{2n+1}
.

In the coordinates
(x^{1},\ldots,x^{2n},x^{0})
defined by the graded basis
\{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{2n},\xi_{0}\}
the group law of
GM(x_{0})
is given by

x\cdot y=\left(x^{1}+y^{1},\ldots,x^{2n}+y^{2n},x^{0}+y^{0}+\frac{1}{2}\sum(x^{n+j}y^{j}-x^{j}y^{n+j})\right).

This is the group law of the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{2n+1}
. The respective model vector fields of the vector fields
X_{j}
,
j=0,1,\ldots,2n
then are

X_{0}^{0}=\partial_{x^{0}},\qquad X_{j}^{0}=\partial_{x^{j}}+\frac{1}{2}x^{n+j}\partial_{x^{0}},\qquad X_{n+j}^{0}=\partial_{x^{n+j}}-\frac{1}{2}x^{j}\partial_{x^{0}},\quad j=1,\ldots,n.

Therefore, the model operator of
\Delta_{H}
is

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}:=

\displaystyle-\left(\left(X_{1}^{x_{0}}\right)^{2}+\cdots+\left(X_{2n}^{x_{0}}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\left\{\left(\partial_{x^{j}}+\frac{1}{2}x^{n+j}\partial_{x^{0}}\right)^{2}+\left(\partial_{x^{n+j}}-\frac{1}{2}x^{j}\partial_{x^{0}}\right)^{2}\right\}.

Note that the model vector fields
X_{0}^{0},\ldots,X_{2n}^{x_{0}}
and the model operator of
\Delta_{0}
do not depend on the point
x_{0}\in M
(which is why
x_{0}
got dropped from the notation).

The fundamental solution
K_{\Delta_{0}}(x,t)
of
\Delta_{0}+\partial_{t}
can be computed explicitly (see, e.g.,
[
13
,
83
,
197
]
; see also
[
154
]
). Namely, if we set
x^{\prime}=(x^{1},\ldots,x^{2n})
, then on
(\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}\times\mathbb{R})\setminus 0
we have

K_{\Delta_{0}}(x,t)=\mathbbm{1}_{(0,\infty)}(t)\frac{1}{2\pi}(4\pi t)^{-n}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{ix^{0}\cdot\xi_{0}}\left(\frac{t\xi_{0}}{\sinh(t\xi_{0})}\right)^{n}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4t}\frac{\tanh(t\xi_{0})}{t\xi_{0}}|x^{\prime}|^{2}\right)d\xi_{0},

where
\mathbbm{1}_{(0,\infty)}(t)
is the characteristic function of the interval
(0,\infty)
. In particular, we get

(11.26)

K_{\Delta_{0}}(0,1)=2(4\pi)^{-(n+1)}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\mu}{\sinh\mu}\right)^{n}d\mu.

Alternatively, the above formula is a special case of the formula (
11.15
) for the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{2n+1}
.

Suppose now that
(x^{1},\ldots,x^{2n},x^{0})
are Carnot coordinates centered at the point
x_{0}\in M
relative to the graded tangent frame
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{2n},X_{0}\}
. Recall that by (
11.25
) we have
n!\nu_{\theta}=|d\theta^{n}\wedge\theta|
, and so in the coordinates
\{x^{j}\}
we have
\nu_{\theta}=\nu_{\theta}(x)dx
, with
\nu_{\theta}(x)=(n!)^{-1}|(d\theta^{n}\wedge\theta)(\partial_{x^{1}},\ldots,\partial_{x^{2n}},\partial_{x^{0}})|
.
At
x=0
the tangent frame
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{2n},X_{0}\}
is the basis
\{\partial_{x^{1}},\ldots,\partial_{x^{2n}},\partial_{x^{0}}\}
, and so at
x=0
the coframe
\{\theta^{1},\ldots,\theta^{2n},\theta\}
is just
\{dx^{1},\ldots,dx^{2n},dx^{0}\}
. Combining this with (
11.24
) we get

d\theta^{n}\wedge\theta\left|{}_{x=0}\right.=n!dx^{1}\wedge dx^{n+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{n}\wedge dx^{2n}\wedge dx^{0}=\pm n!dx^{0}\wedge dx^{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{2n}.

It then follows that
\nu_{\theta}(0)=1
. Moreover, as the sR dimension is
N=2n+2
we have
\Gamma(1+N/2)=\Gamma(n+2)=(n+1)!
. Therefore, Eq. (
11.14
) gives

(n+1)!\gamma_{\Delta_{H}}(x_{0})=K_{\Delta_{0}}(0,1).

Using (
11.26
) we then get

(11.27)

\gamma_{\Delta_{H}}(x_{0})=\frac{2(4\pi)^{-(n+1)}}{(n+1)!}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\mu}{\sinh\mu}\right)^{n}d\mu.

In particular,
\gamma_{\Delta_{H}}(x_{0})
does not depend on
x_{0}
.

By applying Theorem
11.3
we then arrive at the following semiclassical Weyl law.

Theorem 11.14
.

Given any
q>0
and
V\in C(M,\mathbb{R})
, for any energy level
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

(11.28)

\displaystyle\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{2n+2}N\big(h^{2q}\Delta_{H}^{q}+V;\lambda\big)=\gamma(n)\int_{M}(V(x)-\lambda)_{-}^{\frac{n+1}{q}}d\nu_{\theta}(x).

where the constant
\gamma(n)
is equal to the r.h.s. of (
11.27
).

We also obtain the following integration formulas.

Theorem 11.15
.

For every function
f\in C(M)
, the operators
f\Delta_{H}^{-(n+1)}
,
\Delta_{H}^{-(n+1)/2}f\Delta_{H}^{-(n+1)/2}
, and
|\Delta_{H}^{-(n+1)/2}f\Delta_{H}^{-(n+1)/2}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int f\Delta_{H}^{-(n+1)}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}f\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}=\gamma(n)\int_{M}f(x)d\nu_{\theta}(x),

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\big|\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}f\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}\big|=\gamma(n)\int_{M}|f(x)|d\nu_{\theta}(x).

Remark 11.16
.

As mentioned in Remark
11.7
, the integration formula (
11.15
) is known (see
[
114
,
153
]
). However, Theorem
11.15
provides a stronger form of measurability. The integration formula (
11.15
) seems to be new.

Remark 11.17
.

As mentioned in Remark
11.13
, it is expected to have Cwikel-type estimates for contact manifolds. This would allow us to extend the semiclassical Weyl law (
11.28
) to (real-valued) potentials
V
in
L^{\frac{n+1}{q}}(M)
(if
q<n+1
), in
L\!\log\!L(M)
(if
q=n+1
), or in
L^{1}(M)
(if
q>n+1
).

Remark 11.18

(Step 2 Case)
.

The above considerations in the contact case can be extended to all step 2 sub-Riemannian manifolds, since for step 2 graded nilpotent Lie groups the fundamental solution of the heat equation can be computed explicitly as well (see, e.g.,
[
13
,
14
]
for the codimension 1 case). In this setting, the tangent group
GM(x)
may vary with
x
, and so in general
\gamma_{\Delta_{H}}(x)
need not be constant. However, it still can be computed explicitly. Codimension 1 examples include Cauchy-Riemann manifolds of hypersurface type and even-dimensional contact manifolds in the sense of
[
149
]
. Higher codimension examples include contact quaternionic manifolds
[
18
]
and Cartesian products of contact manifolds. We refer to
[
45
]
, and the references therein, for a more detailed discussion of examples of step 2 sub-Riemannian manifolds.

Remark 11.19

(Kohn Laplacian)
.

The above considerations can be further extended to the Kohn Laplacian
[
110
,
112
]
acting on
(p,q)
-forms on a Cauchy-Riemann manifolds of hypersurface type under the so-called
Y(q)
-condition.The fact that the square root of the Kohn Laplacian yields a spectral triple satisfying Condition (H) follows from the results of
[
14
,
154
]
.

11.5.
Extension to singular sub-Riemannian manifolds

It is expected that the semiclassical Weyl law and integration formulas provided by Theorem
11.3
and Theorem
11.6
can be extended to singular sub-Riemannian manifolds, i.e., when we drop the equiregularity condition. This means that the rank of the distributions
H_{w}(x_{0})
,
w=1,\ldots,r
, in (
11.1
) may vary as
x_{0}
ranges over
M
.

First, we still have spectral triple
(C^{\infty}(M),L_{\nu}^{2}(M),\sqrt{\Delta_{H}})
. This can be seen by applying the results of
[
6
]
to the heat operator
\Delta_{H}+\partial_{t}
. It admits a parametrix in the class of pseudodifferential operators in
[
6
]
. Arguing as in
[
67
,
154
]
we then can show that

\sqrt{\Delta_{H}}
is pseudodifferential operator of order 1 in this class of pseudodifferential operators. Such operators commute with functions modulo operators of order
0
. It then follows that the commutators
[\sqrt{\Delta_{H}},f]
,
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, and so
(C^{\infty}(M),L_{\nu}^{2}(M),\sqrt{\Delta_{H}})
is a spectral triple.

The validity of Condition (H) is a bit more subtle. It is ensured by the local Weyl laws of
[
50
, Theorem 7.1]

for equisingular sub-Riemannian manifolds. Roughly speaking this means that the sub-Riemannian structure induces a regular filtered structure on its singular set (see
[
50
]
for the precise definition). For instance, the Baouendi-Grushin and Martinet examples fall on this class of sub-Riemannian manifolds (see
[
50
]
).

In general, the singular set and the regular set have different sR-dimensions. The leading terms in the local Weyl laws is then provided by the greatest of these sR-dimensions, which then gives the degree of summability of our spectral triple. In case this the sR-dimension of the singular set, then this yields to a singular Weyl measure that is supported on the singular set. If the two srR-dimensions agrees, then the leading term has a logarithmic terms, and so this falls out of the scope of this monograph.

We illustrate the type of results we get in the special case of the Baouendi-Grushin example
1
1

1

In the terminology of
[
50
]
this is the 2-Baouendi-Grushin example.
on the 2-torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}
, with

(11.29)

H=\langle X,Y\rangle,\qquad\Delta_{H}=X^{2}+Y^{2},

where

(11.30)

X:=\partial_{x},\qquad Y:=2(1-\cos x)\partial_{y}.

The singular set is
S=\{0\}\times\mathbb{T}
. Near
x=0
we have
Y=x^{2}\partial_{y}+\operatorname{O}(x^{4})
.Thus,

(11.31)

H_{1}=\langle X,Y\rangle,\quad H_{2}=\langle X,Y,[X,Y]\rangle,\quad H_{3}=\langle X,Y,[X,Y],[X,[X,Y]]\rangle.

In particular, on
\mathbb{T}^{2}\setminus S
, we have
H_{1}=H_{2}=H_{3}=T(\mathbb{T}^{2}\setminus S)
, while on
S
we have

H_{1|TS}=H_{2|TS}=0,\qquad H_{3|TS}=TS.

Thus, the sR-dimension of
\mathbb{T}^{2}\setminus S
is 2, while
S
has sR-dimension
3
. In this example the local Weyl law
[
50
, Theorem 7.1]
and the translation invariance with respect to
y
ensure that Condition (H) holds with
p=3
, and

(11.32)

\tau(f)=c\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(0,y)dy,\qquad f\in C^{\infty}(M),

where
c
is some positive constant. It follows that Theorem
11.3
and Theorem
11.6
continue holds by taking
N=3
and replacing the Weyl measure
\gamma(\Delta_{H})dv(x)
by the
singular
measure
c\delta_{S}
.

Arguably, a number of details need to be checked. Nevertheless, we should expect to get the following semiclassical Weyl law and integration formulas, which we state as conjectures.

Conjecture 11.20

(Semiclassical Weyl law; Baouendi-Grushin example)
.

Let
q>0
. For all potentials
V\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{R})
, we have

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{3}N^{-}\big(h^{2q}\Delta_{H}^{q}+V\big)=c\int_{\mathbb{T}}V_{-}(0,y)^{\frac{3}{2q}}dy.

Conjecture 11.21

(Integration formulas; Baouendi-Grushin example)
.

For all
f\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, the operators
f\Delta_{H}^{-3/2}
,
\Delta_{H}^{-3/4}f\Delta_{H}^{-3/4}
, and
|\Delta_{H}^{-3/4}f\Delta_{H}^{-3/4}|
are spectrally measurable, and we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int f\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{3}{2}}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{3}{4}}f\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{3}{4}}=c\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(0,y)dy,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int\left|\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{3}{4}}f\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{3}{4}}\right|=c\int_{\mathbb{T}}|f(0,y)|dy.

11.6.
Appendix: Connes’ Trace Theorem for
\Psi_{H}
DOs

Near the end of the completion of this manuscript, McDonald
[
131
]
posted on arXiv a version of Connes’ trace theorem for the
\Psi_{H}
DO calculus. In this appendix, we compare McDonald’s result with the integration formulas provided by Theorem
11.6
.

In what follows, for
m\in\mathbb{R}
we denote by
\Psi^{m}_{H}(M)
the space of
\Psi_{H}
DOs of order
m
(see, e.g.,
[
68
,
199
]
for the precise definition of
\Psi_{H}
DOs). We set
\Psi^{\mathbb{Z}}(M)=\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\Psi_{H}^{m}(M)
. Note that
\Psi^{\mathbb{Z}}(M)
is a subalgebra of
\mathscr{L}(C^{\infty}(M))
.

The noncommutative residue of Dave-Haller
[
67
]
extends to arbitrary (regular) filtered manifolds the noncommutative residue for classical
\Psi
DOs of Guillemin
[
98
]
and Wodzicki
[
201
]
, as well as the noncommutative residue of
[
153
]
in the step 2 case (see also
[
62
,
131
,
148
]
for alternative approaches).

The definition stems from the following result.

Proposition 11.22

(
[
67
]
)
.

For every
P\in\Psi_{H}^{m}(M)
,
m\in\mathbb{R}
, the function
z\rightarrow\operatorname{Tr}[P\Delta_{H}^{-z/2}]
is holomorphic for
\Re z>m+N
and admits a meromorphic continuation to
\mathbb{C}
with at worst simple pole singularities on
\{m+k;\ k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
.

Definition 11.23

(
[
67
]
)
.

The
noncommutative residue

\operatorname{Res}_{H}:\Psi_{H}^{\mathbb{Z}}(M)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is the linear functional defined by

(11.33)

\operatorname{Res}_{H}(P):=\operatorname{Res}_{z=0}\operatorname{Tr}\big[P\Delta_{H}^{-\frac{z}{2}}\big],\qquad P\in\Psi_{H}^{\mathbb{Z}}(M).

The following proposition summarizes the main properties of Dave-Haller’s noncommutative residue (see also
[
153
]
for the step 2 case).

Proposition 11.24

(
[
67
]
)
.

The following hold.

(1)

The noncommutative residue (
11.33
) is a trace on the algebra
\Psi_{H}^{\mathbb{Z}}(M)
. It is annihilated by differential operators and
\Psi_{H}
DOs of order
\leq-(N+1)
, including smoothing operators.

(2)

For every
P\in\Psi^{\mathbb{Z}}_{H}(M)
, there is a unique function
c_{P}(x)\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that

\operatorname{Res}(P)=\int_{M}c_{P}(x)d\nu(x).

In addition,

c_{fP}(x)=f(x)c_{P}(x)\qquad\forall f\in C^{\infty}(M).

(3)

For
j=0,1,2,\ldots
, we have

(11.34)

c_{(\Delta_{H})^{\frac{j-N}{2}}}(x)=\frac{2}{\Gamma\big(\frac{N-j}{2}\big)}a_{j}(\Delta_{H})(x).

Dave and Haller further conjectured an analogue Connes’ trace theorem for
\Psi_{H}
DOs (see the end of
[
67
, §7]
). That is, every
P\in\Psi_{H}^{-N}(M)
is measurable, and we have

(11.35)

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int P=\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{Res}_{H}(P).

In the step 2 case a version of this result is mentioned in
[
153
]
. In fact, as pointed by McDonald
[
131
]
(and as known by the author and others), it immediately follows from the definition (
11.33
) and the Tauberian criterion of Sukochev-Zanin
[
193
, Theorem 1.2.7]
(see also
[
129
, Theorem 9.1.5.a]
) that we have an even more general result.

Proposition 11.25

(
[
131
]
)
.

Let
P\in\Psi_{H}^{-N}(M)
. For every normalized trace
\varphi:\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, we have

\varphi(P)=\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{Res}(P).

In particular,
P
is strongly measurable and satisfies (
11.35
)

Bearing this in mind, the functional
\tau
in (
11.16
) can be reinterpreted in terms of Dave-Haller’s noncommutative residue.
By (
11.6
) and (
11.34
) we have

\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)=\frac{1}{\Gamma\big(\frac{N}{2}+1\big)}a_{0}(\Delta_{H})(x)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{N}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{N}{2}+1\big)}c_{(\Delta_{H})^{-\frac{N}{2}}}(x)=\frac{1}{N}c_{(\Delta_{H})^{-\frac{N}{2}}}(x).

Thus, given any
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, we have

\tau(f)=\int_{M}f(x)\gamma(\Delta_{H})(x)\nu(x)=\frac{1}{N}\int_{M}f(x)c_{(\Delta_{H})^{-\frac{N}{2}}}(x)\nu(x).

As by Proposition
11.24
we have
f(x)c_{(\Delta_{H})^{-N/2}}(x)=c_{f(\Delta_{H})^{-N/2}}(x)
, we get

\tau(f)=\frac{1}{N}\int_{M}c_{f(\Delta_{H})^{-\frac{N}{2}}}(x)\nu(x)=\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{Res}_{H}\big(f(\Delta_{H})^{-\frac{N}{2}}\big).

As a result, the integration formula (
11.20
) can be equivalently rewritten in the form,

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int f(\Delta_{H})^{-\frac{N}{2}}=\tau(f)=\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{Res}_{H}\big(f(\Delta_{H})^{-\frac{N}{2}}\big).

This is exactly the trace formula (
11.35
) for
P=f(\Delta_{H})^{-N/2}
.

The notion of measurability in Proposition
11.25
is in terms of all normalized traces on
\mathscr{L}_{1,\infty}
is often called
universal measurability
(see, e.g.,
[
129
]
). It is a stronger property than strong measurability. However, it is neither stronger nor weaker than spectral measurability. For one thing, there are universally measurable operators that are not spectrally mesurable. For the other thing, there are spectrally measurable operators that are not universally measurable (see, e.g.,
[
158
]
). Therefore, although Proposition
11.25
covers a larger class of operators and recapsulates the integration formula (
11.20
), it does not provide the spectral measurability asserted by Theorem
11.6
. In addition, we cannot deduce from the trace theorem (
11.35
) the integration formula (
11.21
) for the absolute value
|\Delta_{H}^{-N/4}f\Delta_{H}^{-N/4}|
, since that operator need not be a
\Psi_{H}
DO.

In any case the conjecture mentioned in Remark
11.2
remains open for general
\Psi_{H}
DOs (see the discussion on this in
[
131
]
).
